AEMCA Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Provides medical oversight, delegates controlled acts, and audits patient care documentation): the Base Hospital Physician is the medical authority who certifies paramedics to perform specific controlled acts under standing directives, provides online medical direction when contacted, and reviews patient care records for quality assurance. This oversight relationship is the legal foundation that allows a Primary Care Paramedic to perform delegated procedures without a physician physically present. Dispatch priorities and municipal budgets fall to other bodies entirely.
  2. The correct answer is C (1, 2 and 4: certifying controlled acts, reviewing documentation, and providing online medical direction): all three are core functions of a Base Hospital Medical Director's oversight role over paramedics. Property tax rates and zoning bylaws belong to municipal government, not to a Base Hospital, so any option including statement 3 or 5 is wrong. This distinction matters because it defines where clinical authority begins and ends within the system.
  3. The correct answer is A (The Emergency Health Regulatory and Accountability Branch): EHRAB, part of the Ministry of Health, certifies paramedics provincially, investigates complaints, and maintains the standards paramedics must meet to keep their certification active. The Ministry of Transportation regulates vehicles and driving generally, municipalities operate services but do not certify individuals, and the CPSO regulates physicians, not paramedics.
  4. The correct answer is D (The upper-tier municipality or its designated delivery agent): the Ambulance Act assigns responsibility for delivering land ambulance service to the upper-tier municipality, which may operate the service directly or contract a delivery agent to do so. Base Hospitals provide medical oversight rather than operational delivery, the Ministry of Health funds and regulates the system rather than delivering service directly, and hospitals receive patients but do not run ambulance operations.
  5. The correct answer is B (1, 2 and 5: falsifying records, performing an unauthorized controlled act, and unauthorized disclosure of health information): each is a serious breach of professional and legal standards that could constitute misconduct. Requesting clarification from Base Hospital is good practice, and a capable patient withdrawing consent must be respected, so statements 3 and 4 describe appropriate conduct rather than misconduct.
  6. The correct answer is A (Use calm, non-confrontational communication and maintain a safe distance): de-escalation begins with a calm approach that avoids provoking further agitation while the crew assesses risk. Immediate restraint is disproportionate when there is no physical threat, refusing to engage until police arrive can worsen the crisis unnecessarily, and confrontation increases the likelihood of escalation rather than reducing it.
  7. The correct answer is C (A Critical Incident Stress Management debriefing): CISM is a structured peer and professional support process specifically designed to help emergency responders process the psychological impact of traumatic calls. A Transfer of Care Report is a clinical handoff document unrelated to crew wellbeing, filing a complaint is unrelated to stress management, and returning to duty without any discussion ignores a recognized occupational health need.
  8. The correct answer is D (1, 3 and 4: intrusive memories, irritability, and emotional numbness): these are all well-documented signs of critical incident stress following a traumatic call. Improved sleep and appetite would indicate the absence of stress rather than its presence, and stress symptoms do not typically resolve completely within an hour, so any option including statement 2 or 5 is incorrect.
  9. The correct answer is B (A concise statement of the chief complaint and patient's condition): leading with this orients the physician immediately to the urgency and nature of the call so they can prioritize their attention appropriately. Medication lists and scene descriptions are important supporting details but belong later in a structured report, and identification details alone provide no clinical context.
Case C0010

Your crew is dispatched priority 3 to a residence for 'a person in crisis.' On arrival, the patient's sister meets you at the door and explains her 34-year-old brother, who has a known history of schizophrenia, stopped taking his medication five days ago. Inside, you find the patient pacing in the living room, speaking rapidly, and stating he wants to end his life. He is oriented to person, place, and time. Vital signs: HR 110, RR 22, BP 138/86, SpO2 98% on room air, GCS 15. He refuses to go to the hospital and asks you to leave.

  1. The correct answer is A: police officers have independent authority under section 17 of the Mental Health Act to take a person into custody for a psychiatric examination when they have reasonable grounds to believe the person meets the statutory criteria, without a physician first completing a Form 1. A physician may complete a Form 1 later, based on their own examination once the person reaches hospital. The other three options misstate the legal basis or the required next steps.
  2. The correct answer is C (His capacity must be specifically assessed regarding this decision, since active suicidal ideation raises genuine doubt about his ability to appreciate the consequences of refusing care): capacity under the Health Care Consent Act is decision-specific and must be evaluated in context, not assumed from orientation alone. A mental illness diagnosis does not automatically remove capacity, but expressed suicidal intent is a significant red flag warranting careful assessment before accepting a refusal.
  3. The correct answer is D (Monitor the patient's condition, maintain safety, and document behaviour and vital signs throughout transport): once police have exercised their Form 1 authority, the paramedic's role shifts to safe clinical transport with ongoing assessment and thorough documentation. Administering sedation requires a specific order, allowing an unrestrained bystander in the compartment compromises safety, and refusing transport would abandon a patient now under lawful apprehension.
  4. The correct answer is B (The objective findings, vital signs, behaviour observed, and the fact a Form 1 was completed by police): a Transfer of Care Report must communicate factual, clinically relevant information that supports continuity of care at the receiving facility. Personal opinions about diagnosis are outside a paramedic's role, a bare priority code provides no clinical value, and unrelated family details are not appropriate for the clinical record.
  5. The correct answer is A (Respect the capable patient's autonomous decision after ensuring it is informed): patient autonomy is a foundational ethical and legal principle, and a capable patient who understands the risks has the right to refuse treatment even when a paramedic disagrees clinically. Overriding the decision, falsifying records, or involving family to override the patient all violate the patient's legal right to make their own informed choice.
  6. The correct answer is D (1, 2 and 4: the consent relates to the treatment, is given voluntarily, and is informed): these are the core elements required under the Health Care Consent Act for consent to be legally valid. Consent must come from the capable patient themselves rather than a family member when the patient is capable, and citizenship has no bearing on consent validity, so statements 3 and 5 are incorrect.
  7. The correct answer is C (Implied consent in an emergency situation): the law presumes a reasonable person would consent to life-saving treatment when they are incapable of expressing a wish and no substitute decision-maker is available, allowing paramedics to act in the patient's best interest. Consent is still required as a legal concept even in emergencies, a bystander has no authority to consent on the patient's behalf, and waiting for police would cause a dangerous delay in a time-critical arrest.
  8. The correct answer is B (The patient's spouse or partner, if available): the Health Care Consent Act sets out a ranked hierarchy of substitute decision-makers, and a spouse or partner ranks near the top, well above more distant relatives. A first responder or an uninvolved neighbour has no legal standing to act as a substitute decision-maker under this hierarchy.
  9. The correct answer is A (The Ambulance Act and its regulations, including O.Reg 257/00): together these establish the certification, conduct, vehicle, and equipment standards specific to land ambulance services in Ontario. The Highway Traffic Act governs general road use, the Coroners Act addresses death investigation, and the Mental Health Act governs psychiatric detention, none of which set ambulance service operating standards.
  10. The correct answer is D (1, 2 and 4: vehicle equipment standards, personnel certification, and inspection recordkeeping): these are all genuine requirements imposed on land ambulance services under O.Reg 257/00. Bypassing documentation during busy periods and self-certification without oversight both directly contradict the regulation's accountability requirements, so any option including statement 3 or 5 is incorrect.
  11. The correct answer is C (Dispatching ambulances, prioritizing calls, and coordinating resource allocation): the Central Ambulance Communications Centre receives calls, assigns response priority, and directs the closest appropriate resource to each incident. It does not employ paramedics directly, set clinical scope-of-practice standards, or conduct individual performance reviews, which fall to employers, EHRAB, and Base Hospitals respectively.
  12. The correct answer is B (72 hours): under the Mental Health Act, a person detained under a Form 1 must be assessed by a physician within 72 hours to determine whether further involuntary detention, such as a Form 3, is warranted. Shorter or much longer periods do not reflect the actual statutory timeline set out in the Act.
  13. The correct answer is D (1, 3 and 4: recent threats of serious harm, a recent suicide attempt, and behaviour suggesting a serious mental disorder with imminent risk): these findings align with the statutory criteria supporting an involuntary psychiatric assessment. A patient calmly declining a non-urgent referral, or simply disagreeing with a paramedic's opinion, does not meet the threshold for involuntary intervention, so statements 2 and 5 are incorrect.
  14. The correct answer is A (A sudden, unexpected, or unexplained death, including one occurring outside a hospital): the Coroners Act requires notification in these circumstances so the death can be properly investigated and classified. Refusals of transport, minor treated injuries, and routine stable inter-facility transfers do not involve a death and therefore do not trigger a coroner notification requirement.
  15. The correct answer is C (Paramedics may withhold or terminate resuscitation and recognize death according to established protocols and medical direction, such as when obvious signs of death are present): this authority is delegated through medical directives rather than being unlimited or dependent on a coroner being physically on scene. Paramedics cannot pronounce death on their own unrestricted judgment, and transporting an obviously deceased patient in every case is neither required nor appropriate.
  16. The correct answer is B (Report the suspicion directly and promptly to a children's aid society): the Child, Youth and Family Services Act 2017 creates a direct, immediate duty to report reasonable grounds for suspicion, without waiting for confirmation from another professional. Delaying until a physician confirms the suspicion, documenting only after a fatal outcome, or waiting for police direction all fail to meet this statutory duty.
  17. The correct answer is D (1, 3 and 4: patterned bruising inconsistent with the explanation, an intoxicated caregiver unable to supervise safely, and delayed care for significant injuries): each of these findings would reasonably raise a suspicion warranting a report. A tidy, safe-looking bedroom and seasonally appropriate clothing are reassuring observations rather than red flags, so statements 2 and 5 do not support a reporting decision.
  18. The correct answer is A (Every person, including paramedics, who has reasonable grounds to suspect, has a legal duty to report): the Child, Youth and Family Services Act 2017 imposes this duty broadly on any person, not just designated professionals, and reporting is mandatory rather than optional. Limiting the duty to physicians and nurses, or to a medical director, misstates how broadly the statute applies.
  19. The correct answer is C (Emergency lights and siren must be activated, and the operator must proceed with due regard for safety): the Highway Traffic Act permits certain exemptions for emergency vehicles only when both audible and visual warning devices are in use and the driver still exercises reasonable care. Ambulances are not exempt from all traffic laws unconditionally, siren alone is insufficient without lights, and no provision requires per-intersection dispatch permission.
  20. The correct answer is B (Exercising reasonable caution appropriate to conditions, even when exempted from certain traffic rules): due regard for safety means an emergency exemption does not remove the driver's ongoing duty to drive reasonably given road, weather, and traffic conditions. Maximum speed regardless of conditions, disregarding pedestrian right-of-way, and offloading driving judgment to dispatch all contradict this legal standard.
Case C0030

Your crew is the first ambulance to arrive at a five-vehicle collision on a divided highway, dispatched as a priority 4 call for a multi-vehicle crash with reported entrapment. On arrival you find five patients: one is walking and yelling for help, one is sitting upright and breathing rapidly but responsive, one is lying still and not breathing until you reposition the airway, one is unresponsive with obvious major external bleeding, and one is walking with only a minor laceration. Additional ambulances and fire crews are still several minutes out.

  1. The correct answer is A (Minor, or green): under START triage, a patient who is walking, breathing normally, and has only a minor injury is categorized as minor, or green, since they do not require immediate intervention. Red, yellow, and black categories are reserved for patients with more urgent or non-survivable conditions, none of which describe this patient.
  2. The correct answer is D (Immediate, or red): a patient who was apneic until the airway was repositioned, and who then breathes spontaneously, is triaged as immediate under START because their airway status was unstable and required intervention to survive. This differs from a truly non-breathing patient with no response to airway repositioning, who would be categorized as deceased or expectant instead.
  3. The correct answer is C (Establish scene size-up, declare a multiple casualty incident, and begin triage while requesting additional resources): with five patients and limited resources, the first crew's priority is to size up the scene, formally declare an MCI so appropriate resources are dispatched, and begin rapid triage rather than committing fully to one patient. Loading and transporting a single patient alone, waiting passively for command, or treating only responsive patients would all leave the majority of patients unassessed.
  4. The correct answer is B (The approximate number of patients, their severity mix, and the resources needed): this information allows dispatch and an arriving Incident Commander to allocate the correct number and type of additional resources quickly. Vehicle details, bystander names, and detailed medical histories are not time-critical for initial resource coordination at a multiple casualty incident.
  5. The correct answer is D (To communicate the patient's condition, treatment, and relevant history to the receiving facility): the Transfer of Care Report exists to ensure continuity of care by transferring clinically relevant information from the paramedic crew to hospital staff. It is not a billing document, does not replace the value of a verbal handover, and captures far more than administrative details like mileage.
  6. The correct answer is A (It serves as the legal record of care and may be used in future proceedings or reviews): documentation created at the time of care is treated as the authoritative account of what occurred and can be relied upon in complaints, investigations, or legal proceedings well after the call. Suggesting it has no legal significance, is optional when there are no complications, or only matters after a complaint all understate its ongoing importance.
  7. The correct answer is C (1, 3 and 4: objective findings, treatments and responses, and time-stamped entries): a defensible record is built on factual, chronological, and objective information. Personal speculation about a patient's home life is inappropriate and unsupported opinion, and leaving blanks for someone else to complete later undermines the record's accuracy and integrity, so statements 2 and 5 are incorrect.
  8. The correct answer is B (Respect the capable patient's decision, offer information on support resources, and document the disclosure accurately): a capable adult retains the right to decide whether police become involved in their own situation, and the paramedic's role is to support, inform, and document rather than override that choice. Forcing police involvement, withholding transport, or ignoring the disclosure all fail to respect the patient's autonomy or provide appropriate care.
  9. The correct answer is A (A capable adult's wishes about reporting are generally respected, while suspected child abuse or neglect carries a mandatory duty to report): this reflects the different legal frameworks that apply to autonomous capable adults versus children, who require additional protection under the Child, Youth and Family Services Act 2017. Claiming no difference exists, reversing the obligations, or removing documentation requirements entirely all misstate this distinction.
  10. The correct answer is A (Keeping the load close to the body, bending at the knees, and using proper team lifting techniques): this technique reduces strain on the spine and distributes effort more safely across the body and team. Bending at the waist with a straight back and lifting mainly with the arms, twisting while carrying a load, and lifting alone whenever possible all significantly increase the risk of musculoskeletal injury.
  11. The correct answer is C (1, 3 and 4: communicating before the lift, using mechanical aids, and assessing weight and path beforehand): these are all recognized safe patient-handling practices that reduce injury risk. Twisting at the waist while carrying a load and reaching at maximum extension both increase strain and injury risk significantly, so statements 2 and 5 describe unsafe rather than safe practices.
  12. The correct answer is B (Lights and siren should generally be reserved for situations where the time saved outweighs the increased risk): emergency driving carries measurably higher collision risk, so its use should be justified by genuine clinical urgency rather than applied automatically. Lights and siren are not legally mandated for every transport, and the choice to use them does have a direct bearing on both public and crew safety.
  13. The correct answer is D (Crew members and patients should be secured whenever safely possible, given the elevated risk of ambulance collisions): ambulances are involved in a disproportionate share of serious collisions, making restraint use an important safety practice for everyone in the vehicle. Treating restraints as purely optional, exempting all but the driver, or never securing patients all ignore this well-documented safety risk.
  14. The correct answer is A (Decline to perform the procedure and explain the limits of scope of practice, seeking medical direction if appropriate): a paramedic cannot legally perform acts outside their certified scope and existing directives, regardless of family pressure, and must explain this clearly while offering to contact Base Hospital if further direction might apply. Performing the procedure anyway, doing it covertly, or shifting responsibility to an untrained family member all create serious safety and liability problems.
  15. The correct answer is B (1, 3 and 4): an out-of-parameters presentation, an unusual complication, and a treatment needing specific authorization): each of these genuinely warrants contacting Base Hospital for online medical direction. Confirming a routine, fully authorized skill or discussing unrelated scheduling issues are not appropriate uses of a medical direction call, so statements 2 and 5 are incorrect.
  16. The correct answer is B (The patient's right to privacy and confidentiality of personal health information): sharing identifiable patient details publicly breaches both professional standards and privacy legislation protecting personal health information, regardless of whether the call has ended. This has nothing to do with collision reporting or coroner notification requirements, which govern entirely different situations.
  17. The correct answer is D (That the refusal is informed, voluntary, and thoroughly documented, ideally with Base Hospital contact for guidance): a valid refusal requires confirming the patient understands the risks, is choosing freely, and that this is clearly recorded, often supported by contacting Base Hospital in higher-risk situations. Avoiding documentation, having family override a capable patient, or automatically involving police all fail to protect the patient's rights or the paramedic's legal position.
  18. The correct answer is C (1, 2 and 4: understanding the relevant information, appreciating the consequences, and communicating a consistent choice): these are the recognized components of a capacity assessment under the Health Care Consent Act. Whether the paramedic personally agrees with the decision, or whether the patient has any past mental illness diagnosis, is not part of the legal capacity test, so statements 3 and 5 are incorrect.
  19. The correct answer is A (Active listening, using a calm tone, and validating the patient's feelings): these techniques help a distressed patient feel heard and reduce the intensity of the crisis, supporting a safer interaction. Speaking loudly, interrupting frequently, or avoiding eye contact and staying silent all tend to increase distress or communicate disengagement rather than support.
  20. The correct answer is D (Correct the error following proper documentation amendment practices and notify appropriate parties as required): an identified documentation error must be corrected transparently, typically with a dated addendum rather than an alteration that hides the original entry, and relevant parties should be informed if the error could affect patient care. Leaving it unchanged, destroying the record, or having a colleague alter it covertly all compromise the record's integrity and create serious legal exposure.
  21. The correct answer is B (Paramedics are accountable to their patients, medical direction, their employer, and the regulatory framework governing their certification): professional accountability in Ontario's ambulance system is multi-layered, extending well beyond any single relationship. Limiting accountability to dispatch alone, ending it at hospital transfer, or restricting it to multiple casualty incidents all understate the full scope of a paramedic's professional and legal responsibilities.
Case C0051

You are dispatched code 4 to a single-vehicle rollover on a rural two-lane highway. On arrival you find a 34-year-old male driver who was partially ejected and is now standing beside the heavily damaged vehicle. He is alert, oriented to person and place but slow and inconsistent when asked the date and time, and smells strongly of alcohol. Vitals: BP 132/84, HR 96, RR 18, SpO2 97% on room air, GCS 14. He has a 4 cm forehead laceration and is refusing transport, stating he feels fine and wants to walk home.

  1. The correct answer is A (His inability to reliably state the current date and time and his GCS of 14): capacity under the Health Care Consent Act requires that a patient be able to understand the information relevant to a decision and appreciate the reasonably foreseeable consequences of refusing care. Disorientation to time combined with a reduced GCS directly undermines that ability, regardless of how alert the patient otherwise appears. A laceration or a mildly elevated heart rate do not themselves affect decision-making capacity. The smell of alcohol alone is not proof of incapacity, since intoxication must actually impair understanding to be relevant.
  2. The correct answer is C (The attending paramedic, applying the recognized capacity criteria on scene): Ontario paramedics are trained and expected to assess a patient's capacity to consent to or refuse treatment in real time using recognized criteria, consulting the Base Hospital physician for complex or borderline cases rather than as a routine requirement. Police have no authority to make a medical capacity determination. A hospital physician cannot assess a patient who has not yet been transported, which is precisely the decision facing the crew on scene.
  3. The correct answer is B (Document the capacity assessment, the risks explained, and obtain a witness signature): a defensible refusal of treatment requires clear documentation showing the capacity assessment performed, the risks and consequences explained to the patient, and ideally an independent witness signature confirming the refusal was informed and voluntary. Leaving the report blank or omitting vital signs creates a serious legal and clinical gap. A tow operator has no relevant standing to witness a medical refusal.
  4. The correct answer is D (The driver must exercise due regard for the safety of everyone on the roadway): the Highway Traffic Act grants ambulance drivers certain exemptions when responding with lights and siren, but it explicitly requires the driver to exercise due regard for the safety of all persons using the highway at all times. These privileges do not eliminate liability for a collision, do not permit unlimited speed, and right-of-way applies continuously through an intersection, not only after clearing it.
  5. The correct answer is B (The patient's vital signs, mechanism of injury, and treatment provided en route): a proper patch report gives the receiving facility the clinical information needed to prepare for the patient, including vital signs, mechanism of injury, and any treatment already provided. Vehicle details, the tow company, and the officer's badge number are administrative details that do not belong in a clinical handoff report.
  6. The correct answer is A (Setting provincial standards and certifying paramedics and ambulance services): the Emergency Health Regulatory and Accountability Branch sets the provincial standards for paramedic certification and ambulance service operation and oversees compliance with them. It does not directly employ paramedics, operate dispatch centres, or negotiate union contracts, all of which are handled by individual services, municipalities, and other bodies.
  7. The correct answer is D (1, 3 and 4): anaphylaxis classically produces bronchospasm with wheezing as histamine-mediated bronchoconstriction develops, hypotension with reflex tachycardia as vascular permeability increases and fluid shifts out of the vasculature, and stridor from laryngeal edema as the upper airway swells. Bradycardia with hypertension and pinpoint non-reactive pupils are not features of anaphylaxis and instead suggest other unrelated conditions.
  8. The correct answer is C (Delegation of a controlled act under the Regulated Health Professions Act): a medical directive works by having a physician delegate a controlled act, such as administering a medication, to a certified paramedic in advance under defined conditions, which is authorized under the Regulated Health Professions Act. It is not a per-call verbal permission, a general licence from a physicians' college, or a special exemption limited to volunteers.
  9. The correct answer is C (Required equipment, minimum staffing levels, and vehicle response standards): O. Reg. 257/00 under the Ambulance Act sets the operational standards a certified land ambulance service must meet, including equipment carried, staffing levels, and vehicle standards. Medication dosing is governed by medical directives, wages by collective agreements, and criminal assault by the Criminal Code, none of which fall under this regulation.
  10. The correct answer is B (1, 3 and 4): valid informed consent under the Health Care Consent Act must relate specifically to the treatment being proposed, must be given voluntarily without coercion, and requires that the patient receive the information a reasonable person in the same circumstances would need to make the decision. Written consent is not a general requirement for prehospital care, and no provision requires a police witness for consent to be valid.
  11. The correct answer is A (Offer access to a Critical Incident Stress debriefing and peer support program): services have a responsibility to support crews after traumatic calls through structured critical incident stress programs and peer support, which help reduce the risk of longer-term psychological harm. Ignoring the reaction, reassigning the paramedic without discussion, or discouraging any conversation about the call can all worsen outcomes and are inconsistent with recognized crisis intervention practice.
  12. The correct answer is D (Confidentiality, since patient information was disclosed without consent or clinical need): sharing details of a patient's call with coworkers who had no role in providing care and without the patient's consent is a breach of confidentiality, a core ethical and legal obligation in paramedicine. It does not relate to beneficence, justice, or non-maleficence, which concern the care provided rather than how information about a patient is subsequently handled.
  13. The correct answer is B (1, 3 and 4): in the START triage system, a respiratory rate over 30, an absent radial pulse or delayed capillary refill, and an inability to follow simple commands after airway repositioning all classify a patient as Immediate or Red. A patient who can walk unassisted is triaged Green, and normal respirations with a strong pulse in a patient who otherwise cannot walk would place them in the Delayed, or Yellow, category rather than Red.
  14. The correct answer is C (Decapitation, or a decomposed body showing established rigor mortis): the recognized obvious death criteria include findings such as decapitation, decomposition, and established rigor mortis, which are clearly incompatible with life and permit resuscitation to be withheld. A palpable pulse, any respiratory effort however slow, and reactive pupils are all signs that resuscitation must be attempted rather than withheld.
  15. The correct answer is D (Apprehend and transport the patient for a psychiatric examination by a physician): a Form 2 issued by a Justice of the Peace authorizes police to locate, apprehend, and bring a person to a physician for a psychiatric examination when there is reasonable cause for concern. It does not itself admit the patient to hospital, authorize medication administration by police, or allow a paramedic to independently certify a psychiatric hold.
  16. The correct answer is A (1, 3 and 4): a complete and legally defensible Ambulance Call Report requires accurate timestamps for key events, every vital sign set obtained, and a full record of treatments provided along with the patient's response. Personal opinions about a patient's character have no place in an objective clinical record, and crew home addresses are irrelevant to patient care documentation.
  17. The correct answer is A (A mandatory duty to report the reasonable suspicion to a children's aid society): the Child, Youth and Family Services Act, 2017 imposes a mandatory duty on any person, including a paramedic, who has reasonable grounds to suspect a child may be in need of protection to report that suspicion directly to a children's aid society. Reporting is not conditional on caregiver consent, on a physician's later confirmation, or limited to only the most severe visible injuries.
  18. The correct answer is D (Keeping the load close to the body and lifting primarily with the legs): proper lifting technique keeps the load as close to the body's centre of gravity as possible and uses the strength of the legs rather than the lower back to generate lifting force. Bending at the waist with straight legs, twisting during the lift, and holding the load away from the body all significantly increase the risk of back injury.
  19. The correct answer is C (1, 3 and 5): repeated exposure to severe trauma, chronic short-staffing with mandatory overtime, and repeated exposure to violence against the crew are all recognized contributors to cumulative critical incident stress over a career. Adequate sleep, regular days off, and a strong peer support culture are protective factors that reduce cumulative stress rather than contribute to it.
Case C0070

You respond to a residence for a 27-year-old woman whose sister called 911 after she began pacing, speaking rapidly about being followed, and threatening to jump from the balcony. On arrival, the patient is agitated, pacing near the open balcony door, and refuses to speak with the crew. Police arrive simultaneously and the sister reports the patient has a diagnosed psychiatric illness and stopped her medication one week ago. The patient has no visible injuries and stable vital signs.

  1. The correct answer is B (Section 17 of the Mental Health Act, based on reasonable grounds of danger to herself): section 17 allows a police officer to apprehend a person and bring them for examination without a physician's form when the officer has reasonable and probable grounds to believe the person is acting or has acted in a disorderly manner and has reasonable cause to believe the person is likely to cause serious harm to themselves or others. The Health Care Consent Act, a Base Hospital order, and the Coroners Act have no bearing on this specific apprehension authority.
  2. The correct answer is D (Use a calm voice, keep a safe distance, and acknowledge her feelings openly): effective de-escalation with an agitated patient in psychiatric crisis relies on a calm, non-threatening approach, maintaining a safe physical distance, and validating the patient's emotions rather than dismissing them. Sudden physical contact, commanding tones, and positioning that feels confrontational can all escalate the situation and increase the risk of harm.
  3. The correct answer is B (The Mental Health Act authorizes transport, with treatment following HCCA emergency provisions where needed): the Mental Health Act provides the legal authority for the apprehension and transport of the patient, while any necessary treatment continues to be governed by the Health Care Consent Act, including its emergency treatment provisions where the patient lacks capacity. Police involvement does not eliminate the patient's rights, and a family member's consent cannot override the patient's own refusal outside a recognized substitute decision maker process.
  4. The correct answer is A (Ensure the completed apprehension form accompanies the patient to the receiving hospital): the physician's or Justice of the Peace's form authorizing apprehension must travel with the patient to the receiving facility so hospital staff have the legal documentation supporting the patient's involuntary status. Destroying it, letting the patient hold it unsupervised, or omitting the officer's information would all undermine the continuity and legal integrity of the process.
  5. The correct answer is C (Respect her autonomy, document the findings objectively, and offer support resources): a competent adult patient has the right to make her own decisions, including whether police become involved, and the paramedic's role is to respect that autonomy while still documenting objective findings and offering information about available support services. Reporting against her wishes, withholding care, or contacting her partner directly would all be inappropriate and could increase her risk.
  6. The correct answer is C (1, 3 and 4): bruising in multiple stages of healing, unexplained weight loss with dehydration, and pressure ulcers inconsistent with the documented level of care are all recognized indicators that should raise suspicion of abuse or neglect in a long-term care setting. A single bruise consistent with routine IV access and a clean, well-groomed appearance are expected findings and do not by themselves suggest a problem.
  7. The correct answer is A (Provincial legislation, applicable medical directives, and the paramedic's certification level): a paramedic's scope of practice is defined by the combination of provincial legislation, the medical directives issued through their Base Hospital, and the specific certification level they hold. Personal experience alone, an individual hospital's preferences, or informal arrangements with physicians do not define legal scope of practice.
  8. The correct answer is D (Upper-tier municipalities, or another delivery agent designated to provide the service): under the Ambulance Act, upper-tier municipalities are generally responsible for ensuring the delivery of land ambulance services within their boundaries, either directly or through a designated delivery agent. The federal government, individual hospitals, and provincial police play no role in the statutory responsibility for delivering the service itself.
  9. The correct answer is B (1, 3 and 4): an effective patch report includes the unit identifier, patient age and sex, the chief complaint with relevant assessment findings, and vital signs along with any treatment already given, all of which the receiving physician needs to issue an appropriate order. Personal opinions about the patient and the name of an unrelated family doctor add no clinical value to the report.
  10. The correct answer is B (Use a spotter positioned where the driver can see them at all times): backing an ambulance safely in a busy environment requires a spotter positioned within the driver's clear line of sight to guide the movement and warn of hazards. Relying only on a camera, using a horn instead of a spotter, or rushing the maneuver all increase the risk of a collision with a pedestrian or another vehicle.
  11. The correct answer is D (1, 3 and 4): the Health Care Consent Act hierarchy recognizes a court-appointed guardian, a spouse or partner, and a child of the patient who is at least sixteen years old as potential substitute decision makers, ranked in a specific order of priority. A stranger who witnessed the event and a parking attendant have no legal standing under the hierarchy regardless of their presence at the scene.
  12. The correct answer is C (Leave the body and immediate surrounding scene undisturbed for investigation): when a death appears to require a coroner's investigation, the scene and body should be left as undisturbed as possible to preserve evidence for the coroner and any accompanying police investigation. Moving the body, removing resuscitation equipment, or cleaning the area could all compromise the investigation and should be avoided.
  13. The correct answer is A (An experienced paramedic specifically assigned to carry out that function): the Triage Officer role at a multiple casualty incident is filled by an experienced paramedic assigned specifically to rapidly assess and categorize patients using a system such as START. A police officer without medical training, a bystanding family member, or a physician contacted remotely by phone are not appropriate choices for this hands-on clinical function.
  14. The correct answer is A (1, 3 and 4): once care begins, a paramedic owes a duty not to abandon the patient, a duty to act within the accepted standard of practice, and a duty to provide care consistent with their training and certification. No paramedic can guarantee a full recovery, and there is no legal duty to disclose a patient's condition to bystanders who have no role in their care.
  15. The correct answer is B (It may be used as evidence in court and reflects the standard of care provided): the Ambulance Call Report is a legal document that can be entered as evidence in civil or criminal proceedings and serves as the primary record of the standard of care delivered on a call. It is not purely internal, is not routinely destroyed after a short period, and directly reflects on a paramedic's professional accountability.
  16. The correct answer is D (Come to a complete stop, or slow enough to proceed through safely): drivers responding with emergency signals active must still proceed through a red light only after stopping or slowing enough to safely confirm the intersection is clear, since the exemption does not remove the duty of due regard for other road users. Proceeding at normal speed, assuming others will yield, or treating the red light as a green light all ignore this ongoing legal responsibility.
  17. The correct answer is C (1, 3 and 4): appropriate crisis intervention for a suicidal patient includes directly and calmly asking about a specific plan, removing obvious means of self-harm from the immediate area, and maintaining continuous one-to-one observation during transport. Leaving the patient unattended, even briefly, and arguing with the patient about their feelings are both inappropriate and can increase risk.
  18. The correct answer is D (Autonomy, which obligates the crew to respect a capable patient's own informed choice): the principle of autonomy requires that a capable adult's informed decision, including a refusal of treatment, be respected even when the crew disagrees with it. Beneficence and non-maleficence relate to acting in the patient's interest and avoiding harm, and justice relates to equitable access, none of which override a capable patient's own choice.
  19. The correct answer is C (An infant with injuries that are inconsistent with the caregiver's explanation): an injury pattern that does not match the explanation given is a classic and well-recognized red flag for abuse that meets the reporting threshold under the Child, Youth and Family Services Act, 2017. A well-explained routine bruise, a curfew disagreement, and a single missed immunization do not raise the same reasonable suspicion of protection concerns.
  20. The correct answer is A (1, 2 and 4): Ontario's medical oversight system issues medical directives delegating controlled acts, conducts quality assurance reviews of paramedic performance, and approves new certified skills for practice. Base Hospitals and the province do not directly employ paramedics working for individual services, and setting municipal tax rates has nothing to do with medical oversight functions.
  21. The correct answer is B (Maintaining a therapeutic, professional relationship focused solely on the patient's care): professional boundaries require paramedics to keep their relationship with a patient centred on clinical care rather than personal connection, even when a grateful patient initiates contact. Exchanging personal contact information, accepting gifts, or sharing personal opinions about a patient's life all blur an appropriate professional boundary.
  22. The correct answer is C (Slow down and visually clear each lane before proceeding through the intersection): safe practice at a controlled intersection against the signal requires the driver to slow down and visually confirm that each lane is actually clear before proceeding, since other drivers may not hear or see the ambulance in time. Increasing speed, assuming compliance, or following closely behind another vehicle already crossing all significantly increase collision risk.
  23. The correct answer is D (1, 2 and 5): Incident Commander, Triage Officer, and Treatment Officer are all recognized functional roles within a standard incident management structure used at multiple casualty incidents. A chief financial officer role and a media relations spokesperson drawn from the ambulance crew are not established functions within this structure, which focuses on command, triage, treatment, transportation, and staging.
  24. The correct answer is B (The emergency treatment exception permits necessary care to proceed without consent): the Health Care Consent Act includes an emergency treatment exception that permits urgent, necessary care to proceed without consent when a patient is incapable and no substitute decision maker is available in time to avoid serious harm. Waiting for a court order, a supervisor's waiver, or the patient's own recovery of consciousness would all cause dangerous delay in a genuine emergency.
  25. The correct answer is A (A physician, authorizing up to 72 hours of psychiatric assessment in hospital): a Form 1 under the Mental Health Act is completed by a physician who has personally examined the patient and believes they meet the criteria for involuntary assessment, and it authorizes up to 72 hours of psychiatric assessment in hospital. It is not issued by police, a paramedic supervisor, or a Justice of the Peace, and it does not authorize field sedation or discharge.
  26. The correct answer is A (1, 3 and 4): an acceptable correction involves a single line through the error with initials, a dated addendum if the error is discovered after submission, and writing the correct information clearly alongside the original entry so both remain visible. Using correction fluid to obscure an entry or discarding and rewriting a report from memory both destroy the integrity of the original record and are not acceptable practices.
  27. The correct answer is C (Communicate clearly and move in a slow, controlled, coordinated manner together): moving a patient on a stair chair safely depends on clear communication between crew members and slow, coordinated movement to maintain control throughout the descent. Rushing, leaving the patient's arms unsecured, or having only one crew member maintain contact with the chair all increase the risk of a fall or injury.
  28. The correct answer is D (The privileges exist but do not remove the driver's duty to exercise due regard): the Highway Traffic Act grants real right-of-way privileges to an ambulance responding with emergency signals active, but these privileges never remove the driver's ongoing duty to exercise due regard for the safety of other road users. The privileges apply on all roadways within the province and are not suspended by the presence of another emergency vehicle.
  29. The correct answer is B (Report the error through the service's established incident reporting process): professional accountability requires reporting a protocol deviation through the service's formal incident reporting process, even when the patient was not harmed, so that the issue can be reviewed and addressed. Staying silent, altering the report, or only discussing it informally with a coworker all fail to meet this standard of accountability.
  30. The correct answer is C (Document objective findings and discreetly offer resource information to the patient): when a patient denies concerns in the presence of a controlling companion, the appropriate response is to document objective findings and discreetly provide resource information without pressuring the patient or alerting the companion. Confronting the companion or refusing transport could place the patient in greater danger, and insisting on an admission is not an appropriate use of the crew's role.
  31. The correct answer is D (A sudden death occurring during or shortly after police custody or restraint): the Coroners Act requires notification for deaths that are sudden and unexpected or occur under specific circumstances, including deaths during or shortly after police custody or restraint, so that the circumstances can be independently investigated. An expected palliative death, a death after a long anticipated illness, and an expected death documented in a care plan do not meet this mandatory reporting threshold.
  32. The correct answer is C (Certifying individual paramedics and monitoring land ambulance service performance standards): EHRAB, the Ministry of Health's Emergency Health Regulatory and Accountability Branch, certifies paramedics and monitors service performance under the Ambulance Act. It does not directly employ paramedics, since municipalities and delivery agents are the employers. It does not write medical directives, since Base Hospital Programs and their medical advisory committees do that, and it does not operate 911 call answer centres.
  33. The correct answer is D (Contact the Base Hospital physician for online medical direction before proceeding): situations outside standing medical directives require online, or patch, medical direction from a Base Hospital physician before a paramedic proceeds. Withholding needed care is not appropriate, applying directives beyond one's certification exceeds scope, and a family physician has no role in prehospital medical oversight.
  34. The correct answer is B (1, 2 and 4): Base Hospital Programs provide online medical direction, review documentation for defects as part of quality assurance, and deliver continuing medical education and certification maintenance. Setting municipal tax rates and hiring or dispatching paramedics are municipal and operator functions, not Base Hospital responsibilities.
  35. The correct answer is D (Upper-tier municipalities or their officially designated ambulance delivery agents): the Ambulance Act assigns responsibility for ensuring land ambulance service delivery to upper-tier municipalities or their designated delivery agents. The federal government, hospital emergency departments, and the College of Physicians and Surgeons of Ontario have no such statutory responsibility under this Act.
  36. The correct answer is A (Actively listening and acknowledging the patient's feelings while remaining calm and present): effective crisis intervention involves active listening, empathy, and a calm, present demeanour that validates the patient's emotional state. Minimizing the reaction, deflecting to logistics, or judging the response as disproportionate all undermine trust and worsen the crisis.
  37. The correct answer is D (1, 2 and 4): building rapport before probing for details, maintaining a calm steady tone regardless of the patient's agitation, and giving simple clear explanations of each step are recognized crisis intervention principles. Taking over every decision removes patient autonomy, and avoiding eye contact is not a taught de-escalation technique.
  38. The correct answer is D (To provide peer support and reduce the risk of long-term psychological harm): Critical Incident Stress Management debriefings are structured peer-support sessions meant to help responders process traumatic events and lower the risk of conditions such as PTSD. They are not intended to assign blame, generate disciplinary evidence, or replace mandatory patient documentation.
  39. The correct answer is C (The patient's age, chief complaint, and pertinent vital signs): a concise, structured patch report leads with age, chief complaint, and relevant vitals so the physician can quickly grasp the clinical picture. Personal opinions, an exhaustive history recitation, and unrelated logistics like arrival time at the station do not belong at the start of a patch.
  40. The correct answer is B (1, 3 and 4, matching the timestamps, vitals and identifying details required): a compliant Ambulance Call Report must record dispatch, arrival, and departure times, vital signs and treatments with corresponding times, and patient identification along with the receiving facility. A paramedic's personal cell number and home address have no place in a patient care record.
  41. The correct answer is C (To communicate essential patient information for continuity of care): the verbal Transfer of Care Report ensures the receiving team has the clinical information needed to continue care seamlessly. It is not primarily a billing document, and the paramedic's legal duty of care ends only once the receiving facility has formally accepted responsibility, not simply because a verbal report was delivered.
  42. The correct answer is C (Permitted certain exemptions but must still drive with due regard for the safety of others): the Highway Traffic Act grants specific exemptions to emergency vehicles operating with both lights and siren active, but the driver must still exercise due regard for the safety of others. This is not blanket immunity from all traffic laws, drivers are not restricted to posted limits during an emergency response, and cautious progression through a red light is permitted, not prohibited.
  43. The correct answer is D (1, 3 and 5): lawful use of Highway Traffic Act emergency exemptions requires both audible and visual warning devices to be active, slowing sufficiently to confirm the way is clear, and exercising due regard for the safety of others throughout. There is no minimum years-of-experience requirement, and the exemption is not conditioned solely on how dispatch classified the call.
  44. The correct answer is B (Deceased or expectant, Black, since resuscitation is not attempted here): in START triage, a patient who remains apneic after the airway has been repositioned is classified as deceased or expectant, Black, since limited resources are directed toward salvageable patients first. Red applies to a compromised but treatable airway or breathing, Yellow to delayed treatment needs, and Green to the walking wounded.
  45. The correct answer is A (Using a power lift technique, keeping the load close and lifting with the legs): the power lift, which keeps the back straight, the load close to the body, and the lifting force generated by the legs, is the recognized safe-lifting method. Bending at the waist with straight legs, twisting under load, and rushing the lift all substantially increase injury risk.
Case C0115

Dispatch sends your crew to a multi-vehicle collision on a rural highway reported as multiple patients, vehicles on fire. On arrival you find three vehicles involved. You are the first paramedic crew on scene and are assigned as triage officer while your partner begins scene safety and staging for incoming units. You begin a rapid START-based sweep of the scene.

  1. The correct answer is B (Ensuring overall scene safety and calling for additional resources as needed): a triage officer's very first priority on arrival is confirming scene safety and requesting the resources the incident actually requires, before beginning individual patient assessments. Full physical exams, detailed histories, and interventions like IV access are deliberately deferred until the rapid triage sweep is complete.
  2. The correct answer is C (Minor, Green, since ambulatory ability suggests adequate perfusion): under START triage, a patient who is ambulatory and able to walk to a designated area on command is categorized as Minor, Green, because the ability to walk indicates adequate perfusion, breathing, and mental status for this lower acuity category. Red, Yellow, and Black all describe patients with more significant compromise or who are unsalvageable.
  3. The correct answer is D (Immediate, Red, given the compromised breathing and perfusion shown): a respiratory rate above 30, a weak or absent radial pulse, or an inability to follow simple commands each independently triggers a Red, Immediate classification under START. This patient shows compromise across breathing, perfusion, and mental status simultaneously, placing them clearly in the Immediate category rather than Minor, Delayed, or deceased/expectant.
  4. The correct answer is C (Delayed, Yellow, since breathing, perfusion and mental status are intact): this patient has normal respirations, a strong radial pulse indicating adequate perfusion, and intact mental status, but has an injury requiring treatment that is not immediately life-threatening. That combination places the patient in the Delayed, Yellow category rather than Minor, Immediate, or deceased/expectant.
Case C0119

You respond to a private residence for a 78-year-old woman found by her daughter to be confused and febrile. The daughter states her mother has a history of dementia but has been managing at home with support. On assessment the patient is oriented to person only, has a temperature of 39.4 degrees Celsius, and repeatedly states she does not want to go to the hospital.

  1. The correct answer is B (Whether the patient currently has the capacity to understand and appreciate the consequences of refusing care): under the Health Care Consent Act, 1996, capacity is decision-specific and must be assessed at the time the decision is being made. A dementia diagnosis alone does not establish incapacity, family agreement cannot substitute for the patient's own capacity, and a paramedic's personal opinion of what is best cannot override a capable patient's refusal.
  2. The correct answer is C (Conduct a more thorough capacity assessment and consider contacting a Base Hospital physician for guidance): significant confusion, disorientation to place and time, and a high fever together raise real doubt about whether this patient can understand the risks of refusing care, warranting a more thorough capacity assessment and physician guidance. Restraining without assessment, leaving on the assumption family cannot consent, and having a neighbour co-sign the refusal are all inappropriate given the doubt about capacity.
  3. The correct answer is A (A court-appointed guardian, or otherwise the next ranked person such as a spouse): the Health Care Consent Act sets out a specific hierarchy of substitute decision-makers beginning with a court-appointed guardian, then a named power of attorney for personal care, then a spouse or partner, then children or parents, and so on. A paramedic, a random bystander, and a service manager have no standing anywhere in this hierarchy.
  4. The correct answer is A (The capacity assessment findings, the substitute decision-maker's identity, and the consent given): thorough documentation should capture the capacity assessment findings, who provided substitute consent and their relationship to the patient, and the consent that was ultimately given, since this record may be relied on legally. Omitting the capacity issue, dropping the later consent, or claiming no documentation is needed would all leave an incomplete and legally indefensible record.
  5. The correct answer is D (Authorization to hold the patient for psychiatric assessment for up to 72 hours): a Form 1 under the Mental Health Act authorizes holding a patient for psychiatric assessment for up to 72 hours when a physician has reasonable grounds for concern. It does not transfer decision-making authority to family, does not constitute a criminal charge, and does not itself produce an automatic 30-day involuntary admission.
  6. The correct answer is B (1, 3 and 5): section 17 of the Mental Health Act allows a police officer to apprehend a person when there are reasonable grounds to believe the person has threatened or attempted self-harm, has acted violently or caused fear of bodily harm, or is behaving in a disorderly manner suggesting a mental disorder likely to result in serious harm. An outstanding parking violation and a simple family request are not grounds for apprehension under this section.
  7. The correct answer is A (Disturb the scene as little as possible and notify the appropriate authorities): when death has clearly occurred under unclear circumstances, obligations under the Coroners Act call for minimizing disturbance to the scene and notifying police and the coroner's office. Repositioning the body, cleaning the area, or removing medications could destroy evidence relevant to determining the cause and circumstances of death.
  8. The correct answer is B (A sudden, unexpected death of a healthy young adult with no attending physician): the Coroners Act requires notification for deaths that are sudden, unexpected, or otherwise unexplained, particularly when no physician was attending who can certify the cause. A death under ongoing physician care for a known illness, an expected decline in long-term care, or a death following a consented DNR order are generally not reportable on that basis alone.
  9. The correct answer is D (1, 3 and 5): the Child, Youth and Family Services Act, 2017 creates a duty to report reasonable grounds to suspect physical harm inflicted by a caregiver, denial of necessary medical treatment, or sexual abuse of a child. A child who appears well cared for with no concerns, or a parent declining a non-urgent inspection, does not on its own meet the threshold of reasonable grounds to suspect a child is in need of protection.
  10. The correct answer is C (Directly and without delay, regardless of any other reporting the paramedic believes has occurred): the Child, Youth and Family Services Act, 2017 requires a person who forms reasonable grounds to suspect a child is in need of protection to report directly and promptly to a children's aid society, and this personal duty is not satisfied by assuming someone else has already reported. It does not depend on parental permission, is not deferred until end-of-shift paperwork is finished, and does not require prior supervisor approval.
  11. The correct answer is D (The capable adult generally decides whether to involve police, though facility duties may apply): unlike the mandatory duty to report suspected child abuse, a capable adult generally retains the right to decide whether police or other authorities become involved, though certain facilities such as long-term care homes carry their own separate reporting obligations. There is no identical universal mandatory adult-abuse reporting duty, paramedics are not barred from discussing the concern with the patient, and police authorization is not a precondition for providing care.
  12. The correct answer is B (1, 4 and 5): ethical, legally defensible documentation uses objective, standardized terminology, is charted contemporaneously as close to the event as possible, and corrects any error with a single line through it that is initialed and dated. Altering a prior entry without any indication a change was made, and recording only findings favourable to the treatment given, both undermine the accuracy and integrity of the record.
  13. The correct answer is B (Practising only within the skills and medical directives authorized by their certification and Base Hospital): the professional practice standard for a Primary Care Paramedic is to work strictly within the skills and medical directives their certification and Base Hospital have authorized. Performing an intervention simply because a colleague suggests it, expanding scope independently based on personal confidence, or deferring scope decisions to patient preference all fall outside this standard.
Case C0132

Your crew responds to a pediatric cardiac arrest call. Despite a prolonged resuscitation attempt, the child does not survive. Following the call, you notice your partner is unusually quiet, has difficulty concentrating during the return to base, and later reports trouble sleeping over the following days.

  1. The correct answer is C (Encourage access to a Critical Incident Stress Management peer support debrief): a CISM peer support debrief is an appropriate, low-barrier resource to offer immediately after a traumatic call like a pediatric arrest. Advising avoidance of the topic, adding extra shifts to distract, and telling the partner to suppress the reaction all discourage healthy processing of the event.
  2. The correct answer is B (A possible need for referral to a more structured mental health support beyond peer debriefing): persistent sleep disruption and preoccupation that begin to impair work performance days later suggest the reaction has moved beyond a brief normal response and may need more structured professional mental health support. This is not simply a normal reaction requiring no attention, it is not itself grounds to remove the paramedic from all future high-acuity work, and it says nothing about whether the resuscitation was mishandled.
  3. The correct answer is C (Access to peer support and professional mental health resources should be normalized and encouraged): a healthy organizational culture normalizes and actively encourages access to peer support and professional mental health resources after difficult calls. Treating stress reactions as weakness to be hidden, limiting support only to fatal pediatric calls, or requiring a formal complaint before debriefing all discourage paramedics from seeking help.
Case C0135

You are called to a residence for a 6-year-old child with a laceration to the scalp. The mother states the child fell down the stairs. On examination you note the injury pattern is inconsistent with a simple fall, along with several bruises of varying colours on the child's back and upper arms. The child appears withdrawn and avoids eye contact with the mother.

  1. The correct answer is A (Complete patient care as needed and separately report your reasonable grounds for suspicion to a children's aid society): an injury pattern inconsistent with the stated history, combined with bruises of varying ages and a withdrawn child, meets the threshold of reasonable grounds to suspect abuse under the Child, Youth and Family Services Act, 2017, triggering a mandatory report alongside ongoing patient care. Saying nothing, confronting the mother directly, or waiting for the child to self-report all fail to meet this legal duty and could also compromise both care and any investigation.
  2. The correct answer is D (Bruises of varying colours suggesting injuries occurred at different times, inconsistent with a single fall): bruising of different colours indicates injuries sustained at multiple different times, which is inconsistent with a single fall down the stairs and is a recognized red flag for non-accidental injury. The child's age alone, the laceration alone, or the mother's presence at the scene are not in themselves significant indicators of abuse.
  3. The correct answer is B (No, the duty is triggered by reasonable grounds to suspect, not certainty or proof): the Child, Youth and Family Services Act, 2017 sets the reporting threshold at reasonable grounds to suspect, deliberately lower than certainty or proof, so that children are not left unprotected while evidence is gathered. Reporting is not optional, and it does not require prior physician confirmation of a diagnosis of abuse.
  4. The correct answer is A (Including identifying patient details over a channel the public may scan): dispatch and patch channels can be monitored by members of the public with scanners, so paramedics must be cautious about broadcasting identifying patient information over them. A calm professional tone, accurate vital signs, and a timely request for physician response are all appropriate and do not raise the same privacy concern.
  5. The correct answer is A (1, 3 and 5): during an MCI, best practice is using a standardized tag system to record triage category and time, capturing essential information in the moment with full ACRs completed once resources allow, and communicating triage counts and categories to the incident commander or dispatch as they evolve. Completing a full detailed ACR for each patient before triaging the next one, or relying purely on memory, would both dangerously slow the initial sorting of patients.
  6. The correct answer is C (The emergency exception, permitting treatment when a person is incapable and delay would cause harm): the Health Care Consent Act, 1996 includes an emergency exception permitting necessary treatment when a person is incapable, no substitute decision-maker is available, and delay would cause serious harm. Waiting for police, an age-based rule about implied consent, and a requirement for a court order are not accurate descriptions of this emergency authority.
  7. The correct answer is A (Protect the crew, patients, and bystanders by creating a physical barrier and safe work zone): proper ambulance positioning at a highway collision is intended to create a physical barrier and safe work zone that protects everyone on scene from oncoming traffic. It is not primarily about a faster return route, media visibility, or fuel conservation.
  8. The correct answer is A (1, 3 and 4): a complaint investigation into a paramedic's conduct may properly examine whether they acted within their certified scope of practice, whether documentation accurately reflects the care given, and whether appropriate medical direction was sought when required. The paramedic's personal financial history and political affiliations are unrelated to the call and fall outside the scope of such an investigation.
  9. The correct answer is B (To maintain a consistent and efficient sorting of patients by acuity, avoiding duplicated or missed assessments): a single designated triage officer ensures patients are sorted consistently by acuity without duplicated effort or anyone being missed during the sweep. This role is not about scene cleanup, reducing the number of ambulances needed, or eliminating the separate incident commander role.
  10. The correct answer is C (Using clear verbal commands to coordinate the timing of the lift between both paramedics): clear verbal coordination ensures both paramedics lift and move together, distributing the load evenly and reducing injury risk to both crew and patient. Lifting on independent timing, rushing the lift, or having one paramedic bear most of the weight all increase the risk of injury.
  11. The correct answer is D (1, 3 and 5): capacity assessment properly considers whether the patient can understand the relevant information, appreciate the reasonably foreseeable consequences of the decision, and whether the decision remains consistent throughout the assessment. Age alone is not determinative of capacity, and whether the patient agrees with the paramedic's recommendation has no bearing on whether they are capable of making their own decision.
  12. The correct answer is C (Preserve the scene as found, disturbing only what is necessary, and notify police and the coroner's office): a suspected suicide scene must be preserved as found, with only necessary disturbance, and reported to police and the coroner's office for proper investigation. Moving the body, removing potential evidence, or leaving without notifying anyone would all compromise the investigation and violate legal obligations.
  13. The correct answer is D (A justice of the peace, acting on information provided to them): a Form 2 under the Mental Health Act is an order issued by a justice of the peace directing that a person be taken into custody and examined by a physician. It is distinct from a Form 1, which a physician completes directly, and it is not issued by a police officer or a Base Hospital physician.
  14. The correct answer is A (1, 3 and 5): ethical professional practice includes maintaining confidentiality except where legally required to disclose, treating every patient with respect regardless of circumstances, and obtaining informed consent whenever the patient has capacity. Posting identifiable patient details on social media and basing care on personal judgment of who deserves treatment both violate core ethical obligations.
  15. The correct answer is C (General standards for ambulance services, vehicles, and required equipment): O. Reg. 257/00 under the Ambulance Act sets out general standards governing ambulance services, vehicles, and required equipment across Ontario. It has nothing to do with federal transfer payments, hospital emergency department wait times, or Criminal Code impaired driving offences.
  16. The correct answer is C (Assign someone to briefly and honestly update the spouse while care continues): assigning someone to give brief, honest updates allows the treating crew to stay focused on the resuscitation while still respecting the family's need for information. Ignoring the spouse entirely, offering false reassurance, or asking them to leave immediately are all poor communication practices in this situation.
  17. The correct answer is A (1, 3 and 5): proceeding through a red light without slowing to confirm the intersection is clear, driving at an extreme speed for poor weather conditions, and passing a stopped school bus with its lights flashing without slowing would all be viewed as a failure to exercise due regard for the safety of others. Slowing at a red light and checking cross traffic before proceeding, and maintaining both audible and visual warning devices, are examples of appropriate, safety-conscious driving.
  18. The correct answer is A (Evidence that risks of refusal were explained and the patient demonstrated capacity to understand them): documentation must show that the risks of refusing care were explained and that the patient demonstrated the capacity to understand them, since this is what makes a refusal legally defensible later. A vague note, personal frustration, or a list of bystander names does not establish that informed, capable refusal actually occurred.
  19. The correct answer is B (Maintaining regular peer support, adequate rest, and access to professional resources when needed): sustainable strategies for managing cumulative occupational stress include ongoing peer support, adequate rest, and access to professional mental health resources when needed. Avoiding all discussion indefinitely, working excessive extended shifts, or relying solely on humour do not address the underlying cumulative stress and can worsen it over time.
  20. The correct answer is D (1, 3 and 4): the incident commander is responsible for establishing overall scene organization and resource allocation, coordinating with dispatch about additional resources, and delegating triage, treatment, and transport roles to available personnel. Providing hands-on treatment to every Red patient personally and making every individual clinical decision belong to the treating paramedics, not the incident commander.
  21. The correct answer is D (A death resulting from a workplace accident): a death resulting from a workplace accident is reportable to a coroner because of the circumstances of the death itself, regardless of the specific medical cause. A death during a fully consented medical procedure with no complications, a natural death under ongoing hospital physician care, and an expected death under documented palliative decline generally do not require the same circumstance-based coroner notification.
  22. The correct answer is C (Provide the same standard of professional, unbiased care as with any other patient, maintaining confidentiality): recognizing a patient as a personal acquaintance does not change the paramedic's duty to provide the same professional, unbiased standard of care while maintaining confidentiality. Refusing care due to the personal connection, discussing the patient's history with coworkers afterward, or offering personal follow-up outside of work all breach professional boundaries.
  23. The correct answer is D (1, 3 and 4): retriage during an extended MCI is appropriate when a patient's condition has genuinely deteriorated, new vital signs indicate worsening perfusion or mental status, or additional resources have arrived that change overall scene priorities. Simply wanting a second opinion on a tag colour, or a family request for a different category, are not clinically valid reasons to retriage.
  24. The correct answer is B (The Emergency Health Regulatory and Accountability Branch or the employing service): complaints about a paramedic practising outside their certified scope are typically reviewed through EHRAB and the employing ambulance service's internal investigation process. The Ministry of Transportation, a local fire department, and the College of Nurses of Ontario have no jurisdiction over paramedic scope-of-practice complaints.
  25. The correct answer is C (Using a spotter positioned to guide the driver and communicate clearly throughout the manoeuvre): a spotter providing clear, continuous guidance is standard safe practice for backing an ambulance where visibility is limited. Rushing the manoeuvre, relying solely on a rear camera, or assuming an activated siren makes backing safe are all recognized risk factors for backing collisions.
  26. The correct answer is A (1, 2 and 5): Ontario's Health Care Consent Act sets no fixed minimum age for capacity, assesses capacity based on the minor's actual ability to understand and appreciate the specific decision, and recognizes that capacity can vary with the complexity of the treatment involved. There is no automatic incapacity until age 16, and a parent's consent does not automatically override a capable minor's own informed decision.
  27. The correct answer is A (Providing a rapid, visible indicator of triage category to all responders and transport coordinators): colour-coded triage tags give every responder and transport coordinator an immediate, universally understood indicator of a patient's acuity category. They are not used to identify which paramedic treated a patient, do not record billing or insurance information, and do not eliminate the need for continued reassessment.
  28. The correct answer is C (Temporary difficulty concentrating or intrusive thoughts about the event in the following days): transient difficulty concentrating or intrusive thoughts in the days after a traumatic call are recognized as a normal acute stress reaction. A complete absence of emotional response, a permanent career-ending reaction, or total memory loss of the shift are not the expected typical pattern and, if severe, would warrant closer follow-up.
  29. The correct answer is B (1, 3 and 4): provincial equipment and vehicle standards address the minimum medical equipment an ambulance must carry, vehicle inspection and maintenance requirements, and required communication equipment for dispatch contact. They do not regulate the specific brand of tires purchased or a paramedic's personal footwear, which are not matters covered by these standards.
  30. The correct answer is B (Provide safe transport and care while respecting the legal authority holding the patient): once a valid apprehension under section 17 of the Mental Health Act has occurred, the paramedic's role is to provide safe transport and clinical care within that legal authority.
