Paramedics do not independently adjudicate the legality of the apprehension, do not require separate voluntary consent for transport in this circumstance, and cannot unilaterally release the patient based on their own assessment of calmness.
  31. The correct answer is A (Using appropriate mechanical aids and team-based techniques suited to the patient's size and condition): matching mechanical lifting aids and coordinated team technique to the patient's size and condition is the recognized way to reduce musculoskeletal injury risk during transfers. Avoiding available equipment, rushing the transfer, or working alone on a transfer that requires a team all increase the risk of injury.
  32. The correct answer is A (1, 3 and 5): appropriate interagency communication with police includes sharing scene safety information, clearly coordinating who is responsible for safety versus patient care, and maintaining a professional, respectful tone even under stress. Withholding all relevant safety information, and disclosing patient details beyond what is necessary for safety or legal duties, both fall outside appropriate professional communication.
  33. The correct answer is D (Protected from liability for making the report in good faith): the Child, Youth and Family Services Act, 2017 protects a person who reports in good faith based on reasonable grounds from liability, even if the suspicion is later found to be unfounded. There is no automatic disciplinary consequence, no requirement to publicly retract the report, and no personal liability for investigation costs in that circumstance.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Adjusting speed and following distance to account for reduced traction while maintaining warning devices): even during an urgent emergency response, due regard for safety requires adjusting speed and following distance for hazardous conditions like snow while continuing to use both audible and visual warning devices. Disabling the siren, maintaining normal highway speed regardless of conditions, or declining an urgent cardiac arrest call are all inappropriate responses.
  2. The correct answer is B (1, 3 and 4): staging at a safe distance until the scene is confirmed secure when indicated, maintaining awareness of exit routes throughout patient contact, and using calm non-confrontational de-escalation techniques are all appropriate safety and communication practices with a potentially violent patient. Entering without confirming scene safety and turning your back on a potentially violent patient both meaningfully increase risk to the crew.
  3. The correct answer is A (It is an official record of care usable in legal or quality reviews): the Ambulance Call Report is a legal record of the care provided that may be referenced in legal proceedings, quality assurance reviews, or by the receiving facility for continuity of care. It is not merely an internal document, does not require a patient request to become part of the record, and is retained rather than destroyed after transfer.
Case C0171

You are dispatched to a private residence for a 68-year-old male with a history of severe COPD who is in acute respiratory distress. On arrival, you find him sitting upright in a tripod position, using visible accessory muscles, with a respiratory rate of 32 breaths per minute and an SpO2 of 84 percent on room air. He is able to speak only two to three words at a time before pausing to breathe.

  1. The correct answer is C (Accessory muscle use with a respiratory rate of 32 breaths per minute): this reflects significant increased work of breathing and impending respiratory failure, an immediate primary survey priority. A strong bilateral radial pulse, normal skin signs, and a normal GCS with clear speech are reassuring and do not require urgent intervention. Breathing problems identified in the primary survey must be addressed before proceeding to a detailed secondary survey. Delaying management of severe respiratory distress risks rapid deterioration to respiratory arrest.
  2. The correct answer is A (Apply a non-rebreather mask at the highest tolerated flow rate): a patient in severe distress with an SpO2 of 84 percent is significantly hypoxic and requires aggressive initial oxygenation regardless of COPD history. Concerns about blunting hypoxic drive should never delay treating profound hypoxia in an acutely distressed patient. A nasal cannula or low-flow simple face mask would not deliver enough oxygen to correct this level of hypoxemia. Withholding oxygen pending laboratory results would allow dangerous deterioration to continue.
  3. The correct answer is D (His respiratory rate falls from thirty-two to eight breaths per minute): a sudden drop from tachypnea to a dangerously slow rate in an exhausted COPD patient signals respiratory muscle fatigue and impending arrest, not true improvement. Speech returning to full sentences and a rising SpO2 would both indicate genuine improvement. Apparent calming alone can be misleading in a tiring patient and must not be assumed reassuring without checking respiratory rate and effort together. This finding demands immediate ventilatory support.
  4. The correct answer is B (Confirm the scene is safe and don appropriate personal protective equipment): scene safety and body substance isolation must always be verified before any patient contact begins, protecting both the paramedic and the patient. Introducing yourself and beginning assessment come only after the scene has been confirmed safe. Applying monitoring equipment or beginning the primary survey before this step could put the crew at unnecessary risk. This is a foundational step taught before any patient care action.
  5. The correct answer is A (1, 2 and 4): a patient with adequate air exchange despite a partial obstruction can typically still speak in short phrases, may have audible wheezing, and maintains normal skin colour without cyanosis. Cyanosis and total inability to vocalize are signs of an inadequate or complete obstruction requiring immediate aggressive intervention. Distinguishing adequate from inadequate air exchange determines whether to encourage forceful coughing or proceed directly to relief maneuvers. This distinction is central to correct foreign body airway obstruction management. Stridor, when present, is a sign of a severe or poor-air-exchange obstruction rather than an adequate-exchange finding, and is not interchangeable with wheezing in this context.
  6. The correct answer is C (The events leading up to the illness or injury): the 'E' in SAMPLE specifically captures the sequence of events immediately before the emergency began, helping establish mechanism and context. Allergies, last oral intake, and past medical history are each separate, distinct components of the SAMPLE mnemonic. Understanding the events preceding onset can reveal important clues about the cause of the presentation. This information often shapes both field treatment and hospital handover.
  7. The correct answer is D (Approximately thirty to sixty breaths per minute at rest): infants have naturally faster resting respiratory rates than older children and adults due to smaller tidal volumes and higher metabolic demand. Twelve to twenty breaths per minute reflects a normal adult rate, not an infant's. Sixty to eighty and eight to twelve breaths per minute both fall outside the normal infant range. Knowing age-appropriate normal vital sign ranges is essential to recognizing abnormal findings in pediatric patients.
  8. The correct answer is B (A focused examination limited to the injured extremity): when the mechanism and complaint are isolated and non-critical, a focused examination targeting the specific injury is appropriate rather than a full trauma survey. A rapid head-to-toe trauma survey is reserved for patients with a significant mechanism or multiple potential injuries. Excluding the affected limb from examination would miss the very area of concern. Matching the depth of assessment to the clinical situation is a core secondary survey skill.
  9. The correct answer is C (1, 2 and 4): accessory muscle use, tracheal tugging, and nasal flaring with pursed-lip breathing are all recognized objective signs of increased work of breathing. Slow, unlabored respirations at a normal rate would indicate the opposite, an absence of increased work of breathing. Two-word or three-word dyspnea reflects severe distress but was not included among the statements forming this correct combination. Recognizing these signs early allows the paramedic to intervene before full respiratory failure develops.
  10. The correct answer is A (Jaw-thrust maneuver performed without extending the neck): this technique opens the airway by displacing the mandible forward while keeping the cervical spine neutral and in-line, making it correct when spinal injury is suspected. The head-tilt chin-lift and triple airway maneuver both involve neck extension or movement that could worsen an unstable spinal injury. The Sellick maneuver targets the cricoid cartilage to reduce aspiration risk and is not a primary airway-opening technique. Protecting the cervical spine while managing the airway is a key trauma principle.
  11. The correct answer is B (Deliver alternating back blows and abdominal thrusts until the obstruction is relieved): a conscious adult with a complete obstruction who cannot speak, cough, or breathe requires immediate alternating back blows and abdominal thrusts to generate enough pressure to expel the object. Encouraging further coughing is inappropriate once the patient can no longer cough at all. A blind finger sweep risks pushing the object further into the airway and is not recommended in a conscious patient. Chest compressions are reserved for management once the patient becomes unresponsive.
Case C0182

You are dispatched Code 4 to a restaurant for a 45-year-old female who choked on a piece of steak. Bystanders report she suddenly stopped talking, grabbed her throat, and collapsed. A bystander began chest compressions two minutes before your arrival. You find her unresponsive and not breathing, with no visible chest rise on your initial attempted ventilations.

  1. The correct answer is D (Open the airway and look inside the mouth for the object): before continuing ventilations or compressions, the airway must be opened and visually inspected for a visible foreign body that could be removed. Continuing compressions or transporting without addressing a visible airway obstruction ignores the actual cause of the ventilation failure. Applying a pulse oximeter is a lower priority than correcting an obstructed airway. Identifying and clearing an obstruction is essential before ventilation attempts can succeed.
  2. The correct answer is A (Use a laryngoscope and Magill forceps to remove the object): direct visualization with a laryngoscope combined with Magill forceps allows safe, controlled removal of a visible foreign body from the airway. A blind finger sweep risks pushing the object deeper and is not recommended when the object cannot be directly seen and grasped. Continued ventilation attempts will not resolve a mechanical obstruction on their own. Inserting an airway adjunct would not remove the obstruction and could worsen the situation.
  3. The correct answer is C (Deliver one breath approximately every six seconds with visible chest rise): this rate corresponds to roughly ten breaths per minute, the recommended ventilation rate for an apneic adult, with visible chest rise confirming adequate delivered volume. Delivering breaths every one to two seconds would cause dangerous hyperventilation and gastric distension. A breath every thirty seconds would be far too infrequent to support adequate oxygenation. Withholding ventilation while awaiting an advanced airway would allow ongoing hypoxia.
  4. The correct answer is B (Rising SpO2 with improving skin colour and spontaneous gag reflex): these findings together indicate improving oxygenation and a return toward spontaneous airway protective reflexes, both genuinely positive signs. A falling heart rate below forty beats per minute would instead suggest worsening hypoxia or a pre-arrest state. Increasing cyanosis and fixed, dilated pupils are both signs of deterioration, not improvement. Reassessment findings must be interpreted together to accurately judge whether a patient is truly improving.
  5. The correct answer is D (Approximately ten to fifteen seconds for each suctioning attempt): prolonged suctioning removes oxygen along with secretions and can worsen hypoxia, so attempts are limited to roughly ten to fifteen seconds at a time. Sixty seconds or two to three minutes of suctioning would significantly deplete the patient's oxygen reserve. Thirty to forty-five seconds still exceeds the recommended limit for a single attempt. Oxygenating the patient between suctioning attempts helps offset this risk.
  6. The correct answer is A (1, 3 and 4): excessive ventilation volume or rate can cause gastric distension, which in turn raises the risk of vomiting and aspiration, while excessive pressure can cause barotrauma to the lungs. Improved venous return is not a recognized complication of poor bag-valve-mask technique and is unrelated to this scenario. Poor technique increases, rather than decreases, the risk of hypoxia. Recognizing these complications reinforces the importance of proper rate, volume, and technique during ventilation.
  7. The correct answer is C (Measure from the corner of the mouth to the earlobe): this measurement approximates the distance from the front teeth to the angle of the mandible, ensuring the airway properly aligns the tongue away from the posterior pharynx. Measuring from the nose to the earlobe is the correct technique for sizing a nasopharyngeal airway, not an oropharyngeal one. Measuring to the xiphoid process bears no relationship to oropharyngeal anatomy. Selecting the largest available size without proper measurement risks airway trauma or inadequate positioning.
  8. The correct answer is D (A patient with suspected facial or basilar skull fracture): inserting a nasopharyngeal airway in this situation carries a rare but serious risk of the device passing through a fracture into the cranial vault. An intact gag reflex in a drowsy patient and a postictal patient recovering from a seizure are both reasonable candidates for a nasopharyngeal airway, which is generally better tolerated than an oropharyngeal airway. Mild nasal congestion is not a contraindication to insertion. Recognizing this specific contraindication is essential to avoiding a preventable, serious complication.
  9. The correct answer is B (1, 3 and 5): an intact gag reflex, coughing or retching on insertion, and active resistance or attempts to remove the device all indicate the patient will not tolerate an oropharyngeal airway. Spontaneous eye opening alone does not necessarily indicate an intact gag reflex or intolerance. A Glasgow Coma Scale score of six generally reflects a level of unresponsiveness at which the airway is often tolerated, not rejected. Choosing the correct airway adjunct depends on accurately assessing the patient's protective reflexes.
  10. The correct answer is A (Approximately eighty-eight to ninety-two percent saturation on room air): patients with severe COPD often have a chronically elevated baseline carbon dioxide level and rely on a degree of hypoxic respiratory drive, so a slightly lower target saturation avoids over-oxygenation while still preventing significant hypoxia. Targeting ninety-eight to one hundred percent could blunt respiratory drive in a chronically hypercapnic patient. Seventy to seventy-five percent saturation reflects dangerous hypoxia, not an appropriate target. Ninety-four to ninety-six percent is a reasonable general target but not specific to this population's needs.
  11. The correct answer is D (Approximately ninety to one hundred fifty beats per minute): toddlers have faster resting heart rates than older children and adults due to higher metabolic demands and smaller stroke volumes. Sixty to one hundred beats per minute reflects a normal adult resting range, not a toddler's. Forty to sixty beats per minute would represent significant bradycardia in a two-year-old. One hundred eighty to two hundred beats per minute would represent significant tachycardia requiring further evaluation. Age-appropriate vital sign ranges are essential for recognizing abnormal pediatric findings.
  12. The correct answer is C (1, 3 and 5): downed power lines, an unrestrained aggressive dog, and an ongoing violent altercation are all significant hazards that should prompt requesting additional resources before entry. A calm, cooperative bystander and a clear, well-lit pathway are reassuring findings that support proceeding with scene entry. Recognizing hazards during the scene survey protects paramedic safety before patient contact begins. Failing to identify these hazards could place the crew at serious risk.
  13. The correct answer is B (At least every fifteen minutes throughout the transport period): for a stable patient, ongoing assessment and vital sign reassessment should occur at least every fifteen minutes to detect any subtle changes in condition. Assessing only once at the start of contact or only after hospital arrival would miss any deterioration that develops during transport. Waiting sixty minutes between reassessments is far too infrequent even for a stable patient. Regular reassessment is a fundamental part of ongoing patient care.
  14. The correct answer is A (At least every five minutes or with any change): an unstable patient requires much more frequent reassessment than a stable one, at least every five minutes or immediately whenever any change in condition occurs. Every thirty minutes is far too infrequent for a patient in significant distress. Waiting for the patient to request reassessment ignores the paramedic's responsibility to actively monitor condition. Once every hour would allow a deteriorating unstable patient to go unnoticed for a dangerously long period.
Case C0196

You are called to a residence for a 3-year-old boy who had a witnessed seizure lasting about ninety seconds. His mother reports he has had a fever of 39.5 degrees Celsius since this morning. By the time you arrive, the seizure has stopped. The child is postictal and semi-conscious, lying on the couch with noisy, snoring respirations.

  1. The correct answer is C (Head-tilt chin-lift to reposition the tongue away from the airway): in a postictal, non-trauma child with noisy, snoring respirations, the tongue is the most likely cause of partial obstruction, and a head-tilt chin-lift effectively repositions it. A jaw-thrust alone is generally reserved for suspected trauma and is not indicated here. The Sellick maneuver addresses gastric insufflation risk during ventilation, not airway patency itself. Withholding any maneuver would allow the partial obstruction and noisy breathing to persist or worsen.
  2. The correct answer is D (Measure from the corner of the mouth to the earlobe): this same measurement technique used in adults applies to pediatric patients, scaled appropriately to the child's smaller anatomy, to correctly size an oropharyngeal airway. Selecting an adult-sized airway regardless of the child's smaller anatomy risks improper placement and airway trauma. Measuring from the nose to the sternal notch is not the correct landmark for oropharyngeal airway sizing. Using the same size for every child ignores significant anatomical variation across pediatric age groups.
  3. The correct answer is A (Level of consciousness and airway patency over time): a postictal, febrile child is at risk for airway compromise and further seizure activity, making ongoing monitoring of mental status and airway patency the highest priority during transport. The exact time the fever began is useful history but not an ongoing monitoring priority. A comfort object may help calm the child but has no clinical bearing on the assessment. Continuous reassessment of these parameters allows early recognition of deterioration.
  4. The correct answer is B (1, 3 and 5): restlessness and irritability, nasal flaring with grunting, and tachypnea with increased work of breathing are all early, subtle indicators of hypoxia in children. Bradycardia and cyanosis are actually late signs of pediatric hypoxia, appearing only after significant physiological compensation has failed. Waiting for these late signs before intervening risks a much more critical, harder-to-reverse deterioration. Recognizing early compensatory signs allows for timely intervention before decompensation occurs.
  5. The correct answer is C (One provider uses both hands to seal the mask with a jaw thrust): using both hands to maintain the mask seal while performing a jaw thrust maximizes airway patency and minimizes air leak, which is why two-person technique is preferred. A single-hand seal is more prone to leaking and inadequate ventilation. Holding the mask loosely defeats the purpose of achieving an adequate seal. Sealing only over the nose would fail to deliver ventilations through the mouth as well.
  6. The correct answer is D (A nasal cannula at two to four litres per minute): a patient with only mild distress and a saturation of 93 percent typically requires a lower-flow, less invasive oxygen delivery device to correct the mild hypoxemia. A non-rebreather mask at fifteen litres per minute would be excessive for this level of distress. Withholding oxygen entirely ignores the mildly low saturation that does warrant supplementation. Fully assisted bag-valve-mask ventilation is reserved for patients with inadequate spontaneous breathing, not mild distress with adequate respirations.
  7. The correct answer is A (1, 3 and 5): lubricating the device, inserting it straight back rather than upward, and selecting the more patent nostril are all correct considerations for nasopharyngeal airway insertion. Directing the bevel toward the septum is incorrect technique that increases trauma risk. Forcing the airway past resistance risks significant trauma and bleeding and should never be done. Careful, gentle technique minimizes complications during insertion. The septum is a smooth, flat medial surface, so the bevel should be directed toward it, away from the fragile turbinates on the lateral wall, to reduce the risk of trauma and epistaxis during insertion.
  8. The correct answer is B (The 'P' representing provocation and palliation of the pain): this component specifically asks what factors make the pain worse or better, directly addressing what provokes or relieves the symptom. The 'O' addresses when the pain began, the 'Q' addresses its character or quality, and the 'T' addresses its timing or duration. Each letter of the pain assessment mnemonic captures a distinct, non-overlapping piece of information. Asking about provocation and palliation specifically helps identify aggravating and relieving factors.
  9. The correct answer is C (Approximately one breath delivered every six seconds or so): this corresponds to a rate of roughly ten breaths per minute, the recommended rate for ventilating an apneic adult with a pulse. Delivering a breath every two seconds would cause dangerous hyperventilation and increase the risk of gastric distension. A breath every thirty seconds would be far too infrequent to maintain adequate oxygenation. Delivering five rapid breaths in one second is not a recognized or safe ventilation technique.
  10. The correct answer is A (1, 3 and 5): a strong, regular pulse, warm and pink dry skin, and an alert mental status appropriate for age are all findings consistent with adequate circulation and perfusion during the primary survey. A capillary refill greater than four seconds and mottled, cool skin both indicate poor perfusion, the opposite of what this question asks about. Distinguishing adequate from inadequate perfusion guides whether circulatory support is needed immediately. This assessment is a core component of every primary survey.
  11. The correct answer is D (To avoid administering a medication that could trigger a reaction): documenting allergies before any medication administration helps prevent a potentially serious or life-threatening allergic reaction in the field. Allergy history does not determine the exact cause of the current emergency, calculate body weight, or identify the patient's family physician. This single piece of history has direct, immediate safety implications for any medication given during the call. Confirming allergies is a routine and essential safety check.
  12. The correct answer is B (A non-rebreather mask at a high flow rate): a responsive patient with severe hypoxia but adequate spontaneous breathing benefits from high-concentration oxygen delivered via a non-rebreather mask without the need for assisted ventilation. A nasal cannula at one litre per minute would deliver far too little oxygen for severe hypoxia. Withholding oxygen ignores the patient's clearly identified severe hypoxia. Fully assisted bag-valve-mask ventilation is reserved for patients with inadequate spontaneous breathing, which this patient does not have.
  13. The correct answer is C (1, 2 and 4): visible symmetrical chest rise, improving skin colour, and a rising SpO2 over time are all objective signs that ventilations are effectively reaching and oxygenating the patient. Increasing gastric distension instead suggests air is being delivered too forcefully or entering the stomach rather than the lungs. An audible air leak around the mask would indicate an inadequate seal rather than effective ventilation. Chest rise and improving vital signs remain the most reliable bedside indicators of effective ventilation.
  14. The correct answer is A (The lateral recovery position, positioned on either side): this position helps protect the airway from aspiration by allowing secretions or vomitus to drain away from the airway in a patient with no suspected trauma. A flat supine position offers no such protection and increases aspiration risk in an unresponsive patient. The prone position with arms tucked underneath is not a recognized recovery position and could restrict breathing. An upright seated position with the neck flexed is inappropriate for an unresponsive patient and could compromise the airway.
  15. The correct answer is D (Cold extremities causing poor peripheral perfusion at the probe): poor peripheral circulation reduces the pulsatile blood flow the pulse oximeter needs to generate an accurate reading, often producing a falsely low or unreadable value. Supplemental oxygen at high flow, a calm warm environment, and an oral analgesic do not typically interfere with pulse oximetry accuracy in this way. Recognizing factors that impair accurate readings helps the paramedic avoid over-relying on a potentially inaccurate number. Clinical assessment should always be used alongside pulse oximetry, not in place of it.
  16. The correct answer is C (1, 2 and 4): a significant fall mechanism, midline cervical tenderness, and a diving injury in shallow water are all recognized indicators warranting suspicion for a cervical spine injury. A patient who is alert, calm, and pain-free with a low-risk mechanism, and a minor scrape with no significant reported mechanism, would not typically raise the same level of concern. Correctly identifying these red flags guides the decision to maintain spinal precautions during airway management. Missing a genuine spinal injury indicator could result in further harm during patient handling.
  17. The correct answer is B (It helps assess aspiration risk if airway intervention becomes needed): knowing when the patient last ate or drank helps the paramedic anticipate the risk of vomiting and aspiration during airway management or ventilation. This information does not confirm the exact time of the call, determine hospital preference, or identify medication dosages. Aspiration risk is a genuine clinical concern that can influence airway management decisions in the field. This is why last oral intake is a standard component of the SAMPLE history.
  18. The correct answer is A (Forming a general impression while assessing the patient's responsiveness): the primary survey always begins with a rapid general impression and an assessment of responsiveness before moving into the ABCs. Obtaining a full set of vital signs and performing a detailed physical examination both occur later in the assessment process. Asking about past medical history belongs to the SAMPLE history, taken during the secondary survey, not at the very start of the primary survey. This initial step sets the priority and pace for everything that follows.
  19. The correct answer is D (1, 3 and 5): stridor on inspiration, a hoarse or muffled voice, and difficulty swallowing or drooling are all classic findings of an upper airway obstruction or problem, such as a foreign body or swelling near the larynx. Diffuse expiratory wheezing and crackles at the lung bases instead suggest lower airway or lung parenchymal pathology, such as bronchospasm or fluid in the alveoli. Distinguishing upper from lower airway findings helps direct the correct management approach. Upper airway problems often require more urgent, different interventions than lower airway conditions.
  20. The correct answer is C (A rigid tonsil-tip suction catheter with a wide bore): the wide bore of a rigid tonsil-tip catheter is specifically designed to clear thick, particulate material such as vomitus more effectively than a narrow, flexible catheter. A soft French catheter alone is generally better suited to thinner secretions and is less effective against thick vomitus. A pediatric-sized catheter would be inappropriately small and slow for an adult airway. Relying on gauze wipes alone would be inadequate for clearing a significant amount of vomitus from the oropharynx.
  21. The correct answer is D (It typically achieves a more effective mask seal and tidal volume): with one provider dedicated to maintaining a two-handed mask seal and airway positioning while a second squeezes the bag, ventilation is generally more effective than a single provider attempting both tasks alone. Two-person technique requires more, not fewer, personnel and coordination between providers. It does not eliminate the ongoing need for supplemental oxygen. It also does not inherently change the recommended ventilation rate, only the quality of the seal and delivered volume.
  22. The correct answer is A (1, 3 and 5): a poor mask seal, facial hair preventing a tight seal, and excessive gastric distension pushing on the diaphragm would all reduce the effectiveness of bag-valve-mask ventilation. Correct head positioning with an open airway and an appropriately sized oropharyngeal airway are instead factors that improve, rather than worsen, ventilation effectiveness. Recognizing factors that impair ventilation allows the paramedic to troubleshoot and correct technique in real time. Addressing a poor seal or excessive gastric air are common, correctable causes of ineffective ventilation.
  23. The correct answer is D (Approximately ninety-four to ninety-eight percent on supplemental oxygen): for most acutely ill or injured patients without chronic lung disease, this range provides adequate oxygenation without the risks associated with excessive hyperoxia. Seventy-five to eighty percent and eighty-five to eighty-eight percent both reflect ongoing significant hypoxia that would not be an appropriate treatment target. Requiring exactly one hundred percent saturation in every situation is unnecessary and can expose patients to the risks of over-oxygenation. Appropriate oxygen titration balances correcting hypoxia against avoiding excessive oxygen administration.
  24. The correct answer is C (Interviewing bystanders about the mechanism and events witnessed): when a patient cannot recall the events surrounding an injury, bystanders who witnessed the incident become a critical source of information about the mechanism and sequence of events. Relying only on the patient's own account would miss important available information in this situation. Skipping the history entirely or simply documenting it as unknown without attempting further inquiry would be an incomplete and less thorough approach. Gathering history from all available sources helps guide appropriate assessment and treatment decisions.
  25. The correct answer is A (Chest rise, breath sounds, and improving oxygen saturation): these findings together provide direct, reliable confirmation that an airway adjunct is correctly positioned and effectively supporting ventilation. Blood glucose level has no bearing on confirming airway adjunct placement. The colour of the patient's clothing is entirely irrelevant to this clinical question. The manufacturing date of the device provides no information about whether it is correctly placed in this specific patient.
Case C0221

Dispatch: Priority 4, motor vehicle collision, unknown injuries, rural highway. On arrival you find a single vehicle that has struck a guardrail at moderate speed. The driver, a 34-year-old male, is out of the vehicle and walking toward you, holding his right forearm. He is alert, speaking in full sentences, and states he was the only occupant. His right forearm has an obvious closed deformity. Vital signs: HR 96, RR 18, BP 128/82, SpO2 97% on room air.

  1. The correct answer is A (Confirm the scene is safe from ongoing traffic hazards and secondary collision risk): scene safety is always assessed before any patient contact, since a paramedic who becomes injured cannot help the patient and may create a second casualty. Roadway collisions carry a real risk of secondary vehicle strikes, so confirming safe positioning of the ambulance and traffic control comes first. Only once the scene is confirmed safe should you approach and begin your primary survey. Skipping this step to reach the patient faster is a common but dangerous error.
  2. The correct answer is C (Circulation, including pulse quality, skin colour, and any major external bleeding): the primary survey follows a fixed sequence of airway, breathing, and then circulation, so once airway and breathing have been confirmed adequate, circulation is assessed next. This includes checking pulse rate and quality, skin colour and temperature, and scanning for major bleeding that needs immediate control. The secondary survey and detailed history come later, after the primary survey and any life threats have been addressed. Neurological status is also assessed as part of the primary survey but follows circulation, not before it.
  3. The correct answer is D (All four values fall within the normal adult resting range for each parameter): a heart rate of 96, respiratory rate of 18, blood pressure of 128/82, and SpO2 of 97% are all within accepted normal adult resting ranges. None of these values suggest bradycardia, hypotension, or hyperventilation. Recognizing normal vital signs is as important as recognizing abnormal ones, since it confirms this patient's isolated forearm injury has not caused systemic compromise.
  4. The correct answer is B (Asking about events leading up to the incident, such as any sudden dizziness or distraction): the E in SAMPLE stands for Events leading up to the incident, which is exactly the information needed to understand why the vehicle left the roadway. This could reveal a medical cause such as dizziness or a syncopal episode rather than simple driver error. Allergies, last meal, and a pain rating are all valid SAMPLE or assessment questions but do not address the cause of the collision itself.
  5. The correct answer is A (1, 2, 4 and 5): the primary survey consists of forming a general impression, then assessing airway, breathing, and circulation in that order. A detailed history of present illness belongs to the secondary survey and history-taking phase, not the primary survey itself. Recognizing which steps belong to the primary survey versus later assessment stages is essential to performing the sequence correctly under time pressure.
  6. The correct answer is D (Withdraw to a safe distance and request the fire department or gas utility): a natural gas odour signals a genuine explosion and toxic exposure hazard that is beyond the scope of paramedic entry until the scene is rendered safe by trained personnel. Entering, even briefly or while holding your breath, exposes you to serious risk with no meaningful benefit. Opening windows from inside the structure also requires entering the hazard, which is unsafe. Scene safety always takes priority over speed to the patient.
  7. The correct answer is C (130 beats per minute): infants have significantly faster resting heart rates than adults, with a normal range typically falling roughly between 100 and 160 beats per minute depending on age and activity. A rate of 130 falls comfortably within this expected infant range. The other values are either far too slow or far too fast to be normal for a six-month-old at rest, and misjudging pediatric vital sign ranges against adult norms is a common assessment error.
  8. The correct answer is B (1, 2 and 4): adequate breathing is characterized by a normal rate and rhythm, equal bilateral chest rise, and skin that is warm, pink, and dry, reflecting good oxygenation and perfusion. Deep, irregular gasping respirations and absent breath sounds on one side are signs of inadequate breathing and possible serious pathology such as a pneumothorax. Distinguishing adequate from inadequate breathing findings is a core primary survey skill.
  9. The correct answer is D (Current medications the patient takes, including dose and frequency): in the SAMPLE mnemonic, M specifically stands for Medications, prompting you to document what the patient currently takes, including prescription, over-the-counter, and recreational substances. This information can reveal underlying conditions and help anticipate drug interactions or withdrawal effects. Mechanism of injury, mental status, and appointment history are captured elsewhere in your assessment, not under the M.
  10. The correct answer is A (1, 2, 4 and 5): OPQRST is used to characterize pain or a symptom through Onset, Provocation and palliation, Quality, Region and radiation, Severity, and Time, none of which include financial responsibility for care. Ownership in the sense of who pays for treatment has no place in a clinical history and is not part of any recognized mnemonic. Correctly applying OPQRST helps build a clear clinical picture that guides your differential and treatment priorities.
  11. The correct answer is B (Provocation and palliation): asking what makes a symptom worse or better, such as pain worsening with deep breathing and improving when leaning forward, is exactly what the provocation and palliation component of OPQRST captures. This particular pattern is classically associated with pleuritic or pericardial irritation. Onset refers to what the patient was doing when the pain started, severity refers to intensity, and time refers to duration and progression, none of which this question describes.
  12. The correct answer is C (1, 3 and 5): a jaw thrust is indicated whenever spinal trauma is suspected, such as a fall with possible cervical injury, a diving incident, or a high-speed motor vehicle collision, because it opens the airway without extending the neck. A cardiac arrest patient with no trauma history and an unresponsive medical patient with no trauma mechanism can safely have a head-tilt chin-lift performed, since there is no spinal injury concern to protect against. Selecting the correct manoeuvre based on mechanism is a fundamental airway management decision.
  13. The correct answer is A (Head-tilt chin-lift manoeuvre): in the absence of suspected trauma, the head-tilt chin-lift is the standard and most effective manoeuvre for opening the airway of an unresponsive patient. The jaw thrust is reserved for cases with suspected cervical spine injury, since it avoids neck extension. The Sellick manoeuvre applies cricoid pressure during advanced airway procedures and is unrelated to basic airway opening, and log rolling is a spinal precaution technique, not an airway manoeuvre.
  14. The correct answer is D (Suspected basilar skull fracture with clear fluid draining from the nose): a nasopharyngeal airway is contraindicated with suspected basilar skull fracture because the device could inadvertently pass through a fractured cribriform plate into the cranial vault, causing serious injury. A gag reflex, adequate spontaneous breathing, or being conscious and speaking clearly are not contraindications to an NPA, which is generally better tolerated than an oral airway in a semi-responsive patient. Recognizing signs of basilar skull fracture, such as clear rhinorrhea, is essential before inserting this adjunct.
Case C0235

Dispatch: Priority 2, difficulty breathing, residential apartment. You arrive to find a 68-year-old female sitting upright on the edge of her bed, leaning forward with her hands on her knees, using accessory neck muscles to breathe. She has a known history of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease. She is able to speak only in short two to three word phrases. Vital signs: HR 118, RR 28, BP 152/94, SpO2 84% on room air, with audible expiratory wheezing.

  1. The correct answer is B (Speaking only in short two to three word phrases due to breathlessness): the inability to speak in full sentences is a reliable clinical marker of significant respiratory distress, since it reflects insufficient breath reserve to sustain normal speech. While the elevated heart rate, blood pressure, and known COPD history are all relevant context, none of them directly demonstrates the severity of her current breathing difficulty the way her speech pattern does. This finding, combined with her SpO2 of 84%, confirms she requires immediate intervention.
  2. The correct answer is C (A non-rebreather mask with the reservoir bag pre-filled, titrated to relieve hypoxia): a patient with an SpO2 of 84% and visible respiratory distress needs high-concentration oxygen delivery, which a properly primed non-rebreather mask provides. A low-flow nasal cannula would be inadequate for this level of hypoxia, and withholding oxygen due to COPD history is a dangerous myth that risks the patient's life for a theoretical concern. A Venturi mask fixed at a low percentage would also fail to correct her significant hypoxia quickly enough.
  3. The correct answer is D (Titrating oxygen to relieve hypoxia while monitoring for reduced respiratory drive): oxygen therapy in acute hypoxia should be titrated to relieve the hypoxic state while watching for any change in respiratory effort, particularly in a patient with a chronic respiratory condition. Withholding oxygen or arbitrarily restricting flow to an unsafe low rate would leave her dangerously hypoxic. A single fixed numeric target ignores the need to reassess and adjust based on her actual clinical response.
  4. The correct answer is A (Compensatory mechanisms the body uses to increase work of breathing efficiency): tripod positioning and accessory muscle use are the body's way of maximizing chest expansion and recruiting additional muscles to move air when normal breathing effort is insufficient. These are not voluntary or unrelated behaviours but direct physiological responses to respiratory compromise. They do not by themselves confirm complete airway obstruction, and seeing them is a reason to increase support, not to discontinue oxygen.
  5. The correct answer is C (At a minimum of every five minutes, given her unstable status): patients showing significant respiratory distress and hypoxia are considered unstable, which requires reassessment of vital signs and respiratory status at least every five minutes throughout transport. Waiting for the patient to report feeling worse, reassessing only once, or using the fifteen-minute interval reserved for stable patients would all delay recognition of deterioration in a patient who is already critically compromised.
  6. The correct answer is B (No more than 15 seconds): suctioning attempts are limited to a maximum of 15 seconds in an adult because prolonged suctioning removes oxygen from the airway along with secretions and can worsen hypoxia. Continuous suctioning or attempts lasting up to 60 seconds would significantly compromise oxygenation and risk bradycardia. Suctioning duration is time-limited specifically to balance clearing the airway against maintaining adequate oxygen delivery.
  7. The correct answer is D (1, 2 and 4): suctioning is appropriate whenever visible vomitus, excessive secretions, or blood is actively obstructing or threatening the airway, since these substances must be cleared to allow effective ventilation. Suctioning every patient regardless of presentation is unnecessary and can cause harm through hypoxia or airway trauma, and a clear, patent airway with no visible obstruction is a reason not to suction, not a reason to do so. Suctioning should always be a targeted intervention, not a routine step.
  8. The correct answer is A (About 1 breath every 6 seconds): the recommended ventilation rate for an apnoeic adult with a pulse is approximately one breath every 6 seconds, which corresponds to roughly 10 breaths per minute. Ventilating every 2 to 3 seconds would produce a rate far too fast, causing hyperventilation and reduced venous return, while every 20 seconds would be far too slow to sustain adequate oxygenation. Ventilating as fast as possible risks gastric distension and barotrauma without improving outcomes.
  9. The correct answer is B (1 breath every 2 to 3 seconds): infants and children require a faster ventilation rate than adults, approximately one breath every 2 to 3 seconds, reflecting roughly 20 to 30 breaths per minute for this age group. A rate of every 6 seconds is appropriate for an apnoeic adult, not an infant, and rates of every 10 or 20 seconds would be dangerously slow for a pediatric patient's higher metabolic and oxygen demands.
  10. The correct answer is D (1, 2 and 4): effective bag-valve-mask ventilation is confirmed by visible symmetrical chest rise, stable or improving oxygen saturation, and a heart rate trending toward normal, all of which reflect adequate oxygen delivery. Increasing gastric distension and worsening cyanosis are signs the ventilation technique is failing, often due to excessive volume, rate, or an inadequate mask seal, and should prompt immediate correction of technique rather than being read as reassuring findings.
  11. The correct answer is C (Nasal cannula at 2 to 6 litres per minute): a stable patient with mild hypoxia and no significant distress is appropriately managed with a low-flow device like a nasal cannula delivering 2 to 6 litres per minute. A non-rebreather mask at maximal flow or a bag-valve-mask are reserved for patients with more severe hypoxia or ventilatory failure, and would be excessive and poorly tolerated in a stable, mildly hypoxic patient. Matching the device to the severity of hypoxia avoids both under- and over-treatment.
  12. The correct answer is A (1, 3 and 4): a non-rebreather mask delivers a higher fraction of inspired oxygen than a nasal cannula, relies on one-way valves to prevent rebreathing of exhaled carbon dioxide, and is reserved for patients with significant hypoxia or distress. The reservoir bag should never be allowed to fully deflate, since this indicates inadequate oxygen flow and risks the patient entraining room air, and the device absolutely requires a connected oxygen source to function correctly.
  13. The correct answer is B (Fully inflated so it does not collapse fully during inspiration): the reservoir bag must be prefilled with oxygen before the mask is applied so that it remains adequately inflated throughout the patient's breathing cycle, ensuring a consistently high oxygen concentration. An empty or half-inflated bag would allow the patient to entrain room air, diluting the delivered oxygen concentration. Waiting to inflate the bag until after the mask is on the patient's face delays effective high-concentration oxygen delivery.
  14. The correct answer is D (Approximately 94 percent or higher): for a previously healthy patient without a chronic respiratory condition, oxygen therapy is generally titrated toward a saturation of approximately 94 percent or higher to correct hypoxia without unnecessary hyperoxia. A target below 80 percent would leave the patient dangerously hypoxic, while insisting on a saturation near 100 percent at all times is unrealistic and unnecessary. Oxygen should be provided to a hypoxic patient, so claiming there is no meaningful target is incorrect.
  15. The correct answer is C (1, 2, 4 and 5): universal signs of a severe, complete airway obstruction include clutching the throat, inability to speak or cough forcefully, a silent effort to cough with no air movement, and developing cyanosis, all reflecting a total lack of effective airflow. Calm, quiet breathing with a strong voice is the opposite finding and would indicate a mild or absent obstruction instead. Recognizing these signs immediately is critical since a complete obstruction requires urgent intervention.
  16. The correct answer is A (Deliver alternating back blows and abdominal thrusts until the obstruction clears or he becomes unresponsive): a conscious adult with a complete airway obstruction, unable to speak or cough, requires abdominal thrusts delivered in rapid succession until the object is expelled or the patient loses responsiveness. A blind finger sweep in a conscious patient is not performed and could push the object further into the airway. Lying down or delaying with rescue breaths would not address the mechanical obstruction blocking airflow.
  17. The correct answer is D (Lower the patient to the ground and begin chest compressions, checking the mouth before ventilations): once a patient with a complete airway obstruction becomes unresponsive, the priority shifts to lowering them safely and beginning chest compressions, since compressions can help dislodge the object, checking the mouth for the object before each set of ventilations. Continuing standing thrusts on an unresponsive patient is unsafe and ineffective, and attempting rescue breaths before checking the airway ignores the known obstruction. Waiting for advanced life support delays essential immediate care.
  18. The correct answer is B (1, 2 and 3): an oropharyngeal airway should generally be avoided in a patient with an intact gag reflex due to the risk of vomiting and aspiration, a nasopharyngeal airway is often tolerated by patients who cannot tolerate an oral airway, and correct oropharyngeal sizing is measured from the corner of the mouth to the earlobe or angle of the jaw. No airway adjunct eliminates the need for proper manual positioning, and correct sizing is directly relevant to how well any airway adjunct functions, since an incorrectly sized device can worsen obstruction.
  19. The correct answer is C (Every 15 minutes): a stable patient with no immediate life threats should have vital signs and ongoing assessment repeated at a minimum interval of every 15 minutes throughout transport. Reassessing every 1 or 5 minutes is reserved for unstable patients whose condition requires closer monitoring, and reassessing only once prior to transport would fail to detect any deterioration that occurs during the call.
  20. The correct answer is A (Inspection, palpation, and auscultation of breath sounds): a thorough chest assessment during the secondary survey requires looking at the chest for deformity or asymmetry, feeling for tenderness or crepitus, and listening to breath sounds bilaterally to detect abnormalities such as decreased air entry. Relying on visual inspection alone, percussion alone, or the patient's self-report without a physical exam would each miss significant findings that only a combined hands-on assessment can reveal.
  21. The correct answer is B (78 beats per minute): a resting heart rate of 78 beats per minute is well within the normal adult range and remains a reasonable baseline expectation for a healthy older adult, since normal resting heart rate does not change dramatically with age alone. The other options represent extremes of severe bradycardia or tachycardia that would be abnormal findings requiring further investigation, not expected baseline values.
  22. The correct answer is D (Painful): the AVPU scale classifies a patient's level of consciousness as Alert, responsive to Verbal stimuli, responsive only to Painful stimuli, or Unresponsive. A patient who only responds to a firm sternal rub, a painful stimulus, but does not respond to voice, is classified at the Painful level rather than Verbal or Alert. Correctly applying AVPU quickly communicates the patient's neurological status to other providers.
  23. The correct answer is C (From the corner of the mouth to the angle of the jaw or earlobe): correct oropharyngeal airway sizing is measured from the corner of the patient's mouth to the angle of the jaw or the earlobe, which approximates the distance needed to hold the tongue off the posterior pharynx without extending too far into the airway. Measuring from the nose to the earlobe or from other facial landmarks does not correspond to the anatomical path the device follows and would result in an incorrectly sized airway.
  24. The correct answer is A (1, 2 and 5): an oropharyngeal airway that is too long can push the tongue backward and worsen obstruction, while one that is too short fails to hold the tongue off the posterior pharynx, and persistent or worsening obstruction after insertion is a clear sign the size or placement is wrong. A patient tolerating the airway with no gag response and a flange sitting flush against the lips are actually reassuring signs of correct fit, not signs of a sizing problem.
  25. The correct answer is D (Jaw thrust without head extension): with suspected cervical spine trauma from a fall, the jaw thrust manoeuvre is used because it opens the airway by displacing the mandible forward without extending or moving the neck. A head-tilt chin-lift or a sniffing position both require neck extension, which risks worsening a potential spinal injury. Trendelenburg positioning addresses circulation concerns, not airway opening, and is unrelated to this scenario.
  26. The correct answer is B (Worsening hypoxia and potential bradycardia from prolonged interruption of ventilation): prolonged suctioning removes oxygen along with secretions and interrupts ventilation, which can drive a hypoxic patient further into hypoxia and trigger a reflex bradycardia, particularly in infants and children. Suctioning does not improve oxygenation on its own, and longer suction times do not guarantee faster resolution of obstruction; they simply increase the risk of harm from interrupted ventilation.
  27. The correct answer is C (One rescuer to maintain a proper mask seal while the other ventilates the bag): two-rescuer bag-valve-mask technique allows one provider to focus entirely on maintaining an effective two-handed mask seal and open airway while the second provider squeezes the bag, which significantly improves tidal volume delivery compared to a single rescuer managing both tasks. It does not eliminate the need to monitor chest rise, does not encourage faster uncontrolled rates, and does not reduce the need for supplemental oxygen.
  28. The correct answer is A (1, 2 and 4): inadequate breathing is recognized by a respiratory rate significantly outside the normal range for age, cyanosis, and the use of accessory muscles with nasal flaring, all of which indicate the patient is struggling to move sufficient air. Full, equal chest rise with a regular rhythm and normal skin colour, temperature, and moisture are findings of adequate breathing, the opposite of what this question asks about.
  29. The correct answer is D (It delivers a specific, consistent percentage of oxygen regardless of the patient's breathing pattern): a Venturi mask uses a calibrated jet mixing system to deliver a precise, fixed fraction of inspired oxygen that remains consistent even as the patient's respiratory rate and depth vary, making it valuable when tight oxygen control is needed. It still requires a connected oxygen source to function and is not restricted to apnoeic patients, since it is specifically designed for spontaneously breathing patients who need controlled oxygen delivery.
  30. The correct answer is B (24 breaths per minute): a respiratory rate of 24 breaths per minute falls within the normal resting range for a healthy 4-year-old child, whose baseline respiratory rate is naturally faster than an adult's. A rate of 5 breaths per minute would represent dangerous respiratory depression, while 60 or 90 breaths per minute would represent severe tachypnea, both of which would be clearly abnormal findings requiring urgent evaluation.
  31. The correct answer is C (118/76 millimetres of mercury): a blood pressure of 118/76 falls comfortably within the normal range for a healthy resting adult. A reading of 70/40 reflects significant hypotension, 210/130 reflects a hypertensive emergency, and 60/30 reflects profound and likely life-threatening hypotension, none of which represent a normal baseline value.
  32. The correct answer is A (Anticipate likely injury patterns and guide your assessment priorities): understanding the mechanism of injury, such as a rollover collision, helps predict the likely pattern and severity of injuries a patient may have sustained, which directs where to focus your assessment and what index of suspicion to maintain even before findings appear. Mechanism of injury has no bearing on hospital preference, fault determination, or whether a primary survey is still required, which remains mandatory regardless of mechanism.
  33. The correct answer is D (It helps avoid administering or recommending medications that could trigger a reaction): documenting a known allergy such as penicillin is clinically significant because it directly informs which medications should be avoided during care and ensures this information is communicated to receiving hospital staff, preventing a potentially serious allergic reaction. Allergy history is relevant across all patient types, not only surgical patients, and it has no bearing on whether a patient is eligible for transport.
  34. The correct answer is A (Possible poor peripheral perfusion requiring further assessment): a capillary refill time greater than 2 seconds suggests delayed peripheral perfusion, which can indicate shock, dehydration, or another circulatory problem and warrants further assessment of circulation status. It does not represent normal perfusion, and while it is a useful screening finding, it is not by itself a definitive diagnosis of cardiac arrest, nor does it mean no further assessment is needed.
  35. The correct answer is C (At least every 5 minutes): a haemodynamically unstable patient requires frequent reassessment of vital signs, at a minimum of every 5 minutes, to detect deterioration or response to treatment as early as possible during transport. Intervals of 15 or 30 minutes are appropriate only for stable patients, and reassessing only upon arrival at hospital would leave a critically unstable patient unmonitored throughout the entire transport.
  36. The correct answer is A (Titrating toward approximately 88 to 92 percent while monitoring for reduced respiratory drive): a patient with longstanding COPD and a lower baseline saturation is generally managed with a titrated target of approximately 88 to 92 percent, since correcting saturation all the way to near 100 percent can blunt the hypoxic respiratory drive some chronic COPD patients rely on. Targeting near 100 percent or restricting saturation below 80 percent would both be inappropriate, and rarely giving oxygen to a hypoxic COPD patient would be dangerous and is not supported practice.
Case C0271

You are dispatched Code 4 to a private residence for a 34-year-old female in respiratory distress after eating a meal containing shellfish, a known allergen for her. On arrival you find the patient sitting upright, anxious, with audible stridor, facial and lip swelling, and hives spreading across her chest and arms. Vital signs are heart rate 128, blood pressure 88/54, respiratory rate 28 with wheezing on auscultation, and oxygen saturation 91 percent on room air. Her partner states she has an epinephrine auto-injector at home but has not used it.

  1. The correct answer is B (Airway obstruction from upper airway and laryngeal swelling): stridor combined with facial and lip swelling indicates laryngeal edema, which can fully occlude the airway within minutes. This is a more immediately fatal threat than the tachycardia, hives, or isolated hypotension also present. Airway compromise always takes priority in an anaphylactic presentation. Recognizing stridor as an airway emergency drives the urgency of immediate epinephrine administration.
  2. The correct answer is D (Epinephrine, because it reverses bronchoconstriction, laryngeal edema, and hypotension together): epinephrine is the only medication that acts on all three life-threatening components of anaphylaxis simultaneously through its alpha and beta adrenergic effects. Diphenhydramine and dimenhydrinate only address histamine-related itching and nausea, not airway swelling or shock. Salbutamol treats bronchospasm alone and does nothing for angioedema or hypotension. Epinephrine must always be given first in a severe anaphylactic reaction.
  3. The correct answer is A (Repeat the epinephrine dose per protocol while preparing for rapid transport): persistent hypotension after an initial dose indicates ongoing anaphylactic shock, and repeat dosing is an established part of managing this presentation. Withholding further epinephrine would leave the patient in a life-threatening state. Diphenhydramine does not treat hypotension and is not a substitute for epinephrine. Delaying reassessment risks further deterioration during an unstable period.
  4. The correct answer is C (Salbutamol relieves bronchospasm only, while epinephrine treats airway swelling, bronchospasm, and hypotension): salbutamol's beta-2 agonist action is limited to the bronchial smooth muscle and does nothing for laryngeal edema or vascular collapse. Epinephrine's combined alpha and beta effects make it the only drug that addresses the full spectrum of anaphylactic pathology. This is why epinephrine, not salbutamol alone, is always the first-line treatment. Side effect profile and ease of administration are not the deciding factors in this choice.
  5. The correct answer is A (1, 2 and 4): confirming the medication has not expired, checking for known allergies, and verifying the dose against the patient's weight are all genuine components of safe medication administration. Statement 3 is false because documentation should occur promptly, not delayed until hospital handover. Statement 5 is false because patient identity must be confirmed before any drug is given. These five rights protect against preventable medication errors in the field.
  6. The correct answer is D (1, 2 and 4): swelling, coolness, and loss of flow are classic signs of infiltration, requiring the IV to be discontinued and restarted at another site, with warm compresses potentially useful afterward for comfort and reabsorption. Statement 3 is false and dangerous, since increasing the rate would push more fluid into the surrounding tissue. Statement 5 is false because this cool, swollen presentation is inconsistent with phlebitis, which typically presents with warmth and redness along the vein.
  7. The correct answer is B (0.22 mL): the weight-based dose is calculated as 0.01 mg/kg multiplied by 22 kg, giving 0.22 mg. At a concentration of 1 mg/mL for 1:1000 epinephrine, 0.22 mg corresponds directly to 0.22 mL. This dose falls well under the pediatric maximum, so no capping is required. Drawing up the correct volume ensures the intended dose is delivered without overdosing or underdosing the patient.
  8. The correct answer is C (1, 3 and 5): salbutamol dosing for pediatric bronchospasm uses a weight threshold rather than a strict per-kilogram calculation, giving 2.5 mg to children under 25 kg and 5 mg to those 25 kg or greater, with repeat doses appropriate for ongoing bronchospasm. Statement 2 is false because children under 25 kg receive the lower 2.5 mg dose, not 5 mg. Statement 4 is false because the dosing is threshold-based, not calculated per kilogram for every patient.
  9. The correct answer is D (2, 3 and 5): erectile dysfunction medications can cause profound, dangerous hypotension when combined with nitroglycerin, doses may be repeated every five minutes up to three times if blood pressure remains adequate, and the patient should stay seated or supine to reduce fall risk from hypotension. Statement 1 is false because nitroglycerin should be withheld, not given, when systolic blood pressure is already low. Statement 4 is false because nitroglycerin is administered sublingually, not intramuscularly.
  10. The correct answer is A (0.5 mg IM, since the child weighs less than 25 kg): glucagon dosing for pediatric hypoglycemia follows a weight threshold, with patients under 25 kg receiving 0.5 mg and those 25 kg or greater receiving 1 mg, rather than a per-kilogram calculation. At 19 kg, this toddler falls under the threshold and receives the lower dose. Glucagon is given intramuscularly precisely because IV access is unavailable in this scenario.
  11. The correct answer is C (1, 2, 4 and 5): the dorsal hand veins and median cubital vein are both commonly used sites, the antecubital fossa is often avoided first because it restricts arm movement, and choosing a vein proximal to a prior failed attempt avoids infusing fluid through the damaged vessel wall, which would otherwise leak into the surrounding tissue as it flows toward the heart. Statement 3 is false because an arm with an arteriovenous fistula should be avoided entirely to protect the fistula from damage or infection. This combination reflects sound site-selection practice.
  12. The correct answer is B (Right route matching the drug's intended administration method): right route is one of the recognized rights of medication administration, alongside right patient, right dose, right time, and right documentation. Right diagnosis, right hospital destination, and right family witness are not part of this framework. Confirming the correct route prevents serious errors, such as giving a sublingual medication intramuscularly. These rights exist specifically to reduce preventable medication administration mistakes.
  13. The correct answer is A (1.0 mL): dividing the target dose of 0.4 mg by the vial concentration of 0.4 mg/mL gives exactly 1.0 mL to be drawn up and administered. This concentration is commonly stocked for prehospital naloxone administration in suspected opioid overdose. Drawing the wrong volume could result in an ineffective dose or unnecessary over-administration. Accurate volume calculation is essential whenever a drug is supplied in a specific concentration.
  14. The correct answer is D (To inhibit platelet aggregation and limit clot growth): ASA works by blocking platelet aggregation, which limits the growth of a forming coronary thrombus during acute coronary syndrome. It does not primarily relieve pain, lower blood pressure, or fully dissolve an existing clot, which is the role of fibrinolytic therapy given in hospital. Its antiplatelet effect is why ASA is given promptly to patients with suspected cardiac chest pain and no contraindications.
Case C0285

You are dispatched to a residence for a 61-year-old male found unresponsive by his spouse, who states he collapsed suddenly while watching television. On arrival the patient is unresponsive, pulseless, and apneic. Your partner begins high-quality chest compressions while you prepare the airway and IV equipment. The cardiac monitor shows a wide, disorganized rhythm with no discernible pulse.

  1. The correct answer is B (Continuous high-quality chest compressions with minimal interruption): in cardiac arrest, uninterrupted high-quality compressions are the single most important intervention for maintaining perfusion to vital organs. IV access and epinephrine administration are important but should not delay or interrupt compressions. Gathering a full history from the spouse can wait until resuscitation is underway. Compressions with minimal interruption directly improve the chance of a return of spontaneous circulation.
  2. The correct answer is B (Normal saline 0.9 percent): normal saline is an isotonic crystalloid commonly used to keep an IV line patent and to serve as a carrier fluid for medications during resuscitation. Dextrose-containing solutions are not the standard choice for this purpose and can affect glucose readings unnecessarily. Half-strength saline is hypotonic and not appropriate as a resuscitation carrier fluid. Normal saline's isotonic profile makes it compatible with most emergency medications given during cardiac arrest.
  3. The correct answer is D (1:10,000 concentration given intravenously): in cardiac arrest, epinephrine is given intravenously or intraosseously using the more dilute 1:10,000 concentration, distinct from the 1:1000 concentration used intramuscularly for anaphylaxis. Using the wrong concentration for the intended route could result in a severe overdose or an ineffective dose. This distinction between concentrations by route and indication is a fundamental prehospital pharmacology principle. Confirming the correct concentration before drawing up epinephrine is essential during resuscitation.
  4. The correct answer is A (1, 2 and 4): the intravenous route delivers the fastest onset because it bypasses absorption entirely, the oral route is generally the slowest due to digestion and first-pass metabolism, and the sublingual route allows rapid absorption into the bloodstream while largely bypassing first-pass metabolism. Statement 3 is false because intramuscular onset is slower than intravenous, not faster. Statement 5 is false because subcutaneous absorption is generally slower than intramuscular due to reduced blood flow in fatty tissue.
  5. The correct answer is C (0.5 mg, 0.5 mL, because the calculated dose exceeds the adult maximum): multiplying 0.01 mg/kg by 91 kg gives 0.91 mg, which exceeds the standard adult maximum single dose of 0.5 mg, so the dose is capped at 0.5 mg. At a concentration of 1 mg/mL, this corresponds to 0.5 mL. Administering the uncapped calculated dose would risk dangerous cardiovascular side effects from excess epinephrine. Respecting maximum dose limits is essential even when weight-based math suggests a higher amount.
  6. The correct answer is B (1, 2 and 4): oral glucose is appropriate when a patient can swallow safely and protect their airway, IV dextrose is preferred when the patient is unresponsive or cannot swallow safely, and a typical oral glucose dose is 15 to 20 grams. Statement 3 is false and dangerous because oral glucose risks aspiration if the patient cannot protect their airway. Statement 5 is false because IV dextrose should still be given through an appropriately sized, well-functioning vein rather than forced regardless of vessel size.
  7. The correct answer is D (0.3 mg, because the calculated dose exceeds the pediatric maximum): multiplying 0.01 mg/kg by 34 kg gives 0.34 mg, which exceeds the pediatric maximum single dose of 0.3 mg, so the dose is capped at 0.3 mg rather than given uncapped. Applying the adult maximum of 0.5 mg would be inappropriate since this patient is a pediatric weight. Halving the concentration is an unrelated calculation error. Respecting the pediatric maximum protects against excessive cardiovascular stimulation in a child.
  8. The correct answer is A (1, 3 and 5): diphenhydramine treats histamine-mediated symptoms such as hives and itching, is appropriately given as an adjunct after epinephrine in anaphylaxis, and commonly causes drowsiness as a side effect. Statement 2 is false because diphenhydramine is never first-line for airway compromise, which requires epinephrine. Statement 4 is false because diphenhydramine has a slower onset than epinephrine and does not meaningfully reverse bronchospasm on its own.
  9. The correct answer is B (10 mg): adding the two 5 mg nebulized doses together gives a total of 10 mg administered across the two treatments. Tracking cumulative dosing is important when repeat treatments are given for persistent bronchospasm. Under-reporting or over-reporting the total dose could affect further treatment decisions and handover to receiving hospital staff. Accurate documentation of each treatment ensures the total is correctly recorded.
  10. The correct answer is C (1, 3 and 4): cleansing the skin with antiseptic, using a no-touch technique after cleansing, and disposing of used needles directly in a labelled sharps container are all correct aseptic and safety practices. Statement 2 is false because touching the catheter tip after cleansing reintroduces contamination risk. Statement 5 is false because gloves should always be worn for IV insertion regardless of hand washing, both for infection control and bloodborne pathogen protection.
  11. The correct answer is A (1 mg, since the patient weighs 25 kg or more): glucagon dosing uses a weight threshold, with patients 25 kg or greater receiving the full 1 mg dose regardless of exact adult body weight. This 78 kg adult clearly falls above the 25 kg threshold, so the full adult dose applies. Halving the dose or calculating it per 10 kg of body weight does not reflect how glucagon is actually dosed. Applying a per-kilogram formula, as in option D, is incorrect for this medication.
  12. The correct answer is D (1, 2, 4 and 5): normal saline and Ringer's lactate are both isotonic crystalloids used in the field, crystalloid fluid can leak into surrounding tissue if the IV infiltrates, and fluid boluses should be reassessed frequently to avoid fluid overload. Statement 3 is false because dextrose solutions are used primarily to treat hypoglycemia, not to expand intravascular volume in trauma, where isotonic crystalloids are preferred. Recognizing this distinction guides appropriate fluid selection.
  13. The correct answer is C (Air embolism entering the bloodstream): air bubbles left in the tubing can travel into the bloodstream once the line is connected and running, risking a potentially serious air embolism. Clearing the tubing of air before connection is a routine safety step in IV setup. Phlebitis, infiltration, and catheter shear are unrelated complications caused by different mechanisms, such as vein irritation or improper catheter handling. Prevention of air embolism is one reason careful tubing priming is part of standard technique.
  14. The correct answer is B (It increases the oxygen content delivered to the tissues): supplemental oxygen raises the fraction of inspired oxygen, increasing the amount of oxygen available for gas exchange and delivery to hypoxic tissues. It does not primarily function as an anxiety-reducing agent, and it does not replace the need for airway management if the airway itself is compromised. At a saturation of 88 percent, oxygen has a clear and measurable physiological benefit for this patient.
  15. The correct answer is A (Pharmacodynamics, the drug's effect on the body): pharmacodynamics describes what a drug does to the body, such as relaxing bronchial smooth muscle to relieve wheezing. Pharmacokinetics instead describes what the body does to the drug, including absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion. Bioavailability and half-life are specific pharmacokinetic measures, not descriptions of therapeutic effect. Observing the wheezing improve is an example of the medication's pharmacodynamic action.
Case C0300

You are dispatched to a workplace for a 46-year-old known diabetic found confused and diaphoretic by coworkers. On arrival, the patient is seated, pale, and sweating profusely, able to follow simple commands but disoriented to time. His coworkers state he skipped lunch and took his usual insulin this morning. Vital signs are heart rate 118, blood pressure 116/72, respiratory rate 20, and oxygen saturation 97 percent on room air. A glucometer reading is obtained showing a blood glucose of 2.1 mmol/L.

  1. The correct answer is D (Hypoglycemia from insulin use combined with a missed meal): a blood glucose of 2.1 mmol/L in a diabetic patient who took insulin but skipped a meal, combined with confusion and diaphoresis, is classic for hypoglycemia. Diabetic ketoacidosis typically presents with high, not low, blood glucose, making it inconsistent with this reading. A cerebrovascular event or anxiety reaction would not explain this specific glucose value. The glucometer reading directly confirms the cause of his altered mental status.
  2. The correct answer is B (Administer oral glucose since he can safely swallow and follow commands): a patient who is confused but able to follow commands and protect his airway is an appropriate candidate for oral glucose, the least invasive effective treatment. Withholding treatment would allow his hypoglycemia to worsen unnecessarily. Glucagon and IV dextrose are appropriate when a patient cannot swallow safely or lacks IV access, which does not yet apply here. Oral glucose is preferred whenever it can be given safely.
  3. The correct answer is C (Administer IM glucagon or establish IV access for dextrose): once the patient can no longer safely swallow, oral glucose becomes unsafe due to aspiration risk, so treatment shifts to IM glucagon or IV dextrose depending on what access is available. Continuing oral attempts would risk the airway. Withholding treatment would allow his hypoglycemia to worsen further. Salbutamol has no role in treating hypoglycemia and would not address his mental status.
  4. The correct answer is D (Blood glucose can drop again as glucagon or dextrose effects wear off): both glucagon and dextrose have a limited duration of action, and blood glucose can fall again once their effects diminish, particularly if the underlying cause, such as excess insulin, is still active. Neither treatment provides permanent protection against recurrent hypoglycemia. Continued monitoring and reassessment are necessary to catch a second drop before it causes further deterioration. This is why ongoing observation remains essential even after apparent improvement.
  5. The correct answer is A (1, 2 and 4): anaphylaxis uses the 1:1000 intramuscular concentration while cardiac arrest uses the more dilute 1:10,000 intravenous or intraosseous concentration, and epinephrine increases heart rate and causes vasoconstriction in both scenarios through its adrenergic effects. Statement 3 is false because the concentrations differ specifically by route and indication. Statement 5 is false because epinephrine does have measurable cardiovascular effects, including increased heart rate and blood pressure, when used for anaphylaxis.
  6. The correct answer is C (0.8 mg): adding the initial 0.4 mg dose to the identical repeat 0.4 mg dose gives a cumulative total of 0.8 mg administered.
