Tracking the running total is important because further doses may still be needed depending on the patient's response and the strength of the opioid involved. Accurate cumulative dosing also matters for handover documentation at the receiving hospital. Titrating to effect while tracking the total dose avoids both under- and over-treatment.
  7. The correct answer is B (Dimenhydrinate, since it is indicated for nausea and vomiting): dimenhydrinate is specifically indicated for the treatment of nausea and vomiting, making it the appropriate choice for this patient. Diphenhydramine primarily targets allergic and histamine-related symptoms rather than nausea. Naloxone reverses opioid effects and has no established role treating nausea in a trauma patient with no opioid exposure. Salbutamol is a bronchodilator with no indication for nausea management.
  8. The correct answer is D (1, 2, 4 and 5): phlebitis presents with redness, warmth, and tenderness along the vein, hematoma can occur from a through-and-through puncture, catheter shear can occur if the catheter is drawn back through the introducer needle, and infection risk rises without proper aseptic technique. Statement 3 is false because infiltration typically presents with coolness and swelling rather than the warmth and redness seen in phlebitis, making them clinically distinguishable rather than identical.
  9. The correct answer is A (2.5 mg, since the child weighs less than 25 kg): nebulized salbutamol dosing for pediatric patients follows a weight threshold, giving 2.5 mg to children under 25 kg and 5 mg to those at or above that threshold. This 21 kg child falls below the 25 kg cutoff, so the lower dose applies. Giving the full adult dose or calculating a per-10-kg formula does not reflect how this medication is actually dosed in the field. Applying the correct threshold ensures an appropriate and safe dose.
  10. The correct answer is B (Intramuscular, injected into a large muscle group): glucagon is specifically formulated and used for intramuscular injection, which makes it the preferred treatment for severe hypoglycemia when IV access cannot be obtained. It is not given sublingually, by inhalation, or intravenously in typical prehospital practice. Its intramuscular route is precisely what makes it valuable in patients with poor or absent venous access. This distinguishes it from dextrose, which requires an IV line.
  11. The correct answer is C (1, 3 and 4): absorption describes a drug moving from its administration site into the bloodstream, metabolism describes the body chemically altering the drug, and excretion describes elimination of the drug and its byproducts. Statement 2 is false because distribution, not metabolism, describes how a drug moves throughout body tissues once absorbed. Statement 5 is false because half-life describes the time for drug concentration to fall by half, not the total dose required for a therapeutic effect.
  12. The correct answer is A (50 mL): dividing the target dose of 25 grams by the concentration of 0.5 grams per mL gives exactly 50 mL of D50W to be administered. This calculation ensures the full intended dextrose dose reaches the patient without under- or over-dosing. Miscalculating this volume could result in inadequate correction of hypoglycemia or an unnecessarily large fluid volume. Confirming concentration before calculating volume is essential whenever dextrose is prepared for administration.
  13. The correct answer is D (Withhold ASA due to the allergy and active bleeding): a known allergy to ASA and active gastrointestinal bleeding are both clear contraindications to giving this medication, since it could trigger an allergic reaction or worsen bleeding through its antiplatelet effect. Giving a reduced dose or an alternate route does not eliminate either risk. Chest pain management should continue through other appropriate means while ASA is withheld. Recognizing contraindications before administration is a core safety responsibility.
  14. The correct answer is C (It dilates blood vessels, reducing cardiac workload and improving blood flow): nitroglycerin causes venous and arterial dilation, which reduces the heart's workload and improves coronary blood flow, relieving ischemic chest pain. It does not thin the blood or act as a direct analgesic on chest wall pain receptors. It also does not increase heart rate as a primary mechanism, and doing so would actually worsen myocardial oxygen demand. This vasodilatory action is why blood pressure must be monitored closely after administration.
  15. The correct answer is B (It is typically large, stable, and well-anchored in the tissue): the median cubital vein is favoured for IV access because it tends to be large, relatively stable, and well-supported by surrounding tissue, making successful cannulation more likely. It is not the smallest or thinnest-walled vein, and its location over a joint is actually a drawback rather than an advantage, since movement can dislodge the catheter. Despite this drawback, its size and stability generally make it a reliable first choice in a stable patient.
Case C0315

You respond to a call in a downtown alley for an unresponsive male, approximately 30 years old, reported by a passerby. On arrival you find the patient lying supine, unresponsive to voice or painful stimuli, with slow and shallow respirations at a rate of 6 per minute. His pupils are pinpoint and he has visible track marks on both arms. A used needle is found nearby. His skin is cool and slightly cyanotic around the lips.

  1. The correct answer is D (Opioid toxicity causing respiratory depression): pinpoint pupils, severely slow and shallow respirations, track marks, and a nearby used needle together form a classic presentation of opioid toxicity. Hypoglycemia and stroke would not typically produce pinpoint pupils or this specific combination of findings. A severe allergic reaction would present with airway swelling or hives rather than this pattern. The physical findings point strongly and specifically toward opioid-induced respiratory depression.
  2. The correct answer is A (Support ventilation and oxygenation given his respiratory rate of 6): a respiratory rate of 6 with shallow breathing represents a life-threatening ventilation failure that must be addressed immediately, since naloxone takes time to act even after administration. Chest compressions are not indicated because the patient is not described as pulseless. Naloxone should still be given but supporting his breathing first, or simultaneously, prevents further hypoxic injury while the medication takes effect. Airway and breathing always take priority over drug administration.
  3. The correct answer is C (Re-sedation as the naloxone wears off before the opioid does): naloxone has a shorter duration of action than many opioids, so a patient can become re-sedated and experience recurrent respiratory depression once the naloxone effect fades. This is why continuous monitoring and repeat dosing readiness are essential after an initial response. The reversal is not permanent, contradicting option A. His improvement and combativeness are not related to blood glucose or an allergic reaction to the naloxone itself.
  4. The correct answer is B (1, 3 and 4): right patient requires confirming identity, right time means giving the medication at the clinically appropriate moment, and right documentation means recording the administration promptly and accurately. Statement 2 is false because right dose must always be verified regardless of a paramedic's experience level. Statement 5 is false because right route must match the intended method of administration and cannot be freely substituted for convenience.
  5. The correct answer is D (1:10,000): the standard concentration for intravenous epinephrine during cardiac arrest is 1:10,000, which is more dilute than the 1:1000 concentration used intramuscularly for anaphylaxis. Using the wrong concentration for the intravenous route could result in a dangerous overdose. This distinction between concentrations by route is a foundational prehospital pharmacology safety point. Confirming concentration before drawing up any epinephrine dose is essential.
  6. The correct answer is A (Intranasal, using an atomizer device): naloxone can be administered intranasally using an atomizer device, making it a practical option when IV or IM access is delayed or difficult. It is not given sublingually, rectally, or topically in standard prehospital practice. The intranasal route allows for rapid administration without requiring a needle. This makes it a valuable option in a busy or combative overdose scene.
  7. The correct answer is C (0.46 mg, drawn up as 0.46 mL): multiplying 0.01 mg/kg by 46 kg gives 0.46 mg, which is below the adult maximum of 0.5 mg, so the full calculated dose is used without capping. At a concentration of 1 mg/mL, 0.46 mg corresponds directly to 0.46 mL. Options describing a misread concentration or a misplaced decimal point represent calculation errors rather than the correct dose. Applying the maximum dose unnecessarily, as in option D, is incorrect since the calculated dose already falls under the cap.
  8. The correct answer is B (Intramuscular or intravenous): diphenhydramine is commonly given intramuscularly or intravenously in the prehospital setting when used as an adjunct treatment after epinephrine in anaphylaxis. It is not typically administered sublingually, by nebulizer, or rectally in this context. These routes allow for reasonably prompt systemic absorption compared to oral administration. Choosing an appropriate route supports timely relief of histamine-related symptoms.
  9. The correct answer is A (It relaxes bronchial smooth muscle, reversing bronchoconstriction): salbutamol is a beta-2 agonist that relaxes bronchial smooth muscle, opening the airways and reversing bronchoconstriction associated with asthma. It does not primarily reduce mucus production, reverse laryngeal edema, or sedate the patient. Its bronchodilating action is specific to the lower airways rather than the upper airway swelling seen in anaphylaxis. This mechanism explains why it improves wheezing and work of breathing in bronchospasm.
  10. The correct answer is D (Oral glucose risks aspiration if the patient cannot safely swallow): a patient with a decreased level of consciousness who cannot protect her airway is at risk of aspirating oral glucose into the lungs, making this route unsafe. This has nothing to do with the patient's age, as suggested in option A. Oral glucose is not universally contraindicated at low glucose values, only when swallowing and airway protection are compromised, and it remains effective once absorbed, contradicting option C. IM glucagon or IV dextrose are the appropriate alternatives in this situation.
  11. The correct answer is C (1, 2 and 4): selecting an appropriately sized catheter reduces phlebitis risk, securing the catheter properly prevents accidental dislodgement, and cleansing the site with antiseptic reduces infection risk. Statement 3 is false and unsafe because reusing a catheter after a failed attempt increases infection and contamination risk rather than reducing needle sticks safely. Statement 5 is false because excessive tourniquet time can contribute to vein damage and increased complication risk, contrary to having no effect.
  12. The correct answer is A (Right dose): recalculating and cross-checking a weight-based dose directly protects the right dose component of medication administration, ensuring the patient receives an accurate and safe amount. This action does not primarily address patient identity, timing, or documentation, which are separate rights. Dosage errors, particularly in weight-based calculations, are a well-recognized source of preventable medication mistakes. Double-checking math with a partner is a practical safeguard against this type of error.
  13. The correct answer is B (160 mg): the standard prehospital chewable ASA dose for suspected acute coronary syndrome is approximately 160 mg, commonly given as chewable tablets to allow faster absorption than swallowing whole. The 81 mg dose is a common daily maintenance dose rather than the acute treatment dose. The 325 mg and 500 mg doses exceed the typical prehospital protocol amount. Chewing the tablets, rather than swallowing them whole, speeds absorption and onset of the antiplatelet effect.
  14. The correct answer is D (10 to 30 degrees): a shallow insertion angle of approximately 10 to 30 degrees is used for peripheral IV cannulation to align the needle with the direction of the vein and avoid puncturing through the far wall. A steeper angle, such as 45 to 90 degrees, increases the risk of passing through the vein entirely and causing a hematoma. This shallow angle allows for a controlled approach and visible flashback once the vein is entered. Proper angle technique reduces failed attempts and complications.
  15. The correct answer is C (It stimulates the liver to convert stored glycogen into glucose): glucagon works by triggering the breakdown of glycogen stores in the liver into usable glucose, raising blood sugar in a hypoglycemic patient. It does not directly supply glucose molecules itself, unlike dextrose. It does not permanently suppress insulin release, and it has no significant effect on intestinal sugar absorption. This mechanism explains why glucagon requires functioning liver glycogen stores to be effective.
  16. The correct answer is A (Drowsiness): dimenhydrinate has antihistamine properties that commonly cause drowsiness as a side effect, which paramedics should anticipate, particularly if the patient will be moving or ambulating afterward. It is not typically associated with causing bronchospasm, hypertension, or increased urination. Monitoring for sedation is an important part of ongoing patient assessment after this medication is given. This side effect profile is consistent with other antihistamine-class medications.
  17. The correct answer is B (0.3 mg): a standard adult epinephrine auto-injector delivers a fixed manufacturer-set dose of 0.3 mg, regardless of the patient's actual body weight; it is not calculated from a 0.01 mg/kg formula. The 0.15 mg dose corresponds to the pediatric auto-injector, not the adult version. The 0.5 mg and 1 mg options do not match standard adult auto-injector dosing. Knowing the fixed dose delivered by these devices helps a paramedic anticipate whether additional epinephrine may still be needed.
  18. The correct answer is D (1, 3 and 4): bioavailability describes the proportion of a drug reaching systemic circulation unchanged, side effects are unintended effects occurring alongside the therapeutic effect, and contraindications identify situations where a drug should not be given. Statement 2 is false because onset of action varies considerably by route, as with intravenous versus oral administration. Statement 5 is false because drug interactions can also occur with drugs already present in the body from earlier doses, not only when given at the exact same moment.
  19. The correct answer is C (It competitively blocks opioid receptors, displacing the opioid): naloxone works as a competitive antagonist at opioid receptors, displacing opioid molecules and reversing their effects, including respiratory depression. It does not chemically neutralize the opioid itself or directly stimulate the respiratory centre independent of receptor activity. It also does not act by increasing hepatic metabolism of the opioid. This receptor-blocking mechanism explains why its effect can wear off before a long-acting opioid does, requiring repeat dosing.
  20. The correct answer is A (Isotonic): normal saline at 0.9 percent has an osmolarity similar to blood plasma, making it an isotonic solution that does not cause significant fluid shifts between compartments. Hypotonic and hypertonic solutions have different osmolarities that draw fluid into or out of cells, unlike normal saline. Non-ionic is not a term used to describe fluid tonicity. This isotonic property makes normal saline a safe, versatile choice for general prehospital IV therapy.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Non-rebreather mask with reservoir bag): a non-rebreather mask with a reservoir bag can deliver a high fraction of inspired oxygen, making it appropriate for a significantly hypoxic patient requiring aggressive oxygen therapy. A nasal cannula at low flow and a Venturi mask set to a low percentage deliver much lower oxygen concentrations. A simple face mask at a modest flow rate also provides less oxygen enrichment than a non-rebreather mask. Selecting the correct device ensures adequate oxygen delivery for the severity of hypoxia present.
  2. The correct answer is D (0.5 mg, 0.5 mL, since the calculated dose exceeds the maximum): multiplying 0.01 mg/kg by 104 kg gives 1.04 mg, which far exceeds the adult maximum single dose of 0.5 mg, so the dose must be capped at 0.5 mg rather than given at the full calculated amount. At a concentration of 1 mg/mL, this corresponds to 0.5 mL. Giving the uncapped 1.04 mg dose risks dangerous cardiovascular side effects from excess epinephrine. Respecting dose limits remains essential even for larger patients.
  3. The correct answer is A (Infiltration of fluid into surrounding tissue): swelling, coolness, firmness, and loss of flow despite an open clamp are classic signs that IV fluid is leaking into the surrounding tissue rather than entering the vein. Phlebitis typically presents with warmth and redness rather than coolness and firmness. An allergic reaction to IV fluid would present with different systemic signs, not localized swelling and coolness. Recognizing infiltration promptly allows the paramedic to stop the infusion and restart the line elsewhere.
  4. The correct answer is C (Right route): giving a medication by an unintended route, such as intramuscular instead of sublingual, is a violation of the right route component of medication administration. This error does not necessarily involve the wrong patient, an incorrect dose amount, or a documentation failure, though it should still be documented accurately once recognized. Route errors can significantly affect a drug's onset, absorption, and overall effect. Confirming the intended route before administration helps prevent this type of mistake.
  5. The correct answer is B (Up to three doses total): sublingual nitroglycerin may typically be repeated every five minutes for up to three total doses, provided the patient's blood pressure remains adequate throughout. Giving only one dose with no further doses permitted would under-treat a patient with persistent pain. Allowing five doses or an unlimited number without a defined limit ignores the need to monitor for cumulative hypotension. This repeat-dosing limit balances symptom relief against the medication's blood pressure lowering effect.
  6. The correct answer is D (To reduce the risk of introducing infection into the bloodstream): aseptic technique during IV insertion is primarily intended to minimize the introduction of bacteria into the bloodstream through the puncture site, reducing the risk of local and systemic infection. It is not primarily intended to reduce pain, make catheter threading easier, or shorten insertion time, though careful technique may have minor secondary benefits. Infection control remains a central safety concern whenever the skin barrier is breached for IV access. Consistent aseptic practice protects patients from a serious and preventable complication.
Case C0341

A high priority call is dispatched for a 61-year-old man with chest pain. The crew arrives to find the patient sitting upright in his kitchen, pale and diaphoretic, holding his chest. He reports the pain began 20 minutes ago while resting, describing it as a crushing pressure radiating to his left arm, rated 8 out of 10. He denies any known drug allergies and takes a beta blocker for high blood pressure. Vital signs: pulse 96 and irregular, respirations 22, blood pressure 108/68, SpO2 94 percent on room air, skin cool and diaphoretic.

  1. The correct answer is A (Chest pain with diaphoresis, pallor, and pain radiating to the arm): this constellation of findings, pain radiating to the arm plus autonomic signs, is the classic presentation of acute coronary ischemia and drives the treatment priority. The blood pressure and respiratory rate are relevant vitals but are not in themselves alarming or diagnostic. His antihypertensive medication is background history rather than an acute red flag. Diaphoresis and radiating pain together carry the strongest predictive value for a cardiac event in the field.
  2. The correct answer is C (To identify a dysrhythmia that may be contributing to his symptoms): an irregular pulse in a patient with chest pain raises concern for a dysrhythmia such as atrial fibrillation, which can worsen cardiac ischemia by reducing coronary perfusion. Early ECG monitoring lets the crew detect and trend rhythm changes throughout the call. Blood pressure and oxygen saturation are measured with separate devices, not the ECG. Spinal motion restriction is unrelated to a medical chest pain presentation.
  3. The correct answer is B (Confirming the patient has no allergy and no active bleeding disorder): Acetylsalicylic acid is withheld if the patient is allergic to it or has a condition such as active gastrointestinal bleeding, since it inhibits platelet function and could worsen bleeding. A nitroglycerin prescription and a specific systolic threshold are not prerequisites for ASA. Prior use within a year has no bearing on whether it is safe to give it now.
  4. The correct answer is D (Whether his systolic pressure is high enough to tolerate the drop): Nitroglycerin causes vasodilation and a further drop in blood pressure, so paramedics must confirm the systolic pressure remains high enough to avoid hypotension before assisting with a dose. Recent food intake and how the spray was stored do not affect the immediate safety decision. Oxygen saturation is not the deciding factor for administering a vasodilator.
  5. The correct answer is A (Apply supplemental oxygen titrated to relieve hypoxia and support comfort): With an SpO2 of 94 percent and clear signs of cardiac distress, titrated supplemental oxygen is appropriate to correct hypoxia without over-oxygenating the patient. Current evidence discourages high flow oxygen in normoxic cardiac patients, so titration rather than maximal flow is preferred. Withholding oxygen is not appropriate since 94 percent represents mild hypoxia in this context. Oxygen should not be delayed pending further diagnostics when the patient is symptomatic.
  6. The correct answer is B (1, 2, 3 and 4): Increased vagal tone, sinoatrial node disease, beta blocker overdose, and hypoxia in children, which classically causes bradycardia rather than tachycardia, are all recognized causes of a slow heart rate. Acute anxiety with hyperventilation typically produces tachycardia through sympathetic stimulation, not bradycardia. Statement 5 is the outlier that excludes any option containing it. This distinction matters because pediatric bradycardia should immediately prompt assessment for hypoxia as a reversible cause.
  7. The correct answer is D (Supraventricular tachycardia arising above the ventricles): A regular narrow complex rhythm at 150 beats per minute with no visible P waves is the classic description of supraventricular tachycardia, since the rapid rate and re-entry circuit obscure or bury the P wave. Sinus tachycardia requires a visible P wave before each QRS, which is absent here. Ventricular tachycardia produces a wide QRS, not a narrow one. Second degree block type one shows progressively lengthening PR intervals, which is not described in this strip.
  8. The correct answer is C (Atrial fibrillation with a rapid ventricular response): The combination of an irregularly irregular rhythm, absent P waves, and a chaotic fibrillatory baseline is the hallmark of atrial fibrillation, and a rate of 130 reflects a rapid ventricular response. Sinus arrhythmia is a regularly irregular pattern tied to breathing, not chaotic. Bigeminal PVCs would show a regularly alternating pattern of normal and widened beats. Third degree block produces a regular, slow escape rhythm with complete dissociation, not an irregular rapid one.
  9. The correct answer is A (Reduced ventricular filling time lowers stroke volume and cardiac output): At very fast rates, diastole shortens dramatically, leaving less time for the ventricles to fill, which reduces stroke volume; since cardiac output equals stroke volume multiplied by rate, output and blood pressure can actually fall. Coronary perfusion pressure typically decreases, not increases, at very high rates because diastolic filling time is also when the coronaries fill. Tachycardia does not directly raise peripheral resistance. There is no automatic reflex that converts tachycardia into bradycardia in this setting.
  10. The correct answer is D (1, 2, 3 and 4): Bilateral crackles, pink frothy sputum, jugular venous distension, and orthopnea are all classic findings of acute pulmonary edema from left heart failure, reflecting fluid backing up into the lungs and venous system. Unilateral leg swelling with calf tenderness instead suggests a localized process such as deep vein thrombosis, not pulmonary edema. Statement 5 is the outlier excluded from the correct combination. Recognizing this cluster of findings helps differentiate cardiac pulmonary edema from other causes of respiratory distress.
  11. The correct answer is B (Ventricular fibrillation with chaotic, disorganized electrical activity): A chaotic, undulating baseline of varying amplitude with no identifiable QRS complexes or rate describes ventricular fibrillation, the disorganized quivering of the ventricles that produces no effective cardiac output. Pulseless electrical activity shows an organized complex on the monitor despite no palpable pulse, which contradicts the description given. True asystole appears as a nearly flat, isoelectric line, not a chaotic undulating one, and must be confirmed in more than one lead. First degree block requires an organized rhythm with a prolonged PR interval, which is not present here.
  12. The correct answer is C (1, 2, 3 and 4): Severe hypovolemia, tension pneumothorax, hypoxia, and hyperkalemia are all classic reversible causes of pulseless electrical activity commonly remembered through the H's and T's framework. A simple vasovagal syncope episode causes transient hypotension and bradycardia but resolves on its own and is not a recognized cause of PEA arrest. Statement 5 is the outlier that excludes any option containing it. Identifying and treating a reversible cause is central to managing PEA effectively.
  13. The correct answer is B (Sinus bradycardia with normal atrioventricular conduction): A regular slow rate with a normal P wave preceding every QRS and a constant PR interval describes sinus bradycardia, where impulses still originate normally from the sinoatrial node but at a reduced rate. Complete heart block shows P waves and QRS complexes occurring independently of one another, which is not described here. Atrial flutter classically shows a sawtooth flutter wave pattern rather than a single normal P wave per beat. A junctional rhythm typically shows an absent, inverted, or retrograde P wave, not a normal one preceding the QRS.
  14. The correct answer is D (1, 2, 3 and 5): A hypertensive emergency is defined by severely elevated blood pressure combined with evidence of acute end organ damage, such as altered mental status, visual disturbance, chest pain suggesting aortic involvement, or pulmonary edema. Isolated epistaxis without other symptoms or signs of organ damage does not by itself indicate an emergency, since nosebleeds are common and usually unrelated to blood pressure severity. Statement 4 is the outlier that excludes any option containing it. Recognizing end organ signs is what distinguishes an emergency requiring urgent transport from less urgent elevated blood pressure.
  15. The correct answer is C (Continue high quality CPR and confirm airway and IV access): Asystole is a non-shockable rhythm, so management focuses on high quality chest compressions, airway management, and vascular access rather than defibrillation. Delivering a shock, whether defibrillation or synchronized cardioversion, will not organize a flatline rhythm and is not indicated. Withholding all resuscitation is inappropriate, since confirmed asystole with reversible causes still warrants continued CPR and treatment attempts. Confirming the rhythm in multiple leads before declaring true asystole is an important step already reflected in the scenario.
  16. The correct answer is B (The sinoatrial node located in the right atrium): The sinoatrial node is the heart's natural pacemaker, generating the impulse that spreads through the atria and initiates each normal cardiac cycle. The atrioventricular node briefly delays the impulse before it passes to the ventricles but does not normally initiate the beat. The bundle of His and Purkinje fibres conduct the impulse rapidly through the ventricles but act only as backup pacemakers if higher structures fail. Understanding this hierarchy helps explain why sinoatrial node disease produces bradycardia rather than a complete absence of rhythm.
  17. The correct answer is A (Begin CPR immediately and prepare to defibrillate as soon as possible): A wide complex tachycardia in a pulseless, unresponsive patient represents pulseless ventricular tachycardia, a shockable cardiac arrest rhythm requiring immediate CPR and defibrillation. Nitroglycerin is not appropriate in a pulseless patient and does not address the arrest. Withholding compressions delays treatment of a rhythm that will not resolve on its own. Vagal manoeuvres are reserved for a stable patient with a pulse, not a pulseless arrest presentation.
  18. The correct answer is D (1, 2, 4 and 5): Confirming the patient is in arrest with a shockable rhythm, ensuring everyone is clear before the shock, and removing a transdermal patch that could cause a burn or block pad contact are all appropriate safety and clinical considerations. Delivering a shock while the patient remains on a wet metal surface is unsafe and should be corrected first, since it creates a conduction and safety hazard for both patient and crew. Statement 3 is the outlier that excludes any option containing it. Safety checks are as essential to defibrillation as confirming the shockable rhythm itself.
  19. The correct answer is B (Conversion from atrial fibrillation back to normal sinus rhythm): The reappearance of a regular rate, a normal narrow QRS, and a distinct P wave before each complex indicates the heart has converted from the previously irregular, P wave absent rhythm back to normal sinus rhythm. A new ventricular tachycardia would show a wide QRS, not a narrow one, and this description does not support that. Complete heart block would show P waves and QRS complexes with no fixed relationship, which is not the case here. A fibrillatory rhythm would remain irregular and lack organized P waves, which contradicts this strip.
Case C0360

A priority one call is dispatched for an unresponsive man collapsed in a shopping mall. Bystanders report he suddenly clutched his chest and fell to the floor approximately three minutes ago. A bystander has started chest compressions. On arrival, the crew finds a 58-year-old man who is unresponsive, apneic, and pulseless. An AED applied by a bystander is analyzing the rhythm.

  1. The correct answer is C (Ensure all rescuers are clear of contact with the patient): Before any defibrillation shock is delivered, the top priority is confirming that no one is touching the patient to prevent inadvertent shock to a rescuer. Advanced airway placement and intravenous access can wait until after the shock and resumed compressions. Repositioning the patient is not the immediate priority when a shock has already been advised and is ready to deliver.
  2. The correct answer is A (Resume chest compressions immediately without pausing to check a pulse): Current resuscitation guidelines call for chest compressions to resume immediately after a shock without pausing to check for a pulse, since even a successfully defibrillated heart often needs a brief period of compressions to restore an effective rhythm and perfusion. Delaying compressions to check a pulse or reanalyze the rhythm wastes valuable time when the heart is not yet reliably perfusing. A second shock is not delivered until the device reanalyzes after the next compression cycle.
  3. The correct answer is C (To increase coronary and cerebral perfusion pressure during CPR): Epinephrine's alpha adrenergic effect causes peripheral vasoconstriction, which raises aortic diastolic pressure and improves coronary and cerebral perfusion during chest compressions. It does not directly convert a shockable or non-shockable rhythm into an organized one on its own. It increases, rather than reduces, myocardial oxygen demand through its beta adrenergic stimulation. Epinephrine increases heart rate and contractility rather than slowing the heart.
  4. The correct answer is D (The patient has achieved return of spontaneous circulation): A sudden sharp rise in end tidal carbon dioxide combined with a palpable pulse is a reliable early indicator that the heart has resumed an effective, spontaneous rhythm, known as return of spontaneous circulation. A dislodged tube typically causes end tidal carbon dioxide to fall toward zero, not rise. A tension pneumothorax would be expected to worsen perfusion and lower, not raise, the reading. Shallow compressions would also be expected to lower rather than raise end tidal carbon dioxide.
  5. The correct answer is B (1, 2, 4 and 5): Family history of premature disease, diabetes, tobacco use, and elevated LDL cholesterol are all well established, evidence based risk factors for coronary artery disease. Regular aerobic exercise is protective and actually reduces cardiovascular risk rather than increasing it, making it the outlier. Statement 3 is the item that excludes any option containing it. Recognizing modifiable risk factors like smoking and cholesterol helps explain why patient education is part of ongoing cardiac care.
  6. The correct answer is A (Atrial flutter with four to one atrioventricular conduction): A sawtooth baseline at a regular atrial rate near 300 beats per minute is the classic appearance of atrial flutter, and a ventricular rate of 75 reflects a four to one conduction ratio through the atrioventricular node. Atrial fibrillation shows a chaotic baseline without organized flutter waves, which does not match this description. Ventricular tachycardia would show a wide QRS complex, not a narrow one. Sinus tachycardia would show a single upright P wave before each QRS rather than a sawtooth flutter pattern.
  7. The correct answer is B (The right coronary artery in most patients): In the majority of people, the right coronary artery supplies the inferior wall of the heart, so inferior ischemia is most often linked to a problem in that vessel. The left anterior descending artery predominantly supplies the anterior wall and septum, not the inferior wall. The circumflex artery supplies the inferior wall in only a minority of patients with a different coronary dominance pattern, so it is not the most common source. The pulmonary artery carries deoxygenated blood to the lungs and plays no role in supplying the myocardium.
  8. The correct answer is D (Second degree atrioventricular block, Mobitz type one): A progressively lengthening PR interval that culminates in a dropped QRS complex, in a repeating pattern, is the defining feature of second degree atrioventricular block, Mobitz type one, also called Wenckebach. First degree block shows a constant, prolonged PR interval with no dropped beats, which does not match this pattern. Third degree block shows P waves and QRS complexes occurring completely independently with no relationship at all. A premature atrial contraction produces an early beat, not a progressively lengthening interval with a dropped complex.
  9. The correct answer is C (1, 2, 3 and 5): Low systolic blood pressure, recent use of erectile dysfunction medications that potentiate hypotension, suspected right ventricular infarction, and heart rates outside the acceptable range are all recognized reasons to withhold nitroglycerin because of the risk of severe hypotension or inadequate right-sided filling. Mild nausea alone is a common symptom accompanying cardiac chest pain and is not, by itself, a reason to withhold the medication. Statement 4 is the outlier that excludes any option containing it. These contraindications exist because nitroglycerin's vasodilating effect can be dangerous in a patient who is already borderline or pressure dependent.
  10. The correct answer is B (Pulseless electrical activity requiring a search for a reversible cause): An organized electrical rhythm on the monitor with no corresponding palpable pulse is, by definition, pulseless electrical activity, and management centres on identifying and correcting a reversible underlying cause. Ventricular fibrillation would show a chaotic, disorganized waveform, not an organized narrow complex. Sinus rhythm implies effective perfusion producing a pulse, which contradicts the pulseless finding described. Asystole would show a flat line rather than an organized QRS complex.
  11. The correct answer is D (Right-sided heart failure causing systemic venous congestion): Peripheral edema, jugular venous distension, and hepatomegaly with clear lungs reflect blood backing up into the systemic venous system, which is characteristic of right-sided heart failure. Left-sided heart failure would be expected to produce pulmonary findings such as crackles, which are notably absent here. Cardiogenic shock would present with hypotension and poor perfusion rather than isolated systemic congestion. Compensated hypertension without heart failure would not produce jugular venous distension or hepatomegaly.
  12. The correct answer is B (Place the pad at least eight centimetres away from the device): When a pacemaker or implanted defibrillator is present, pads should be positioned a reasonable distance, typically around eight centimetres, away from the device to avoid damaging it while still allowing effective current delivery. Defibrillation is not cancelled simply because a pacemaker is present, since untreated ventricular fibrillation is immediately life threatening. Placing a pad directly over the device risks damaging it and may reduce shock effectiveness. Standard defibrillation energy settings are still used and are not routinely reduced for a pacemaker patient.
  13. The correct answer is C (A rising end tidal carbon dioxide trend during compressions): A rising end tidal carbon dioxide reading reflects improving pulmonary blood flow and is considered one of the most objective, reliable indicators that compressions are generating meaningful perfusion. Fixed, dilated pupils throughout resuscitation instead suggest poor cerebral perfusion, not adequate perfusion. Respiratory rate on the bag-valve-mask is set by the rescuer and does not indicate perfusion. Checking a radial pulse after every single compression is impractical and would interrupt compressions rather than confirm their effectiveness.
  14. The correct answer is A (Sinus tachycardia related to an underlying physiological stressor): A regular rate with a normal, upright P wave before every narrow QRS and a normal PR interval describes sinus tachycardia, which commonly results from stressors such as pain, anxiety, fever, or dehydration. Supraventricular tachycardia usually presents at a faster rate with P waves that are absent or buried within the QRS, which is not described here. Ventricular tachycardia would show a wide QRS complex rather than a narrow one. Atrial fibrillation is, by definition, irregular and lacks a distinct P wave, which contradicts this strip.
  15. The correct answer is C (Jugular venous distension consistent with a failing heart pump): Jugular venous distension reflects blood backing up behind a failing heart and is a key finding that points toward cardiogenic rather than hypovolemic shock. Flat, collapsed jugular veins instead suggest low circulating volume, which is more typical of hypovolemic shock. A rapid heart rate is a nonspecific compensatory finding present in both types of shock and does not help differentiate them. Cool, clammy skin from vasoconstriction can also occur in both types of shock and is not distinguishing on its own.
  16. The correct answer is B (Apply continuous positive airway pressure if available and tolerated): Continuous positive airway pressure helps push fluid out of the alveoli and reduces the work of breathing in acute pulmonary edema, making it an appropriate next step when the patient remains hypoxic despite oxygen alone. A fluid bolus would worsen pulmonary congestion in a patient who is already fluid overloaded. Placing the patient supine would worsen, not reduce, breathing difficulty in pulmonary edema, since these patients breathe easier sitting upright. Withholding oxygen in a severely hypoxic, distressed patient would be inappropriate and dangerous.
  17. The correct answer is D (2, 3, 4 and 5): Fatigue, epigastric discomfort mistaken for indigestion, isolated jaw or neck discomfort, and sudden dyspnea without chest pain are all well documented atypical presentations of cardiac ischemia, especially in women, elderly, and diabetic patients whose pain perception may differ. Sharp, well-localized pain that worsens with palpation is more typical of a musculoskeletal cause, since cardiac pain is usually diffuse, pressure-like, and not reproducible by touch. Statement 1 is the outlier that excludes any option containing it. Recognizing atypical presentations prevents under-triage of patients who do not fit the classic crushing chest pain picture.
  18. The correct answer is A (Immediate defibrillation after confirming the shockable rhythm): A coarse, chaotic waveform with no organized complexes in a pulseless patient is ventricular fibrillation, a shockable arrest rhythm that requires prompt unsynchronized defibrillation as the definitive treatment. Synchronized cardioversion is used for organized rhythms with a pulse, not for a chaotic pulseless rhythm without identifiable QRS complexes to synchronize to. Vagal manoeuvres are appropriate for a stable patient with a pulse, not for cardiac arrest. Oxygen alone will not correct the underlying electrical chaos and defibrillation must not be delayed.
  19. The correct answer is D (Pre-plan tasks and rotate compressors every two minutes to limit fatigue): Rotating compressors roughly every two minutes, before fatigue sets in, and pre-planning roles such as airway and medication tasks helps maintain high quality, minimally interrupted compressions. Stopping compressions completely for routine airway tasks is unnecessary with modern techniques that allow continued compressions during many airway interventions. Rhythm and pulse checks are performed periodically, not after every single cycle, since frequent checks themselves interrupt compressions. Allowing a rescuer to fatigue fully before rotating leads to progressively shallower, less effective compressions.
  20. The correct answer is C (1, 2, 3 and 5): ASA administration, cardiac monitoring with a 12-lead ECG, titrated oxygen, and positioning for comfort are all standard, evidence based components of prehospital acute coronary syndrome care. Delaying transport until the pain fully resolves is inappropriate, since definitive treatment such as reperfusion therapy is time sensitive and transport should not be held up waiting for symptom resolution. Statement 4 is the outlier that excludes any option containing it. Rapid, efficient prehospital care paired with timely transport gives these patients the best chance at a good outcome.
  21. The correct answer is B (1, 2, 4 and 5): Synchronized cardioversion is used for an unstable but perfusing patient with an organized rhythm that has an identifiable QRS complex to synchronize to, when instability such as a falling blood pressure or altered mentation is present. A pulseless, unresponsive patient has no organized perfusion to cardiovert and instead requires immediate CPR and defibrillation if the rhythm is shockable. Statement 3 is the outlier that excludes any option containing it. Synchronizing the shock to the QRS avoids delivering energy during the vulnerable T wave, which could otherwise induce ventricular fibrillation.
  22. The correct answer is A (Premature ventricular contractions arising from an irritable ventricular focus): Wide, bizarre, early QRS complexes with no preceding P wave and a compensatory pause are the classic description of premature ventricular contractions, which originate from an irritable focus within the ventricle itself. Premature atrial contractions would show an early but usually normal, narrow QRS preceded by an abnormal P wave, which is not described here. Normal sinus beats with a bundle branch delay would occur on time, not prematurely, and would still typically be preceded by a P wave. Atrial flutter waves produce a sawtooth atrial pattern, not the isolated wide ventricular complexes described.
  23. The correct answer is B (Continue compressions during ventilation once an advanced airway is placed, using brief coordinated pauses for bag-valve-mask breaths beforehand): current resuscitation guidance favours continuous compressions with asynchronous ventilation once an advanced airway is in place, since interruptions in compressions reduce coronary perfusion pressure; before an advanced airway is placed, bag-valve-mask ventilation is still coordinated with brief pauses in a 30:2 pattern. Pausing for a full minute for every airway attempt is excessive and inconsistent with minimizing interruptions. Airway management should not be arbitrarily delayed until after a set number of shocks, since oxygenation and ventilation remain important throughout resuscitation. Hyperventilating the patient at an excessive rate increases intrathoracic pressure and can reduce venous return and cardiac output.
  24. The correct answer is D (The bradycardia is accompanied by hypotension and altered mentation): Bradycardia becomes a treatment priority when it is symptomatic, meaning it is accompanied by findings such as hypotension, altered level of consciousness, or signs of shock attributable to the slow rate. Whether the rate is an even or odd number has no clinical significance whatsoever. Age alone does not determine whether bradycardia requires immediate treatment. A narrow QRS complex describes the rhythm's morphology but does not by itself indicate the patient is unstable.
  25. The correct answer is C (A physiological sinus response to fever and anxiety): A regular rhythm with a normal P wave before every narrow QRS in a febrile, anxious patient describes sinus tachycardia, a normal physiological response to increased sympathetic drive from fever, pain, or anxiety. A re-entry dysrhythmia would typically show a faster rate with P waves that are absent or buried, not a normal P wave before each beat. A ventricular ectopic focus would produce a wide QRS complex, not a narrow one. A conduction block would be expected to slow, not increase, the heart rate.
  26. The correct answer is A (Third degree atrioventricular block with a ventricular escape rhythm): Complete dissociation between P waves and QRS complexes, with a slow, wide escape rhythm, describes third degree atrioventricular block, in which no atrial impulses reach the ventricles and a lower pacemaker takes over. First degree block shows a constant relationship between each P wave and QRS with only a prolonged interval, not complete dissociation. Sinus bradycardia maintains a normal one-to-one relationship between P waves and QRS complexes, which is absent here. Mobitz type one shows a progressively lengthening PR interval before a dropped beat, not complete independence between P waves and QRS complexes.
  27. The correct answer is D (Cardiac tamponade compressing the heart's ability to fill): Hypotension, muffled heart sounds, and jugular venous distension together form Beck's triad, a classic though not always complete presentation of cardiac tamponade, where fluid in the pericardial sac restricts the heart's ability to fill. A simple pneumothorax does not typically cause muffled heart sounds or this specific combination of findings. Massive hemothorax more often causes hypotension with hypovolemia and decreased, not muffled, breath sounds rather than muffled heart sounds. Isolated rib fractures would not be expected to cause jugular venous distension or muffled heart sounds.
  28. The correct answer is C (1, 2, 3 and 5): Early recognition and activation, early high quality CPR, early defibrillation, and early advanced and post-arrest care are the recognized links in the cardiac chain of survival, each one improving the odds of a good outcome. Deliberately delaying advanced care until hospital arrival contradicts the entire concept of the chain of survival, which emphasizes acting as early as possible at every link. Statement 4 is the outlier that excludes any option containing it. Every link in the chain must function well, since a weak link at any point reduces the patient's overall chance of survival.
  29. The correct answer is B (1, 2, 3 and 5): Being on a blood thinner alone, chest pain consistent with ischemia, no allergy to acetylsalicylic acid, and a safe blood pressure are all factors consistent with proceeding with ASA administration, since routine anticoagulant use is not itself a contraindication. Active gastrointestinal bleeding is a genuine contraindication because ASA further impairs platelet function and could worsen active bleeding. Statement 4 is the outlier that excludes any option containing it. Distinguishing a chronic medication like an anticoagulant from an active bleeding contraindication is an important clinical judgment.
  30. The correct answer is B (Attempt vagal manoeuvres before considering pharmacological treatment): In a stable patient with supraventricular tachycardia, vagal manoeuvres such as bearing down are an appropriate low risk first step that can terminate the re-entry circuit before moving to medications or electrical therapy. Unsynchronized defibrillation is reserved for pulseless, chaotic rhythms and would be inappropriate and dangerous in a stable, perfusing patient. Chest compressions are not indicated since the patient has a palpable pulse and stable blood pressure. Withholding all treatment ignores the patient's symptoms of palpitations and lightheadedness, which still warrant an intervention attempt in a stable patient.
  31. The correct answer is D (1, 2, 4 and 5): Unresponsiveness, occasional agonal gasping, the absence of a palpable pulse within ten seconds, and pale or cyanotic skin together confirm cardiac arrest, since agonal gasps are not effective breathing and must not be mistaken for a sign of life. A strong, regular, easily palpable pulse would rule out cardiac arrest rather than support it, making it the outlier here. Statement 3 is the item that excludes any option containing it. Recognizing agonal gasping as a component of arrest, not normal breathing, is a critical skill that prevents a dangerous delay in starting CPR.
Case C0391

You are dispatched code 4 (lights and sirens) to a private residence for a 58-year-old male who collapsed while shovelling snow. On arrival you find the patient unresponsive on the driveway, his wife performing chest compressions taught to her by the dispatcher. The patient is pulseless and apneic. Your partner attaches the AED while you continue compressions. The AED analyzes the rhythm and advises a shock.

  1. The correct answer is B (Confirm no one is touching the patient, state "clear," and deliver the shock immediately): once the AED has finished its analysis and advised a shock, delaying delivery reduces the chance of successful defibrillation and lowers survival rates. A pulse check is not performed before a defibrillation attempt because the AED has already identified a shockable rhythm through its own analysis. Ensuring everyone is clear of the patient prevents inadvertent shock to a rescuer. Interrupting compressions for ventilation at this point would only further delay the time-critical shock.
  2. The correct answer is C (Resume chest compressions immediately without pausing to check for a pulse): current resuscitation guidelines direct rescuers to resume high-quality chest compressions immediately after a shock is delivered, without stopping to feel for a pulse. Even a successful defibrillation often leaves the heart in a non-perfusing rhythm for a short period, so immediate compressions maintain circulation to vital organs. Pausing to check a pulse or waiting for the AED to reanalyze wastes valuable compression time. The AED will prompt the crew when it is ready to reanalyze after the standard two-minute cycle.
  3. The correct answer is A (An acute coronary artery occlusion triggering ventricular fibrillation): a prior major heart attack combined with risk factors such as high cholesterol and hypertension strongly points to underlying coronary artery disease. Acute plaque rupture and thrombus formation in a coronary artery is the most common cause of sudden cardiac arrest in adults, typically precipitating ventricular fibrillation. Pulmonary embolism, cerebral aneurysm rupture, and vasovagal bradycardia are all less consistent with this patient's cardiac history and presentation. The scenario's clinical picture is classic for an ischemic cardiac event.
  4. The correct answer is D (Frequently reassess airway, breathing, circulation and vital signs while preparing for rapid transport): patients who achieve return of spontaneous circulation remain highly unstable and can re-arrest at any time, so continuous reassessment of ABCs and vital signs is essential. Oxygen therapy should be continued and titrated, not discontinued, since post-arrest patients often remain hypoxic or hemodynamically fragile. Allowing the patient to stand risks sudden collapse, and delaying transport for an extensive on-scene survey wastes critical time when definitive hospital care is needed. Rapid, closely monitored transport is the priority.
  5. The correct answer is B (1, 3 and 4): ventricular fibrillation is chaotic, disorganized electrical activity that produces no effective cardiac output, and early defibrillation dramatically improves survival because the rhythm becomes harder to convert the longer it persists. Coarse VF, with larger amplitude waveforms, generally responds better to defibrillation than fine VF, which reflects a more depleted myocardium. Statement 2 is false because VF produces no organized QRS or P wave at all, and statement 5 is false because VF is treated with unsynchronized defibrillation, not synchronized cardioversion.
  6. The correct answer is A (1, 3 and 5): pressure-like pain radiating to the arm or jaw, associated diaphoresis and nausea, and atypical or absent pain in diabetic patients are all classic and well-documented presentations of acute coronary syndrome. Sharp, well-localized pain that worsens with palpation is more typical of musculoskeletal chest wall pain, and pain relieved by a deep breath suggests a pleuritic or non-cardiac cause. Recognizing these atypical presentations, especially in diabetics, is important because delayed recognition can delay life-saving treatment.
  7. The correct answer is C (Supraventricular tachycardia): a regular, narrow-complex rhythm at a rapid rate around 150 beats per minute with no visible P waves is the classic presentation of supraventricular tachycardia, since the P wave is typically buried within or immediately after the preceding QRS complex. Sinus tachycardia would show a visible, normal P wave before each QRS. Ventricular tachycardia produces a wide, not narrow, QRS complex, and atrial fibrillation is irregularly irregular rather than perfectly regular.
  8. The correct answer is D (Atrial fibrillation): an irregularly irregular rhythm with narrow QRS complexes and no discernible, organized P waves, replaced by a chaotic fibrillatory baseline, is the hallmark of atrial fibrillation. Sinus arrhythmia varies with respiration but still shows normal P waves before each QRS. Multifocal atrial tachycardia shows at least three distinct P wave morphologies rather than a chaotic baseline, and atrial flutter classically produces a regular sawtooth pattern rather than an irregular chaotic one.
  9. The correct answer is A (Begin CPR and defibrillate as soon as possible): a wide, regular, rapid QRS rhythm in a pulseless, unresponsive patient represents pulseless ventricular tachycardia, which is managed identically to ventricular fibrillation with immediate CPR and defibrillation. Synchronized cardioversion is reserved for a patient who has a pulse but is unstable, not for a pulseless patient. Nitroglycerin has no role in a pulseless cardiac arrest, and a vagal manoeuvre is used for stable narrow-complex tachycardias, not for a pulseless wide-complex rhythm.
  10. The correct answer is B (1, 2 and 4): hypovolemia, tension pneumothorax, and hypoxia are all recognized reversible causes of pulseless electrical activity that must be actively considered and addressed during resuscitation. Hyperglycemia is not one of the classically taught reversible H and T causes; hypoglycemia, not hyperglycemia, is the metabolic derangement included in that framework. Systematically working through the reversible causes gives the best chance of identifying and correcting the underlying problem driving the arrest.
  11. The correct answer is D (Sinus bradycardia): a regular rhythm below 60 beats per minute with a normal P wave before every narrow QRS and a constant PR interval originates from the sinus node conducting normally, just at a slow rate, which defines sinus bradycardia. First-degree AV block would show a prolonged, not simply constant, PR interval. Junctional rhythm typically lacks a normal preceding P wave, and sinus arrest presents as a pause in rhythm rather than a sustained slow regular rate.
  12. The correct answer is C (Begin high-quality CPR, confirm the rhythm in a second lead, and continue resuscitation per protocol): a flat, straight baseline with no organized electrical activity represents asystole, which is not a shockable rhythm, so defibrillation and cardioversion are inappropriate. Because fine ventricular fibrillation can occasionally mimic a flat line in a single lead, confirming asystole in a second lead is an important step before continuing standard resuscitation. High-quality CPR and identification of reversible causes remain the priority, and withholding all care is never appropriate while resuscitation is being attempted.
  13. The correct answer is B (1, 2 and 4): before giving ASA the paramedic should confirm the patient is not allergic to it, does not have active gastrointestinal bleeding, and has not already taken an adequate dose that day, since these are recognized contraindications or duplications of therapy. A systolic blood pressure below 90 is not a specific concern for ASA administration itself, unlike medications such as nitroglycerin where hypotension is a direct contraindication. ASA works by inhibiting platelet aggregation and is generally safe across a wide range of blood pressures.
  14. The correct answer is B (Sinus tachycardia): a regular rhythm with a rate of 100 beats per minute, narrow QRS complexes, and a normal P wave with a normal, constant PR interval before every QRS represents sinus tachycardia, simply the sinus node firing faster than normal. Atrial flutter typically shows a sawtooth flutter pattern rather than discrete normal P waves. Junctional tachycardia would lack a normal preceding P wave, and supraventricular tachycardia typically presents at a faster rate with no visible P wave, unlike this clearly identifiable sinus pattern.
  15. The correct answer is D (Acute pulmonary edema secondary to left heart failure): sudden dyspnea with pink frothy sputum, bilateral crackles, and severe hypertension is a classic presentation of acute cardiogenic pulmonary edema, in which the failing left ventricle causes fluid to back up into the lungs. Pneumothorax typically presents with unilateral, not bilateral, findings and diminished breath sounds rather than crackles. Pulmonary embolism does not typically produce pink frothy sputum, and an asthma exacerbation would present with wheezing rather than crackles and frothy sputum.
  16. The correct answer is C (Accelerated idioventricular rhythm): a regular, wide-complex rhythm at a rate between roughly 40 and 100 beats per minute with no visible P waves, occurring in a stable, conscious patient with a pulse, is characteristic of accelerated idioventricular rhythm arising from a ventricular ectopic pacemaker. Ventricular tachycardia typically occurs at a faster rate, generally above 100 to 120 beats per minute. A bundle branch block would still show a preceding P wave since the sinus node remains the pacemaker, and junctional rhythm typically produces a narrow rather than wide QRS complex.
  17. The correct answer is A (1, 2 and 4): before giving nitroglycerin the paramedic must confirm the patient's blood pressure meets the protocol's minimum threshold, ask about recent erectile dysfunction medication use because of a dangerous interaction that can cause profound hypotension, and reassess blood pressure after every dose since the medication is a potent vasodilator. Suspected right ventricular infarction is actually a contraindication to nitroglycerin, not a consideration that supports giving it, because these patients are preload-dependent and can become severely hypotensive.
  18. The correct answer is B (Immediate defibrillation): chaotic, disorganized electrical activity too fast to count, with irregular QRS complexes of varying shape and amplitude in a pulseless patient, describes ventricular fibrillation, which requires immediate unsynchronized defibrillation as the definitive treatment. Synchronized cardioversion requires an organized rhythm to synchronize with, which VF does not have. Vagal manoeuvres are used for stable narrow-complex tachycardias, and nitroglycerin has no role in the management of a pulseless cardiac arrest.
  19. The correct answer is D (Atrial flutter with 2:1 conduction): a sawtooth pattern of atrial activity at approximately 300 beats per minute with a regular ventricular rate of exactly half that, around 150 beats per minute, is the textbook description of atrial flutter conducting through the AV node in a fixed 2:1 ratio. Atrial fibrillation produces an irregularly irregular rhythm rather than this regular pattern, supraventricular tachycardia and sinus tachycardia do not classically produce a sawtooth flutter wave pattern.
  20. The correct answer is C (1, 2 and 4): the recognized links in the cardiac chain of survival are early recognition and activation of emergency response, early high-quality CPR, early defibrillation, early advanced life support, and post-arrest care. Statement 3, deliberately delaying defibrillation, directly contradicts the well-established principle that early defibrillation dramatically improves survival from a shockable rhythm, so it cannot be part of the chain of survival.
  21. The correct answer is B (Sinus tachycardia): a regular, narrow-complex rhythm at 130 beats per minute with a normal-appearing P wave preceding every QRS confirms the impulse is originating from the sinus node, simply firing faster than normal, which defines sinus tachycardia. Supraventricular tachycardia and junctional tachycardia are both associated with an absent or abnormal P wave, and atrial flutter typically shows a sawtooth pattern rather than a normal, discrete P wave morphology.
  22. The correct answer is D (Junctional (AV nodal) tissue): a regular, narrow-complex rhythm with an inverted P wave appearing immediately after the QRS complex is characteristic of a junctional rhythm, where the impulse originates near the AV node and conducts backward, or retrograde, into the atria after first activating the ventricles. An impulse from the sinoatrial node produces an upright P wave before the QRS, an atrial ectopic focus produces an abnormal but usually preceding P wave, and ventricular myocardial impulses typically produce a wide, not narrow, QRS complex.
  23. The correct answer is A (Second-degree AV block, Type I (Wenckebach)): a progressively lengthening PR interval over several beats followed by a single dropped QRS complex, with the cycle then repeating, is the defining pattern of Mobitz Type I, also known as Wenckebach. Type II block shows a constant PR interval before a sudden dropped beat rather than progressive lengthening. Third-degree block shows complete dissociation between P waves and QRS complexes, and first-degree block does not drop any beats at all.
  24. The correct answer is C (It causes venodilation, reducing preload and myocardial oxygen demand): nitroglycerin primarily dilates the venous system, decreasing the volume of blood returning to the heart, which lowers ventricular wall tension and reduces the heart's oxygen demand, relieving ischemic pain. It does not increase contractility or heart rate, and it has no direct thrombolytic effect on a coronary clot. Its coronary vasodilating effect is secondary to its dominant venodilatory action on preload.
Case C0415

You are dispatched to a workplace for a 55-year-old male complaining of central chest pressure that began 30 minutes ago while lifting boxes. He describes the pain as an 8 out of 10, radiating to his left shoulder, and is accompanied by nausea and diaphoresis. He is conscious, alert, and denies any known drug allergies. His vital signs are: heart rate 96, blood pressure 148/92, respiratory rate 20, and oxygen saturation 95 percent on room air.

  1. The correct answer is D (Apply oxygen if indicated, obtain vital signs and a focused history, and prepare to administer ASA per protocol): for a patient with suspected cardiac ischemia, the priority is a systematic assessment including vital signs and history, correcting hypoxia if present, and administering ASA per protocol once contraindications are ruled out. Giving nitroglycerin before any assessment risks administering it to a hypotensive patient, placing the patient supine with legs elevated is not indicated for suspected cardiac chest pain, and delaying ASA until a 12-lead confirms ST elevation would unnecessarily delay a beneficial, low-risk medication.
  2. The correct answer is A (Withhold nitroglycerin for now given the recent sildenafil dose the patient reported taking): sildenafil requires a minimum 24-hour interval (tadalafil requires 48 hours) before nitrate administration because of the risk of profound, refractory hypotension from combined vasodilation. An interval of only 18 hours does not meet that threshold, so nitroglycerin must be withheld and medical direction sought before considering any nitrate.
  3. The correct answer is A (Progression of myocardial ischemia with possible evolving cardiogenic compromise): worsening chest pain accompanied by new diaphoresis, pallor, and a significant drop in blood pressure suggests the ischemic event is progressing and the heart's pumping ability may be deteriorating, raising concern for cardiogenic shock. This combination of findings is not consistent with simple anxiety, and it clearly represents worsening rather than resolution of the cardiac event. Oxygen therapy does not typically cause hypotension and diaphoresis in this manner, making an adverse reaction an unlikely explanation.
  4. The correct answer is C (Treat as pulseless electrical activity, beginning CPR and searching for reversible causes): an organized electrical rhythm on the monitor with no palpable pulse defines pulseless electrical activity, and the presence of a pacemaker does not change this definition since a pacemaker only generates an electrical signal, not mechanical contraction. CPR should begin immediately and the crew should search for and treat reversible causes. Defibrillation is not indicated for an organized rhythm without evidence of a shockable arrhythmia, and CPR must never be withheld pending specialist evaluation of a device.
  5. The correct answer is D (Reduced arterial wall elasticity increasing resistance to blood flow): as people age, the walls of the arteries progressively stiffen and lose elasticity, a process sometimes called arteriosclerosis, which increases resistance to the blood ejected from the left ventricle and raises systolic pressure. Increased vagal tone would slow heart rate rather than raise blood pressure, decreased blood volume would tend to lower blood pressure, and increased venous return alone does not explain the sustained rise in systolic pressure seen with normal aging.
  6. The correct answer is B (1, 3 and 4): appropriate management of a hypertensive emergency includes keeping the patient calm to avoid further raising blood pressure, performing a focused neurological assessment to detect signs of end-organ damage, and transporting promptly for definitive care. Attempting to rapidly normalize blood pressure with field medications is inappropriate and dangerous, since a too-rapid drop can precipitate ischemic stroke or other organ hypoperfusion; blood pressure in a hypertensive emergency should be lowered gradually under controlled hospital conditions, not aggressively in the field.
  7. The correct answer is C (The sinoatrial node and inferior wall of the left ventricle): in the majority of the population, the right coronary artery supplies the sinoatrial node, the atrioventricular node, and the inferior wall of the left ventricle, which is why right coronary artery occlusions commonly produce inferior wall infarctions and conduction disturbances. The left ventricular anterior wall and apex are typically supplied by the left anterior descending artery, and the left atrium receives blood primarily from branches of the left coronary circulation rather than the right coronary artery.
  8. The correct answer is A (To generate and coordinate electrical impulses that trigger organized myocardial contraction): the cardiac conduction system, beginning at the sinoatrial node and travelling through the atria, AV node, bundle of His, and Purkinje fibres, exists to generate and coordinate the electrical signals that cause the atria and ventricles to contract in an organized, sequential manner. It plays no direct role in regulating blood volume return, filtering blood, or producing hormones that control vascular resistance, all of which are functions of other body systems.
  9. The correct answer is D (1, 3 and 4): jugular venous distension, pulmonary crackles, and delayed capillary refill are all consistent findings in cardiogenic shock, reflecting the combination of backward failure causing fluid congestion and forward failure causing poor peripheral perfusion. Bounding, strong peripheral pulses would not be expected in cardiogenic shock; instead, weak, thready pulses are typical because the failing heart cannot generate adequate stroke volume, making that finding inconsistent with this diagnosis.
  10. The correct answer is B (The beginning of ventricular systole, producing the first heart sound): closure of the mitral and tricuspid, or atrioventricular, valves occurs at the very start of ventricular systole as rising ventricular pressure exceeds atrial pressure, and this closure produces the first heart sound, S1. The second heart sound, S2, is instead produced by closure of the aortic and pulmonic valves at the end of systole. Atrial contraction and atrial repolarization are electrical and mechanical events that occur before this valve closure, not as a result of it.
  11. The correct answer is A (1, 2 and 4): before applying an AED the paramedic should ensure the chest is dry, remove and wipe away any transdermal medication patches to prevent burns and ensure good pad contact, and continue chest compressions while the pads are being applied to minimize interruptions. Pads should never be placed directly over an implanted pacemaker or defibrillator generator; they should instead be positioned at least a few centimetres away to avoid interfering with the device and reducing shock effectiveness.
  12. The correct answer is C (Automaticity): automaticity is the term for cardiac tissue's unique ability to spontaneously generate its own electrical impulses without requiring stimulation from the nervous system, a property centred in the sinoatrial node but present throughout specialized conduction tissue. Contractility refers to the strength of muscle contraction, refractoriness refers to the period during which cardiac cells cannot be restimulated, and conductivity refers to the ability to transmit an impulse, none of which describe the ability to generate the impulse itself.
  13. The correct answer is B (Continue monitoring, supportive care, and transport without attempting to convert the rhythm): a patient with known, well-controlled atrial fibrillation who is stable, asymptomatic, and hemodynamically normal does not require any attempt to convert the rhythm in the field. Synchronized cardioversion and defibrillation are reserved for unstable patients or specific shockable rhythms, neither of which applies here, and withholding cardiac monitoring in any patient with a known dysrhythmia would be inappropriate regardless of symptom status.
  14. The correct answer is D (1, 3 and 4): peripheral dependent edema, jugular venous distension, and hepatic congestion with ascites are classic findings of right-sided heart failure, reflecting the backup of blood into the systemic venous circulation. Pulmonary crackles and frothy sputum are instead hallmark findings of left-sided heart failure, where fluid backs up into the pulmonary circulation rather than the systemic venous system, making that finding inconsistent with a right-sided failure pattern.
  15. The correct answer is A (It represents atrial depolarization): the P wave is the first waveform seen in a normal cardiac cycle and represents the spread of electrical depolarization across the atria, which triggers atrial contraction. Ventricular depolarization is instead represented by the QRS complex, and ventricular repolarization is represented by the T wave. The AV node's refractory period is not directly visualized as a distinct waveform on a standard ECG tracing.
Case C0430

You are called to a retirement residence for an 81-year-old female with sudden onset severe shortness of breath that woke her from sleep. She is sitting bolt upright, using accessory muscles to breathe, and is visibly anxious. Auscultation reveals bilateral crackles throughout both lung fields. Her skin is pale and diaphoretic. Vital signs are: heart rate 118 irregular, blood pressure 168/98, respiratory rate 32, oxygen saturation 84 percent on room air. Her daughter reports a history of two prior heart attacks and poorly controlled high blood pressure.

  1. The correct answer is C (Acute decompensated heart failure with pulmonary edema): a history of prior heart attacks and poorly controlled hypertension combined with sudden nocturnal dyspnea, bilateral crackles, and severe hypoxia is a classic presentation of acute decompensated heart failure causing pulmonary edema. Asthma would more typically present with wheezing rather than crackles, a spontaneous pneumothorax would produce unilateral rather than bilateral findings, and there is no history or presentation here suggestive of an allergic trigger for anaphylaxis.
  2. The correct answer is D (Keep the patient in an upright position of comfort and provide oxygen to correct her hypoxia): patients in acute pulmonary edema breathe more easily and have improved venous pooling in an upright, seated position, which also reduces preload on the failing heart, and correcting her severe hypoxia with oxygen is an immediate priority. Placing her supine would worsen both her breathing and preload on the heart, withholding oxygen from a hypoxic patient is inappropriate, and delaying treatment for a 12-lead ECG would unnecessarily postpone urgently needed care.
  3. The correct answer is B (Atrial fibrillation, a common rhythm in patients with structural heart disease): an irregular heart rate in a patient with a history of prior myocardial infarction and chronic hypertension is highly consistent with atrial fibrillation, a dysrhythmia strongly associated with underlying structural heart disease and heart failure. Sinus arrhythmia is a benign variation typically seen in younger patients tied to respiration, third-degree block would present as a very slow rather than fast irregular rate, and ventricular tachycardia with a pulse is typically a regular, not irregular, rhythm.
  4. The correct answer is A (The volume of blood ejected by the left ventricle with each single contraction): stroke volume specifically refers to the amount of blood pumped out by the left ventricle during one single heartbeat. Cardiac output, not stroke volume, refers to the total volume pumped per minute, calculated as stroke volume multiplied by heart rate. The volume remaining after systole is known as end-systolic volume, and the volume returning to the right atrium per minute relates to venous return, a distinct physiological concept.
  5. The correct answer is C (Delayed or abnormal conduction through the ventricles): a normal QRS complex duration is generally under 0.12 seconds, so a measurement of 0.14 seconds indicates the electrical impulse is taking longer than normal to spread through the ventricular myocardium, consistent with a bundle branch block, ventricular ectopy, or another conduction abnormality. It does not reflect normal conduction, and the QRS complex specifically reflects ventricular, not atrial, depolarization, nor does it directly measure SA node refractory timing.
  6. The correct answer is D (1, 2 and 4): increased heart rate, increased afterload, and increased wall tension all directly raise the workload and oxygen consumption of the myocardium, since the heart must work harder and more often against greater resistance. Decreased contractility would actually reduce the force and energy expenditure of each contraction rather than increase oxygen demand, making it an inconsistent inclusion among factors that raise myocardial oxygen requirements.
  7. The correct answer is B (To allow the atria time to fully contract and complete ventricular filling before ventricular contraction): the brief delay built into AV nodal conduction ensures that atrial contraction is completed and the ventricles are optimally filled with blood before the ventricles themselves contract, maximizing stroke volume through the atrial kick. It does not prevent ventricular contraction altogether, it does not increase overall heart rate, and the AV node is not the heart's primary pacemaker, a role held by the sinoatrial node.
  8.
