The correct answer is C (Ensure adequate oxygenation and prepare for transport while continuously monitoring for further deterioration): symptomatic bradycardia with hypotension and altered mental status requires supportive management including oxygenation, continuous monitoring, and prompt transport, since paramedic-level intervention for bradycardia is largely supportive pending advanced care. Synchronized cardioversion and defibrillation are inappropriate for a slow rhythm, since both are used for tachydysrhythmias, and nitroglycerin would further lower this patient's already low blood pressure, worsening his condition.
  9. The correct answer is A (1, 2 and 4): obvious signs of death such as rigor mortis or decomposition, a valid do not resuscitate order, and injuries clearly incompatible with life, such as decapitation, are all recognized and accepted reasons to withhold or terminate resuscitation in the prehospital setting. A patient's age alone, without any of these other factors, is never an acceptable reason to withhold otherwise indicated resuscitation, since elderly patients are entitled to the same standard of emergency care as any other patient.
  10. The correct answer is D (It may indicate rising intracranial pressure as part of Cushing's triad along with bradycardia and irregular respirations): a widening pulse pressure, together with bradycardia and irregular respirations, forms Cushing's triad, a late and ominous sign of rising intracranial pressure as the brain attempts to maintain cerebral perfusion against increasing pressure within the skull. A widened pulse pressure does not indicate hypovolemia, which typically narrows pulse pressure instead, and while it can raise concern in aortic dissection it does not confirm that diagnosis on its own, and it is never clinically insignificant in this context.
  11. The correct answer is B (1, 3 and 4): correct defibrillator use includes placing one pad on the upper right chest below the clavicle and the other on the lower left ribcage at the midaxillary line, quickly removing excess chest hair when it prevents good pad adhesion, and moving the patient away from standing water or wet surfaces before delivering a shock to prevent electrical hazard. Pads must never be placed directly over a medication patch, which should instead be removed and the area wiped, since the patch can block adhesion and even ignite or cause skin burns during shock delivery.
  12. The correct answer is C (The sinoatrial node located in the right atrium): The SA node is the heart's primary pacemaker, normally firing at 60 to 100 beats per minute. It lies near the junction of the superior vena cava and right atrium. Its impulse spreads across both atria before reaching the AV node and continuing into the ventricular conduction system.
  13. The correct answer is A (Supraventricular tachycardia): A narrow QRS complex under 0.12 seconds means the impulse is travelling through the normal conduction pathway, ruling out a ventricular origin. The regular rate near 190 with buried or absent P waves is characteristic of SVT. Ventricular tachycardia would show a wide QRS, and atrial fibrillation would be irregularly irregular rather than perfectly regular.
  14. The correct answer is B (Ventricular fibrillation; defibrillate as soon as possible): A chaotic waveform with no organized P waves, QRS complexes, or T waves in a pulseless patient is ventricular fibrillation. It is a shockable rhythm, so early defibrillation combined with high-quality CPR is the priority. Pacing is not indicated for VF, and amiodarone is given only after initial shocks, not before the first one.
  15. The correct answer is D (1 and 2): A confirmed allergy to ASA and active gastrointestinal bleeding are genuine contraindications because either can cause a serious adverse reaction or worsen bleeding. A single daily low-dose ASA taken that morning does not by itself prevent an additional dose per most protocols. An elevated blood pressure of 150 over 90 and mild nausea are not contraindications to ASA administration.
  16. The correct answer is A (Sinus bradycardia): The rhythm is regular with a normal P wave before every QRS and a normal PR interval of 0.16 seconds, so conduction is normal. The only abnormal feature is the rate of 48, which is below 60 beats per minute, making this sinus bradycardia. This finding is common and benign in a well-conditioned athlete.
  17. The correct answer is C (Acute cardiogenic pulmonary edema): Orthopnea, bilateral crackles, and pink frothy sputum together point to fluid backing up into the lungs from left-sided heart failure. Pneumothorax typically presents with unilateral absent breath sounds rather than bilateral crackles. Asthma produces wheezing rather than crackles and frothy sputum, and pneumonia usually develops more gradually with fever and localized findings.
  18. The correct answer is B (1, 3 and 4): Everyone must be clear of the patient both during rhythm analysis and before a shock is delivered, and chest compressions should resume immediately once a shock is delivered to minimize interruption. Chest compressions must actually stop during analysis so movement does not distort the rhythm reading. A manual pulse check before every single analysis is not standard AED practice and would waste critical time.
  19. The correct answer is D (Ventricular tachycardia): A regular, wide QRS rhythm above 0.12 seconds at a fast rate, especially in a patient with prior myocardial infarction and scarred ventricular tissue, should be treated as ventricular tachycardia until proven otherwise. While SVT with aberrancy can occasionally look similar, the safer prehospital approach is to assume the wide-complex tachycardia is ventricular in origin. Atrial flutter and sinus tachycardia typically produce a narrow QRS complex.
  20. The correct answer is A (Evidence of end-organ dysfunction such as altered mental status): A hypertensive emergency is defined by severely elevated blood pressure combined with signs of active organ damage, such as confusion, chest pain, or visual disturbance, not by the number alone. A high systolic reading by itself, without organ involvement, is better classified as severe hypertension. A history of chronic hypertension or an old headache does not establish an emergency.
Case C0450

Medics are dispatched at the highest priority for a 61-year-old man who collapsed while shovelling snow in his driveway. Bystander CPR was started within one minute and a neighbour retrieved a nearby public-access AED. On arrival, the patient is unresponsive, apnoeic, and pulseless. The AED has already delivered one shock, and the neighbour reports it advised 'shock' on its very first analysis. Chest compressions are ongoing but appear inconsistent in rate.

  1. The correct answer is D (100 to 120 compressions per minute at a depth of 5 to 6 centimetres): This rate and depth are the recommended targets for adult chest compressions and allow adequate coronary and cerebral perfusion during resuscitation. Rates or depths outside this range are either too shallow to generate adequate blood flow or risk excessive injury. Consistent, high-quality compressions are essential throughout this arrest.
  2. The correct answer is B (Ventricular fibrillation or pulseless ventricular tachycardia): An AED only advises a shock when it detects a shockable rhythm, which in adult cardiac arrest means ventricular fibrillation or pulseless ventricular tachycardia. Asystole and pulseless electrical activity are both non-shockable rhythms that the device would not advise shocking. A sinus rhythm with a pulse would not trigger cardiac arrest resuscitation at all.
  3. The correct answer is C (Continue about two minutes of uninterrupted high-quality CPR): Current resuscitation practice calls for roughly two minutes of continuous, high-quality compressions after a shock before the rhythm is reanalyzed, giving the heart the best chance to sustain any return of organized activity. Pausing early to check for a pulse or shocking again immediately interrupts perfusion without benefit. Transport should not begin while compressions are needlessly paused.
  4. The correct answer is A (No more than about 10 seconds): Interruptions in chest compressions rapidly reduce coronary perfusion pressure, so pauses for rhythm analysis or shock delivery should be kept as brief as possible, ideally under 10 seconds. Longer pauses of 30 seconds or a full minute significantly worsen outcomes by allowing perfusion to fall. There is a well-established target, not an open-ended allowance.
  5. The correct answer is D (Atrial fibrillation with a controlled ventricular response): The absence of true P waves combined with a fine, wavy baseline reflects chaotic atrial electrical activity, which defines atrial fibrillation. The irregularly irregular rhythm at a ventricular rate of 88 is described as controlled rather than rapid. First-degree AV block requires a visible P wave with a prolonged PR interval, which is not present here.
  6. The correct answer is B (Hypotension possibly related to right ventricular involvement): Inferior myocardial infarctions can involve the right ventricle, which depends heavily on adequate preload to maintain cardiac output. Nitroglycerin dilates veins and reduces preload, and in a right ventricular infarction this can cause a severe, dangerous drop in blood pressure. The chest pain itself, the inferior location alone, and mild nausea are not reasons to withhold the medication.
  7. The correct answer is A (1, 3 and 5): Hypoxia, tension pneumothorax, and severe hypovolemia are all recognized reversible causes of cardiac arrest that fall within the well-known H's and T's framework paramedics are trained to consider. A chronic cough and a controlled seasonal allergy have no established link to causing or sustaining cardiac arrest. Identifying and correcting a true reversible cause can be the difference between a successful and unsuccessful resuscitation.
  8. The correct answer is C (Asystole; not shockable): A flat baseline with no identifiable electrical activity, confirmed across a second lead to rule out a technical error, represents asystole. It is not a shockable rhythm, so management focuses on high-quality CPR and identifying reversible causes rather than defibrillation. Fine ventricular fibrillation can sometimes resemble a nearly flat line but still shows faint chaotic activity, unlike a truly flat asystole tracing.
  9. The correct answer is D (Charging the defibrillator while compressions are still ongoing): Charging the device during ongoing compressions, then briefly pausing only to clear the patient and deliver the shock, keeps the hands-off interval as short as possible. Waiting to charge until compressions fully stop unnecessarily lengthens the pause. Manual pulse checks after every cycle and minute-long pauses both interrupt perfusion far more than current practice recommends.
  10. The correct answer is B (1, 2 and 4): A normal sinus rhythm requires a rate between 60 and 100, a P wave before each QRS, and a QRS duration under 0.12 seconds, all of which are satisfied here. A PR interval of 0.28 seconds is prolonged beyond the normal 0.12 to 0.20 second range and would instead indicate a first-degree AV block. An irregularly irregular R-R interval is inconsistent with sinus rhythm, which should be regular.
  11. The correct answer is B (Sinus tachycardia): The presence of a normal, upright P wave before every QRS with a stable, normal PR interval confirms the impulse is originating from the SA node, simply firing faster than usual. Supraventricular tachycardia and junctional tachycardia typically lack a clearly visible, normally shaped P wave preceding each QRS. Atrial flutter classically shows a sawtooth flutter wave pattern rather than discrete P waves.
  12. The correct answer is C (Withhold routine supplemental oxygen since her saturation is already adequate): Current evidence shows that routine high-flow oxygen in a well-saturated cardiac patient offers no benefit and may even be harmful. Supplemental oxygen is reserved for patients whose saturation falls below the target threshold or who show signs of hypoxia or respiratory distress. Oxygen should never be withheld outright when actually indicated, nor should administration depend solely on a patient's personal request.
  13. The correct answer is D (2, 3 and 4): Recent use of a PDE5 inhibitor can cause a severe, life-threatening drop in blood pressure when combined with nitroglycerin. A systolic blood pressure of 88 mmHg is below the threshold considered safe for administration, and suspected right ventricular infarction carries the same preload-dependent risk of profound hypotension. A blood pressure of 128 mmHg and an isolated ASA allergy have no bearing on whether nitroglycerin can be safely given.
  14. The correct answer is B (Pulseless electrical activity; search for and treat reversible causes): An organized electrical rhythm on the monitor without a corresponding palpable pulse defines pulseless electrical activity. It is not a shockable rhythm, so management centres on high-quality CPR, epinephrine per protocol, and actively identifying a reversible cause such as hypovolemia or tension pneumothorax. The monitor clearly shows an organized rhythm, so this is neither ventricular fibrillation nor asystole.
  15. The correct answer is A (The atrioventricular junction, at approximately 40 to 60 beats per minute): If the SA node fails, the AV junction is the heart's next natural pacemaker and typically maintains a slower intrinsic rate of 40 to 60 beats per minute. Should the AV junction also fail, the ventricular Purkinje system can take over, but at an even slower rate of about 20 to 40 beats per minute, not 60 to 100. The right atrium and bundle of His are conduction structures, not independent backup pacemaker sites at those rates.
Case C0465

Paramedics are dispatched for a 49-year-old man with sudden onset crushing chest pain that began 40 minutes ago while he was at work. He describes the pain as an 8 out of 10, radiating to his jaw, and reports associated diaphoresis and nausea. He denies any recreational drug use. His vital signs are heart rate 96, blood pressure 138 over 88 mmHg, respiratory rate 18, and oxygen saturation 97 percent on room air. A 12-lead ECG is acquired.

  1. The correct answer is D (Radiation of the pain to the jaw with associated diaphoresis): Pain radiating to the jaw combined with sweating and nausea is a classic presentation pattern for acute coronary ischemia, reflecting the visceral, autonomic nature of cardiac pain. A normal respiratory rate and oxygen saturation are reassuring but do not point toward or away from a cardiac cause. Pain severity alone, without character or associated symptoms, is not a reliable indicator of the underlying cause.
  2. The correct answer is C (Any recent use of a PDE5 inhibitor medication): PDE5 inhibitors, commonly prescribed for erectile dysfunction or pulmonary hypertension, can interact with nitroglycerin to cause severe, dangerous hypotension. This question is a standard part of screening before nitroglycerin administration regardless of whether the patient reports recreational drug use. Antibiotics, antihistamines, and inhaled bronchodilators do not carry this same dangerous interaction with nitroglycerin.
  3. The correct answer is B (Inferior wall): Leads II, III, and aVF view the inferior surface of the heart, so ST elevation localized to these leads indicates an inferior wall myocardial infarction, typically supplied by the right coronary artery. Anterior involvement would show changes in the precordial leads V1 through V4, and lateral involvement would appear in leads I, aVL, V5, and V6. Given this pattern, the paramedic should also consider possible right ventricular involvement.
  4. The correct answer is A (Assess for signs of right ventricular involvement and hypotension risk): Inferior myocardial infarctions carry a real risk of right ventricular involvement, and nitroglycerin can cause severe hypotension in that setting because right ventricular function depends heavily on preload. Doubling the dose is never appropriate and increases risk. ASA should not be delayed once indicated, and ongoing vital sign monitoring remains essential throughout transport.
  5. The correct answer is D (Ventricular fibrillation and pulseless ventricular tachycardia): These are the only two rhythms in adult cardiac arrest for which defibrillation is indicated, because both involve chaotic or extremely rapid ventricular electrical activity that a shock can potentially terminate. Asystole and pulseless electrical activity are both non-shockable and require CPR plus treatment of reversible causes instead. Atrial fibrillation and flutter are not cardiac arrest rhythms at all in a patient who still has a pulse.
  6. The correct answer is C (1, 2 and 4): Positioning the patient upright reduces venous return to an already overloaded heart, high-flow oxygen supports gas exchange in a hypoxic patient with pulmonary edema, and CPAP can improve oxygenation and reduce the work of breathing when available. Nitroglycerin is not withheld here since the patient's blood pressure is adequate and it actually helps reduce preload in pulmonary edema. Lying the patient flat would worsen breathing by increasing venous return and should be avoided.
  7. The correct answer is B (First-degree AV block): A PR interval prolonged beyond the normal 0.12 to 0.20 second range, while every P wave is still followed by a QRS complex, defines first-degree AV block, reflecting simple delayed conduction through the AV node. Third-degree block shows complete dissociation between P waves and QRS complexes rather than a consistent, if prolonged, relationship. Ventricular tachycardia and Wolff-Parkinson-White involve entirely different QRS and conduction patterns.
  8. The correct answer is D (It increases coronary and cerebral perfusion pressure through vasoconstriction): Epinephrine's alpha-adrenergic effects cause peripheral vasoconstriction, which raises diastolic pressure and improves blood flow to the heart and brain during CPR. It does not directly restart an organized rhythm, and high-quality chest compressions remain essential throughout resuscitation regardless of medication administration. Epinephrine does not address an underlying reversible cause on its own; that still requires specific identification and treatment.
  9. The correct answer is A (1, 2, 4 and 5): a wet chest should be dried before pad placement so the electrical current travels through the patient rather than across the skin surface, a medication patch should be removed and the area wiped before placing a pad directly over it, a visible pacemaker generator requires placing the pad a short distance away from the device rather than directly over it, and thick chest hair may need to be quickly shaved or trimmed for the pads to make adequate contact. A metal surface under the patient does not itself require a specific pad-placement action, as long as rescuers avoid direct contact with the patient during the shock.
  10. The correct answer is C (The rhythm is originating above or at the level of the AV node): A narrow QRS reflects rapid, coordinated conduction through the normal His-Purkinje system, which only happens when the impulse originates in the atria or AV junction rather than deep within diseased ventricular tissue. A wide QRS, by contrast, suggests a ventricular origin or an abnormal conduction pathway. Narrow-complex rhythms can be reasonably classified from the monitor strip alone without necessarily requiring a full 12-lead.
  11. The correct answer is B (SA node, AV node, bundle of His, Purkinje fibres): The impulse begins at the SA node, spreads through the atria to reach the AV node where it is briefly delayed, then travels down the bundle of His into the bundle branches and finally the Purkinje fibres to depolarize the ventricles. Any sequence starting elsewhere reverses this normal, physiologically essential order. This delay at the AV node is what allows the atria to finish contracting before the ventricles begin.
  12. The correct answer is D (2, 3 and 5): Prehospital management of a hypertensive emergency is largely supportive, focused on rapid transport, frequent reassessment, and minimizing patient stress, since paramedics typically do not carry medications intended to rapidly and precisely titrate blood pressure. Aggressively lowering blood pressure in the field can cause dangerous swings in perfusion to already compromised organs and is not appropriate. Administering an oral antihypertensive to rapidly normalize pressure falls outside standard paramedic scope and carries the same risk of overcorrection.
  13. The correct answer is A (30 compressions to 2 ventilations): This is the standard adult ratio used before an advanced airway is in place, balancing adequate perfusion from compressions with enough ventilation to maintain oxygenation. A 15 to 2 ratio is used in specific pediatric two-rescuer scenarios, not standard adult single-rescuer CPR. Compressions should never be delivered with absolutely no ventilation in a standard arrest, since oxygenation still matters over a prolonged resuscitation.
  14. The correct answer is C (2 and 4): A persistently low capnography reading despite what appears to be good technique suggests compressions are not generating adequate circulation and should prompt reassessment, while progressively shallower compressions from rescuer fatigue directly reduce effectiveness and signal a need to switch compressors. Visible chest rise relates to ventilation rather than compression quality. A palpable pulse felt during compressions and full chest recoil are both reassuring signs that current technique is working well, not signs to change it.
  15. The correct answer is B (60 beats per minute): The 6-second strip method estimates heart rate by counting the QRS complexes within a 6-second segment and multiplying by 10, since 6 seconds is one-tenth of a minute. Six complexes multiplied by 10 gives an estimated rate of 60 beats per minute. This quick bedside method is useful for rapidly estimating rate, especially with irregular rhythms, without needing to measure exact R-R intervals.
  16. The correct answer is D (Continue CPR and consider that resuscitation efforts may need to be prolonged): Severe hypothermia can protect the brain and other organs from the effects of low oxygen delivery, meaning patients have occasionally been successfully resuscitated after prolonged arrest times that would otherwise be considered unsurvivable. Resuscitation should not be withheld or stopped early based on a standard time limit alone in this setting. Defibrillation energy settings should follow standard device guidance rather than being arbitrarily doubled.
Case C0481

You are dispatched Code 4 for a 68-year-old male reported to have sudden facial droop and slurred speech. On arrival you find the patient seated in a chair, alert, with obvious right-sided facial droop and left arm drift when asked to hold both arms out. His speech is slurred but he can follow commands. Vitals: HR 92, BP 168/94, RR 18, SpO2 96% on room air, blood glucose 6.8 mmol/L. Bystanders report he was last seen normal 40 minutes ago while gardening.

  1. The correct answer is C (The left arm drift when both arms are raised): The FAST screen's A stands for arm weakness or drift, tested by having the patient hold both arms outstretched and watching for one arm to drift downward. Facial droop corresponds to the F, and slurred speech corresponds to the S. The glucose reading is not part of FAST but is still essential to rule out hypoglycemia as a stroke mimic.
  2. The correct answer is B (The exact time he was last seen acting normally): This time anchors the stroke centre's decision about whether the patient falls within the window for fibrinolytic therapy or endovascular thrombectomy. Every other detail is useful background, but none of it changes eligibility for time-sensitive stroke treatment the way the last-known-well time does. Delivering this clearly and early lets the receiving team begin preparing before arrival.
  3. The correct answer is A (Hypoglycemia mimicking a stroke presentation): Severe hypoglycemia can produce focal neurological deficits that closely resemble stroke, so checking glucose is a mandatory step before treating a presentation as a true stroke. A normal reading of 6.8 mmol/L effectively removes hypoglycemia from consideration. It does not distinguish between ischemic and hemorrhagic stroke, which require imaging to differentiate.
  4. The correct answer is B (A sudden, severe headache described as the worst ever felt): A thunderclap headache of sudden onset is a classic feature of hemorrhagic stroke, particularly subarachnoid hemorrhage, from the abrupt rise in intracranial pressure as blood collects. Ischemic strokes typically cause painless focal deficits without this dramatic headache. Gradual symptom evolution and transient, resolving symptoms are more suggestive of a TIA than a hemorrhage.
  5. The correct answer is B (Prompt transport to the closest appropriate stroke centre): Time is brain, so minimizing on-scene time and transporting promptly to a facility capable of definitive stroke care gives this patient the best chance at a good outcome. Repeating a full neurological exam on scene only delays transport without changing prehospital management. His oxygen saturation is normal, so an upright position for breathing is not the deciding factor here.
  6. The correct answer is A (1, 2 and 3): Soot around the airway, singed facial hair and a hoarse voice are all classic signs of thermal or chemical injury to the upper airway and suggest impending airway compromise from inhalation injury. A normal SpO2 reading can be falsely reassuring in carbon monoxide exposure because pulse oximetry cannot distinguish oxyhemoglobin from carboxyhemoglobin. Mild anxiety alone with no airway signs does not indicate inhalation injury.
  7. The correct answer is D (1 and 4): A GCS eye-opening score of 2 is reserved for eyes that open only in response to a painful stimulus, such as a sternal rub, which is exactly what statements 1 and 4 describe. Eyes opening spontaneously scores a 4, and eyes opening to a verbal command or sound scores a 3. Eyes opening briefly without any stimulus and then closing does not fit the standard scoring criteria for any of these levels.
  8. The correct answer is C (1, 3 and 4): High-concentration oxygen, manual stabilization of the flail segment when ventilation is compromised, and assisting ventilations with positive pressure when the patient's own effort deteriorates are all appropriate prehospital priorities for flail chest. Circumferential strapping is outdated and dangerous because it restricts the entire chest wall and worsens ventilation. Oxygen should never be withheld pending imaging, since flail chest is a clinical diagnosis made on scene.
  9. The correct answer is B (1, 3 and 5): Protecting the patient from surrounding hazards, positioning to manage secretions, and timing the seizure are all safe and appropriate actions during active convulsions. Attempting to insert an airway between clenched teeth risks dental and oral injury and rarely succeeds during the tonic-clonic phase. Restraining the limbs does not stop a seizure and can cause fractures or dislocations from the forceful, uncontrolled movements.
  10. The correct answer is D (1, 3 and 4): Pursed-lip breathing with accessory muscle use, a long tobacco history, and a prolonged expiratory phase with wheezing are all hallmark features of a COPD exacerbation caused by airway obstruction and air trapping. Sudden unilateral leg swelling raises suspicion for deep vein thrombosis or pulmonary embolism instead. Crushing central chest pain radiating to the jaw is a classic cardiac finding, not a typical COPD symptom.
  11. The correct answer is A (1, 2 and 3): Widening pulse pressure, progressive bradycardia, and an irregular respiratory pattern together form Cushing's triad, the body's reflex response to rising intracranial pressure compressing the brainstem. A rapidly improving level of consciousness would be reassuring and is the opposite of what increased intracranial pressure typically causes. Pinpoint pupils are more suggestive of opioid toxicity or a pontine lesion than isolated rising intracranial pressure.
  12. The correct answer is C (2, 3 and 4): Tracheal deviation away from the affected side, jugular venous distension, and progressive hypotension all result from the mounting intrathoracic pressure of a tension pneumothorax compressing the great vessels and shifting the mediastinum. Absent breath sounds occur in both simple and tension pneumothorax, so it does not distinguish between them on its own. Equal bilateral chest rise would be inconsistent with either type of pneumothorax.
  13. The correct answer is D (1, 4 and 5): Facial asymmetry, slurred speech, and unilateral arm drift are the three physical findings the FAST screen tests for, and any one of them being new and abnormal makes the screen positive. A normal blood glucose reading rules out hypoglycemia as a mimic but is not itself a FAST finding. Onset during sleep affects the last-known-well time used for treatment decisions, but it is not part of the physical screening itself.
  14. The correct answer is B (1, 3 and 5): Midline spinal tenderness, new numbness or tingling, and a focal neurological deficit are all recognized clinical indicators that raise concern for spinal injury and support applying spinal motion restriction. Full, pain-free range of motion and being alert, oriented, and sober are reassuring findings that, combined with the absence of the above, may support clearing the spine using a validated clinical decision rule. On their own, however, they do not override a positive finding elsewhere in the assessment.
Case C0495

You respond Code 4 to a private residence for a 74-year-old female with a long history of COPD who called 911 for worsening shortness of breath over the past two days. On arrival you find her sitting upright in a tripod position, using accessory muscles, with audible expiratory wheezing. She is diaphoretic and can only speak in two- to three-word sentences. Vitals: HR 118, BP 152/88, RR 28 and laboured, SpO2 86% on room air, and she is using a home oxygen concentrator at 2 L/min nasal cannula that she removed prior to your arrival.

  1. The correct answer is A (Speaking only in two- to three-word sentences): The inability to speak in full sentences is a reliable bedside indicator of severe work of breathing and impending respiratory failure, since the patient cannot generate enough sustained airflow to complete a sentence. Tachycardia and elevated blood pressure are nonspecific stress responses that can accompany many conditions. A chronic history of lung disease provides context but does not by itself indicate the severity of this particular episode.
  2. The correct answer is C (Administer oxygen titrated to relieve hypoxia while monitoring closely): Significant hypoxia should always be treated with supplemental oxygen titrated to an appropriate target while the patient is reassessed continuously for changes in respiratory status. The old concern that oxygen suppresses the hypoxic drive in COPD patients should never lead to withholding oxygen from a hypoxic patient in distress. Restricting her to her baseline home flow rate ignores the severity of her current exacerbation.
  3. The correct answer is D (An inhaled beta-agonist bronchodilator delivered via nebulizer): A nebulized beta-agonist bronchodilator relaxes bronchial smooth muscle and reduces the airway narrowing responsible for wheezing in a COPD exacerbation. Needle decompression treats a tension pneumothorax, which is not indicated by these findings. Supine positioning would worsen her breathing, since patients in respiratory distress typically breathe more effectively sitting upright.
  4. The correct answer is A (Increased effort needed to overcome airway obstruction and air trapping): In COPD, narrowed airways and hyperinflated lungs force the patient to recruit accessory muscles and lean forward in a tripod position to mechanically assist exhalation and improve diaphragmatic function. This presentation is a direct result of the underlying respiratory pathology, not a cardiac, hematologic, or metabolic problem. Recognizing this posture helps paramedics quickly identify the severity of the respiratory compromise.
  5. The correct answer is B (Turbulent airflow through the narrowed bronchial passages): Wheezing is produced when air is forced through airways that have been narrowed by bronchospasm, inflammation, and mucus, creating turbulent airflow that generates the characteristic musical sound. Pulmonary capillary blood flow and alveolar surface tension relate to gas exchange, not the airway narrowing that causes wheeze. Pleural pressure changes are associated with pneumothorax, not bronchospasm.
  6. The correct answer is C (Community-acquired pneumonia): Fever, productive cough, pleuritic chest discomfort, and localized crackles reflect infection and fluid accumulation within the alveoli of one lung region, which is the classic presentation of pneumonia. Pulmonary embolism more typically causes sudden pleuritic pain without a productive cough or fever pattern like this. Pneumothorax and asthma exacerbation do not typically produce crackles or fever.
  7. The correct answer is D (Acute pulmonary embolism from a lower extremity clot): Recent surgery, especially orthopedic surgery, is a major risk factor for venous thromboembolism, and a dislodged clot travelling to the pulmonary circulation causes sudden pleuritic pain, dyspnea, and tachycardia. Pneumonia typically develops more gradually with fever and productive cough. Tension pneumothorax requires a mechanism such as trauma or barotrauma, which is absent from this history.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Spontaneous simple pneumothorax without hemodynamic compromise): Tall, thin young patients are a classic demographic for spontaneous pneumothorax from rupture of a small subpleural bleb, producing sudden pleuritic pain and diminished breath sounds without the tracheal deviation or hypotension seen in a tension pneumothorax. The absence of tracheal shift and hemodynamic instability argues against tension physiology. This presentation does not fit a cardiac or thromboembolic cause.
  2. The correct answer is A (Relaxing smooth muscle in the airway walls to widen the lumen): Bronchodilators such as beta-agonists act on receptors in bronchial smooth muscle, causing it to relax and widen the airway lumen, which reduces resistance to airflow and eases breathing. They have no direct effect on pleural fluid volume, cardiac contractility, or peripheral vascular tone. This targeted airway effect is why bronchodilators are first-line treatment for bronchospasm.
  3. The correct answer is D (Atelectasis and reduced gas exchange in the affected lung): Pain-limited shallow breathing after rib fractures reduces lung expansion, allowing alveoli to collapse over time, a process called atelectasis that impairs oxygenation and increases the risk of pneumonia. This is a gradual complication of inadequate ventilation, not an immediate mechanical injury like tension pneumothorax. Rib fractures have no direct link to stroke or pulse pressure changes.
  4. The correct answer is C (Paradoxical movement, caused by loss of chest wall stability): When enough adjacent ribs fracture in more than one place, the flail segment loses its bony connection to the rest of the chest wall and moves opposite to normal respiratory mechanics, called paradoxical movement. This is distinct from subcutaneous emphysema, jugular venous distension, and tracheal deviation, which are separate findings associated with other chest injuries.
  5. The correct answer is B (Apply a vented occlusive dressing taped on three sides): A three-sided or vented occlusive dressing allows air to escape during exhalation while preventing air from being drawn into the pleural space during inhalation, reducing the risk of the open wound converting into a tension pneumothorax. Packing the wound or sealing it on all four sides can trap air inside the chest and precipitate a tension pneumothorax. Leaving it uncovered allows continued air movement through the defect and worsens ventilation.
  6. The correct answer is A (Tracheal deviation away from the affected side): Tracheal deviation away from the injured side reflects the mediastinal shift caused by rapidly accumulating pressure in the pleural space, a finding specific to tension pneumothorax and typically absent in a simple pneumothorax. Diminished breath sounds, a rapid respiratory rate, and pleuritic pain can all occur in either simple or tension pneumothorax, so they do not reliably distinguish between them.
  7. The correct answer is D (Passive diffusion driven by differences in gas concentration): Oxygen and carbon dioxide move across the thin alveolar-capillary membrane by passive diffusion, travelling from an area of higher concentration to an area of lower concentration, without requiring cellular energy. Active transport and osmosis both involve different mechanisms not used in normal gas exchange. Filtration by hydrostatic pressure governs fluid movement in capillaries generally, not the exchange of respiratory gases.
  8. The correct answer is C (The brain and the spinal cord): The central nervous system consists specifically of the brain and spinal cord, which together process and integrate information from the rest of the body. Spinal and cranial nerves, along with the autonomic and somatic divisions, belong to the peripheral nervous system. Sympathetic and parasympathetic fibres are subdivisions of the autonomic nervous system, itself part of the peripheral nervous system.
  9. The correct answer is B (1, 3 and 4): The GCS assesses eye, verbal, and motor response separately, the highest achievable total score is 15, and the motor response component is scored from 1 to 6. The lowest possible total score is 3, not 0, since each of the three categories has a minimum score of 1. Verbal response is scored from 1 to 5, not 1 to 4, so that statement is also incorrect.
  10. The correct answer is A (Ischemic stroke involves a blocked vessel, hemorrhagic involves bleeding): An ischemic stroke results from a clot or narrowing that blocks blood flow to part of the brain, while a hemorrhagic stroke results from a ruptured vessel bleeding into or around brain tissue. Both types can affect patients of any age and can range from mild to severe depending on the location and extent of brain involvement. Neither statement about age exclusivity or guaranteed severity is accurate.
  11. The correct answer is D (Face, Arm, and Speech): The FAST screen checks for facial droop, arm drift or weakness, and speech difficulty, three physical findings that together identify most stroke presentations quickly in the field. The T reminds responders to note the Time symptoms began. None of the other listed word groupings correspond to the actual components of this screening tool.
  12. The correct answer is A (An absence seizure): An absence seizure produces a brief, quiet lapse in awareness without convulsive movements, often lasting only seconds, and the patient typically returns immediately to their prior activity with no postictal confusion. A generalized tonic-clonic seizure involves obvious convulsive movement and a period of postictal recovery. A focal seizure with impaired awareness and a febrile seizure both present differently from the brief blank-staring episode described here.
  13. The correct answer is A (Postictal state following a tonic-clonic seizure): Confusion and drowsiness following the resolution of convulsive movements describe the postictal state, a period of recovery during which the brain gradually returns to baseline function. Status epilepticus refers to ongoing or repeated seizure activity without recovery between episodes, which is not the case here since the movements have stopped. A transient ischemic attack does not typically follow a witnessed convulsive episode in this pattern.
  14. The correct answer is B (1, 3 and 5): Cushing's triad consists of irregular respirations, widening pulse pressure, and bradycardia, a reflex pattern that develops as rising intracranial pressure compresses the brainstem and triggers a protective but ultimately dangerous physiological response. Tachycardia and hypotension are not part of this triad; in fact, the triad specifically features slowing of the heart rate and a widening, not narrowing, gap between systolic and diastolic pressures.
  15. The correct answer is D (Hypertension, bradycardia, and irregular respirations): Cushing's triad reflects the body's attempt to maintain cerebral perfusion against rising intracranial pressure, producing hypertension to push blood past the pressure, reflex bradycardia, and an irregular respiratory pattern from brainstem compression. It is considered a late and grave sign requiring urgent transport and airway management. None of the other combinations describe this recognized physiological pattern.
  16. The correct answer is C (Metabolic, toxicological, infectious, structural, and other systemic causes): Altered level of consciousness has a broad differential that includes metabolic derangements such as hypoglycemia, toxicological causes such as overdose, infection, structural brain lesions, and other systemic illnesses, so a systematic approach is necessary rather than assuming one single category. Assuming a purely cardiac, traumatic, or psychiatric cause without a broader assessment risks missing a treatable and potentially life-threatening condition.
  17. The correct answer is B (A high index of suspicion for spinal injury given the mechanism): Ejection from a vehicle during a high-speed rollover is a significant mechanism strongly associated with spinal trauma, so paramedics should maintain a high index of suspicion regardless of the patient's initial presentation. A patient walking around at the scene can still have an unstable spinal injury, so ambulatory status alone should never be used to rule it out. Assessment should never be limited only to visible wounds or delayed until pain is volunteered.
  18. The correct answer is A (A structured clinical assessment using validated decision criteria): Contemporary protocols use validated clinical decision rules that weigh mechanism, pain, neurological findings, and patient reliability to determine whether spinal motion restriction is warranted, replacing the older practice of routinely immobilizing every trauma patient. This evidence-based approach reduces unnecessary immobilization while still protecting patients with genuine indications. Patient comfort preference, the number of people involved, and vehicle characteristics are not valid clinical criteria for this decision.
  19. The correct answer is D (1, 2, 4 and 5): Maintaining a patent airway, providing appropriate supplemental oxygen, positioning for comfort, and frequent reassessment are universal priorities in managing respiratory distress regardless of the specific underlying cause. Withholding vital sign assessment until hospital arrival would leave the paramedic unable to detect deterioration or guide treatment during transport, which is inappropriate for any patient in distress. Continuous reassessment is essential precisely because respiratory conditions can change rapidly.
  20. The correct answer is C (Emphysema involves destruction of alveolar walls reducing surface area): Emphysema is characterized by destruction of the alveolar walls, which reduces the surface area available for gas exchange and causes air trapping, distinguishing it from chronic bronchitis, which primarily involves airway inflammation and excess mucus production. Emphysema is not caused by a single viral infection and is not confined to young patients. It is a chronic, largely irreversible structural change, not something resolved by one dose of medication.
  21. The correct answer is B (Titrate oxygen to relieve hypoxia while monitoring the patient closely): Current guidance calls for titrating oxygen to an appropriate saturation target while closely monitoring the patient's response, correcting hypoxia without over-oxygenating unnecessarily. Categorically withholding oxygen from a hypoxic COPD patient can cause serious harm and is not supported by current evidence. Applying maximum flow without titration, or basing treatment on patient request rather than clinical need, does not reflect proper oxygen therapy practice.
  22. The correct answer is A (A significantly increased work of breathing requiring close monitoring): Recruitment of accessory muscles such as the sternocleidomastoids and intercostals indicates the patient is struggling to move air and reflects a moderate to severe attack requiring close monitoring and prompt treatment. This finding should never be dismissed as mild or treatment-free, since it signals significant respiratory compromise. It is directly related to attack severity, not an incidental or normal finding.
  23. The correct answer is D (Status epilepticus, which risks prolonged cerebral hypoxia and injury): Continuous or repeated seizure activity lasting five minutes or more without recovery between episodes is defined as status epilepticus, a true emergency because prolonged seizure activity can cause cerebral hypoxia, metabolic derangement, and lasting brain injury if not treated promptly. This is not a simple, self-limiting seizure and requires urgent intervention rather than observation alone. A transient ischemic attack does not present with ongoing convulsive activity.
  24. The correct answer is C (A basilar skull fracture): Periorbital ecchymosis known as raccoon eyes, bruising behind the ear known as Battle's sign, and clear fluid drainage suggestive of cerebrospinal fluid leakage together form a classic pattern strongly associated with a basilar skull fracture. A simple nasal fracture or isolated soft tissue contusion would not typically produce this combination of findings. This presentation has nothing to do with an isolated peripheral nerve injury.
  25. The correct answer is A (A score of 3, for eye opening to verbal stimulus): Eyes that open specifically in response to a spoken or shouted stimulus, rather than opening on their own, are scored as 3 on the eye-opening component of the GCS. A score of 4 requires the eyes to open spontaneously without any prompting, a score of 2 requires a painful stimulus, and a score of 1 means no eye opening occurs at all regardless of stimulus.
  26. The correct answer is B (Poorly controlled hypertension over many years): Chronic, poorly controlled hypertension damages blood vessel walls over time and is one of the most significant modifiable risk factors for both ischemic and hemorrhagic stroke. Sex and age are established risk factors but are non-modifiable, meaning they cannot be changed through treatment or lifestyle. A family history of an unrelated genetic condition has no established link to stroke risk.
  27. The correct answer is D (Neurogenic shock from disrupted sympathetic nervous system tone): A high spinal cord injury can disrupt sympathetic nervous system output below the level of injury, causing widespread vasodilation and loss of the normal compensatory tachycardia, resulting in hypotension paired with a relatively slow or normal heart rate. Hypovolemic and septic shock would typically still produce a compensatory tachycardia. Cardiogenic shock arises from primary heart dysfunction, not spinal cord injury, and does not fit this clinical picture.
  28. The correct answer is C (Fever accompanied by a productive, purulent cough): Fever with a productive, purulent cough reflects an infectious process consistent with pneumonia, which develops from bacterial or viral invasion of lung tissue rather than a mechanical air leak. Sudden absent breath sounds and tracheal deviation are more typical of pneumothorax from lung collapse and mediastinal shift. The absence of fever or prior respiratory symptoms would argue against an infectious process like pneumonia.
  29. The correct answer is A (Position the patient in the recovery position and suction as needed): Placing a spontaneously breathing postictal patient in the recovery position allows secretions to drain away from the airway by gravity, and suctioning can clear any remaining fluid to prevent aspiration. Inserting an oral airway against an intact gag reflex can trigger vomiting or further seizure activity. Endotracheal intubation is not indicated for a patient who is breathing adequately on their own, and withholding all airway support risks aspiration of the pooled secretions.
Case C0531

You are dispatched code 4 (lights and sirens) for a 24-year-old male with difficulty breathing. On arrival you find the patient sitting upright in the tripod position on his porch, visibly using his accessory muscles to breathe. He is diaphoretic and can only speak in two- to three-word sentences. Bystanders state he has a history of asthma and used his salbutamol inhaler twice with no improvement. Initial vitals: HR 128, RR 32 and laboured, BP 138/86, SpO2 89% on room air, and bilateral expiratory wheezes with a prolonged expiratory phase on auscultation.

  1. The correct answer is B (The patient's work of breathing is severe enough to limit airflow for speech): a patient who can only manage two- to three-word phrases is diverting nearly all available breath toward moving air rather than forming sentences. This is a reliable bedside marker of significant respiratory distress and correlates with the degree of airway obstruction present. It is not caused by a blood sugar problem, an unrelated speech disorder, or deliberate withholding of information. Recognizing this sign early helps paramedics grade severity quickly without waiting for a full set of vitals.
  2. The correct answer is C (A respiratory rate of 32 breaths per minute with significant accessory muscle use): a markedly elevated rate combined with visible accessory muscle recruitment shows the patient is working hard just to maintain minute ventilation, and this effort cannot be sustained indefinitely. As fatigue sets in, the rate and effort can paradoxically drop, giving a false impression of improvement right before arrest. The blood pressure and heart rate alone are less specific indicators of ventilatory failure, and a prior diagnosis of asthma is background history rather than a current severity marker.
  3. The correct answer is A (Administer supplemental oxygen and continue bronchodilator therapy per protocol while preparing for possible deterioration): a poor response to an initial dose of a beta-2 agonist does not mean bronchodilator therapy should be abandoned, since repeated or continuous dosing per local protocol is standard for a severe attack. Oxygen supports the hypoxic patient while further treatment takes effect. Needle decompression is reserved for a suspected tension pneumothorax, which is not indicated here, and lying supine would worsen this patient's work of breathing rather than help it.
  4. The correct answer is D (Narrowing of the bronchioles from bronchospasm and airway inflammation): air moves out more slowly than normal because the smaller airways are constricted and inflamed, which increases resistance specifically during exhalation. This produces the classic prolonged expiratory phase and wheeze heard on auscultation in bronchospastic conditions like asthma. Alveolar fluid accumulation is more typical of pulmonary edema, upper airway collapse would produce stridor rather than wheeze, and diaphragm paralysis would not selectively affect the expiratory phase this way.
  5. The correct answer is A (1, 2 and 3): soot around the nose and mouth, singed nasal hair, and a hoarse voice are all direct signs that heat or combustion products have reached the upper airway, raising concern for progressive airway swelling. Normal breath sounds and a clear productive cough are reassuring findings that do not fit an inhalation injury pattern. Because airway swelling from thermal injury can worsen over minutes to hours, these three findings should prompt close, ongoing airway monitoring even if the patient looks stable on first contact.
  6. The correct answer is B (Within the alveoli, where thin walls allow oxygen and carbon dioxide to diffuse): the alveoli are surrounded by a dense network of pulmonary capillaries and have extremely thin walls, which is exactly what allows efficient diffusion of oxygen into the blood and carbon dioxide out of it. The trachea, bronchioles and larynx are all conducting structures that move air toward the alveoli but are not built for gas exchange themselves. This distinction matters clinically because any process that damages or floods the alveoli directly impairs oxygenation.
  7. The correct answer is A (Narrowing of the airway lumen that increases resistance to airflow): bronchospasm constricts smooth muscle around the airway, inflammation swells the airway wall, and mucus further clogs the lumen, and all three combine to make the passage smaller and harder to move air through. This is the opposite of widening the airway, and it primarily affects the airways themselves rather than causing alveolar collapse or diaphragm paralysis. Understanding this mechanism explains why bronchodilators, which relax the smooth muscle, are a first-line treatment.
  8. The correct answer is C (1, 2 and 3): a barrel-shaped chest reflects chronic hyperinflation from air trapping, pursed-lip breathing is a compensatory technique patients use to keep airways open longer during exhalation, and accessory muscle use reflects increased work of breathing, all classic features of a COPD exacerbation. Bradycardia at rest and high-pitched inspiratory stridor are not typical findings in COPD and instead point toward other problems such as cardiac conduction issues or upper airway obstruction. Recognizing this cluster helps distinguish COPD from other causes of dyspnea in an older smoker.
  9. The correct answer is C (Stimulating beta-2 receptors to relax bronchial smooth muscle): salbutamol is a beta-2 agonist that binds receptors on airway smooth muscle, causing relaxation and bronchodilation, which reduces resistance to airflow. It does not block histamine, has minimal direct effect on secretion thickness, and does not act centrally to suppress cough. Because its target is the muscle layer itself, it is most effective for the bronchospastic component of conditions like asthma and less effective for the fixed airway narrowing seen in advanced emphysema.
  10. The correct answer is D (Several days of fever, productive cough and focal crackles on auscultation): pneumonia typically develops over days rather than suddenly, and the combination of fever, a productive cough and localized crackles reflects an infectious process filling one region of the lung with fluid and inflammatory debris. Sudden pleuritic pain with leg swelling points more toward a pulmonary embolism, painless progressive weakness with no fever is not an infectious pattern, and wheeze that fully resolves with one bronchodilator puff is more typical of reactive airway disease than pneumonia.
  11. The correct answer is B (1, 2 and 3): a recent long flight, unilateral leg swelling and tenderness, and recent lower limb surgery are all recognized risk factors for deep vein thrombosis, which can embolize to the lungs and cause a pulmonary embolism. A history of well-controlled asthma and a week of productive cough with green sputum point toward a respiratory infection rather than a clotting risk. Recognizing these venous thromboembolism risk factors helps paramedics raise clinical suspicion even before definitive hospital testing is available.
  12. The correct answer is A (The deep veins of the legs or pelvis): most pulmonary emboli begin as thrombi that form in the deep venous system of the lower limbs or pelvis, often related to immobility, recent surgery or trauma, and then dislodge and travel through the venous system into the pulmonary arteries. Coronary and carotid arteries are part of the arterial system and are not the typical source of a venous clot. Recognizing this pathway explains why leg swelling and tenderness are important clues to ask about in a dyspneic patient.
  13. The correct answer is B (Apply an appropriate dressing to seal the wound and monitor closely for signs of tension developing): an open chest wound that allows air to move in and out with respiration needs to be covered to restore more normal chest mechanics, while watching closely for signs that a tension pneumothorax is developing from trapped air. Leaving the wound fully open allows continued paradoxical air movement, packing it tightly can trap air inside the pleural space, and using a finger to occlude the wound is not a practical or effective prehospital technique.
  14. The correct answer is D (1, 2, 3 and 5): jugular venous distension, tracheal deviation away from the injured side, absent breath sounds on that side, and hypotension with tachycardia together reflect the progressive buildup of pressure in the chest that compresses the lung, great vessels and heart. Bilaterally equal breath sounds would argue against a one-sided process like tension pneumothorax rather than support it. This combination represents a true prehospital emergency requiring prompt recognition and decompression per protocol.
  15. The correct answer is D (A tension pneumothorax produces progressive hemodynamic compromise from increasing intrathoracic pressure, while a simple pneumothorax typically does not): in a tension pneumothorax, air continues to accumulate in the pleural space with no way to escape, progressively compressing the lung, great vessels and heart and causing shock. A simple pneumothorax involves a fixed volume of air with no ongoing buildup of pressure, so it does not typically cause this same escalating compromise. Not every pneumothorax needs immediate decompression, and pain can be present in either type.
  16. The correct answer is C (The spleen on the left or the liver on the right): the spleen sits under the lower left ribs and the liver under the lower right ribs, so fractures in this region can lacerate these solid organs and cause significant internal bleeding. The thyroid and larynx are located in the neck, well away from the lower ribs, the kidneys are more posterior and higher risk from flank trauma rather than isolated lower rib injury specifically, and the brain and cervical spine are unrelated to thoracic rib injuries.
  17. The correct answer is C (1, 2, 3 and 4): a widening pulse pressure, a progressively decreasing level of consciousness, a falling heart rate and a rising blood pressure together reflect the body's compensatory response to rising intracranial pressure, sometimes culminating in Cushing's triad. Rapid, shallow, regular respirations that never change over time do not reflect the evolving pattern typically seen as pressure rises, since respiratory pattern often becomes irregular as pressure increases. Recognizing this trend early allows paramedics to anticipate and communicate a deteriorating neurological status.
  18. The correct answer is B (The central nervous system consists of the brain and spinal cord, while the peripheral nervous system includes the nerves branching from them): this is the fundamental anatomical division of the nervous system, with the central nervous system serving as the main processing centre and the peripheral nervous system carrying signals to and from the rest of the body. The brain and spinal cord are never considered part of the peripheral system, the central nervous system includes both sensory and motor pathways, and the peripheral nervous system is not limited to cardiac control alone.
  19. The correct answer is A (Transmitting electrical and chemical signals throughout the body): neurons are specialized cells built to generate and propagate electrical impulses and to communicate with other neurons and tissues through chemical neurotransmitters at synapses. Producing red blood cells is a function of bone marrow, filtering waste from the blood is primarily a kidney function, and storing glycogen is mainly a function of the liver and skeletal muscle. Understanding this core role helps explain why neurological injury disrupts communication throughout the body rather than a single organ system.
Case C0550

You respond to a private residence for a 71-year-old woman found by her daughter slumped in her chair, unable to speak clearly. The daughter states her mother was normal about 45 minutes ago. On assessment, the patient has right-sided facial droop, right arm drift when asked to hold both arms up, and slurred speech. She is alert but frustrated by her inability to communicate. Vitals: HR 92 irregular, RR 18, BP 176/98, SpO2 96% on room air, blood glucose 5.8 mmol/L.

  1. The correct answer is C (Facial droop, arm drift, speech difficulty and time of onset): this pattern, commonly summarized with a FAST-style acronym, targets the specific findings most sensitive for detecting an acute stroke in the field. Fever, appetite, sweating and thirst, or fluid intake and sleep pattern, are unrelated general wellness questions rather than a validated stroke screening tool. Documenting time of onset alongside the physical findings is essential because it directly affects what treatment options remain available at the receiving hospital.
  2. The correct answer is A (It establishes a last known well time critical for determining eligibility for time-sensitive stroke treatment): hospital stroke teams rely heavily on an accurate last known well time to determine whether certain time-limited treatments can still be safely offered. This information can dramatically change the patient's treatment options and should always be gathered and relayed clearly. It is not irrelevant to prehospital care, it does not rule out a stroke on its own, and it has no bearing on whether the symptoms are psychiatric in origin.
  3. The correct answer is D (Atrial fibrillation, which promotes clot formation that can embolize to the brain): an irregular heart rhythm in an older adult raises concern for atrial fibrillation, a condition in which blood can pool and clot in the atria, and those clots can travel to the brain and cause an embolic stroke. This is a clinically significant finding rather than a benign variant, and it is not explained by hypoglycemia in this scenario since the patient's blood glucose is normal. Recognizing this link helps paramedics understand the underlying cause behind many acute stroke presentations.
  4. The correct answer is B (Transport promptly, notify the receiving facility, and consider a designated stroke centre given the time-sensitive nature of stroke care): early hospital notification allows the stroke team to prepare and can shorten the time to treatment, and stroke care is highly time-sensitive so delays should be minimized. Being alert and able to breathe on her own does not reduce the urgency of a suspected acute stroke, and refusing transport or waiting for family logistics would inappropriately delay care that depends heavily on rapid recognition and transport.
  5. The correct answer is C (Blockage of a cerebral artery by a clot that cuts off blood supply to brain tissue): an ischemic stroke occurs when a clot, often originating elsewhere in the body, lodges in a cerebral artery and interrupts blood flow to the brain tissue it supplies, leading to cell death if flow is not restored. This is distinct from a hemorrhagic stroke, which involves bleeding from a ruptured vessel rather than a blockage. Excess cerebrospinal fluid and optic nerve swelling describe entirely different processes unrelated to the typical mechanism of ischemic stroke.
  6. The correct answer is A (1, 2 and 4): a sudden, severe worst-of-life headache, vomiting occurring soon after onset, and a history of uncontrolled hypertension are classic features associated with bleeding into or around the brain. A gradual onset with no headache and a recent minor viral illness with fever are more consistent with an evolving ischemic process or an unrelated infectious cause rather than a hemorrhagic event. Recognizing this pattern helps paramedics communicate a higher index of suspicion for hemorrhage to the receiving hospital.
  7. The correct answer is D (Chronic, poorly controlled hypertension): sustained high blood pressure weakens and damages the walls of cerebral blood vessels over time, making them more prone to rupture and cause a hemorrhagic stroke. A recent minor viral illness, seasonal allergies and mild dehydration are common and generally low-risk conditions that are not established major risk factors for bleeding into the brain. Understanding this link reinforces why blood pressure history is an important part of the assessment in any suspected stroke call.
  8. The correct answer is B (Unilateral arm weakness or drift, which suggests a focal neurological deficit): asking a patient to hold both arms outstretched with eyes closed tests for symmetric strength, and one arm drifting downward or pronating indicates weakness on that side, a classic sign of a focal neurological problem such as stroke. This test is not designed to assess leg strength, peripheral sensory neuropathy, or general fatigue, and its specific value lies in detecting asymmetry between the two sides of the body.
  9. The correct answer is B (2 and 5): localizing to a painful stimulus and withdrawing from pain are both purposeful or semi-purposeful movements scored under the motor response portion of the Glasgow Coma Scale. Spontaneous eye opening belongs to the eye opening category, confused conversation belongs to the verbal category, and incomprehensible sounds also belong to the verbal category rather than motor. Correctly separating these categories is essential for calculating an accurate total GCS score.
  10. The correct answer is A (Sustained muscle rigidity and stiffening of the body): the tonic phase of a generalized tonic-clonic seizure is characterized by sudden, sustained contraction of muscles throughout the body, often causing the patient to stiffen and sometimes fall. This is followed by the clonic phase, which involves rhythmic jerking movements, so rigidity is not simply flaccid paralysis and jerking alone does not describe the tonic phase. A gradual return to alertness describes the postictal phase that comes after the seizure activity has ended.
  11. The correct answer is C (A prolonged seizure or repeated seizures without full recovery of consciousness between episodes): status epilepticus is a true emergency defined by ongoing or repetitive seizure activity without the patient regaining a normal level of consciousness in between, increasing the risk of airway compromise and lasting neurological injury. A single brief seizure lasting under thirty seconds or resolving spontaneously within a minute does not meet this definition, and having a known seizure disorder does not by itself make every seizure an episode of status epilepticus.
  12. The correct answer is D (1, 2, 4 and 5): a first-ever seizure, a seizure lasting longer than five minutes, a second seizure beginning without full recovery, and pregnancy are all features that raise clinical concern and warrant closer monitoring and prompt transport. A patient who returns to baseline within two minutes and has a known seizure disorder is showing a more reassuring, typical postictal recovery pattern for that individual. Distinguishing these red flags helps paramedics prioritize which seizure patients need the most urgent attention.
  13. The correct answer is D (The score tends to decline as the level of consciousness deteriorates): as intracranial pressure rises and compresses brain tissue, the patient's ability to open their eyes, speak appropriately and respond to commands typically worsens, which is directly reflected in a falling Glasgow Coma Scale score over serial assessments. The score does not reliably improve or stay unchanged in this setting, and a genuinely random pattern would not have the consistent clinical meaning that trending GCS scores are relied upon for in the field.
  14. The correct answer is A (A score of 2, reflecting eye opening only to a painful stimulus): on the Glasgow Coma Scale eye opening component, a score of 4 is spontaneous opening, 3 is opening to voice, 2 is opening only to a painful stimulus, and 1 is no eye opening at all. This patient opens their eyes only when a painful stimulus is applied, which places them at the second-lowest level on this component. Accurately scoring each component separately is necessary before the total GCS score can be calculated correctly.
  15. The correct answer is B (1, 2, 4 and 5): midline spinal tenderness, a distracting painful injury, altered level of consciousness, and a focal neurological deficit are all recognized findings that support the need for spinal motion restriction because they interfere with a reliable clinical clearance of the spine. Full, pain-free range of neck motion is a reassuring finding that argues against the need for restriction rather than supporting it. Applying these criteria consistently helps avoid both over-immobilizing low-risk patients and missing genuinely unstable injuries.
  16. The correct answer is B (Hypoglycemia, which can mimic stroke and seizure presentations and is rapidly checked and treated): low blood sugar can produce altered level of consciousness, focal weakness and confusion that closely resemble a stroke, but unlike a stroke it can often be rapidly identified with a glucose check and corrected in the field. An untreatable brain tumour, a chronic degenerative disease and a congenital heart defect are not reversible causes that can be addressed immediately by paramedics, which is why hypoglycemia should be considered and ruled out early.
Case C0566

You are called to a construction site for a 39-year-old male who fell approximately four metres from scaffolding and landed on his left side. He is conscious but in obvious distress. On exam you note paradoxical movement of a segment of his left chest wall during breathing, along with crepitus and bruising over the lateral ribs. His trachea is midline, breath sounds are present but diminished on the left, and his SpO2 is 91% on room air. Vitals: HR 118, RR 28 and shallow, BP 104/68.

  1. The correct answer is A (Flail chest from multiple adjacent rib fractures): paradoxical movement, where a segment of chest wall moves inward during inspiration and outward during expiration, occurs when enough adjacent ribs are fractured in enough places that the segment loses continuity with the rest of the rigid chest wall. A simple pneumothorax and a myocardial contusion do not cause this specific paradoxical wall motion, and a ruptured diaphragm affects abdominal organ position rather than producing this chest wall sign.
  2. The correct answer is C (Tracheal deviation away from the affected side with absent breath sounds): a developing tension pneumothorax classically shifts the trachea away from the affected side and eliminates breath sounds on that side entirely as pressure builds and the lung collapses further. This patient's midline trachea and preserved, if diminished, breath sounds are more consistent with an evolving but not yet tension physiology. A completely normal chest wall or an unrelated productive cough would not be expected findings in either presentation.
  3. The correct answer is D (Provide manual stabilization or supportive splinting of the segment and administer supplemental oxygen while monitoring for deterioration): gently stabilizing the flail segment reduces paradoxical movement and pain while supplemental oxygen supports the patient's impaired ventilation from the underlying chest wall injury and likely pulmonary contusion. Applying outward traction is not an appropriate prehospital technique and could worsen injury, withholding oxygen would harm an already hypoxic patient, and encouraging deep coughing would increase pain and could worsen the injury.
  4. The correct answer is C (A high-speed motor vehicle collision with significant vehicle deformity): high-energy mechanisms like a high-speed collision with significant vehicle deformity transmit substantial force to the spine and are strongly associated with a real risk of spinal fracture or cord injury. A minor ground-level fall with no pain, a twisted ankle, or a minor bicycle tip-over with only a superficial scrape are all low-energy mechanisms that carry a much lower likelihood of a clinically significant spinal injury. Mechanism of injury remains an important part of the overall assessment.
  5. The correct answer is A (1 and 2): a silent chest with minimal air movement and an inability to speak in full sentences both indicate that airflow has become so severely restricted that even the wheeze itself may no longer be audible, marking a life-threatening exacerbation. A loud wheeze without a stethoscope, while notable, does not carry the same ominous meaning as a silent chest, and mild wheeze with a normal oxygen saturation instead points toward a much milder presentation rather than a severe or life-threatening one.
  6. The correct answer is D (Neurogenic shock involves loss of vascular tone causing hypotension, while spinal shock refers to a temporary loss of reflexes and function below the injury level): these two terms describe related but distinct processes following spinal cord injury, with neurogenic shock affecting blood vessel tone and blood pressure and spinal shock affecting the temporary loss of motor, sensory and reflex function below the level of injury. Treating them as identical terms, or defining either one too narrowly by a single feature, misses the clinically important distinction between them.
  7. The correct answer is A (To limit further movement of an unstable spine that could worsen neurological injury): the core purpose of spinal motion restriction is to prevent additional movement at a potentially unstable fracture site that could shift bone fragments and cause new or worsening damage to the spinal cord. It is not primarily intended for comfort, it has no direct role in preventing unrelated cardiac arrhythmias, and reducing hospital workload is never an appropriate justification for a clinical intervention decision.
  8. The correct answer is C (1, 2, 4 and 5): rib fractures can be complicated by an underlying pulmonary contusion, laceration of the spleen or liver from lower rib fractures, a pneumothorax if a fractured rib punctures the pleura, and an increased risk of pneumonia from pain-limited shallow breathing over time. Pain-limited breathing actually reduces lung expansion rather than improving it, so that statement describes a real risk rather than a benefit. Recognizing this full range of complications is important even when the fractures themselves seem straightforward.
  9. The correct answer is B (The oculomotor nerve, which controls pupillary constriction and most eye movements): the oculomotor nerve carries the parasympathetic fibres responsible for constricting the pupil in response to light and also controls most of the muscles that move the eye. The trigeminal nerve handles facial sensation and chewing, the vagus nerve is primarily involved in parasympathetic control of the heart and digestive tract, and the hypoglossal nerve controls tongue movement, none of which govern the pupillary light reflex.
  10. The correct answer is C (Chronic bronchitis, defined by a persistent productive cough for most days over an extended period): chronic bronchitis is clinically defined by a productive cough occurring on most days for at least a set duration over consecutive years, reflecting long-standing airway inflammation and excess mucus production. Acute bronchiolitis is a separate condition seen mainly in young children, pure emphysema is defined by alveolar destruction rather than a chronic cough pattern, and a restrictive pattern describes reduced lung volumes rather than the obstructive, cough-predominant picture described here.
  11. The correct answer is D (1, 2, 3 and 5): hypoglycemia, an acute ischemic stroke, an unwitnessed postictal state, and alcohol intoxication can all produce confusion and slurred speech that closely mimics a stroke, which is why each should be considered in the differential. An old, well-healed surgical scar has no bearing on a patient's current neurological presentation and does not belong in this list of stroke mimics. Considering this broader differential helps prevent tunnel vision toward stroke alone in an undifferentiated altered patient.
  12. The correct answer is D (A tall, thin young adult male with no history of chest trauma): spontaneous pneumothorax classically occurs in tall, thin young adult males, often related to the rupture of small blebs near the top of the lung, even without any preceding trauma. An elderly patient with well-controlled hypertension, a young child with a common cold, or a middle-aged patient with a healthy body build and no lung disease do not fit this classic higher-risk demographic pattern for a spontaneous event.
  13. The correct answer is B (A potentially ominous sign of critically reduced airflow, sometimes called a silent chest): in a patient with known severe asthma, wheezing disappearing while distress worsens usually means airflow has dropped so low that turbulent air movement can no longer generate an audible wheeze, a dangerous finding often called a silent chest. This is not a reassuring sign of improvement, it does not disprove the patient's asthma history, and oxygen therapy should never be withheld from a patient in this critical condition.
  14. The correct answer is B (1 and 2): the brain and spinal cord together make up the central nervous system, serving as the body's main integration and processing centre for neural signals. Peripheral nerves branching from the spinal cord and the cranial nerves are both part of the peripheral nervous system rather than the central nervous system, and cardiac muscle tissue is part of the cardiovascular system entirely, unrelated to this anatomical classification of neural structures.
  15. The correct answer is A (The underlying pulmonary contusion and impaired chest wall mechanics reducing effective ventilation): the real danger in flail chest usually comes less from the visible paradoxical movement itself and more from the underlying lung bruising and the mechanical disruption that together reduce how effectively the patient can move air. An absence of pain is not typical of this painful injury, an isolated heart rate change does not capture the ventilatory problem, and a flail segment does not guarantee a matching fracture on the opposite side of the chest.
  16.
