The correct answer is D (A score of 3, reflecting words that are clear but inappropriate or out of context): on the Glasgow Coma Scale verbal component, a score of 5 reflects fully oriented speech, 4 reflects confused conversation, 3 reflects inappropriate words that are still clearly articulated but do not fit the context, 2 reflects only incomprehensible sounds, and 1 reflects no verbal response at all. This patient's clearly spoken but nonsensical words fit the definition of inappropriate words rather than any of the other categories.
Case C0582

You are called to a school for a 16-year-old male who reportedly fell from a set of bleachers and struck his head, then had a witnessed generalized seizure lasting about 90 seconds. On your arrival, the seizure has stopped and the patient is in a postictal state, breathing but not yet responsive to voice. There is a laceration and swelling over the right temporal region. Vitals: HR 58, RR 10 and irregular, BP 168/94, pupils unequal with the right larger than the left and sluggish to light.

  1. The correct answer is B (Possible increased intracranial pressure causing compression of the oculomotor nerve on the affected side): a dilated, sluggish pupil on one side after head trauma often results from rising intracranial pressure or brain shift compressing the oculomotor nerve as it travels near the edge of the tentorium, and this is a serious warning sign in a trauma patient. This finding is not a normal variant unrelated to the injury, it does not represent bilateral optic nerve transection, which would affect vision rather than pupil size, and dehydration does not typically produce this specific one-sided pattern.
  2. The correct answer is C (Cushing's triad, suggesting rising intracranial pressure): the combination of a slowing heart rate, rising blood pressure and an irregular breathing pattern together forms Cushing's triad, a late but important sign that intracranial pressure is rising and beginning to compromise brainstem function. This is not a normal postictal pattern, which would not typically include this specific vital sign combination, and it is the opposite of what would be expected in early hypovolemic shock or a simple vasovagal response, both of which usually cause tachycardia rather than bradycardia.
  3. The correct answer is A (Position to protect the airway, prepare suction, support ventilation as needed, and reassess frequently given the risk of deterioration from rising intracranial pressure): a postictal, unresponsive patient with irregular respirations and signs suggestive of rising intracranial pressure needs proactive airway protection and close reassessment, since deterioration can occur quickly. Withholding airway intervention simply because the seizure activity has stopped ignores the ongoing risk, deliberately hyperventilating to a very low rate is not appropriate management, and sitting the patient fully upright is not a safe or practical option for an unresponsive trauma patient.
  4. The correct answer is C (Bradycardia, hypertension and irregular respirations occurring together as pressure rises): Cushing's triad classically consists of a slowing heart rate, a rising blood pressure and an irregular respiratory pattern, reflecting the body's attempt to maintain cerebral perfusion against rising intracranial pressure. A pattern of tachycardia with either hypotension or hypertension does not match this classic triad, and apnea alone with hypotension does not capture the full three-part pattern that defines this important late finding.
  5. The correct answer is A (Bruising around both eyes (raccoon eyes) or behind the ears (Battle's sign)): these two bruising patterns result from blood tracking along fascial planes after a fracture at the base of the skull, and both are recognized clinical indicators that should raise suspicion for a basilar skull fracture. A simple scalp laceration or isolated forearm bruising does not carry this same specific significance, and a normal ear canal with no drainage does not support the diagnosis, since basilar skull fracture can sometimes present with cerebrospinal fluid drainage from the ear instead.
  6. The correct answer is B (It prepares the body for a fight-or-flight response, increasing heart rate and redirecting blood flow to skeletal muscle): the sympathetic nervous system is responsible for the body's stress response, increasing heart rate, dilating airways and shifting blood flow toward the muscles needed for rapid action. It does not exclusively slow the heart rate during rest, which is instead a parasympathetic function, and it clearly does influence both heart rate and vessel diameter, remaining active during waking states rather than only during sleep.
  7. The correct answer is C (1, 2 and 3): fever and chills, a productive cough with discoloured sputum, and crackles localized to one lung field together form a classic infectious pattern consistent with pneumonia rather than a clot-related process. Sudden pleuritic pain after a long flight and unilateral leg swelling point instead toward a pulmonary embolism, since these reflect a venous clot risk rather than an infectious process. Distinguishing these clusters helps paramedics form a more accurate working impression even before hospital testing is available.
  8. The correct answer is C (Certain time-sensitive treatments for ischemic stroke are only effective within specific windows from onset): hospital-based treatments for ischemic stroke are only effective and safe within defined windows measured from the time symptoms began, which makes an accurate onset time essential information for the receiving team. This detail does have a direct impact on the patient's treatment eligibility, it is far more than a documentation formality, and it plays no role in determining which ambulance station gets dispatched to the call.
  9. The correct answer is D (An early sensory or perceptual symptom marking the onset of abnormal electrical activity in the brain): an aura represents the very beginning of a seizure, often experienced as an unusual smell, visual disturbance or other sensation, before the more obvious seizure activity develops. It is not a hallucination unrelated to the seizure process, it does not indicate the seizure has already ended, and while auras can occur with epileptic seizures, describing them as exclusive to psychogenic non-epileptic events is inaccurate.
  10. The correct answer is A (Secondary brain injury caused by inadequate oxygen and blood flow to already injured tissue): the primary brain injury happens at the moment of impact and cannot be reversed, but secondary injury from hypoxia or hypotension can compound the damage over the following minutes to hours, which is why maintaining oxygenation and perfusion is a central prehospital priority. This focus is not aimed at preventing an unrelated allergic reaction or cardiac dysrhythmia, and it has no direct bearing on how quickly a scalp laceration heals.
  11. The correct answer is D (1, 2, 3 and 5): maintaining adequate oxygenation, avoiding unnecessary hyperventilation, elevating the head of the stretcher slightly when not contraindicated, and closely monitoring level of consciousness and pupils together represent appropriate supportive management aimed at protecting brain perfusion in a patient with suspected rising intracranial pressure. Deliberately allowing blood pressure to drop as low as tolerated would reduce cerebral perfusion pressure and worsen the injury rather than help it, which is why that action does not belong in an appropriate management list.
  12. The correct answer is B (To cover the trachea and prevent food or liquid from entering the lower airway): the epiglottis is a flap of cartilage that folds down over the opening of the trachea during swallowing, directing food and liquid safely into the esophagus instead of the airway. Voice production depends on the vocal cords rather than the epiglottis, filtering and humidifying inhaled air is mainly a function of the nasal passages, and mucus production for trapping particles occurs along the respiratory mucosa rather than at the epiglottis itself.
  13. The correct answer is C (A possible high-level spinal cord injury affecting autonomic function): priapism, a sustained and often painful erection unrelated to sexual stimulation, can occur after a significant spinal cord injury due to disruption of the autonomic pathways that normally regulate this response. It is not explained by an unrelated urinary infection or a simple bruise, and while dehydration can cause many nonspecific symptoms, it is not a recognized cause of priapism in the trauma setting.
  14. The correct answer is D (Associated hives, facial swelling and hypotension following a known allergen exposure): anaphylaxis typically involves a broader systemic reaction, including skin findings like hives, swelling of the face or airway, and a drop in blood pressure, often following a clear exposure to a known allergen, none of which are typical features of an isolated asthma exacerbation. A mildly elevated respiratory rate, a prior history of asthma, or bilateral wheeze alone can occur in either condition and do not reliably distinguish between them.
  15. The correct answer is A (1, 2, 3 and 5): low back pain, numbness in both feet, bilateral heel or calcaneus fractures, and loss of bladder control are all findings that raise real concern for an associated spinal injury following an axial-loading fall onto the feet, since the force can transmit upward through the legs into the spine. A fully normal, pain-free neurological exam with no midline tenderness is a reassuring finding that argues against a significant spinal injury rather than supporting one.
  16. The correct answer is B (A pulmonary embolism, since breath sounds are often preserved and equal): a pulmonary embolism frequently causes sudden pleuritic chest pain and dyspnea without any change in breath sounds or tracheal position, because the underlying problem is a blocked blood vessel rather than a collapsed or compressed lung. A tension pneumothorax and an open chest wound would both be expected to produce abnormal breath sounds or tracheal shift, and a simple rib fracture alone does not typically cause this sudden onset of pleuritic pain with normal lung findings.
  17. The correct answer is A (Excessive hyperventilation can cause cerebral vasoconstriction that further reduces blood flow to injured brain tissue): driving carbon dioxide levels too low through aggressive hyperventilation causes blood vessels in the brain to constrict, which can dangerously reduce blood flow to tissue that is already vulnerable after a head injury. Hyperventilation does not come without downside even though it can lower intracranial pressure in the short term, it does have a real and measurable effect on cerebral vessels, and this concern applies across age groups rather than only to young children.
  18. The correct answer is D (Optimizes accessory muscle use and diaphragm excursion to ease the work of breathing): the tripod position allows the patient to brace their arms and use their accessory muscles and diaphragm more effectively, which can meaningfully ease the work of breathing during significant respiratory distress. This positioning does not eliminate the underlying cause of the bronchospasm, it cannot guarantee a normal oxygen saturation reading, and it does not prevent further deterioration on its own without additional treatment.
  19. The correct answer is C (Cervical, then thoracic, then lumbar, and finally the sacral region at the base): from top to bottom, the spinal column is organized into the cervical region in the neck, the thoracic region in the upper back, the lumbar region in the lower back, and finally the sacral region at the base of the spine. Any sequence that reorders these regions, such as placing the sacral or thoracic region first, does not reflect the actual superior-to-inferior anatomical arrangement of the spinal column.
Case C0601

You are dispatched Code 4 (lights and sirens) to a residence for a 58-year-old male "not acting right," per a worried spouse. On arrival you find the patient sitting on the couch, diaphoretic, pale, and confused, oriented only to person. His spouse states he is a known type 1 diabetic who took his morning insulin but skipped breakfast after a stomach bug. Vital signs: HR 118, BP 100/64, RR 20, SpO2 96% on room air, skin cool and moist. Blood glucose reads 2.1 mmol/L.

  1. The correct answer is A (Give oral glucose gel between his cheek and gum since he can safely swallow, then reassess): the patient is confused but responsive and can still protect his airway, so buccal glucose gel is appropriate first-line treatment for symptomatic hypoglycemia within paramedic scope. Withholding treatment or delaying with a meal risks worsening neuroglycopenia while a confused patient cannot reliably chew and swallow solid food safely. Insulin would further lower an already dangerously low blood glucose and is the opposite of what this patient needs.
  2. The correct answer is B (Improving level of consciousness with resolving diaphoresis and a heart rate trending toward baseline): as blood glucose rises toward normal, the catecholamine-driven signs of hypoglycemia such as diaphoresis and tachycardia should ease alongside a clearing mental status. Worsening confusion or an unchanged glucose reading would instead suggest the treatment failed or was insufficient. Kussmaul respirations are a sign of diabetic ketoacidosis, not resolving hypoglycemia, and would be an unexpected and concerning new finding.
  3. The correct answer is D (A sympathetic nervous system response triggered by low brain glucose levels): when glucose falls this low, the body releases catecholamines like epinephrine as a protective compensatory response, producing the diaphoresis, tachycardia, and pallor seen here. Insulin itself is not directly cardiotoxic at these levels, and osmotic diuresis and ketoacidosis-related fluid shifts are features of hyperglycemia, which is the opposite metabolic state from this patient's presentation. Recognizing this catecholamine surge helps explain why symptoms improve so quickly once glucose is restored.
  4. The correct answer is C (Repeat glucose checks and continued monitoring, since insulin effects can outlast one dose of glucose): his long-acting insulin dose can continue to lower blood glucose well after oral glucose has been absorbed, so recurrent hypoglycemia is a real risk during transport. Assuming one improved reading means the problem is solved risks a dangerous relapse. Giving more insulin would be directly harmful, and refusal of transport is inappropriate given his recent altered level of consciousness and ongoing risk.
  5. The correct answer is B (Diabetic ketoacidosis resulting from insulin deficiency and hyperglycemia): the combination of a very high glucose reading, deep rapid Kussmaul respirations, and a fruity acetone odour on the breath is a classic triad for diabetic ketoacidosis. Insulin overdose would instead cause hypoglycemia with a low, not elevated, glucose reading. An anxiety attack or alcohol intoxication would not explain the elevated glucose or the characteristic breath odour caused by circulating ketone bodies.
  6. The correct answer is A (1, 3 and 5): buccal glucose gel administration, a repeat glucose check, and reassessment of level of consciousness are all standard and appropriate steps after treating a symptomatic hypoglycemic patient. Statement 2 describes an action that makes no clinical sense, since a glucose meter is a measuring device rather than something ingested. Statement 4 is dangerous, because administering insulin to an already hypoglycemic patient would further lower blood glucose and worsen the emergency.
  7. The correct answer is D (Tachycardia and hypotension resulting from significant fluid loss): high blood glucose in diabetic ketoacidosis drives osmotic diuresis, causing substantial fluid loss that leads to dehydration, compensatory tachycardia, and eventually hypotension as the body decompensates. Bradycardia with hypertension and a normal heart rate with bounding pulses do not match the volume-depleted physiology of this condition. Slow, shallow respirations are also inconsistent, since the body typically responds to the underlying acidosis with deep, rapid Kussmaul breathing rather than shallow breaths.
  8. The correct answer is C (Hyperosmolar hyperglycemic state, common in type 2 diabetics): type 2 diabetics often retain enough residual insulin production to suppress significant ketone formation even with extremely elevated glucose, producing severe dehydration and altered mentation without the classic ketoacidosis breath odour or Kussmaul respirations. Simple hypoglycemia is inconsistent with a very high glucose reading. Stroke and adrenal insufficiency do not adequately explain this specific combination of extreme hyperglycemia and profound dehydration in a diabetic patient.
  9. The correct answer is A (1, 2, 4 and 5): a low glucose reading with a history of insulin use and missed food, cool diaphoretic skin, and safe oral intake all fit hypoglycemia, for which oral glucose is appropriate treatment. Statement 3 describes warm dry skin with deep laboured breathing, which is a ketoacidosis presentation and directly contradicts the cool, diaphoretic skin actually described and the low glucose reading given. Including statement 3 would misrepresent this patient's true clinical picture.
  10. The correct answer is D (Pain that is disproportionate and worsens with passive muscle stretch): compartment syndrome develops as pressure builds within a closed fascial space, and severe pain out of proportion to the injury, especially on passive stretching, is typically one of the earliest reliable signs. Pulselessness and paralysis are late findings that indicate the condition has already significantly progressed. Warm, well-perfused skin and simple bruising alone do not indicate the rising intracompartmental pressure that defines this condition.
  11. The correct answer is B (Immobilize the joint above and the joint below the injury): stabilizing both adjacent joints prevents movement at the fracture site, which reduces pain and further tissue damage during handling and transport. Straightening every angulated fracture regardless of circulation can worsen vascular or nerve injury rather than help it. Overly tight splinting risks compromising distal circulation, and jewellery must be removed from the injured limb itself, not only the uninjured one.
  12. The correct answer is C (1, 2 and 4): checking distal circulation, motor, and sensory function before and after splinting, recognizing worsening pain as a possible sign of compartment syndrome, and understanding that an overly tight splint can itself cause the condition are all clinically accurate. Statement 3 is incorrect because tight circumferential bandaging can directly compress the limb and precipitate compartment syndrome rather than prevent it. Statement 5 is incorrect because ongoing reassessment throughout transport is essential to catch a developing complication.
  13. The correct answer is D (Minimize pelvic movement, apply a binder if indicated, and transport promptly): an unstable pelvic fracture carries a high risk of major internal hemorrhage, so limiting movement, stabilizing the pelvis per local protocol, and transporting quickly are priorities. Repeatedly manipulating the pelvis to test stability can worsen bleeding and is not recommended once instability is already suspected. Allowing the patient to walk or applying limb traction splints does nothing to stabilize the pelvis itself and could aggravate the underlying injury.
  14. The correct answer is A (Control bleeding, cover the wound, and splint the limb as found): direct pressure to control hemorrhage, a sterile dressing over the exposed bone, and splinting in the position found are the appropriate initial steps for an open fracture. Attempting to push exposed bone back beneath the skin is outside prehospital scope and introduces additional infection risk. A tourniquet is reserved for hemorrhage that direct pressure cannot control, and irrigating an open fracture wound is not an appropriate prehospital step.
Case C0615

You respond Code 4 to a single-vehicle collision on a rural highway. A 24-year-old male driver is found still seated, conscious and alert, complaining of severe right thigh pain after his leg was pinned against the dashboard. The thigh is visibly deformed, swollen, and tender, with no open wound. Distal pulses are present but weak, and the foot is pale and cool with delayed capillary refill. He rates his pain 9 out of 10 and reports increasing tightness in the leg despite the extremity being immobilized in a neutral position. Vital signs: HR 108, BP 118/76, RR 18, SpO2 98%.

  1. The correct answer is C (Apply a traction splint if no contraindication exists): a traction splint helps realign a closed midshaft femur fracture, reduce pain, and limit ongoing internal blood loss, provided there is no suspected pelvic, hip, or lower-leg injury that would contraindicate it. A rigid short splint or figure-of-eight bandage does not provide adequate stabilization for this type of injury. Leaving the leg unsplinted allows continued bone-end movement, worsening pain and increasing the risk of further vascular or soft tissue damage.
  2. The correct answer is B (Developing compartment syndrome within a closed fascial space): rising tightness and pain out of proportion, combined with distal pallor, coolness, and delayed capillary refill, are classic signs of pressure building within the thigh's closed fascial compartments after significant swelling. This is not a normal or expected reaction to appropriate splinting. Neurogenic shock would present with warm, flushed skin and relative hypotension rather than a cool, poorly perfused limb, and simple bruising would not cause this degree of distal perfusion compromise.
  3. The correct answer is A (A weak, pale, cool distal pulse suggesting compromised perfusion): a weak pulse combined with pale, cool skin and delayed capillary refill signals compromised distal circulation, which is a time-sensitive finding that should drive rapid transport. Pain alone, without objective perfusion changes, does not carry the same urgency for circulatory compromise. Mild ecchymosis or slight swelling without colour or temperature changes are far less concerning findings on their own and do not indicate the same level of vascular threat.
  4. The correct answer is D (A heart rate of 108, reflecting early compensation): young, healthy patients can compensate for significant blood loss for a period of time by increasing heart rate while blood pressure remains deceptively normal, making tachycardia an earlier and more sensitive warning sign than blood pressure alone. A normal blood pressure of 118 over 76 can mask ongoing internal bleeding until compensation fails. A normal respiratory rate and oxygen saturation do not reliably reflect early volume loss in this scenario.
  5. The correct answer is C (Repeated distal pulse, motor, and sensory checks throughout transport): compartment syndrome and vascular compromise can develop or worsen after initial splinting, so repeated distal circulation, motor, and sensory checks are essential to catch any deterioration early. Assuming assessment is finished once the splint is applied risks missing a worsening complication in transit. Monitoring only the uninjured limb ignores the injured limb entirely, and removing the splint periodically would undo the stabilization it provides without adding meaningful information.
  6. The correct answer is B (Apply direct pressure, elevate the limb, and add a pressure dressing if needed): firm direct pressure combined with limb elevation, when no fracture is suspected, is the standard first-line approach to controlling external bleeding, with a pressure dressing added if bleeding continues. A tourniquet is reserved for hemorrhage that direct pressure fails to control, not as the automatic first step. Cleaning the wound or applying ice before controlling active bleeding delays the most time-critical intervention.
  7. The correct answer is D (1, 2 and 4): applying a tourniquet proximal to the wound when direct pressure fails, documenting and communicating the application time, and leaving a properly functioning tourniquet in place until hospital care are all correct practices. Loosening a tourniquet periodically is dangerous, since it can allow renewed uncontrolled bleeding and does not provide any real benefit once life-threatening hemorrhage has been identified. Abandoning direct pressure is also incorrect, since it may still be applied alongside or while preparing the tourniquet.
  8. The correct answer is A (Cool with room-temperature water and cover with a dry dressing): cooling a partial thickness burn with room-temperature water or saline followed by a dry sterile dressing limits further tissue damage while avoiding the harm caused by ice. Ice can cause additional tissue injury and worsen the burn through excessive cooling. Butter and other household remedies can trap heat and introduce infection, and intentionally breaking blisters increases infection risk without any clinical benefit.
  9. The correct answer is B (Wrap in saline-moistened gauze, place in a sealed bag, and keep cool on ice): wrapping the tissue in moist gauze, sealing it in a bag, and keeping that bag cool on ice preserves tissue viability while preventing direct contact between the tissue and the ice itself, which can cause additional damage. Discarding the amputated part removes any chance of surgical reattachment. Placing tissue directly on ice or submerging it in saline can cause freezing injury or tissue maceration.
  10. The correct answer is C (1, 3 and 5): significant internal bleeding can occur beneath intact skin, close vital sign monitoring is essential for evolving shock, and ongoing reassessment is important because closed injuries can silently worsen over time. Statement 2 is incorrect because the absence of external bleeding provides no reassurance about internal blood loss, and statement 4 is incorrect because a large closed soft tissue injury absolutely can cause meaningful haemodynamic instability from concealed hemorrhage.
  11. The correct answer is D (Stabilize the object in place and transport it undisturbed): impaled objects should be manually stabilized with bulky dressings and left in place, since removal can release tamponade pressure and cause severe uncontrolled bleeding. It should only be removed if it obstructs the airway or interferes with chest compressions, which is not the case with a thigh injury. Pushing the object in further or removing it based on the patient's preference alone would both introduce unnecessary additional harm.
  12. The correct answer is C (Crush syndrome from sudden reperfusion releasing toxins and potassium): prolonged compression followed by sudden release of pressure can cause a surge of potassium and other toxic byproducts into circulation, potentially triggering dangerous cardiac arrhythmias and systemic complications known as crush syndrome. Assuming immediate improvement with no concern ignores this well-documented risk. A simple tendon rupture or localized skin dryness would not account for the systemic danger associated with prolonged limb entrapment of this duration.
  13. The correct answer is A (1, 2, 4 and 5): direct pressure with a bulky dressing, gently repositioning an attached avulsed flap before dressing, monitoring for shock, and preserving any completely separated scalp tissue like an amputated part are all appropriate. Statement 3 is false and dangerous, since the scalp is highly vascular and large avulsions can cause significant, even life-threatening, blood loss despite the relatively small surface area involved.
  14. The correct answer is B (Treat for shock, protect the airway, and transport promptly): vomiting bright red blood in a patient with heavy alcohol use raises concern for a significant upper gastrointestinal bleed, so treating for potential hypovolemic shock, protecting the airway from aspiration, and transporting promptly are the priorities. Encouraging oral intake risks further vomiting and aspiration. Withholding oxygen or delaying transport both ignore the real possibility of substantial ongoing internal blood loss requiring hospital intervention.
  15. The correct answer is D (Peritoneal irritation resulting from a serious intra-abdominal process): a rigid, board-like abdomen with severe diffuse pain and reluctance to move are classic signs of peritonitis, which can result from a perforated organ, significant internal bleeding, or another serious intra-abdominal emergency. Indigestion, constipation, and muscular strain typically produce far milder discomfort without true abdominal rigidity or guarding. Recognizing peritoneal signs helps paramedics appropriately prioritize rapid transport for this patient.
  16. The correct answer is A (1, 2, 4 and 5): melena reflects partially digested upper gastrointestinal blood, orthostatic lightheadedness can signal meaningful blood loss, and careful vital sign monitoring alongside oxygen and prompt transport are appropriate given the risk of hypovolemic shock. Statement 3 is incorrect, since melena with orthostatic symptoms represents a potentially serious, time-sensitive presentation rather than a minor complaint that can be managed casually.
  17. The correct answer is C (Injury to underlying abdominal organs not immediately obvious): seatbelt bruising across the lower abdomen, sometimes called the seatbelt sign, is associated with a meaningfully higher risk of underlying bowel, spleen, or other solid organ injury that may not be visible from the outside. Treating this as isolated skin bruising or a simple muscle strain risks missing a serious internal injury. A rib fracture alone does not explain the pattern of bruising or abdominal tenderness described here.
  18. The correct answer is B (Appendicitis, given this classic migrating pattern of abdominal pain): pain that starts near the umbilicus and migrates to the right lower quadrant, together with nausea and low-grade fever, is a textbook presentation of appendicitis. Renal colic more typically causes flank pain radiating to the groin rather than this specific migratory pattern. Gastroenteritis usually presents with more diffuse cramping and diarrhea, and testicular torsion is an unrelated genital complaint that would not present with this abdominal migration pattern.
  19. The correct answer is A (1, 2, 4 and 5): variceal bleeding from underlying liver disease can cause massive, rapid hemorrhage, so airway protection, high-flow oxygen, prompt transport, and side-lying positioning if tolerated are all appropriate priorities. Statement 3 is incorrect and dangerous, since bright red vomited blood with pallor, diaphoresis, and tachycardia in a patient with liver disease is a classic presentation of life-threatening variceal hemorrhage rather than a minor bleed.
  20. The correct answer is D (Renal colic caused by an obstructing kidney stone): sudden, severe, colicky flank pain radiating toward the groin, with nausea but no fever, is the classic presentation of a kidney stone obstructing the ureter. Cholecystitis typically causes right upper quadrant pain rather than flank-to-groin radiation. Appendicitis follows a different migratory pattern toward the right lower quadrant, and a perforated ulcer would typically cause diffuse abdominal rigidity rather than isolated colicky flank pain.
  21. The correct answer is B (A mechanical bowel obstruction preventing normal passage of contents): progressive distension, cramping, vomiting, and the inability to pass stool or gas over several days together strongly suggest a mechanical bowel obstruction blocking normal intestinal transit. Viral gastroenteritis typically causes diarrhea rather than a complete absence of stool and gas. Reflux disease and simple food intolerance would not explain this degree of distension or the total cessation of bowel output described.
  22. The correct answer is C (1, 3, 4 and 5): rapid fluid shifts during dialysis can cause hypotension, stopping dialysis and positioning the patient supine per staff guidance is appropriate management, the vascular access must be protected, and frequent reassessment of blood pressure and mentation is essential. Statement 2 is false, since dialysis patients are actually at elevated risk of dangerous arrhythmias from electrolyte shifts, making this a critical concern rather than something that cannot occur.
  23. The correct answer is D (Apply firm, focal pressure without fully occluding the fistula): focal direct pressure controls bleeding from the fistula while trying to avoid fully occluding this critical vascular access point that the patient depends on for future dialysis. A tourniquet placed directly over the fistula risks damaging or destroying this delicate access. Elevating the arm alone without pressure and avoiding the site entirely both fail to actually address the active bleeding present.
  24. The correct answer is A (Hyperkalemia, which can cause dangerous, potentially fatal cardiac arrhythmias): missed dialysis allows potassium to accumulate because the kidneys can no longer excrete it, and elevated potassium is a well-recognized cause of weakness and dangerous cardiac arrhythmias including an irregular pulse. Hypernatremia is not typically the primary concern with missed dialysis and would not explain this exact presentation. Hypocalcemia and hypoglycemia do not directly account for the combination of weakness and irregular pulse described here.
  25. The correct answer is B (1, 2, 4 and 5): fluid overload from missed dialysis can lead to pulmonary edema, blood pressure should be avoided on the fistula or graft arm to protect that vascular access, missed sessions allow potassium and toxin accumulation, and careful respiratory and vital sign assessment is important. Statement 3 is incorrect and potentially dangerous, since liberal intravenous fluid administration in a patient already at risk of fluid overload from missed dialysis could worsen pulmonary edema significantly.
  26. The correct answer is C (Pyelonephritis, a serious infection involving the kidney itself): the combination of fever, chills, flank pain, and dysuria points toward an infection that has ascended to involve the kidney, known as pyelonephritis, rather than a lower urinary tract infection confined to the bladder. Simple cystitis usually causes dysuria without fever, chills, or true flank pain. Testicular torsion is unrelated to urinary infection symptoms, and uncomplicated renal colic from a stone would not typically produce fever and chills without an accompanying infection.
  27. The correct answer is A (Impaired kidney function limits the ability to excrete excess fluid): a patient in renal failure has a reduced ability to excrete excess fluid, so large volumes of intravenous fluid can build up in the circulation and lungs, increasing the risk of fluid overload and pulmonary edema. Intravenous fluids are not contraindicated outright in every renal patient, only used with more caution. Renal failure patients absolutely can become fluid overloaded, and intravenous access itself remains appropriate in dialysis patients when clinically indicated.
  28. The correct answer is D (1, 3, 4 and 5): the fistula or graft arm should generally be avoided when another site is available, extra caution should be used if access on that arm is truly unavoidable, blood pressure cuffs should also generally be avoided there, and damage to a fistula can seriously jeopardize the patient's future dialysis access. Statement 2 is incorrect, since prioritizing the fistula arm for routine access rather than protecting it works directly against preserving this critical, hard-to-replace vascular access site.
  29. The correct answer is C (Ectopic pregnancy, a potentially life-threatening condition requiring prompt care): a missed period followed by sudden severe abdominal pain, vaginal spotting, and dizziness in a woman of childbearing age is a classic and dangerous presentation of ectopic pregnancy, which can rupture and cause life-threatening internal hemorrhage. Treating this as a urinary tract infection or normal pregnancy discomfort risks missing a surgical emergency. An ovarian cyst alone would not typically explain the missed period and dizziness together.
  30. The correct answer is B (Recognize a possible torsion and transport promptly given the time-sensitive risk): sudden severe unilateral testicular pain without trauma in an adolescent male is a classic presentation of testicular torsion, a genuine surgical emergency where prompt transport is critical because delayed treatment risks permanent loss of the testicle. Reassuring the patient that this can wait risks a preventable, serious complication. Applying ice, discharging on scene, or having the patient attempt self-manipulation are all inappropriate substitutes for urgent hospital evaluation.
  31. The correct answer is A (1, 3, 4 and 5): this presentation may represent a ruptured ectopic pregnancy, shock signs in a patient of childbearing age with abdominal pain should raise suspicion for intra-abdominal bleeding, and prompt recognition, rapid transport, and standard shock treatment are all appropriate. Statement 2 is incorrect, since a patient's belief about her pregnancy status is not always reliable and should never override clinical suspicion based on the presenting signs and symptoms actually observed.
  32. The correct answer is D (Pelvic inflammatory disease, an infection of the reproductive organs): lower abdominal pain, fever, abnormal vaginal discharge, and pelvic tenderness together are characteristic of pelvic inflammatory disease, an infection involving the female reproductive organs. Testicular torsion is an unrelated male condition and cannot apply here. A simple urinary tract infection would not typically cause abnormal vaginal discharge, and renal colic presents with colicky flank pain rather than this combination of pelvic findings.
  33. The correct answer is B (Placenta previa, a serious cause of painless third-trimester bleeding): painless bright red vaginal bleeding late in pregnancy is a classic warning sign of placenta previa, where the placenta covers or is near the cervical opening, and this always warrants urgent evaluation. This is never a normal or reassuring finding in pregnancy. A urinary tract infection and round ligament pain are both unrelated conditions that do not explain painless vaginal bleeding of this nature.
  34. The correct answer is C (A ruptured or twisted ovarian cyst causing sudden pain): sudden, sharp, one-sided lower abdominal pain that begins during physical activity, without fever or bleeding, is a reasonable presentation for a ruptured or twisted ovarian cyst. Testicular torsion is a male-only condition and cannot explain this patient's presentation. Pyelonephritis typically presents with fever and flank pain rather than sudden activity-related pain, and appendicitis cannot be definitively confirmed in the field without further diagnostic workup at hospital.
  35. The correct answer is D (Recognize a urologic emergency and transport promptly for evaluation): a persistent, painful erection unrelated to sexual activity, known as priapism, is a genuine urologic emergency where prolonged duration can cause permanent tissue damage, making prompt transport for definitive evaluation the priority. Reassuring the patient that no transport is needed risks a preventable, serious complication. Ice and self-directed manual reduction are not appropriate or definitive treatments for this time-sensitive condition.
  36. The correct answer is A (Assess and treat for shock, encourage fundal massage if trained, and transport promptly): postpartum hemorrhage can cause rapid, significant blood loss, so assessing and treating for shock, encouraging uterine fundal massage if within training and protocol, and transporting promptly are the correct priorities. Withholding oxygen ignores the real risk of hypovolemia in this situation. Packing the vagina with gauze is not an appropriate prehospital intervention, and delaying transport in hopes bleeding will stop on its own risks a life-threatening delay in definitive care.
  37. The correct answer is B (Administer oral glucose gel or a sugary drink since he can swallow safely): a conscious patient who can protect his own airway and swallow safely should receive oral glucose as the fastest and least invasive treatment for hypoglycemia. Glucagon is reserved for patients who cannot swallow safely or lack intravenous access, and withholding treatment risks worsening neurological injury from prolonged hypoglycemia. Intravenous dextrose is appropriate when oral intake is not possible, not the first choice in a cooperative patient.
  38. The correct answer is D (The body compensating for metabolic acidosis caused by excess ketone accumulation in the blood): in diabetic ketoacidosis, insulin deficiency forces the body to break down fat for energy, producing ketone acids that lower blood pH. Kussmaul respirations are the body's compensatory attempt to blow off carbon dioxide and reduce acidosis, and the fruity odour comes from acetone in the exhaled breath. Bronchospasm, anxiety, and pulmonary embolism do not explain the combination of hyperglycemia and a fruity breath odour.
  39. The correct answer is A (1, 3 and 4): diabetic ketoacidosis typically develops gradually over hours as ketones accumulate, and it classically presents with warm, dry skin along with deep, rapid, fruity-smelling respirations as the body compensates for acidosis. Hypoglycemia, by contrast, usually has a sudden onset within minutes and presents with cool, diaphoretic skin. Combining cool diaphoretic skin or sudden onset with the DKA findings would incorrectly mix the two presentations.
  40. The correct answer is C (Administer glucagon by intramuscular injection since intravenous access is not yet available): glucagon works by stimulating the liver to release stored glucose and can be given intramuscularly without requiring intravenous access, making it appropriate when a hypoglycemic patient cannot safely take anything orally. Giving oral glucose to a patient who cannot protect his airway risks aspiration, and simply reassessing later or withholding glucose treatment delays correcting a dangerously low blood sugar.
  41. The correct answer is B (Insulin lowered his blood glucose without enough food intake to balance its effect): taking a normal insulin dose without eating removes the carbohydrate intake insulin is meant to be balanced against, so blood glucose falls too low and produces classic adrenergic symptoms like diaphoresis, tremor, and anxiety. His pancreas has not stopped producing insulin, he has not developed sudden insulin resistance, and ketone production is associated with hyperglycemic states, not this hypoglycemic presentation.
  42. The correct answer is C (2, 3 and 5): oral glucose is safe only when a patient can swallow and protect their own airway, when hypoglycemia has been confirmed by a blood glucose reading, and when the patient is alert enough to follow commands and cooperate with administration. A decreased level of consciousness with a poor gag reflex or active vomiting both create a significant aspiration risk and should prompt an alternate route of treatment instead.
  43. The correct answer is A (Immobilize the joint located above the fracture and the joint located below it): this principle prevents movement at the fracture site by controlling motion at both adjacent joints, reducing pain and the risk of further soft tissue or neurovascular damage. Immobilizing only the fracture site while leaving the joints free allows continued movement and instability, forcibly straightening a deformity is not appropriate with intact circulation, and splinting must occur before movement, not after loading the patient.
  44. The correct answer is D (Compartment syndrome from rapidly rising pressure building within the injured limb): pain that is disproportionate to the visible injury and worsens with passive stretching of the digits is a classic early sign of compartment syndrome, caused by swelling within a closed fascial compartment compressing nerves, muscle, and blood vessels. A simple contusion would not produce pain out of proportion to the injury, and neither referred hip pain nor a vasovagal response explains localized pain worsened by passive stretch.
  45. The correct answer is B (1, 2 and 4): pain that increases with passive stretching, pallor and coolness of the distal limb, and paresthesia or numbness distal to the injury are all early warning signs of rising pressure within a closed muscle compartment compressing nerves and vessels. Distal pulses and capillary refill often remain normal until compartment syndrome is advanced, so their presence does not rule it out and should not be combined as reassuring findings.
Case C0660

You are dispatched code 4 (lights and sirens) to a construction site for a 34-year-old male who fell approximately 4 metres from scaffolding. On arrival you find the patient supine on the ground, alert and oriented, complaining of severe pain in his right thigh. His right leg is visibly deformed with the thigh externally rotated and shortened compared to the left. Vital signs: HR 118, BP 100/62, RR 22, SpO2 96% on room air, skin pale and diaphoretic.

  1. The correct answer is A (Rule out life-threatening injuries to the head, chest, spine, and abdomen before anything else): a fall from four metres is a significant mechanism of injury that can cause hidden life-threatening trauma beyond the obvious deformity, so a rapid primary survey must take priority over managing an isolated extremity injury. Splinting before completing the primary survey, having the patient attempt to bear weight, or fully undressing the patient before assessment could all delay recognition of more urgent injuries.
  2. The correct answer is D (A traction splint to realign the limb and reduce ongoing internal blood loss): a traction splint applies gentle in-line pull to realign an angulated mid-shaft femur fracture, which reduces muscle spasm, pain, and the space available for internal bleeding around the fracture site. A rigid cardboard splint alone cannot provide adequate traction for this fracture pattern, a sling and swathe is used for upper extremity injuries, and a pelvic binder addresses a different anatomical injury entirely.
  3. The correct answer is C (Early compensated hypovolemic shock from significant blood loss into the injured thigh): a closed femur fracture can cause significant internal blood loss into the thigh, and the combination of tachycardia, a narrowing pulse pressure, and pale diaphoretic skin reflects the body compensating for that volume loss. These findings are not simply anxiety or a vasovagal response, and a heart rate of 118 with these skin signs is not within a normal range for this presentation.
  4. The correct answer is A (Check and document distal pulses, motor function, and sensation both before and after splinting): assessing and documenting neurovascular status before and after any splinting confirms whether the device has helped or compromised circulation and nerve function in the limb. Circulation should never simply be assumed to be intact, elevating a suspected femur fracture is not appropriate, and neurovascular checks should occur immediately, not delayed until hospital arrival.
  5. The correct answer is B (Apply a commercial pelvic binder or wrap a sheet snugly around his pelvis): stabilizing an unstable pelvis reduces the pelvic volume, helps tamponade internal bleeding, and limits further movement of fracture fragments that could worsen hemorrhage. Repeatedly rocking the pelvis to test for instability can worsen internal bleeding, independent log rolling risks further pelvic movement, and delaying stabilization for imaging ignores the urgency of hemodynamic instability.
  6. The correct answer is D (Control the bleeding, cover the wound with a sterile dressing, and splint without repositioning): open fractures should have bleeding controlled and the wound covered to reduce further contamination, then be splinted in the position found without pushing exposed bone back beneath the skin, which risks introducing infection deeper into the tissue. Applying a tourniquet is reserved for uncontrolled life-threatening bleeding, not routinely for every open fracture, and field irrigation is not standard prehospital care for this injury.
  7. The correct answer is C (1, 2 and 4): removing jewelry before swelling worsens, splinting a limb in the position found when a pulse is present and transport time is short, and padding rigid splints to prevent pressure injury are all accepted splinting principles. Straightening a severely angulated fracture is reserved specifically for situations with absent distal pulses, and traction should always be applied gently, never with maximum force regardless of the patient's pain.
  8. The correct answer is B (Apply a tourniquet several centimetres proximal to the wound and record the application time): when direct pressure fails to control severe, life-threatening arterial bleeding, a tourniquet applied proximal to the wound with the application time documented is the appropriate next step to prevent fatal blood loss. Elevation and a loose bandage will not control arterial hemorrhage, loosely packing the wound is insufficient for this level of bleeding, and ice does not address a bleeding source of this severity.
  9. The correct answer is A (Pack the wound firmly with hemostatic gauze and apply continuous direct pressure): junctional areas like the groin cannot be effectively controlled with a standard limb tourniquet, so wound packing with hemostatic gauze combined with sustained direct pressure is the recommended technique to control this type of hemorrhage. A high thigh tourniquet cannot compress a groin wound effectively, leaving the wound open allows continued blood loss, and a cold compress will not control significant active bleeding.

Practice Test 5 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Ongoing blood loss is causing compensated hypovolemic shock that requires urgent management): pallor, tachycardia, and anxiety developing alongside active bleeding are classic signs the body is compensating for volume loss, and this progression indicates the hemorrhage is ongoing and clinically significant. This presentation is not simply anxiety or a vasovagal reaction unrelated to blood loss, and assuming the bleeding has already stopped would delay necessary hemorrhage control and monitoring.
  2. The correct answer is D (Gently reposition the flap of skin into its normal anatomical position before dressing): repositioning a viable avulsed flap back into its natural anatomical location helps preserve blood supply to the tissue and improves the chance the flap can be salvaged. Completely removing or discarding the flap sacrifices tissue that may still be viable, and folding the flap under the wound edge does not restore normal anatomy or protect the tissue appropriately.
  3. The correct answer is A (An upper gastrointestinal bleed, such as bleeding from a peptic ulcer): coffee-ground emesis reflects blood that has been partially digested by stomach acid, while bright red blood indicates a more active bleed, and both findings together point to a source above the small intestine such as a peptic ulcer. A lower gastrointestinal bleed would more typically present with rectal bleeding rather than vomited blood, and a pulmonary source or swallowed dental blood would not produce this specific coffee-ground pattern.
  4. The correct answer is C (Diaphragmatic irritation from internal bleeding related to a likely splenic injury): blood pooling in the left upper quadrant from a splenic injury can irritate the underside of the diaphragm, and because the diaphragm shares nerve roots with the shoulder, this irritation is felt as referred shoulder pain, a finding known as Kehr's sign. A clavicle fracture would produce localized shoulder pain rather than referred pain tied to abdominal palpation, and cardiac ischemia or a cervical nerve issue would not explain the connection to abdominal trauma.
  5. The correct answer is B (2, 3 and 5): peritonitis classically presents with a rigid, tender abdomen with guarding, a patient who lies very still because movement worsens the pain, and diminished bowel sounds from inflammation slowing intestinal activity. A soft, non-tender abdomen or pain that has completely resolved with free movement would be inconsistent with active peritoneal irritation and should not be grouped with these findings.
  6. The correct answer is D (Cover the exposed bowel with a moist sterile dressing without pushing it back in): exposed bowel should be kept moist to prevent tissue damage from drying and covered with a sterile dressing, but never pushed back into the abdominal cavity, which risks introducing infection and further injury. Applying firm direct pressure onto exposed bowel can damage the tissue, and leaving the wound uncovered increases the risk of contamination and fluid loss.
Case C0675

You respond to a private residence for a 72-year-old female complaining of sudden onset severe abdominal and back pain that began 30 minutes ago. She appears pale and diaphoretic. Her history includes hypertension and long-term smoking. On palpation you note a pulsatile mass in the mid-abdomen. Vital signs: HR 128, BP 88/54, RR 24, SpO2 94% on room air.

  1. The correct answer is C (A ruptured or actively leaking abdominal aortic aneurysm requiring emergency surgical intervention): sudden severe abdominal and back pain with hypotension and a palpable pulsatile mass in a patient with vascular risk factors like hypertension and smoking is a classic presentation of a ruptured or leaking abdominal aortic aneurysm. Gastroenteritis and simple back strain would not produce a pulsatile mass or hemodynamic instability, and renal colic typically causes colicky flank pain rather than a palpable pulsatile abdominal mass.
  2. The correct answer is B (Repeatedly palpating the pulsatile abdominal mass or applying direct pressure to it): repeated palpation or pressure on a suspected abdominal aortic aneurysm can increase the risk of further rupture and accelerate bleeding, so this should be avoided once the diagnosis is suspected. Establishing intravenous access, continuous vital sign monitoring, and early hospital notification are all appropriate and necessary parts of ongoing patient care during transport.
  3. The correct answer is A (Titrate fluid conservatively to maintain adequate perfusion without fully normalizing her blood pressure): a permissive hypotension approach avoids raising blood pressure to a fully normal level, since higher pressures can increase the rate of bleeding from a leaking aneurysm before surgical control is achieved. Rapidly infusing large fluid volumes to fully normalize blood pressure can worsen hemorrhage, while withholding all fluids or delaying them until hospital arrival ignores the need to maintain some perfusion during transport.
  4. The correct answer is D (Provide rapid, smooth transport to a facility capable of emergency vascular surgery): a suspected ruptured abdominal aortic aneurysm is a true surgical emergency, so rapid, smooth transport to a hospital with emergency vascular surgical capability gives the patient the best chance of survival. A routine unhurried transport pace or delaying for blood pressure to normalize on scene wastes critical time, and a walk-in clinic cannot provide the emergency surgical care this patient requires.
  5. The correct answer is B (Avoid the fistula arm entirely and select a suitable vein in the opposite limb): an arteriovenous fistula is a surgically created connection essential for the patient's ongoing dialysis access, so it must be protected from needle sticks, blood pressure cuffs, and unnecessary manipulation to preserve its function. Preferentially using the fistula arm, applying a cuff over it, or palpating it firmly all risk damaging this vital, difficult-to-replace access site.
  6. The correct answer is C (1, 2 and 4): missed dialysis allows potassium to accumulate in the blood, and dangerous hyperkalemia classically produces widened QRS complexes, tall peaked T waves on the cardiac monitor, and muscle weakness or flaccid paralysis as the electrical and neuromuscular effects of elevated potassium develop. A normal ECG or rapid symptom resolution after drinking water would not be consistent with clinically significant hyperkalemia and should not be included as supporting findings.
  7. The correct answer is D (Fluid overload resulting from his missed dialysis session, causing pulmonary edema): without dialysis to remove excess fluid, patients with end-stage renal disease can rapidly accumulate volume, leading to pulmonary edema with crackles and jugular venous distension from fluid backing up into the lungs and venous system. Asthma, anaphylaxis, and pneumothorax would not typically produce jugular venous distension and are not directly explained by a missed dialysis session.
  8. The correct answer is A (A ruptured ectopic pregnancy causing internal bleeding and early signs of shock): sudden, sharp, one-sided abdominal pain with vaginal spotting and developing signs of shock in a pregnant patient are classic warning signs of a ruptured ectopic pregnancy causing internal hemorrhage. Normal early pregnancy symptoms, a urinary tract infection, or round ligament pain would not typically produce signs of shock or this pattern of acute one-sided pain.
  9. The correct answer is C (Testicular torsion, which requires urgent surgical evaluation to preserve the testicle): sudden severe unilateral scrotal pain with swelling and nausea, especially waking a patient from sleep, is a classic presentation of testicular torsion, a time-critical condition where delayed treatment risks permanent loss of the testicle. A urinary tract infection or muscle strain would not typically cause this abrupt, severe onset, and this pain pattern is not simply normal pubertal tenderness.
  10. The correct answer is B (1, 2 and 4): sudden severe one-sided lower abdominal pain, shoulder pain referred from diaphragmatic irritation while lying flat, and tachycardia with hypotension together suggest significant internal bleeding from a ruptured ectopic pregnancy. Stable vital signs with no tachycardia, or pain that has remained unchanged for weeks, would suggest a more chronic or less acute process rather than an actively ruptured, bleeding ectopic pregnancy.
  11. The correct answer is D (Placental abruption, which typically presents with painful bleeding and a rigid uterus): placental abruption occurs when the placenta separates prematurely from the uterine wall, classically causing severe pain, dark vaginal bleeding, and a firm, rigid, tender uterus, all of which match this patient's presentation. Placenta previa typically causes painless bright red bleeding rather than pain and rigidity, and Braxton Hicks contractions or a urinary tract infection would not explain this combination of findings.
  12. The correct answer is A (Hyperosmolar hyperglycemic state resulting from severe dehydration and very high blood glucose): hyperosmolar hyperglycemic state occurs in type 2 diabetics, typically develops with severe dehydration and extremely elevated blood glucose, and lacks the significant ketone production and fruity breath odour seen in diabetic ketoacidosis. Alcohol intoxication and a simple febrile illness would not explain a blood glucose this high, and insulin overdose would cause hypoglycemia, the opposite of this patient's elevated reading.
  13. The correct answer is B (2, 3 and 5): an open fracture is defined by a break in the skin communicating with the fracture site, so a visible bone fragment through broken skin, an actively bleeding wound at the fracture, and broken skin with visible contamination all indicate an open injury. Intact skin over the deformity or no wound present anywhere near the site would instead describe a closed fracture, where the skin barrier remains unbroken.
  14. The correct answer is C (Crush syndrome, with release of toxins that can cause life-threatening cardiac arrhythmias): prolonged compression of muscle tissue causes cell breakdown, and when the limb is released, potassium, myoglobin, and other toxins are suddenly reintroduced into circulation, which can cause dangerous cardiac arrhythmias and kidney injury. This is not a simple contusion, injury does not resolve immediately once pressure is relieved, and prolonged compression does not promote spontaneous fracture healing.
  15. The correct answer is A (Renal colic caused by a kidney stone obstructing the ureter on that side): sudden, severe, colicky flank pain radiating toward the groin, combined with nausea and visible blood in the urine, is the classic presentation of a kidney stone obstructing the ureter and causing renal colic. An abdominal aortic aneurysm would more typically present with a pulsatile mass and hypotension rather than blood in the urine, and appendicitis and simple muscle strain would not explain hematuria or this colicky pain pattern.
  16. The correct answer is D (1, 3 and 4): a large volume of bright red vomited blood, tachycardia with orthostatic dizziness, and black, tarry stools together indicate substantial, ongoing gastrointestinal blood loss that requires urgent transport and management. A single episode of mild nausea without vomiting or stable vital signs with no reported blood loss would instead suggest a minor or non-urgent presentation, not a severe bleed.
  17. The correct answer is A (2, 3 and 4): soot around the mouth or nose, a hoarse voice with stridor, and unreliable pulse oximetry are all accurate findings in smoke inhalation with possible carbon monoxide poisoning, since standard oximeters cannot distinguish carboxyhemoglobin from oxyhemoglobin. Cherry-red skin is a rare, late, and unreliable sign that does not confirm carbon monoxide poisoning, and singed nasal hair is actually a significant red flag for inhalation injury, not an insignificant finding. Recognizing this pattern quickly should prompt high-flow oxygen and close airway monitoring.
  18. The correct answer is C (Respiratory rate of 6 breaths per minute with miosis): opioid toxicity classically produces the triad of respiratory depression, pinpoint pupils, and decreased level of consciousness. Dilated pupils, hyperthermia, and dry flushed skin are features of sympathomimetic or anticholinergic toxidromes, not opioid toxicity. Recognizing this pattern quickly guides the paramedic toward airway support and naloxone administration.
  19. The correct answer is B (Anticholinergic toxidrome, marked by dry, flushed skin and dilated pupils): the mnemonic red as a beet, dry as a bone, blind as a bat, and mad as a hatter describes flushed dry skin, dilated pupils, urinary retention, and delirium seen with anticholinergic agents such as diphenhydramine. Cholinergic toxicity instead produces excessive secretions and sweating, sympathomimetic toxicity produces sweating with agitation, and opioid toxicity produces miosis with respiratory depression, none of which match this dry, flushed, confused presentation. This pattern recognition helps guide safe supportive management in the field.
  20. The correct answer is D (Excess bronchial secretions with wheezing from bronchoconstriction and bradycardia): cholinergic toxicity from organophosphate exposure causes the killer B's of bronchorrhea, bronchoconstriction, and bradycardia alongside the drooling, sweating, and vomiting already described. Dry mucous membranes, dilated pupils, and warm dry skin are the opposite pattern seen in anticholinergic poisoning, not cholinergic excess. Airway management and suction are priorities because secretions can rapidly compromise breathing.
Case C0695

Your crew is dispatched code 4 to a lake access point in early spring for a report of a capsized canoe. On arrival you find a 34-year-old male who was pulled from the water by bystanders approximately 12 minutes ago. He is shivering violently, conscious but confused, with slurred speech. His skin is pale and cool to the touch. Vital signs: HR 58 and irregular, RR 14, BP 100/64, SpO2 94% on room air, core temperature estimated at 31°C by tympanic thermometer. He is wet and lying on the wet ground when you arrive.

  1. The correct answer is A (Moderate hypothermia, generally defined as a core temperature between twenty-eight and thirty-two degrees): a core temperature around 31°C with confusion, slurred speech, and shivering that is still present is classified as moderate hypothermia. Mild hypothermia typically preserves clear mentation, while severe hypothermia usually presents with loss of shivering and a high risk of cardiac arrhythmia. Recognizing the moderate range guides the crew toward careful handling and passive rewarming rather than aggressive manipulation.
  2. The correct answer is C (Remove wet clothing, insulate, and shield him from wind and further heat loss): preventing further heat loss is the first priority in any hypothermic patient, achieved by removing wet garments, drying the skin, and wrapping him in dry insulating layers and a vapour barrier. Direct hot packs on the skin risk burns and peripheral vasodilation, walking increases the risk of a dangerous arrhythmia from muscle activity redistributing cold peripheral blood, and limb submersion is not an appropriate field intervention. Gentle handling matters because a cold, irritable myocardium can fibrillate with rough movement.
  3. The correct answer is D (It can trigger ventricular fibrillation in a cold, irritable heart): a hypothermic myocardium is electrically unstable, and rough or jarring movement can precipitate ventricular fibrillation that is often refractory to defibrillation until the patient is rewarmed. Frostbite is a separate localized cold injury unrelated to handling technique, rough handling does not cause rewarming at all, and this risk is well above the risk of moving a normothermic patient. This is why gentle, horizontal handling and minimal movement are emphasized throughout hypothermia care.
  4. The correct answer is C (Cold hearts may be less responsive to defibrillation and cardiac medications): a cold myocardium metabolizes drugs more slowly and can be resistant to both defibrillation and pharmacological therapy until core temperature rises. Defibrillation is still attempted for a shockable rhythm rather than withheld entirely, and medication intervals are typically spaced further apart, not given at standard frequency, because of reduced clearance. This is a key reason why hypothermic cardiac arrest patients are transported for rewarming rather than having resuscitation efforts terminated in the field based on rhythm alone.
  5. The correct answer is A (Protect the area from further trauma and avoid rewarming if refreezing is possible): frostbite is best managed by gently protecting the injured tissue and avoiding rewarming in the field unless the crew can guarantee the area will not refreeze, since freeze-thaw-refreeze cycles cause far more tissue damage than a single freeze. Rubbing the skin worsens tissue injury from ice crystal damage, direct heat pack contact risks burns to already-compromised tissue, and blisters should never be deliberately punctured. Definitive rewarming is best performed in a controlled hospital setting.
  6. The correct answer is D (1, 2 and 4): altered mental status combined with hot dry skin is the hallmark that separates heat stroke from heat exhaustion, heat exhaustion usually presents with heavy sweating and near-normal temperature, and heat stroke is genuinely life-threatening and requires urgent recognition. Rapid cooling is actually the highest priority intervention in heat stroke, not a low priority, and oral fluids should never be given to a patient with altered mental status because of aspiration risk. This is why cooling and airway protection take precedence over hydration in a confused, hyperthermic patient.
  7. The correct answer is B (Evaporative cooling achieved by fanning wet, exposed skin surfaces): spraying or wetting the skin with tepid water and fanning the patient promotes rapid evaporative heat loss and is one of the most practical and effective field cooling methods for exertional heat stroke. Ice packs applied to a few small areas alone cool far more slowly, warm blankets would worsen hyperthermia, and giving large volumes of oral fluid is unsafe in a confused patient because of aspiration risk. Rapid cooling reduces the risk of organ damage from prolonged core temperature elevation.
  8. The correct answer is C (Delayed hepatotoxicity can occur even when the patient appears well initially): acetaminophen overdose is dangerous precisely because early symptoms can be minimal or absent while liver injury develops over the following one to several days, so a patient who looks well must still be transported for timed blood levels and possible antidote therapy. Acetaminophen does not cause airway swelling or the sedative and pupillary changes typical of opioids, and toxicity is not reliably symptomatic within the first thirty minutes. This delayed-onset pattern is why history and timing of ingestion are critical information to relay to the receiving hospital.
  9. The correct answer is A (1, 3 and 4): salicylate toxicity classically produces tinnitus, hyperventilation from direct respiratory centre stimulation, nausea, vomiting, diaphoresis, and a mixed acid-base picture combining respiratory alkalosis with metabolic acidosis. It causes hyperventilation and tachypnea rather than bradypnea, and it is a potentially serious, sometimes fatal ingestion rather than a mild self-limiting one. Recognizing hyperventilation with tinnitus in an overdose patient should raise strong suspicion for salicylate toxicity.
  10. The correct answer is D (Positioning to protect the airway and prevent aspiration): an unresponsive patient with snoring respirations and vomitus near the mouth is at immediate risk of airway obstruction and aspiration, so positioning, suction, and airway protection take priority over any diagnostic or pharmacological step. Blood alcohol level has no field treatment value and delays care, naloxone will not reverse alcohol intoxication since it targets opioid receptors, and oxygen should not be withheld from a patient with an airway or breathing concern. Airway management remains the foundational priority regardless of the presumed cause of unresponsiveness.
  11. The correct answer is C (The patient should be closely monitored because naloxone may wear off before the opioid does): naloxone has a shorter duration of action than many opioids, particularly long-acting or high-potency ones, so a patient can slip back into respiratory depression after apparently improving and needs ongoing monitoring and possible repeat dosing. Airway and ventilatory support remain essential throughout, since naloxone only reverses the opioid effect and does not replace supportive care. Because of this re-sedation risk, transport and continued observation are still required even after a good initial response.
  12. The correct answer is B (1, 3 and 4): high-flow oxygen is appropriate for any suspected carbon monoxide exposure because it accelerates carboxyhemoglobin elimination, the flu-like presentation of headache, nausea, and dizziness commonly delays recognition, and every occupant of an affected space should be evaluated because exposure and symptom severity can vary between individuals. Standard pulse oximetry cannot distinguish carboxyhemoglobin from oxyhemoglobin and will often display a falsely reassuring reading, and carbon monoxide is colourless and odourless, which is exactly why it is so dangerous. Multiple household members with similar vague symptoms should raise immediate suspicion for a shared environmental cause.
  13. The correct answer is A (Hyperbaric oxygen therapy administered at a specialized treatment facility): hyperbaric oxygen dramatically shortens the half-life of carboxyhemoglobin and reduces the risk of delayed neurological injury in severe carbon monoxide poisoning, so hospitals may transfer eligible patients for this therapy. Naloxone has no effect on carbon monoxide, and activated charcoal or gastric lavage are gastrointestinal decontamination methods with no role in a poisoning that occurs through inhalation. Prehospital care remains high-flow oxygen and removal from the source while this decision is made at hospital.
  14. The correct answer is C (The cold zone, upwind and uphill from the hazard): paramedics without specialized hazmat protective equipment and training must remain in the cold zone, positioned upwind and uphill, and treat only patients who have already been decontaminated and brought to them. The hot zone is reserved for hazmat-trained, properly equipped responders, and the warm zone is where decontamination itself takes place, not general patient care. Staying in the cold zone protects the crew from becoming secondary casualties of the same hazard.
  15. The correct answer is B (1, 3 and 4): pinpoint pupils, excessive secretions, and muscle twitching across multiple patients simultaneously is a classic cholinergic pattern consistent with nerve agent exposure, and any suspected CBRNE incident requires the crew to stage safely outside the contaminated area rather than rushing in, since multiple casualties with an unusual shared symptom pattern is itself a major red flag. Standard gloves and a surgical mask do not provide adequate chemical protection against a nerve agent, and a suspected chemical mass-casualty incident requires notification of multiple agencies including hazmat, fire, and public health, not law enforcement alone. Recognizing this pattern early protects responders from becoming additional casualties.
  16. The correct answer is C (The primary problem in drowning is hypoxia from fluid interference with gas exchange): regardless of whether the water is fresh or salt, the core problem in drowning is hypoxia caused by water or laryngospasm interfering with oxygen exchange in the lungs, and prehospital management for both is essentially the same, focused on oxygenation and ventilation. Aspirated water volume is often small and variable, not consistently large, and drowning victims can develop delayed pulmonary complications such as secondary respiratory distress even after apparent initial recovery, so ongoing monitoring is essential. This is why oxygenation and airway support, not water type, guide field treatment.
  17. The correct answer is D (Transport for evaluation because delayed complications such as pulmonary edema can develop): even a drowning patient who regains spontaneous breathing and appears improved can develop secondary complications such as non-cardiogenic pulmonary edema or hypoxia hours later, so transport and hospital observation are required regardless of apparent recovery at the scene. Discharging the patient in the field is inappropriate, oxygen should still be applied and titrated as needed rather than withheld, and ongoing monitoring is necessary even after initial rescue breathing was effective. This delayed-deterioration risk is a defining feature of drowning incidents.
Case C0712

You are dispatched to a residential structure fire where a 45-year-old male occupant was rescued from a smoke-filled kitchen by firefighters. He is conscious, coughing, with soot around his mouth and nostrils and a hoarse voice. Vital signs: HR 118, RR 26, BP 128/82, SpO2 99% on room air by pulse oximetry. He complains of a headache and mild nausea. His coworker who was also inside reports similar symptoms.

  1. The correct answer is A (The SpO2 reading may be falsely reassuring and cannot rule out carbon monoxide poisoning): standard pulse oximeters cannot differentiate carboxyhemoglobin from oxyhemoglobin, so a patient with significant carbon monoxide poisoning can still display a normal or near-normal SpO2 reading. A normal SpO2 does not rule out either carbon monoxide poisoning or an inhalation airway injury, both of which are strongly suggested by this patient's soot exposure, hoarse voice, and headache. High-flow oxygen should still be applied based on the history and symptoms, not withheld because of a reassuring number.
  2. The correct answer is C (Early upper airway thermal or chemical injury with potential for swelling): a hoarse voice after smoke and heat exposure is a red flag for thermal or chemical injury to the upper airway, which can progress to significant swelling and airway obstruction even when the patient initially appears stable. This finding should not be attributed to anxiety, a pre-existing condition, or simple dehydration, since doing so would miss a potentially rapidly deteriorating airway. Close airway monitoring and early transport are essential whenever hoarseness follows smoke exposure.
  3. The correct answer is B (A shared toxic exposure such as carbon monoxide from the fire): when two or more people who were in the same environment develop similar nonspecific symptoms like headache and nausea at the same time, a shared toxic exposure such as carbon monoxide from the fire should be strongly suspected rather than coincidence. Anxiety and dehydration would not typically produce this identical symptom pattern in two separate individuals simultaneously. Both patients should receive high-flow oxygen and be transported for evaluation given the shared exposure history.
  4. The correct answer is B (Frostnip, a mild and fully reversible superficial cold injury): frostnip is a mild, reversible cold injury involving numbness and pale skin that resolves completely with warming and does not cause tissue freezing or lasting damage, unlike frostbite which involves actual tissue freezing. Trench foot results from prolonged wet cold exposure rather than brief cold contact, and chilblains are a separate inflammatory reaction to repeated cold exposure over time. Because frostnip is fully reversible, ongoing rewarming and protection from further cold exposure are generally sufficient.
  5. The correct answer is D (Acute mountain sickness, the mildest and most common altitude illness): headache, nausea, fatigue, and poor sleep after rapid ascent without confusion or ataxia is the classic presentation of acute mountain sickness, the mildest and most common form of altitude illness. High altitude cerebral edema would additionally involve confusion or ataxia, high altitude pulmonary edema would present with breathlessness and cough, and decompression sickness is related to diving, not altitude ascent alone. The recommended treatment is halting further ascent and, if symptoms worsen, descending to a lower elevation.
  6. The correct answer is B (1, 2 and 4): breathlessness at rest with a dry cough progressing to pink frothy sputum is characteristic of high altitude pulmonary edema, and both immediate descent and this condition's potential lethality if ascent continues are well established.
