Supplemental oxygen is actually a key and effective field treatment, not something without a role, and this presentation reflects a serious altitude-related emergency rather than a simple viral respiratory infection and must not be treated as one. Rapid recognition and descent can be lifesaving in this condition.
  7. The correct answer is C (Decompression sickness caused by nitrogen bubble formation in the tissues): joint pain, fatigue, and mottled skin developing after surfacing from a dive are classic signs of decompression sickness, caused by dissolved nitrogen forming bubbles in tissues and blood as ambient pressure decreases too quickly. Arterial gas embolism typically presents immediately and dramatically at or shortly after surfacing with neurological or cardiac symptoms, not this more gradual joint and skin pattern, a musculoskeletal strain would not explain the skin mottling, and ear barotrauma causes localized ear pain rather than this systemic picture. High-flow oxygen and transport to a facility capable of hyperbaric therapy are indicated.
  8. The correct answer is D (Arterial gas embolism from gas forced into the arterial circulation): a rapid, uncontrolled ascent can force expanding gas from the lungs into the arterial circulation, producing sudden neurological symptoms such as loss of consciousness or focal deficits like facial droop almost immediately upon surfacing. Nitrogen narcosis occurs at depth, not after surfacing, and would not explain a focal deficit, and neither panic nor unrelated hypoglycemia accounts for the abrupt timing linked directly to the ascent. This patient should be placed supine, given high-flow oxygen, and transported urgently to a facility with hyperbaric capability.
  9. The correct answer is A (1, 2 and 4): under the rule of nines an entire arm is approximately 9 percent of total body surface area, the anterior torso is approximately 18 percent, and partial thickness burns by definition involve the epidermis and part of the underlying dermis. Superficial burns are excluded from total body surface area calculations used for fluid resuscitation because they do not disrupt the skin barrier in the same way, and full thickness burns typically have reduced or absent pain sensation to light touch because nerve endings in the affected skin have been destroyed. Accurate body surface area estimation directly affects fluid resuscitation decisions at the hospital.
  10. The correct answer is C (Airway swelling may progress rapidly even though the patient appears stable now): facial burns, singed eyebrows, and hoarseness after an enclosed-space fire are strong red flags for impending airway compromise, and swelling can progress rapidly even in a patient who currently appears to be speaking and breathing well. Waiting for visible distress, loss of consciousness, or worsening symptoms before acting risks losing the airway entirely, so early recognition, oxygen, and close monitoring with rapid transport are essential. This is one of the clearest examples in prehospital care of a patient looking deceptively stable while a serious threat develops underneath.
  11. The correct answer is B (Burned tissue releases mediators that increase capillary permeability, causing fluid shifts and potential shock): significant burns trigger the release of inflammatory mediators that increase capillary permeability, allowing large volumes of intravascular fluid to shift into the surrounding tissue and third space, which can lead to hypovolemic shock if not addressed. Burns generally do not cause the same kind of direct blood loss as a laceration, fluid resuscitation is not primarily a pain management strategy, and it is not intended simply to dilute absorbed toxins. This fluid shift explains why burn patients require careful, calculated fluid administration guided by their total body surface area involved.
  12. The correct answer is D (1, 2 and 4): both entry and exit wounds should be identified and documented since this information helps the trauma team assess the likely wound track, high-velocity projectiles can cause tissue damage well beyond the visible wound through temporary cavitation, and rapid transport to a facility capable of definitive surgical intervention is a core priority in penetrating trauma. Impaled objects are generally stabilized in place and left for surgical removal rather than pulled out in the field because they may be tamponading a bleeding vessel, and scene safety must always be confirmed before any patient care begins, not after external bleeding control. This case illustrates why penetrating trauma management focuses on rapid, safe transport rather than prolonged scene time.
  13. The correct answer is A (Stabilize the fragment in place and apply direct pressure or a tourniquet as needed around it): impaled material should be stabilized rather than removed because it may be partially tamponading a bleeding vessel, while direct pressure or a tourniquet is applied around it to control ongoing hemorrhage. Removing the fragment can provoke sudden, severe bleeding, packing directly over an impaled object without controlling the underlying hemorrhage is inadequate, and leaving a bleeding wound uncovered delays necessary hemorrhage control. This approach balances hemorrhage control with avoiding further injury from object removal.
  14. The correct answer is B (Level A, providing fully encapsulated, vapour-tight protection with self-contained breathing apparatus): Level A protection provides the highest level of respiratory, skin, and eye protection using a fully encapsulated, vapour-tight suit with self-contained breathing apparatus, and is used when the hazard is unknown or highly toxic by any route of exposure. Level D is essentially standard work clothing with minimal protection, Level C uses an air-purifying respirator with a known and identified hazard, and Level B provides a high level of respiratory protection but not full vapour-tight skin protection. Choosing the correct level depends on what is known about the substance and its exposure risk.
  15. The correct answer is C (To reduce the risk of secondary contamination to responders and equipment): decontamination removes hazardous substances from the patient's skin, hair, and clothing before treatment begins, protecting paramedics, the ambulance, and hospital staff from becoming secondarily contaminated. It is a mandatory safety step rather than an optional or purely comfort-related measure, and while efficient decontamination can support timely transport, that is not its primary purpose. Skipping decontamination risks spreading the hazardous substance to every subsequent responder and location the patient contacts.
  16. The correct answer is D (Move him to a cool environment, remove excess clothing, and give oral fluids if tolerated): heat exhaustion in an alert, oriented patient is managed with passive cooling measures such as moving to shade or air conditioning, removing excess clothing, and oral rehydration if the patient can tolerate fluids safely. Aggressive ice water immersion and the rapid cooling priority described for heat stroke are reserved for patients with altered mental status and very high core temperatures, and withholding fluids from an alert patient who can protect his airway is unnecessary. Distinguishing heat exhaustion from heat stroke changes the aggressiveness of field cooling required.
Case C0728

You are called to a residential kitchen fire where a 28-year-old woman sustained burns when a pot of boiling oil splashed across her forearm and hand. On assessment, the skin is red, blistered, moist, and extremely painful to touch. There is no involvement of her face, airway, or torso. Vital signs are within normal limits and she is alert and anxious.

  1. The correct answer is A (Partial thickness, second degree burn involving blistering and significant pain): red, blistered, moist skin that is extremely painful is the classic description of a partial thickness burn, which involves the epidermis and part of the underlying dermis with intact nerve endings causing significant pain. A superficial burn would present as red and painful but without blistering, and a full thickness burn typically appears dry, leathery, or charred with reduced or absent pain because nerve endings have been destroyed. Paramedics can and should classify burn depth in the field based on these visual and sensory characteristics to guide initial care and hospital reporting.
  2. The correct answer is C (Apply cool (not ice-cold) running water or saline and cover loosely with a clean, dry dressing): cool water or saline helps stop the burning process and reduce pain without causing the tissue damage and hypothermia risk that ice can produce, and a clean, dry, loose dressing protects the wound from contamination while minimizing further pain from air exposure. Ice can worsen tissue injury through vasoconstriction and cold injury, home remedies such as butter can trap heat and introduce infection, and blisters should be left intact since they provide a natural barrier against infection. This approach balances pain relief with wound protection during transport.
  3. The correct answer is B (Burns involving the hands, face, feet, joints, or genitals typically warrant specialist burn centre evaluation): certain body regions, including the hands, face, feet, joints, and genitalia, are considered high-risk burn locations because of their functional and cosmetic importance, and burns there typically require specialist evaluation regardless of total body surface area involved. Total body surface area threshold alone does not determine which specific burns need specialist care, normal vital signs do not eliminate the need for evaluation of a functionally significant burn, and hand burns in particular carry real risk to future function. Transport decisions for burns should account for anatomical location, not size alone.
  4. The correct answer is D (Apply an occlusive or vented chest seal dressing): a sucking chest wound indicates an open pneumothorax, and applying an occlusive or vented chest seal prevents air from continuing to enter the pleural space through the wound while allowing trapped air to escape, reducing the risk of a tension pneumothorax developing. Leaving the wound open allows continued air entry with each breath, sealing all four sides with a completely non-vented dressing can trap air and precipitate a tension pneumothorax, and delaying treatment risks rapid respiratory deterioration. Prompt recognition and correct dressing technique are critical in this time-sensitive injury.
  5. The correct answer is A (A decreasing level of consciousness increases the risk of aspiration if charcoal is given orally): activated charcoal requires a patient to be able to protect his or her own airway, and giving an oral substance to a patient with a decreasing level of consciousness or active vomiting creates a significant aspiration risk. Activated charcoal does have real uses for certain oral poisonings when appropriate, it works by binding many oral toxins including some solid medications, and it is administered orally or via tube, never intravenously. Airway protection status is therefore a key consideration before any oral decontamination therapy is considered.
  6. The correct answer is B (1, 2, 3 and 5): sympathomimetic toxicity from stimulant use classically produces tachycardia, hypertension, diaphoresis, dilated pupils, hyperthermia requiring active cooling, and in severe agitated states can precipitate rhabdomyolysis from prolonged muscle exertion. This presentation is essentially the opposite of opioid toxicity, which produces bradycardia, respiratory depression, and pinpoint pupils, so treating them identically would be dangerously wrong. Recognizing the sympathomimetic pattern guides the paramedic toward cooling, careful monitoring, and de-escalation rather than opioid-directed interventions like naloxone.
  7. The correct answer is C (Stage at a safe distance until law enforcement confirms the scene is secure): scene safety must be confirmed before any patient contact, and an unconfirmed, potentially still-dangerous scene requires paramedics to stage nearby and wait for law enforcement clearance, even when the patient's injury is time-critical. Entering an unsecured scene, sending a single crew member in alone, or relying on personal protective equipment as a substitute for actual scene clearance all place responders at unacceptable risk of becoming additional casualties. A paramedic who becomes injured or killed can no longer help the original patient at all, which is the core reasoning behind this priority.
  8. The correct answer is D (Active core rewarming with warmed intravenous fluids and passive external rewarming can both be used): appropriate prehospital rewarming measures include passive insulation to prevent further heat loss along with active measures such as warmed intravenous fluids where available, both aimed at gently raising core temperature. Vigorously rubbing the limbs can dislodge cold, acidotic peripheral blood toward the heart and precipitate arrhythmia, rewarming extremities before the trunk risks the same dangerous afterdrop effect, and cold intravenous fluids would worsen rather than treat hypothermia. Gentle, core-focused rewarming techniques are preferred throughout prehospital hypothermia management.
  9. The correct answer is A (1, 2, 4 and 5): carbonaceous sputum is a strong indicator of inhalation injury and possible impending airway compromise, which is why advanced providers may consider early intubation before visible swelling makes the airway much harder to secure, high-flow oxygen should be applied regardless of a pulse oximetry reading because of possible concurrent carbon monoxide exposure, and fluid resuscitation is calculated from the total body surface area of partial and full thickness burns. Airway urgency in this patient is driven by the inhalation injury findings, not the burn surface area alone, since a patient could have significant airway compromise with a relatively smaller burn or vice versa. This scenario highlights why airway and burn assessments are evaluated as separate, equally important priorities.
  10. The correct answer is C (It can be given by several routes, including intranasal and intramuscular, based on local protocol): naloxone is effective by multiple routes, and paramedics commonly use intranasal or intramuscular administration when intravenous access is not readily available, allowing rapid treatment of respiratory depression. It does not require intravenous administration to work, waiting until cardiac arrest develops would allow preventable harm to occur first, and naloxone specifically reverses opioid receptor effects rather than acting broadly across all sedative classes such as benzodiazepines. Route flexibility is one of the reasons naloxone is well suited to prehospital and even community-based overdose response.
  11. The correct answer is B (Position the ambulance upwind, uphill, and at a safe distance while awaiting hazmat assessment): when an unknown or unidentified hazardous vapour is present, paramedics must position themselves upwind and uphill from the hazard and maintain a safe distance until specialized hazmat resources can identify the substance and establish safe zones. Approaching downwind or briefly entering the vapour cloud both risk direct crew exposure, and standard surgical or N95 masks are not designed to protect against unknown chemical vapours. This cautious approach prioritizes responder safety, which ultimately protects the patients as well by keeping rescuers available and uninjured.
  12. The correct answer is D (1, 3 and 4): bag-valve-mask ventilation should begin immediately to correct the profound respiratory depression while naloxone is prepared, ongoing reassessment is essential because re-sedation can occur once naloxone's effect wears off, and potent synthetic opioids like fentanyl can require repeat dosing or higher total naloxone doses compared to less potent opioids. Naloxone should not be delayed for intravenous access when intranasal or intramuscular routes are available and ventilation is already being supported, and transport remains necessary even after apparent improvement because of the significant re-sedation risk with fentanyl specifically. This case reflects the real prehospital pattern seen with high-potency synthetic opioid overdoses.
  13. The correct answer is A (Recognize the unusual clustering and report it through appropriate channels for further investigation): an unusual cluster of patients with similar unexplained symptoms at the same event over a short period is a recognized early warning sign of a possible biological or chemical incident, and paramedics play an important surveillance role by recognizing and reporting such patterns through proper channels such as dispatch, medical control, or public health. Treating each patient in isolation without recognizing the pattern, assuming a benign cause without justification, or waiting for an arbitrarily large number of cases before acting could all delay a critical public health response. Early recognition and reporting by frontline responders is a key part of CBRNE and biological incident preparedness.
Case C0741

Your crew is dispatched Code 4 (lights and siren) to a downtown Toronto rooming house for a man not breathing. On arrival you find a 34-year-old male slumped in a bathroom with a syringe on the floor beside him. He is unresponsive to voice and pain, with slow, shallow respirations at 4 breaths per minute and pinpoint pupils. His skin is cool and cyanotic around the lips. Initial vitals: HR 58, BP 92/58, SpO2 78% on room air, BGL 5.8 mmol/L.

  1. The correct answer is B (Opioid toxidrome, marked by CNS and respiratory depression with miosis): This patient's markedly decreased respiratory rate, decreased level of consciousness, and pinpoint pupils are the classic triad of opioid toxicity, further supported by the syringe found at the scene. Sympathomimetic and anticholinergic toxidromes typically present with tachycardia, hypertension, and dilated rather than constricted pupils. A cholinergic toxidrome would be expected to show excessive secretions and bronchospasm rather than isolated respiratory and CNS depression, making opioid toxicity the best explanation here.
  2. The correct answer is D (Begin bag valve mask ventilation with supplemental oxygen): A respiratory rate of four breaths per minute with an oxygen saturation of 78 percent represents an immediate life threat from inadequate ventilation and oxygenation. Correcting hypoxia and hypoventilation takes priority over advanced airway placement, glucose testing, or cardiac monitoring, since the patient will suffer irreversible harm from hypoxia before any of those other steps could help. Naloxone administration should follow or accompany ventilatory support, not replace the need to immediately ventilate a critically hypoventilating patient.
  3. The correct answer is A (Continued assisted ventilation, since naloxone's effect can wear off before the opioid does): Naloxone has a relatively short duration of action compared to many opioids, particularly long acting or high potency formulations, so a patient can relapse into respiratory depression once the naloxone effect diminishes. The crew must continue supporting ventilation and closely reassess the patient rather than assuming one dose provides lasting reversal. Expecting an immediate full return to baseline mental status is unrealistic and can lead to premature withdrawal of support.
  4. The correct answer is C (Naloxone's duration of action is shorter than many opioids, so respiratory depression can return): Because naloxone typically wears off faster than many opioids remain active in the body, a patient who appears improved shortly after a dose can deteriorate again en route or after arrival if not transported and monitored. This is the primary clinical reason paramedics strongly encourage transport rather than accepting refusal after apparent improvement. There is no fixed twenty four hour legal observation requirement, and repeat naloxone dosing is permitted in the prehospital setting when clinically indicated.
  5. The correct answer is D (Naloxone reverses opioid induced respiratory depression but its effect may be shorter than the opioid's, requiring ongoing reassessment): Naloxone competitively displaces opioids from receptors and can rapidly restore adequate breathing, but because its own duration of action is often shorter than that of the opioid involved, respiratory depression can recur. This means ongoing reassessment and readiness to support ventilation or redose remain essential rather than assuming the problem is permanently solved. Naloxone should never be withheld until a patient is fully apneic and pulseless, since earlier intervention prevents further deterioration.
  6. The correct answer is D (1, 3 and 5): Diaphoresis, tachycardia, and hypertension are classic features of a sympathomimetic toxidrome caused by excess catecholamine or catecholamine-like stimulation. Dry, flushed skin and urinary retention, statements 2 and 4, are instead hallmark findings of an anticholinergic toxidrome, which blocks rather than stimulates sympathetic sweating and bladder emptying. Recognizing which specific findings belong to each toxidrome helps guide appropriate prehospital assessment and communication with receiving facilities.
  7. The correct answer is B (Anticholinergic toxidrome): Dry, flushed skin, dilated pupils, urinary retention, and agitated confusion together reflect blockade of acetylcholine receptors throughout the body, the hallmark of an anticholinergic toxidrome. Opioid toxicity would instead cause pinpoint pupils and sedation rather than agitation, and cholinergic toxicity classically produces excessive secretions rather than dryness. A sympathomimetic toxidrome typically presents with diaphoresis rather than the dry skin described in this patient.
  8. The correct answer is A (Running a fuel powered generator or vehicle engine in an attached garage or enclosed space): Combustion engines and generators produce carbon monoxide as a byproduct, and running them in an attached garage or any poorly ventilated enclosed space allows the gas to accumulate and migrate into occupied living areas. Properly vented outdoor equipment and well ventilated spaces greatly reduce this risk, while sitting near an open window in cold weather does not itself generate carbon monoxide. This scenario, particularly with the connecting door left open, represents the classic mechanism behind most residential carbon monoxide poisoning calls.
  9. The correct answer is C (1, 2 and 4): Acetaminophen overdose is dangerous precisely because early symptoms are often mild or entirely absent even with a potentially lethal ingestion, hepatotoxicity is the primary life threatening complication, and obtaining an accurate history of timing and amount ingested is critical for guiding hospital treatment decisions. Inducing vomiting at home, statement 3, is not recommended prehospital practice and can introduce additional risks. A reassuring appearance on scene, statement 5, does not confirm the ingestion is safe, since toxic liver injury develops over subsequent hours to days.
  10. The correct answer is C (Provide supportive care, monitor vital signs closely, and transport promptly while gathering ingestion history): Salicylate overdose can cause a dangerous mixed acid base disturbance, hyperthermia, and multi organ effects, so prehospital management focuses on supportive care, close monitoring, and prompt transport while gathering as much history as possible about the ingestion. Administering acetaminophen would not address the underlying toxicity and could add additional risk. Delaying transport to confirm an exact tablet count is inappropriate given the potential for rapid clinical deterioration.
  11. The correct answer is D (Heat exhaustion, since mental status remains intact and temperature is only mildly elevated): This patient's preserved alertness and orientation, combined with a core temperature under 40 degrees Celsius, point toward heat exhaustion rather than heat stroke, which is defined by significant central nervous system dysfunction and typically much higher core temperatures. Heat stroke is a true emergency requiring aggressive cooling, while heat exhaustion generally responds to rest, fluids, and passive cooling measures. Malignant hyperthermia and anticholinergic toxidrome do not fit this exertional, exposure related presentation.
  12. The correct answer is A (1, 3 and 4): Salicylate toxicity classically causes tinnitus, hyperventilation from direct respiratory centre stimulation and acid base disturbance, and hyperthermia from uncoupling of cellular metabolism. Bradypnea and bradycardia, statements 2 and 5, are the opposite of what is typically seen, since salicylate toxicity usually produces tachypnea and often tachycardia rather than slowed vital signs. Recognizing hyperventilation as an expected finding helps avoid mistaking it for an unrelated respiratory problem.
  13. The correct answer is A (Passive external rewarming with insulation and gentle handling is appropriate, while aggressive active rewarming is generally avoided in the field): Severely hypothermic patients are at risk of dangerous dysrhythmias, so prehospital care focuses on gentle handling, removing wet clothing, and passive insulation rather than aggressive active rewarming techniques that could trigger cardiac instability. Hot beverages and vigorous limb massage are avoided because they can worsen circulatory shifts and do not safely address core temperature. Hot water immersion is not an appropriate prehospital field intervention for this level of hypothermia.
  14. The correct answer is B (Frostbite involves actual tissue freezing and potential permanent damage, while frostnip is reversible with rewarming): Frostnip is a milder, fully reversible cold injury without actual tissue freezing, while frostbite involves ice crystal formation within tissue and carries a real risk of permanent damage if not managed appropriately. The firmness and persistent numbness described in this patient are more consistent with frostbite than the fully reversible changes of frostnip. Blistering may occur with either condition depending on severity, so its presence alone does not define the distinction.
  15. The correct answer is B (1, 2, 4 and 5): Hypoglycemia can present with or mimic alcohol intoxication, a head injury sustained in the fall could independently explain altered mental status, checking blood glucose is appropriate whenever equipment is available, and vital signs with repeat neurological assessment help detect a change in condition. Statement 3 is incorrect and must be excluded, since the smell of alcohol on a patient's breath does not rule out other causes of altered mental status such as trauma or hypoglycemia. Assuming alcohol as the sole cause without further assessment risks missing a genuinely life threatening condition.
  16. The correct answer is D (Hypoxia from impaired gas exchange is the primary insult driving drowning related complications regardless of water type): Regardless of whether fresh or salt water is aspirated, the dominant physiological problem in drowning is hypoxia from impaired gas exchange, and resuscitation approaches are not meaningfully different based on water type. Laryngospasm can and does occur in some drowning events, contributing to the process, and significant hypoxia can develop within minutes rather than requiring prolonged submersion. Prehospital management therefore focuses on rapidly correcting hypoxia through ventilation and oxygenation.
  17. The correct answer is C (Pulmonary barotrauma from lung overexpansion during rapid ascent): Breath holding during a rapid ascent allows expanding gas in the lungs to overexpand and potentially rupture alveoli, producing immediate chest pain and breathing difficulty right at the surface. Decompression sickness typically develops over minutes to hours after surfacing rather than immediately, since it results from dissolved nitrogen bubble formation rather than lung overexpansion. Nitrogen narcosis occurs at depth rather than at the surface and does not explain this immediate post ascent presentation.
Case C0758

Your unit responds to a construction site on a July afternoon with a heat index of 42 degrees Celsius for a 58-year-old male, confused. Coworkers report he has been labouring outdoors for six hours with minimal water intake and stopped sweating about twenty minutes ago. On arrival he is agitated and disoriented, skin hot and dry to touch, HR 138, BP 88/50, RR 28, SpO2 94% on room air, tympanic temperature 41.2 degrees Celsius.

  1. The correct answer is D (Altered mental status combined with hot, dry skin and a markedly elevated core temperature): The combination of confusion, hot dry skin from failed thermoregulation, and a core temperature above 40 degrees Celsius is the defining feature that separates heat stroke from heat exhaustion. Tachycardia, hypotension, and tachypnea can all occur in significant heat exhaustion as well, so none of those findings alone confirms heat stroke. The presence of altered mental status with markedly elevated temperature is what makes this presentation a true emergency requiring aggressive cooling.
  2. The correct answer is A (Aggressive active cooling, such as removing clothing and applying cold packs to the neck, axillae and groin): Rapid reduction of core temperature is the single most important intervention in heat stroke, since prolonged hyperthermia drives ongoing cellular and organ damage. Oral rehydration is inappropriate given this patient's altered mental status and aspiration risk, and warming the patient would worsen an already dangerous hyperthermia. Cooling should never be withheld pending hospital confirmation, since delay directly increases the risk of severe complications and death.
  3. The correct answer is C (Seizure activity and a deteriorating level of consciousness from cerebral effects of hyperthermia): Extreme hyperthermia can directly injure the central nervous system, so seizures and a worsening level of consciousness are recognized complications that the crew should anticipate and be prepared to manage during transport. An urticarial rash and progressive bradycardia are not typical expected complications of heat stroke. Confusion in heat stroke does not resolve immediately simply from moving the patient into a cooler environment, since underlying core temperature and organ dysfunction take time to correct even with aggressive cooling.
  4. The correct answer is B (Establish IV access and administer isotonic fluid while continuing active cooling and monitoring): This patient's hypotension and tachycardia suggest significant volume depletion in addition to hyperthermia, so establishing IV access and administering isotonic fluid alongside continued active cooling addresses both problems simultaneously. Withholding fluids based on an assumption of fluid overload is incorrect, since heat stroke patients are frequently volume depleted from sweating and poor intake. Antipyretic medications are not effective for environmentally driven hyperthermia, and elevating the head to sixty degrees would not appropriately address hypotension.
  5. The correct answer is A (Acute mountain sickness, which typically responds to rest, hydration, and halting further ascent): Headache, fatigue, nausea, and sleep disturbance without ataxia or significant neurological findings represent classic acute mountain sickness, the mildest and most common form of altitude illness. High altitude cerebral edema would be expected to produce ataxia and more significant confusion, while high altitude pulmonary edema would present with respiratory symptoms rather than this picture. Decompression sickness is unrelated to altitude gain on foot and instead relates to diving physiology.
  6. The correct answer is D (1, 3 and 5): Appropriate frostbite management includes removing wet or constrictive clothing and jewellery, protecting the injured tissue from further mechanical trauma or refreezing, and splinting and loosely wrapping the affected area for transport. Vigorously rubbing frostbitten tissue, statement 2, causes additional mechanical damage to already fragile, frozen tissue and should never be done. Rewarming over an open flame, statement 4, risks severe burns to already compromised, often numb tissue and is not an appropriate field technique.
  7. The correct answer is B (Superficial (first degree) burn, involving only the epidermis): Redness, dryness, and pain without blistering or loss of skin integrity are characteristic of a superficial burn limited to the epidermis, the most common presentation of ordinary sunburn. Full thickness and fourth degree burns involve much deeper tissue destruction with a leathery or charred appearance rather than simple redness. A deep partial thickness burn would typically show blistering and a more mottled appearance, which are absent in this presentation.
  8. The correct answer is D (Twenty seven percent, combining eighteen percent for the anterior trunk and nine percent for the leg): The adult rule of nines assigns eighteen percent to the entire anterior trunk, combining the chest and abdomen, and nine percent to the entire front surface of one leg, which together total approximately twenty seven percent of total body surface area. Selecting only nine or eighteen percent would undercount either the trunk or the leg contribution, and thirty six percent would represent double counting one of these regions. Accurate surface area estimation guides both severity assessment and fluid resuscitation decisions at the receiving facility.
  9. The correct answer is A (1, 3 and 4): Gentle handling to reduce the risk of triggering a dysrhythmia, removing wet clothing while insulating the patient from further heat loss, and performing an adequately prolonged pulse check before assuming pulselessness are all appropriate principles for managing significant hypothermia. Vigorous limb rubbing and rapid hot water immersion, statements 2 and 5, both risk worsening circulatory instability and should be avoided in the prehospital setting. A cold heart is especially prone to dysrhythmia, which is why the extended pulse check and gentle handling are both emphasized.
  10. The correct answer is C (IV access and isotonic fluid administration should be initiated per local protocol while monitoring for signs of shock): Extensive burns cause significant fluid loss through the damaged skin barrier, and early recognition of developing shock with appropriate fluid resuscitation per local protocol helps prevent hypoperfusion during a lengthy transport. Completely withholding fluids or offering only oral fluids to a patient developing shock would allow ongoing deterioration. Fluid resuscitation should not be delayed entirely until arrival at a specialized burn centre, since prehospital time can be substantial for a lengthy transport.
  11. The correct answer is A (Progressive hoarseness and stridor suggesting developing upper airway swelling): Worsening hoarseness and any stridor indicate developing swelling within the upper airway from heat and chemical injury, which can progress to complete obstruction and represents the most urgent airway concern in an inhalation burn patient. Soot around the nose and mouth and singed nasal hair are supportive red flags but a normal or unchanged voice throughout assessment would actually be reassuring rather than concerning. A burn limited to the forehead alone does not by itself indicate airway involvement.
  12. The correct answer is C (1, 3 and 5): In a drowning patient with a pulse, prioritizing early ventilation and oxygenation over chest compressions addresses the primary hypoxic insult, vomiting is common during drowning resuscitation and should be anticipated, and rewarming measures should begin if the patient is hypothermic from cold water exposure. Cervical spine precautions are not automatically indicated for every drowning unless the mechanism suggests possible trauma such as a diving injury, making statement 2 incorrect as an absolute rule. Resuscitation should not be withheld based solely on estimated submersion time, since some drowning victims, particularly in cold water, have been successfully resuscitated after longer intervals.
  13. The correct answer is B (Stabilize the object in place with bulky dressings and transport promptly): Impaled objects should be left in place and stabilized with bulky dressings to prevent movement, since removal can cause severe uncontrolled bleeding if the object is tamponading an injured vessel. Rotating or probing the object risks causing further internal injury, and pushing it in further would worsen the wound. Prompt transport with the object secured allows definitive removal to occur in a controlled surgical environment.
  14. The correct answer is C (Paramedics without specialized hazmat training generally lack the PPE and training to safely enter contaminated zones): Standard paramedic uniforms and gloves do not provide adequate protection against many hazardous substances, and entering the hot zone requires specialized training and chemical protective equipment that most paramedic crews do not carry. Recognizing this limitation and staging appropriately until the scene is assessed by qualified hazmat personnel protects both the crew and any patients awaiting care. PPE selection remains important even after triage, since ongoing contamination risk does not end once patients have been sorted.
  15. The correct answer is B (1, 3 and 5): Decompression sickness typically develops over minutes to hours after surfacing as dissolved nitrogen forms bubbles in tissues and the bloodstream, commonly producing joint and limb pain along with neurological symptoms such as confusion or weakness. Immediate onset upon rapid ascent, statement 2, along with chest pain occurring during descent, statement 4, are more characteristic of pulmonary barotrauma from lung overexpansion, a distinct mechanism from decompression sickness. Distinguishing the delayed onset pattern of decompression sickness from the immediate onset of barotrauma helps guide appropriate field impression and hospital communication.
  16. The correct answer is D (An unusual cluster of severe illness in an unexpected location with no clear natural source): A cluster of unusually severe illness occurring together in time and place without an identifiable natural cause is a recognized warning sign for a possible deliberate biological release and should prompt heightened situational awareness and appropriate notification. A single patient with ordinary seasonal illness or a gradually worsening chronic condition does not carry the same significance. A slowly evolving, widespread common cold outbreak over several weeks follows a pattern much more consistent with a typical natural illness than a deliberate exposure.
  17. The correct answer is A (Acute opioid withdrawal precipitated by naloxone can cause agitation, and safety along with ongoing risk must be carefully assessed): Naloxone can precipitate acute opioid withdrawal, producing agitation and combativeness, and the crew must carefully balance patient safety, scene safety, and the ongoing risk of recurrent respiratory depression when a patient wants to refuse further care. Physical restraint and sedation are not appropriate as an automatic first response in every case and should be reserved for situations that genuinely require them. A refusal made during acute agitation or altered capacity is not automatically legally binding, and careful judgment and documentation are required.
Case C0775

Dispatch sends your crew to a single family home at 2 a.m. for multiple patients feeling unwell. A neighbour called 911 after finding the family difficult to wake. On your approach you note a generator running in the attached garage with the door to the house left open. Inside you find a mother and two children, ages 8 and 11, all reporting headache and nausea; the mother is also mildly confused. No smoke or flames are visible.

  1. The correct answer is A (Withdraw to a safe distance, request the fire department, and avoid entering an atmosphere with unconfirmed carbon monoxide levels): Entering an environment with unknown and potentially dangerous carbon monoxide levels without proper detection equipment and ventilation puts the crew at serious risk of becoming additional casualties. Requesting fire department resources with gas detection and ventilation capability, then withdrawing to a safe distance, is the appropriate first priority before any patient contact occurs. Attempting to personally ventilate the home or shut off the generator exposes the crew to the same hazardous atmosphere affecting the family.
  2. The correct answer is C (Standard pulse oximetry cannot reliably distinguish carboxyhemoglobin from oxyhemoglobin and may read falsely normal): Standard two wavelength pulse oximetry cannot differentiate carboxyhemoglobin from oxyhemoglobin, so a patient with significant carbon monoxide poisoning can display a falsely reassuring, near normal oxygen saturation reading. This is a critical limitation that must be recognized so that treatment is guided by clinical suspicion and history rather than pulse oximetry alone. Cherry red skin colouring is a classically described but actually uncommon finding in real cases, and a strong detectable odour is not reliable since carbon monoxide itself is odourless.
  3. The correct answer is D (High concentration supplemental oxygen delivered by non-rebreather mask): High concentration oxygen accelerates the displacement of carbon monoxide from hemoglobin, shortening its half life and speeding recovery, making it the cornerstone of prehospital treatment for suspected carbon monoxide poisoning. Activated charcoal has no role in treating a gas exposure, and sedating a confused patient without first addressing the underlying hypoxic cause is inappropriate. A low flow trickle of oxygen would not deliver the high concentration needed to meaningfully accelerate carbon monoxide elimination.
  4. The correct answer is B (Carbon monoxide toxicity severity depends on individual factors and can worsen after removal from the source, so mild symptoms do not exclude significant exposure): Individual susceptibility to carbon monoxide varies, and symptoms can evolve or worsen even after a patient is removed from the source, meaning current symptom severity does not reliably predict a person's underlying carboxyhemoglobin level or eventual outcome. All exposed individuals, not only the most symptomatic person, warrant careful evaluation given this unpredictability. Children are not inherently less affected by carbon monoxide, and symptoms do not always resolve quickly with fresh air alone once significant absorption has occurred.
  5. The correct answer is A (Hepatotoxicity develops gradually over one to several days as toxic metabolites accumulate in the liver): Acetaminophen is metabolized into a toxic byproduct that overwhelms the liver's protective mechanisms at high doses, and liver injury develops gradually over one to several days rather than producing immediate obvious symptoms. This delayed, initially silent course is precisely why a well appearing patient still requires hospital evaluation and blood testing after a significant ingestion. Acetaminophen overdose does not cause immediate respiratory arrest or severe abdominal pain within the first hour in most cases, which can falsely reassure family members.
  6. The correct answer is B (Alcohol can impair glucose production by the liver and mask or mimic hypoglycemia): Alcohol impairs the liver's ability to produce glucose, and hypoglycemia can either coexist with intoxication or closely mimic its presentation, so checking blood glucose is an essential step whenever altered mental status is present regardless of apparent alcohol use. Assuming intoxication as the sole explanation risks missing a treatable and potentially life threatening cause of the patient's declining condition. Alcohol does not typically raise blood glucose, and hypoglycemia certainly can occur in a patient who has also consumed alcohol.
  7. The correct answer is A (1, 2 and 4): Full thickness burns characteristically appear leathery, white, or charred, are often painless at the burn site itself because nerve endings have been destroyed, and show loss of normal skin elasticity. Blistering with a moist, red, and very painful wound bed, statement 3, along with blanching to light pressure, statement 5, are instead findings typical of a partial thickness burn where some nerve function and capillary response remain intact. Distinguishing these findings helps guide burn depth classification, which affects both treatment priorities and destination decisions.
  8. The correct answer is C (Carbon monoxide binds hemoglobin with much greater affinity than oxygen, displacing oxygen from red blood cells): Carbon monoxide binds hemoglobin far more strongly than oxygen does, effectively displacing oxygen and forming carboxyhemoglobin, which severely reduces the blood's capacity to carry and deliver oxygen to tissues despite normal appearing lung function. This explains why pulse oximetry, which cannot distinguish the two forms of hemoglobin, can appear falsely reassuring. Carbon monoxide does not directly destroy red blood cells or cause immediate mechanical airway obstruction, making hemoglobin binding the key mechanism behind its toxicity.
  9. The correct answer is D (Central nervous system effects of severe hyperthermia disrupting normal brain function): Severe, sustained hyperthermia in heat stroke can directly injure and disrupt normal brain function, and new onset seizures are a recognized and expected complication that the crew should be prepared to manage while continuing aggressive cooling. This is not a normal or benign response to cooling itself, and assuming an unrelated pre-existing seizure disorder ignores the far more likely explanation given the clinical context. Cooling interventions themselves do not typically cause hypoglycemia severe enough to independently explain a new seizure.
  10. The correct answer is D (1, 2 and 4): Singed nasal hair with soot around the mouth and nose, hoarseness or a changing voice, and stridor or other audible upper airway sounds are all recognized red flags suggesting developing airway swelling after an inhalation or facial burn injury. Burns confined only to the arms and hands, statement 3, do not by themselves suggest airway involvement, and a clear, normal voice with no cough, statement 5, is actually reassuring rather than concerning. Prompt recognition of these red flags allows early airway management before swelling progresses to complete obstruction.
  11. The correct answer is B (A cold, irritable myocardium can develop dangerous dysrhythmias, including ventricular fibrillation, with rough handling): A severely hypothermic heart is electrically irritable, and rough handling or unnecessary jostling can trigger dangerous dysrhythmias including ventricular fibrillation that may be extremely difficult to convert while the patient remains cold. This is why paramedics move hypothermic patients gently and minimize unnecessary manipulation throughout assessment, packaging, and transport. Rough handling is never required to stimulate circulation, and hypothermic patients remain fully capable of developing serious cardiac dysrhythmias despite reduced overall metabolic activity.
  12. The correct answer is A (Hard, white, waxy skin that remains firm and numb even after gentle warming attempts): Skin that stays hard, white, waxy, and numb despite gentle warming attempts suggests that deeper tissue layers have frozen, indicating a more severe frostbite injury with greater risk of permanent damage. Mild redness and tingling that resolve quickly, or normal colour and sensation, suggest a much more superficial or minimal cold injury. Recognizing these deep tissue warning signs helps the crew appropriately protect the extremity and communicate injury severity to the receiving facility.
  13. The correct answer is B (1, 2 and 4): Partial thickness burns covering more than ten percent of total body surface area, burns involving specialized areas such as the face, hands, feet, or genitalia, and circumferential burns of a limb or the chest are all recognized indications for transport to a specialized burn centre due to their potential for serious functional or circulatory complications. A small superficial sunburn or a limited partial thickness burn on one forearm, statements 3 and 5, generally does not meet criteria requiring specialized burn centre care. Recognizing these referral criteria helps guide appropriate destination decisions for burn patients.
  14. The correct answer is D (Delayed pulmonary complications such as secondary drowning can develop hours after an initially reassuring presentation): Even a small amount of aspirated water can cause delayed pulmonary complications, sometimes called secondary drowning, that develop hours after an initial event despite the patient appearing well at the time of assessment. This delayed risk is precisely why hospital observation is recommended even for submersion patients who look clinically well on scene. Assuming that an initially normal assessment excludes any future complication ignores this well documented delayed presentation pattern.
  15. The correct answer is C (Ascending rapidly without appropriate safety stops after a deep or lengthy dive): Rapid ascent without appropriate safety stops does not allow dissolved nitrogen to be safely eliminated from tissues, increasing the likelihood that it will form bubbles and cause decompression sickness, particularly after a deep or lengthy dive. Descending slowly and remaining well within recommended time limits, along with diving in shallow water, are protective factors rather than risks. Breathing normal compressed air, as opposed to a specialized nitrogen enriched mixture, does not itself increase decompression sickness risk when combined with an appropriately conducted ascent.
  16. The correct answer is C (1, 3 and 4): Rapid transport with minimal scene time, covering the wound appropriately without removing any impaled object, and treating for shock while closely monitoring vital signs en route are all appropriate priorities for a penetrating abdominal gunshot wound, since definitive surgical care is the only way to control internal bleeding. Probing the wound to determine bullet trajectory, statement 2, risks worsening internal injury and provides no meaningful prehospital benefit. Delaying transport to fully document entry and exit wounds, statement 5, unnecessarily prolongs scene time for a patient who needs rapid surgical intervention.
  17. The correct answer is A (High altitude pulmonary edema, requiring descent and supplemental oxygen): Progressive shortness of breath, pink frothy sputum, and diffuse crackles on lung auscultation at altitude are the classic presentation of high altitude pulmonary edema, a serious complication requiring prompt descent and supplemental oxygen to prevent further deterioration. This presentation is distinct from a simple respiratory infection, which would not typically develop so specifically in the context of high altitude exposure. Acute mountain sickness alone would not be expected to cause this degree of respiratory compromise, and this scenario is unrelated to diving related decompression sickness.
  18. The correct answer is C (Deep partial thickness burn extending into the deeper dermis): A mottled, red to white wound bed with decreased sensation to light touch but preserved deep pressure sensation reflects injury extending into the deeper dermis while some nerve function remains, characteristic of a deep partial thickness burn. A superficial burn would show simple redness without this mottled appearance or altered sensation, while a full thickness burn typically destroys sensation completely rather than only reducing it. This burn is significant enough to require ongoing evaluation rather than being dismissed as too minor to assess further.
  19. The correct answer is A (1, 2 and 3): The hot zone represents the area of highest contamination risk and requires specialized PPE and training to enter safely, paramedics without hazmat specific training generally operate in the cold zone, and the warm zone is where decontamination typically occurs before a patient moves toward definitive treatment. Patient treatment and transport preparation typically occur in the cold zone rather than the hot zone, statement 4, since the cold zone is considered clean and safe for standard operations. Scene zoning remains necessary regardless of apparent patient symptom severity, since contamination risk to responders exists independently of how sick a patient appears.
  20. The correct answer is B (A child's head represents a proportionally larger percentage of total body surface area than an adult's): Young children have a proportionally larger head and smaller legs relative to total body surface area compared to adults, so applying the standard adult rule of nines without adjustment would significantly misestimate burn size in a pediatric patient. This adjustment matters because accurate surface area estimation affects both severity classification and fluid resuscitation planning at the receiving facility. Children certainly can and do sustain burns requiring surface area estimation, and pediatric burn assessment tools exist specifically to address this anatomical difference.
  21. The correct answer is D (Entry and exit wounds or additional injuries may be missed without a thorough, fully exposed examination): A single gunshot can produce multiple wounds, and additional entry, exit, or associated injuries can easily be missed if the patient is not fully exposed and thoroughly examined, particularly in poor lighting or with bulky clothing present. This thorough examination directly affects treatment priorities and the information communicated to the receiving trauma team. Relying only on the patient's report of pain is unreliable, since significant injuries can be present without the patient being aware of them due to shock or altered sensation.
  22. The correct answer is D (1, 2 and 4): Multiple patients collapsing together with no apparent trauma, an unusual odour or visible vapour cloud, and dead animals or vegetation in the surrounding area are all recognized warning signs that should raise suspicion for a chemical, biological, radiological, nuclear, or explosive incident rather than a purely ordinary medical event. A single patient with a clear cardiac history and typical chest pain, statement 3, or a scene with an obviously isolated mechanical injury, statement 5, does not carry the same significance. Recognizing these pattern based warning signs helps protect responders by prompting appropriate scene safety measures before entering an unrecognized hazardous environment.
  23. The correct answer is A (Decontamination prevents the spread of a contaminant to treatment areas and personnel who lack specialized protective equipment): Performing decontamination in the warm zone before moving a patient to the cold zone prevents the hazardous substance from being carried into treatment areas where paramedics and other personnel do not have specialized protective equipment. This zoning system protects both responders and other patients from secondary contamination while still allowing definitive treatment and transport to proceed once the patient is safe to approach. Skipping this step would defeat the entire purpose of establishing hazmat scene zones in the first place.
  24. The correct answer is D (Secondary devices are sometimes deliberately placed to target first responders and bystanders who gather after an initial explosion): Secondary explosive devices have been deliberately used in real incidents specifically to target first responders and bystanders who gather at the scene of an initial explosion, making ongoing situational awareness essential for all responding personnel, not only fire or police. This risk does not disappear at any arbitrary distance from the original scene, and paramedics share responsibility for maintaining awareness alongside other responding agencies. Staging safely and following direction from scene command helps protect the crew from this recognized secondary threat.
  25. The correct answer is B (1, 2 and 4): Excessive salivation and lacrimation, bronchospasm with excessive bronchial secretions, and bradycardia are all classic findings of a cholinergic toxidrome caused by acetylcholine excess from organophosphate exposure. Dilated, fixed pupils and dry mouth and dry skin, statements 3 and 5, are instead findings associated with an anticholinergic toxidrome, essentially the opposite physiological state. Recognizing the cholinergic pattern, often remembered through excessive secretions and a slowed heart rate, helps guide correct field impression and appropriate scene safety precautions given the ongoing exposure risk.
  26. The correct answer is A (Immediate assisted ventilation with a bag valve mask and high concentration oxygen): A respiratory rate of two breaths per minute represents a critical, immediately life threatening level of hypoventilation that requires immediate assisted ventilation with high concentration oxygen before or alongside any other intervention including naloxone administration. Delaying to perform a full neurological exam or establish IV access first would allow ongoing hypoxia to cause irreversible harm. Waiting for the patient to become fully apneic before intervening ignores the fact that this patient is already in critical respiratory failure and needs immediate support.
  27. The correct answer is B (Activated charcoal is not appropriate for a patient with a decreased level of consciousness due to aspiration risk): A patient with a decreased level of consciousness cannot reliably protect their own airway, so administering activated charcoal creates a significant risk of aspiration and should generally be avoided in this situation. Activated charcoal is not appropriate for every poisoned or overdosed patient, since it has specific indications, contraindications, and limited effectiveness depending on the substance and timing involved. It also has no meaningful role in treating carbon monoxide poisoning, which is a gas rather than an ingested toxin that charcoal can bind.
  28. The correct answer is C (2, 3 and 5): Ataxia with worsening confusion, a severe headache that is unrelieved by rest, and a progressively decreasing level of consciousness together suggest progression from simple acute mountain sickness toward high altitude cerebral edema, a life threatening emergency requiring urgent descent. Mild headache and fatigue alone, statement 1, are consistent with uncomplicated acute mountain sickness rather than a dangerous progression, and a headache that resolves with adequate hydration, statement 4, actually suggests improvement rather than worsening illness. Recognizing these warning signs early allows the crew to advocate for immediate descent before neurological deterioration becomes severe or irreversible.
  29. The correct answer is D (Heat exhaustion can progress to heat stroke if the underlying heat exposure and fluid loss are not adequately corrected): Heat exhaustion exists on a continuum with heat stroke, and without adequate correction of the underlying heat exposure, dehydration, and electrolyte loss, a patient's condition can progress to the far more dangerous heat stroke even after initial improvement. Continued monitoring allows the crew to detect early signs of this progression, such as developing confusion or a rising core temperature, before the situation becomes critical. Feeling somewhat better after initial measures does not guarantee that the underlying physiological problem has been fully resolved.
  30. The correct answer is A (Cold blood returning from the extremities to the core can further lower core temperature and worsen cardiac instability): As peripheral vessels dilate during rewarming, cold, poorly circulated blood from the extremities returns to the core, and this afterdrop phenomenon can transiently lower core temperature further and worsen an already unstable cardiac rhythm. Recognizing this risk is why rewarming techniques and monitoring must be applied carefully rather than aggressively in the field. Afterdrop is not a beneficial process and carries no protective effect, and it is a recognized risk during rewarming generally rather than being limited only to patients who are actively exercising.
  31. The correct answer is C (Refreezing thawed tissue causes significantly greater tissue damage than leaving it frozen until definitive care): Once frostbitten tissue has been thawed, refreezing causes dramatically more severe cellular and vascular damage than if the tissue had simply remained frozen throughout transport, making field rewarming inappropriate whenever refreezing is a realistic possibility before reaching a controlled hospital environment. This is why paramedics generally protect and insulate frostbitten tissue rather than actively rewarming it in the field under uncertain conditions. Frostbitten tissue absolutely can sustain additional, often much worse, damage from this thaw refreeze cycle.
  32. The correct answer is B (A diver found unresponsive in shallow water after apparently diving headfirst into it): A headfirst dive into water of unknown or shallow depth carries a significant risk of axial loading injury to the cervical spine, making this scenario the one requiring the highest suspicion for an associated spinal injury among the options presented. A gradually submerging fatigued swimmer, a toddler in a bathtub, or an adult who fainted while standing in shallow water do not carry the same mechanism of injury risk. Recognizing this specific high risk mechanism guides the crew's decision to apply spinal precautions during rescue and assessment.
  33. The correct answer is B (Nitrogen narcosis occurs at depth from breathing compressed nitrogen, while decompression sickness develops after ascent from bubble formation): Nitrogen narcosis results from the direct effect of breathing compressed nitrogen at depth and typically resolves quickly with ascent to shallower water, while decompression sickness develops after surfacing as dissolved nitrogen forms bubbles in tissues and blood, often over minutes to hours. These represent two distinct mechanisms with different timing, causes, and management implications despite both being nitrogen related diving conditions. Confusing the two could lead to inappropriate reassurance or delayed recognition of a genuinely developing decompression sickness case.
  34. The correct answer is C (Immediate descent to a lower altitude along with supplemental oxygen if available): Descent to a lower altitude is the single most effective treatment for worsening altitude illness, since it directly reverses the low oxygen environment driving the underlying pathophysiology, and supplemental oxygen provides additional benefit when available during descent or while arranging evacuation. Continuing to ascend or remaining at the same altitude while waiting for symptoms to resolve allows the underlying condition to worsen and become more dangerous. Medications such as acetaminophen do not address the underlying cause and should never substitute for descent when significant symptoms are present.
  35. The correct answer is D (Remove contaminated clothing and irrigate the area with copious amounts of water): Removing contaminated clothing and irrigating a chemical burn with copious amounts of water dilutes and physically removes the offending substance, limiting ongoing tissue damage and is the standard prehospital approach for most chemical burns. Attempting to apply a neutralizing agent can generate additional heat through an exothermic reaction and often worsens the injury rather than helping it. Covering the burn without irrigation, or waiting until hospital arrival to begin decontamination, allows the chemical to continue causing damage during the entire transport time.
  36. The correct answer is A (Stage at a safe distance until law enforcement confirms the scene is secure before entering): Scene safety must be confirmed before paramedics enter an unsecured scene involving a weapon, since entering prematurely risks the crew becoming additional casualties and being unable to help anyone, including the original patient. Staging at a safe distance until law enforcement confirms the scene is secure is standard practice for any potentially violent scene. Relying on a patient's own account of the shooter's whereabouts is unreliable and does not substitute for confirmation from law enforcement who have actually assessed and secured the area.
Case C0811

Your crew is dispatched Echo priority to a downtown transit station for a 34-year-old male described by bystanders as pacing and shouting incoherently. Police are already on scene and report the patient is not armed but will not sit still. On approach he is diaphoretic, talking rapidly, and will not make eye contact. Vital signs obtained after he allows a brief assessment: HR 118, RR 22, BP 148/92, SpO2 97% on room air, temperature 38.1C, GCS 15 but disoriented to time.

  1. The correct answer is A (Confirm the scene is secured by police with no weapons or threats present before approaching): scene safety always precedes patient contact, especially with an agitated, unpredictable patient in a public space. Paramedics rely on police confirmation that the environment and the patient himself pose no immediate weapon or violence risk before approaching. Rushing in from behind or allowing bystanders to physically restrain the patient increases the risk of injury to everyone involved and can escalate the crisis. Only once the scene is confirmed safe should the crew begin a calm, non-threatening approach.
  2. The correct answer is D (Step back to increase personal space, lower voice volume, and slow the questioning): de-escalation works by reducing the sensory and social pressure driving the patient's arousal, not by adding to it. Increasing distance, softening tone, and slowing speech signal non-threat and give the patient room to regain a sense of control. Raising volume, closing distance, and forcing eye contact are provocative behaviours that tend to intensify agitation. Restraint should remain a last resort reserved for situations where verbal de-escalation has genuinely failed and safety is at immediate risk.
  3. The correct answer is B (The elevated temperature of 38.1C combined with rapid, disorganized speech): fever accompanying acute agitation is a classic red flag for an organic cause such as infection, sympathomimetic toxicity, or serotonin syndrome rather than a purely primary psychiatric event. Primary psychiatric decompensation typically does not produce objective vital sign abnormalities like fever. Refusing eye contact, being in public, and police presence are behavioural or situational observations, not physiological clues. Any new agitated patient with abnormal vitals should be treated as a possible medical emergency until proven otherwise.
  4. The correct answer is C (Continuously monitor airway, vitals, and level of consciousness as a possible medical emergency): given the fever, tachycardia, and disorientation, this patient needs ongoing physiological monitoring rather than being managed as a straightforward behavioural call. Continuous reassessment allows the crew to detect deterioration such as worsening level of consciousness or developing hypotension. Letting an altered patient walk unmonitored or skipping reassessment risks missing a rapidly evolving medical crisis. Complete documentation of both the behavioural and physiological findings is essential for safe handover.
  5. The correct answer is A (Neurogenic shock from disrupted sympathetic tone below the injury level): a high spinal cord injury interrupts sympathetic signals that normally maintain vascular tone and heart rate, producing widespread vasodilation, hypotension, and a paradoxical bradycardia rather than the tachycardia seen in most other shock states. The resulting vasodilation also keeps skin warm and dry instead of the cool, clammy skin typical of hypovolemic shock. Hypovolemic and cardiogenic shock both typically present with tachycardia and poor perfusion, not bradycardia. Septic shock would also usually present with tachycardia and often fever, which is absent here.
  6. The correct answer is B (1, 3 and 5): early hypovolemic shock produces compensatory vasoconstriction, giving cool, pale, clammy skin, a narrowing pulse pressure as diastolic pressure rises to maintain perfusion, and a rising heart rate with a weak, thready pulse as the heart tries to maintain output with less circulating volume. Warm, flushed skin with bounding pulses describes early distributive shock, where vasodilation dominates. Bradycardia with warm, dry skin describes neurogenic shock, not hypovolemic shock. Recognizing these differing skin and pulse patterns helps the paramedic distinguish shock types in the field.
  7. The correct answer is D (Cardiogenic shock from pump failure): distended neck veins, bilateral pulmonary crackles, and hypotension after a recent myocardial infarction point to the heart itself failing as a pump, causing blood to back up into the lungs and systemic venous system. Hypovolemic shock would typically show flat neck veins rather than distention, since circulating volume is low rather than backed up. An allergic reaction would more likely present with hives, wheeze, and warm skin, and a pneumothorax would present with unilateral absent breath sounds rather than bilateral crackles. Cardiogenic shock requires careful management to avoid worsening pulmonary congestion with excessive fluid.
  8. The correct answer is C (Tension pneumothorax causing mediastinal shift): trapped air under increasing pressure in the pleural space collapses the lung, pushes the mediastinum toward the opposite side, and kinks the great vessels, obstructing venous return. This produces the classic combination of absent unilateral breath sounds, tracheal deviation away from the affected side, and distended neck veins. A haemothorax would more likely cause hypotension from blood loss without tracheal deviation, and cardiac tamponade produces muffled heart sounds rather than unilateral absent breath sounds. Rapid recognition is critical because tension pneumothorax can progress quickly to cardiac arrest.
  9. The correct answer is A (1, 2, 3 and 5): early compensated shock is driven by a surge of catecholamines that raises heart rate and respiratory rate while diverting blood flow away from the skin and gut toward the brain, heart, and lungs through peripheral vasoconstriction. Systolic blood pressure is typically maintained in this compensated phase and is not the first sign of trouble, since the body works hard to preserve it until compensation fails. A falling systolic pressure is actually a late finding signalling decompensation, not an early compensatory response. Recognizing tachycardia and vasoconstriction before blood pressure drops allows paramedics to identify shock earlier.
  10. The correct answer is D (Radiation, to determine whether pain spreads elsewhere): asking whether pain moves or extends to another body area, such as the arm or jaw, is specifically assessing radiation within the OPQRST framework. Onset addresses timing, provocation addresses aggravating or relieving factors, and quality addresses whether the pain is sharp, dull, crushing, or burning. Radiation is clinically significant because pain radiating to the jaw or left arm is a classic feature suggesting cardiac ischemia. A complete OPQRST assessment helps paramedics build an accurate differential for the chief complaint.
  11. The correct answer is B (Access peer support or a critical incident stress program and seek help if symptoms persist): paramedics routinely encounter traumatic calls, and services provide structured peer support and critical incident stress management resources specifically to help process these reactions in a healthy way. Avoiding all discussion or masking symptoms with stimulants does not resolve the underlying stress response and can allow symptoms to worsen. Persistent anxiety and sleep disturbance after a traumatic call are signals that professional support may be needed, not something to push through silently. Recognizing and addressing occupational stress protects both the paramedic's wellbeing and their long-term ability to safely care for patients.
  12. The correct answer is C (1, 3 and 4): effective de-escalation relies on a calm tone, offering the patient meaningful choices to rebuild a sense of autonomy, and maintaining an open, non-confrontational posture with an accessible exit for both parties. Blocking a patient's path can feel threatening and increase the risk of a defensive or aggressive reaction. Frequently interrupting an agitated person tends to increase frustration rather than calm the situation, since it signals the person is not being heard. Combining verbal calmness with safe positioning gives the paramedic the best chance of resolving the crisis without escalation.
  13. The correct answer is D (Whether the patient currently has capacity to understand the risks of refusing care): a valid refusal of treatment requires that the patient understand the nature of their condition and the consequences of refusing help, and active suicidal ideation is a strong indicator that capacity may be impaired. Being a familiar caller or having mobile family members present does not establish capacity to make this specific decision. Physical mobility, such as being able to walk, is unrelated to decision-making capacity. When capacity is in doubt and safety is at risk, paramedics should involve police and follow local protocols for apprehension rather than simply accepting the refusal.
  14. The correct answer is A (Hyperkalemia from renal failure): peaked T waves progressing to a widening QRS complex are a classic electrocardiographic signature of dangerously elevated serum potassium, which accumulates when the kidneys can no longer excrete it, as in a missed dialysis session. Hyponatremia typically produces confusion and seizures rather than these specific ECG changes. Hypocalcemia and hypoglycemia can each cause weakness or altered mentation but do not produce this peaked T wave and widening QRS pattern. Recognizing hyperkalemia in a dialysis patient is important because it can progress rapidly to lethal arrhythmias.
  15. The correct answer is D (1, 3 and 4): dry mucous membranes, decreased skin turgor, and sunken eyes are classic clinical signs of significant fluid volume deficit, particularly in an elderly patient who has been unable to take in fluids. Bounding, full pulses and elevated blood pressure are inconsistent with dehydration, which more typically produces weak, thready pulses and low or normal blood pressure as compensatory mechanisms are exhausted. Recognizing dehydration early allows paramedics to anticipate the risk of progression to hypovolemic shock, particularly in older patients with reduced physiological reserve.
  16. The correct answer is D (Chemotherapy-induced neutropenia can allow infection to progress to sepsis rapidly): chemotherapy frequently suppresses bone marrow production of neutrophils, the white blood cells that normally contain early infection, so even a modest fever in this population can represent a serious infection capable of progressing to septic shock within hours. This is considered an oncologic emergency requiring prompt transport and early hospital notification. The other options make claims that are not clinically accurate, since capacity and legal refusal rules are unchanged by chemotherapy status and fever does not automatically mean treatment failure. Recognizing neutropenic fever as high-risk changes the urgency of the paramedic's response even when the patient otherwise appears well.
  17. The correct answer is B (A possible infection of the vascular access device needing urgent evaluation): localized redness, warmth, and tenderness at an implanted port site combined with fever suggests a device-related infection, which can seed bacteria directly into the bloodstream and progress quickly to sepsis in an immunocompromised oncology patient. This is not an expected or benign reaction and should never be dismissed as simple irritation from a dressing. A purely mechanical malfunction would not typically produce fever or localized inflammation. Prompt recognition and communication of these findings to the receiving hospital is important given the elevated infection risk in cancer patients.
  18. The correct answer is D (1, 3 and 4): varicella spreads both by airborne transmission of small respiratory particles and by direct contact with the characteristic vesicular rash, so it requires airborne precautions with a fitted N95 or equivalent respirator in addition to contact precautions for the skin lesions, layered on top of standard precautions used for every patient. A surgical mask alone is insufficient because it does not filter the small airborne particles capable of transmitting varicella over distance. Droplet precautions alone would underestimate the transmission risk of this particular illness. Correct precaution selection protects both the paramedic and other patients from exposure.
  19. The correct answer is C (Wash the site thoroughly, report the exposure immediately, and follow post-exposure protocol): a needle stick from a source patient of unknown status requires immediate wound care and prompt reporting so that post-exposure risk assessment and any indicated prophylaxis can begin as soon as possible, since effectiveness of some interventions decreases with delay. Continuing the call without reporting or waiting until the end of the shift risks losing valuable time for early intervention. Disposing of the needle without reporting the injury ignores the paramedic's own health and the service's legal reporting obligations. Prompt, transparent reporting protects the paramedic and supports appropriate follow-up testing and counselling.
Case C0830

You are dispatched to a private residence for a 9-year-old girl who developed sudden difficulty breathing shortly after eating at a birthday party. The mother reports a known peanut allergy and possible contaminated snack. The child has widespread hives, facial and lip swelling, and audible wheezing. Vitals: HR 138, RR 32 with audible wheeze and mild stridor, BP 84/50, SpO2 91% on room air. The mother has an epinephrine auto-injector prescribed for the child.

  1.
