The correct answer is B (Skin involvement together with respiratory compromise and hypotension across two or more systems): anaphylaxis is defined by rapid-onset involvement of two or more body systems after likely allergen exposure, and this child clearly demonstrates skin findings, respiratory compromise from wheeze and stridor, and circulatory compromise from her low blood pressure. Hives alone, or facial swelling alone, without other system involvement, would be classified as a less severe allergic reaction rather than full anaphylaxis. A known allergy history alone, without current symptoms, does not meet diagnostic criteria. Recognizing multi-system involvement is essential because it determines the urgency of epinephrine administration.
  2. The correct answer is D (Administer intramuscular epinephrine promptly and support her airway and breathing): epinephrine is the first-line, life-saving treatment for anaphylaxis, working to reverse airway swelling, bronchospasm, and vasodilation causing hypotension, and delay significantly increases the risk of respiratory or cardiovascular collapse. Oral antihistamines act far too slowly to address this rapidly progressing emergency and should never be used as the sole initial treatment. Prior receipt of epinephrine is not a prerequisite for administering it now, and delaying treatment until hospital arrival risks the child deteriorating en route. Paramedics should be prepared to support airway and ventilation throughout transport in case symptoms do not fully resolve.
  3. The correct answer is A (A biphasic reaction can occur, with symptoms recurring hours later): a meaningful proportion of anaphylaxis cases experience a second wave of symptoms after initial improvement, sometimes hours later, so ongoing hospital observation is required even when the child appears to be recovering well in the field. Symptom improvement after epinephrine does not mean the underlying allergic process is fully resolved or that further monitoring is unnecessary. It also does not retrospectively change the diagnosis, since the initial multi-system presentation already met criteria for anaphylaxis. Continued transport and observation protect against a potentially dangerous recurrence.
  4. The correct answer is A (Beck's triad, suggesting cardiac tamponade): muffled heart sounds, jugular venous distention, and hypotension together form Beck's triad, which classically indicates blood or fluid accumulating in the pericardial sac and compressing the heart, preventing it from filling adequately. Cushing's triad instead describes hypertension, bradycardia, and irregular respirations associated with rising intracranial pressure, an unrelated condition. Virchow's triad describes risk factors for clot formation rather than an acute physical exam finding. Recognizing Beck's triad in a penetrating chest trauma patient should prompt rapid transport, since tamponade can progress to obstructive shock and cardiac arrest.
  5. The correct answer is B (1, 2, 4 and 5): recent surgery and immobility are well-established risk factors for deep vein thrombosis and subsequent pulmonary embolism, and a sufficiently large embolism can mechanically obstruct pulmonary blood flow, producing obstructive shock through reduced cardiac output. The combination of sudden pleuritic chest pain and tachycardia in this clinical context should raise strong suspicion for pulmonary embolism, and prehospital management appropriately focuses on supportive care such as oxygen and rapid transport rather than delaying for field diagnosis. Statement 3 is incorrect, since this presentation is very much consistent with an obstructive shock process. Recognizing this pattern helps paramedics prioritize rapid transport for a time-critical condition.

Practice Test 6 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Progression from warm to cold septic shock as compensation fails): early septic shock often presents with warm, flushed skin due to widespread vasodilation, but as the condition worsens and compensatory mechanisms fail, blood is shunted away from the periphery, producing cool, mottled skin, weak pulses, and worsening hypotension. This transition is a sign of clinical deterioration, not resolution or improvement, and indicates the patient is moving toward decompensated shock. It is not explained by a separate, unrelated cardiac event given the clear infectious context. Recognizing this progression should prompt aggressive supportive care and urgent transport.
  2. The correct answer is A (Treat this as anaphylaxis and prepare to administer epinephrine promptly): a first-time exposure can absolutely trigger a severe, life-threatening allergic reaction, and airway symptoms such as throat tightness and tongue swelling are high-risk features that warrant prompt epinephrine regardless of whether the patient has reacted before. Withholding treatment because there is no prior history ignores that anaphylaxis can occur on any exposure once sensitization has happened. Dismissing airway symptoms as anxiety is dangerous, since airway compromise can progress rapidly to obstruction. Skin findings like hives are not required for a diagnosis of anaphylaxis when airway or respiratory symptoms are already present.
  3. The correct answer is C (2 and 4): airway compromise such as stridor and difficulty swallowing, and circulatory compromise such as hypotension with dizziness following allergen exposure, both represent life-threatening features of anaphylaxis that warrant prompt epinephrine administration. Isolated mild itching, hives without other system involvement, or a localized reaction at a sting site are generally managed as milder allergic reactions and do not on their own meet the threshold for epinephrine. Distinguishing mild, localized reactions from true multi-system anaphylaxis is essential for appropriate and timely treatment decisions in the field.
  4. The correct answer is D (Properly splint and immobilize the limb to reduce movement at the injury): effective splinting reduces pain by limiting motion of the fractured bone ends and surrounding soft tissue, which is one of the most important non-pharmacological pain management steps a paramedic can provide. Encouraging movement of an injured limb would worsen pain and risk further tissue or vascular injury. Leaving the limb unsupported or delaying splinting allows ongoing movement and pain rather than reducing it. Good splinting technique complements any pharmacological pain management and improves patient comfort throughout transport.
  5. The correct answer is B (Check the patient's blood glucose level to rule out hypoglycemia): agitation, combativeness, diaphoresis, and slurred speech in a known diabetic patient are classic signs of hypoglycemia, a rapidly reversible and potentially life-threatening cause of altered behaviour that must be ruled out before attributing symptoms to a psychiatric cause. Family assumptions, even from those who know the patient well, should never replace an objective assessment of a treatable medical condition. Requesting restraint or transporting without further assessment both skip an essential and simple diagnostic step. Identifying and correcting hypoglycemia can resolve the entire presentation without any need for behavioural management.
  6. The correct answer is A (1, 2 and 4): finding 1 reflects early compensated shock, where tachycardia and mild anxiety appear while blood pressure is still preserved through compensatory mechanisms; finding 2 reflects progressing shock, where blood pressure begins to fall and mentation deteriorates as compensation weakens; and finding 4 reflects severe decompensated shock, with a very high heart rate, unresponsiveness, and barely obtainable blood pressure indicating the body can no longer compensate. Findings 3 and 5 describe normal or near-normal states rather than points along a worsening shock progression. Recognizing this progression allows paramedics to identify deterioration before a patient reaches the most severe stage.
  7. The correct answer is D (Establish large-bore access and administer isotonic crystalloid while reassessing the response): standard prehospital management of hypovolemic shock involves gaining reliable large-bore access and administering an isotonic crystalloid solution, with ongoing reassessment guiding how much fluid is appropriate for that specific patient. Uncontrolled, unlimited fluid administration can dilute clotting factors and worsen bleeding in trauma patients, so fluid is titrated rather than given without limit. Avoiding IV access entirely would leave the patient without a route for fluid or medication administration if needed. Hypertonic saline is not the standard first-line fluid for most hypovolemic shock scenarios in this setting.
  8. The correct answer is C (Hyponatremia from water intoxication): excessive intake of plain water without adequate electrolyte replacement during prolonged exertion can dilute serum sodium to dangerously low levels, producing cerebral edema that presents as confusion, headache, and seizures. Hyperkalemia typically presents with cardiac and neuromuscular findings rather than this specific neurological picture, and hypercalcemia and hypomagnesemia are less consistent with this endurance-event context. Recognizing exertion-associated hyponatremia is important because further plain water intake would worsen rather than help the condition. Prompt hospital transport is needed for definitive electrolyte correction.
  9. The correct answer is B (1, 3 and 5): tuberculosis, measles, and chickenpox are all transmitted through small airborne particles that can remain suspended and travel over distance, requiring airborne precautions with a fitted N95 or equivalent respirator. Seasonal influenza is generally managed with droplet precautions because it spreads through larger respiratory droplets over shorter distances. A localized skin infection without respiratory involvement would typically call for contact precautions rather than airborne precautions. Correctly matching the precaution type to the specific disease is essential to prevent unnecessary or insufficient protective measures.
  10. The correct answer is A (Record the reason for restraint, the method used, and ongoing circulation and breathing checks): restraint is a significant intervention with real risks, so thorough documentation of the justification, the method applied, and ongoing monitoring of the patient's airway, breathing, and circulation while restrained is essential for both patient safety and legal accountability. Failing to document at all leaves no record justifying this restrictive intervention. Recording only the start time omits critical safety monitoring information. Documentation should be completed promptly, not delayed for days, to ensure accuracy and continuity of care at handover.
Case C0845

Your crew responds to a long-term care facility for a 78-year-old woman with decreased level of consciousness. Staff report a fever and reduced oral intake for two days, with a suspected urinary tract infection. She is found in bed, initially warm and flushed to the touch, difficult to rouse, and confused when she does respond. Vitals: HR 128, RR 26, BP 78/44, SpO2 93% on room air, temperature 39.4C.

  1. The correct answer is D (Widespread vasodilation from an overwhelming systemic inflammatory response): in early septic shock, the body's inflammatory response to overwhelming infection causes widespread vasodilation, which drops systemic vascular resistance and blood pressure while producing warm, flushed skin rather than the cool, clammy skin seen in hypovolemic shock. This is a systemic process, not a localized change confined to the urinary tract. There is no indication here of direct cardiac muscle injury or a mechanical obstruction to venous return, both of which would produce a different clinical picture. Recognizing this warm, vasodilated phase helps distinguish septic shock from other shock types early in its course.
  2. The correct answer is B (Support airway and breathing, give oxygen, position appropriately, and arrange rapid transport with early notification): septic shock management in the field centres on supportive care and rapid transport, since definitive treatment such as antibiotics and vasopressor support requires hospital resources. Even with a saturation above 90 percent, this hypotensive, confused patient benefits from supplemental oxygen to support tissue oxygenation while compensatory mechanisms are strained. Paramedics do not carry antibiotics to treat the underlying infection directly in the field. Early hospital notification allows the receiving team to prepare for a time-critical sepsis presentation.
  3. The correct answer is C (Signs of progression to the cold, decompensated phase such as worsening hypotension): septic shock can rapidly progress from a warm, vasodilated phase to a cold, decompensated phase as compensatory mechanisms fail, so ongoing vigilance for worsening hypotension, cool extremities, and declining mental status is essential throughout transport. Assuming improvement without reassessment risks missing rapid deterioration in an unstable patient. A return to warm skin alone would not reliably indicate clinical improvement without corresponding changes in blood pressure and mentation, and fever resolution alone does not capture the full picture of perfusion status. Continuous reassessment allows the crew to detect and respond to deterioration promptly.
  4. The correct answer is A (Reduced peripheral perfusion consistent with early shock): delayed capillary refill reflects reduced blood flow to the peripheral tissues, often an early compensatory response as the body shunts blood toward vital organs during developing shock. This is not a normal or expected finding and should prompt further assessment of vital signs and perfusion status rather than being dismissed. It does not indicate improved cardiac output, since delayed refill reflects the opposite, reduced peripheral flow. Capillary refill is a simple, rapid bedside tool that helps paramedics detect early perfusion problems even before blood pressure changes are apparent.
  5. The correct answer is D (1, 2 and 3): advanced age, residence in a long-term care facility, and recent invasive devices such as a urinary catheter are all well-recognized risk factors that increase susceptibility to infection and subsequent progression to sepsis, particularly in patients with reduced physiological reserve. Regular exercise with no chronic illness and a history of well-controlled seasonal allergies are not recognized sepsis risk factors and instead reflect a generally healthy baseline. Recognizing these risk factors helps paramedics maintain a higher index of suspicion for sepsis in vulnerable patient populations.
  6. The correct answer is C (Tumor lysis syndrome causing dangerous electrolyte shifts): when chemotherapy rapidly destroys a large number of tumor cells, intracellular contents including potassium and phosphate are released into the bloodstream faster than the kidneys can clear them, producing dangerous electrolyte imbalances that can cause muscle cramps, nausea, and arrhythmias. This is not a benign or expected side effect and should be treated as a potential oncologic emergency requiring prompt transport and cardiac monitoring. The timing shortly after starting chemotherapy for a large tumor burden makes an unrelated viral illness or a purely coincidental arrhythmia less likely explanations. Recognizing tumor lysis syndrome helps paramedics appreciate the urgency of new cardiac irregularities in this population.
  7. The correct answer is B (Contact precautions including gloves and a gown for direct contact): organisms spread primarily through direct contact with the patient's skin or contaminated surfaces, such as many resistant bacteria, require contact precautions using gloves and a gown to prevent transfer to the paramedic or subsequent patients and equipment. Airborne and droplet precautions target respiratory transmission routes, which are not the primary concern for this contact-spread organism. Relying on a surgical mask alone would not address the actual route of transmission for this infection. Selecting the correct precaution category based on the organism's transmission route is essential for effective infection control.
  8. The correct answer is A (1, 3 and 5): common recognized triggers for anaphylaxis include foods, insect stings, and medications, though a proportion of cases occur without any identifiable trigger, and epinephrine administration should always follow the applicable local medical directive for dosing and route. Statement 2 is false, since biphasic reactions can occur even after a patient improves following epinephrine, which is exactly why ongoing monitoring matters. Statement 4 is also false, since transport remains important even after apparent field resolution due to the real risk of a delayed second reaction. Understanding these facts together supports safe, complete management of anaphylaxis rather than premature closure of the call.
  9. The correct answer is D (Hypovolemic shock secondary to gastrointestinal blood loss): coffee-ground emesis indicates upper gastrointestinal bleeding, and significant blood loss reduces circulating volume, producing the pale, diaphoretic, and lightheaded presentation described, particularly when symptoms worsen with positional change. There is no indication of a spinal injury, cardiac event, or embolism in this presentation to support neurogenic, cardiogenic, or obstructive shock instead. Recognizing gastrointestinal bleeding as a hypovolemic shock cause is important because it may not be as visually obvious as external trauma bleeding. Rapid transport and supportive care are appropriate while monitoring for further deterioration.
  10. The correct answer is C (The hypotension is from lost sympathetic tone, not volume loss, so excess fluid risks overload): in neurogenic shock, the underlying problem is loss of vascular tone from disrupted sympathetic signalling rather than a true deficit in circulating blood volume, so aggressive fluid resuscitation as used in hypovolemic shock can lead to fluid overload without correcting the underlying vasodilation. This patient's presentation does not indicate large-volume blood loss requiring aggressive fluids, unlike true hypovolemic shock. Fluids are not universally contraindicated in trauma, but the approach must match the actual underlying shock mechanism. Warm skin in this context reflects the pathophysiology of neurogenic shock and does not mean the patient is stable or requires no further treatment; keeping the patient warm and monitoring closely remain important.
  11. The correct answer is A (1, 2 and 4): matching the pain assessment tool to the patient is important, using a verbal numeric scale for cooperative, oriented adults, a behavioural scale observing facial expression and movement for nonverbal or cognitively impaired patients, and a pediatric-specific faces scale to help young children express pain intensity in an age-appropriate way. Statement 3 is false, since pain can still be assessed in nonverbal patients through behavioural observation rather than being considered unassessable. Statement 5 is also false, because vital signs can be influenced by many factors besides pain and should not be treated as a fully reliable stand-alone substitute for a proper pain assessment tool. Using the correct tool improves the accuracy of pain assessment across diverse patient populations.
  12. The correct answer is B (Recent prolonged immobility, sudden pleuritic chest pain, and unexplained tachycardia): prolonged immobility is a major risk factor for clot formation, and the sudden onset of pleuritic chest pain together with unexplained tachycardia is a classic combination suggesting pulmonary embolism, which can obstruct pulmonary blood flow severely enough to cause obstructive shock. A gradual asthma exacerbation or a slow, progressive presentation over weeks fits a different, less acute clinical pattern. While anxiety can produce some overlapping symptoms, it does not explain this specific combination of risk factors and objective findings. Recognizing this pattern supports timely, high-priority transport for a potentially life-threatening condition.
  13. The correct answer is D (Ensure ongoing direct observation and involve additional resources per local protocol): a disclosure of active suicidal ideation with a specific plan is a high-risk finding requiring continuous direct observation to prevent self-harm, along with involvement of police or other resources as directed by local protocol. Changing the subject or leaving the patient alone, even briefly, removes the safety oversight this situation requires and could allow an opportunity for self-harm. Simply documenting the disclosure without altering the management plan fails to respond appropriately to an identified immediate risk. Taking this disclosure seriously and acting on it is essential to patient safety.
  14. The correct answer is C (1, 3 and 4): routine precautions applied to every patient include consistent hand hygiene before and after contact, treating all body fluids as potentially infectious regardless of how healthy the patient appears, and prompt, safe disposal of sharps in puncture-resistant containers. Gloves should be used for any anticipated contact with body fluids, mucous membranes, or non-intact skin, not only when blood is visibly present. Basing protective equipment decisions purely on a patient's outward appearance is unreliable, since many infectious conditions produce no visible signs. Consistent application of routine precautions on every call protects both paramedics and patients regardless of known infection status.
  15. The correct answer is A (The failing heart cannot handle added volume, risking worse pulmonary edema): in cardiogenic shock, the heart's pumping ability is impaired, so additional intravenous fluid can back up into the lungs and worsen the pulmonary edema already causing the crackles heard on auscultation, rather than improving perfusion. Fluid administration does not cure the underlying pump failure and can be actively harmful in this setting, unlike in hypovolemic shock. This patient still has circulating volume; the problem is the heart's ability to move it effectively, not an absolute volume deficit. Bilateral crackles in this context reflect fluid backing up from heart failure, not dehydration, and should guide the paramedic toward cautious, judicious fluid use.
  16. The correct answer is B (Continuously reassessing perfusion, mental status, skin signs, and vital sign trends guides timely management): regardless of the underlying cause, shock represents inadequate tissue perfusion, and ongoing reassessment of mental status, skin colour and temperature, and trends in vital signs allows paramedics to detect deterioration or improvement over time rather than relying on a single snapshot. Blood pressure alone can remain deceptively normal during early compensated shock, making it an incomplete measure on its own. Skipping reassessment during transport risks missing important changes in the patient's condition. Definitive identification of the exact cause is often not possible or necessary in the field, since supportive management and rapid transport remain priorities regardless of the specific underlying shock type.
  17. The correct answer is C (Maintain a calm, non-threatening posture and speak slowly at a normal volume): a calm approach with open body language, adequate personal space, and unhurried speech reduces the perceived threat and helps de-escalate an agitated patient. Approaching from behind (A) increases startle and perceived threat. A firm commanding tone (B) can escalate fear and agitation in a person experiencing psychosis. Immediate restraint (D) should be reserved for situations where less restrictive measures have failed and there is imminent danger, not as a first response.
  18. The correct answer is A (A verbal numeric rating scale from zero to ten): a verbal patient who can communicate clearly should self-report pain using a standard numeric scale, which is reliable and allows comparison over time. The FACES scale (B) is intended for young children or patients with communication or cognitive barriers, not a clearly communicating adult. Relying solely on behavioural cues (C) is appropriate for nonverbal patients but is less accurate when self-report is possible. A family member's estimate (D) is a proxy measure and should not replace the patient's own report when she is capable of providing one.
  19. The correct answer is D (Tall, peaked T waves with a a normal, narrow QRS complex.): early hyperkalemia classically produces tall, peaked T waves, and as potassium rises further the QRS widens rather than narrows, progressing toward a sine-wave pattern if untreated. Flattened T waves and U waves (B) are more typical of hypokalemia. A shortened QT with diffuse ST elevation (C) is not a recognized hyperkalemia pattern. Delta waves (A) are associated with Wolff-Parkinson-White syndrome, an unrelated conduction abnormality.
  20. The correct answer is B (Neurogenic shock from disrupted sympathetic nervous system outflow): spinal cord injury can interrupt sympathetic tone below the level of injury, causing vasodilation, warm flushed skin, hypotension, and a paradoxical bradycardia instead of the expected tachycardic compensation. Hypovolemic shock (A) alone would typically produce tachycardia and cool, pale skin, not warmth and bradycardia. Cardiogenic shock (C) would present with signs of pump failure such as pulmonary congestion, not flushed skin. Septic shock (D) is unsupported by this acute traumatic mechanism and timeline.
  21. The correct answer is C (Intramuscular injection into the anterolateral thigh muscle): the anterolateral thigh has a large muscle mass with good blood supply, giving epinephrine faster and more reliable absorption than other intramuscular sites during anaphylaxis. Subcutaneous injection (A) results in slower, less predictable absorption than the intramuscular route. Rapid intravenous push (B) is not standard practice at this level of care and risks dangerous cardiac dysrhythmias. Sublingual administration (D) is not an approved or effective route for epinephrine in anaphylaxis.
  22. The correct answer is A (1, 3 and 5): cardiogenic shock from pump failure produces hypotension with a narrow pulse pressure, pulmonary crackles from backward congestion, and a compensatory tachycardia with weak thready pulses reflecting poor forward flow. Jugular venous distension with clear lungs (statement 2) suggests right-sided or obstructive causes rather than left-sided pump failure with pulmonary congestion. Bounding pulses with warm extremities (statement 4) reflect vasodilation typical of distributive shock, the opposite of the cold, poorly perfused presentation seen in cardiogenic shock.
  23. The correct answer is D (2, 3 and 5): active listening, maintaining safe distance with calm body language, and involving a calm trusted support person are all recognized de-escalation strategies that build rapport and reduce agitation. Positioning yourself between the patient and the exit (statement 1) blocks her egress and can feel confrontational, increasing rather than decreasing perceived threat. Speaking over her (statement 4) interrupts and dismisses what she is communicating, which typically escalates rather than calms an agitated patient.
  24. The correct answer is B (1, 2, 4 and 5): early septic shock is a distributive process, so warm flushed skin with bounding pulses, fever with chills, a widened pulse pressure from vasodilation, and a recent worsening infection together fit the classic warm phase of sepsis. Cool, mottled skin from the earliest onset (statement 3) is more typical of late or decompensated shock, or of hypovolemic and cardiogenic shock where vasoconstriction dominates from the start rather than vasodilation.
  25. The correct answer is A (1, 3 and 4): suspected active tuberculosis requires airborne precautions, meaning the paramedic wears a fit-tested N95 respirator rather than a surgical mask, a mask is placed on the patient if tolerated to reduce droplet spread, and ventilation is improved where possible to dilute airborne particles. A standard surgical mask on the paramedic alone (statement 2) does not filter the small airborne particles that spread tuberculosis. Relying on hand hygiene alone (statement 5) does not address respiratory transmission and is insufficient protection against an airborne pathogen.
  26. The correct answer is D (1, 2 and 5): a tension pneumothorax causes air to accumulate under pressure in the pleural space, producing tracheal deviation away from the affected side, jugular venous distension from impaired venous return, and hyperresonance over the affected hemithorax. Muffled heart sounds with distant weak pulses (statement 4) are classically associated with cardiac tamponade, a different obstructive cause. Bilateral equal breath sounds with a normal chest wall (statement 3) contradicts the stated absent breath sounds on the affected side and is inconsistent with this presentation.
  27. The correct answer is D (1, 3 and 4): chemotherapy can cause neutropenia that allows infections to progress rapidly, so the port site should be assessed for signs of local infection and the risk of catheter-related bloodstream infection must be considered. Treating fever as low priority (statement 2) is dangerous, since fever in a neutropenic patient is a medical emergency requiring urgent transport, not the reverse. Statement 5 is incorrect for the same reason: oncology patients with fever or signs of infection often need expedited, not routine, transport.
  28. The correct answer is B (1, 2 and 4): prolonged vomiting and diarrhea cause volume and electrolyte losses, producing decreased skin turgor, muscle cramps and weakness from electrolyte imbalance, and orthostatic hypotension as the body struggles to compensate for reduced circulating volume. Bounding pulses with elevated blood pressure (statement 3) and warm, well-perfused skin with a normal heart rate (statement 5) do not fit a picture of significant volume depletion and would be atypical findings in this presentation.
  29. The correct answer is C (Compensated shock with blood pressure still being maintained): in compensated shock the body's reflexes, such as tachycardia and vasoconstriction, maintain blood pressure despite ongoing volume loss, though early signs like anxiety and tachycardia are present. Decompensated shock (A) is marked by falling blood pressure and weakening pulses, which have not yet occurred here. Irreversible shock (B) involves established organ failure that this early presentation does not show. Refractory shock (D) describes a later stage that fails to respond to treatment, not an untreated early presentation.
  30. The correct answer is D (Airborne illness requires a fit-tested N95 respirator; droplet illness typically requires a surgical mask): airborne pathogens like measles travel on tiny particles that remain suspended in air and require N95-level filtration, while droplet pathogens like pertussis are spread by larger respiratory droplets over shorter distances and are adequately blocked by a surgical mask. Option B understates the protection needed for measles. Option C reverses the correct precautions for the two illnesses. Option A ignores transmission risk even before symptoms are confirmed absent.
Case C0875

Paramedics are called to a long-term care home for a 82-year-old woman who has become increasingly confused over the past 12 hours. Staff report she was treated for a urinary tract infection five days ago but her symptoms have worsened. On assessment she is febrile at 39.2 degrees Celsius, her skin is warm and flushed, her blood pressure is 84/50 mmHg, her heart rate is 128 beats per minute, and her respiratory rate is 24 breaths per minute. She is oriented to person only.

  1. The correct answer is C (Sepsis progressing toward distributive (septic) shock): a worsening known infection combined with fever, warm flushed skin, hypotension, tachycardia, and new confusion is the classic pattern of sepsis progressing toward septic shock. A simple febrile illness (A) would not include hypotension and altered mental status. Cardiogenic shock (B) is not supported since there is no evidence of cardiac pump failure and the warm flushed skin points away from it. Isolated delirium (D) ignores the vital sign abnormalities that indicate a systemic haemodynamic problem.
  2. The correct answer is B (An elevated shock index indicating significant haemodynamic compromise): dividing her heart rate of 128 by her systolic pressure of 84 gives a shock index above 1.5, well above the normal range of roughly 0.5 to 0.7, signalling significant compensatory strain and haemodynamic instability. A normal index (A) does not match these numbers. The shock index can clearly be calculated from the given values (C), and there is no reason the calculation would be falsely low (D) given the numbers provided.
  3. The correct answer is A (High-flow oxygen, an intravenous fluid bolus, and rapid transport to hospital): a patient showing early signs of septic shock needs oxygen support, fluid resuscitation to support perfusion, and prompt transport so definitive care such as antibiotics and further fluids can begin. Withholding oxygen and fluids (B) delays necessary support for a deteriorating patient. Antipyretics (C) do not address the underlying haemodynamic instability and are not the priority. Delaying transport (D) risks further deterioration and is inappropriate given her unstable vital signs.
  4. The correct answer is D (Her warm, flushed skin rather than cool and clammy skin): cardiogenic shock typically produces cool, clammy, poorly perfused skin from compensatory vasoconstriction, while her warm, flushed skin reflects the vasodilation characteristic of early distributive shock from sepsis. Tachycardia (A) occurs in both shock types as a compensatory response and does not distinguish them. Altered consciousness (B) can occur in either type from reduced cerebral perfusion. Hypotension (C) is also common to both shock types and does not by itself point to one cause over the other.
  5. The correct answer is B (Suspected sepsis from a urinary source, hypotensive and tachycardic, given oxygen and a fluid bolus en route): an effective handover clearly states the suspected diagnosis, key abnormal vital signs, and treatment already provided, allowing the receiving team to continue care efficiently. Option A omits vital signs and the suspected cause entirely. Option C is vague and provides no clinically useful information. Option D fails to convey the urgency, vital sign abnormalities, or treatment given, all of which are essential for a proper handover.
  6. The correct answer is B (He should be observed for a possible biphasic reaction after treatment): a biphasic anaphylactic reaction can recur hours after initial symptoms resolve, so ongoing observation is recommended even after successful treatment. Option A ignores this recognized risk of recurrence. Option C is incorrect since biphasic reactions can occur regardless of whether epinephrine was given initially. Option D understates the need for continued monitoring given the possibility of delayed recurrence.
  7. The correct answer is A (A catheter-related bloodstream infection originating from the vascular device): fever and chills combined with localized redness around an implanted vascular access device strongly suggest infection introduced through the catheter, a well-recognized complication in oncology patients. A viral illness (B) would not typically explain the localized redness at the port site. An allergic reaction (C) would present with different features such as rash or swelling unrelated to a fixed device site. Dehydration (D) does not explain fever, chills, or localized redness.
  8. The correct answer is C (Recognize this as a potential medical emergency requiring prompt transport): fever during chemotherapy can indicate neutropenic sepsis, a life-threatening condition that can progress extremely quickly, so it must be treated with urgency even when the patient looks otherwise well. Advising home monitoring (A) risks dangerous delay in a patient whose immune defenses may be severely compromised. Treating the call as low-acuity (B) misjudges the real risk. Waiting for further symptoms (D) could allow rapid deterioration before treatment begins.
  9. The correct answer is B (Spinal injury disrupts sympathetic response, preventing the expected tachycardia): neurogenic shock impairs the sympathetic nervous system's ability to increase heart rate in response to hypotension, producing bradycardia instead, while hypovolemic shock from bleeding still triggers a normal compensatory tachycardia. Option A is incorrect because the two shock mechanisms produce opposite heart rate responses. Option C wrongly equates tachycardia with lower severity, when it actually reflects an appropriate compensatory response to significant blood loss. Option D ignores the clear physiological link between each mechanism and its heart rate pattern.
  10. The correct answer is C (Applying a cold pack and properly immobilizing the injured limb): cold application and proper immobilization reduce swelling and limit movement at the fracture site, both of which help decrease pain without medication. Encouraging movement of the injured limb (A) would worsen pain and risk further injury. Telling the patient to ignore the pain (B) is dismissive and does nothing to actually manage it. Removing the splint periodically (D) would undo the immobilization benefit and likely increase pain and risk of further injury.
  11. The correct answer is A (A properly fit-tested N95 respirator worn throughout patient contact): active pulmonary tuberculosis is spread by airborne transmission, requiring a fit-tested N95 respirator that filters the small particles capable of remaining suspended in air. A standard surgical mask (B) does not provide adequate filtration against airborne particles. Gloves alone (C) incorrectly assumes tuberculosis is not airborne, when respiratory droplet nuclei are its primary transmission route. Requiring no protection once diagnosed (D) is dangerous and contradicts standard airborne precaution practice.
  12. The correct answer is C (Biphasic reactions can occur several hours after the initial reaction resolves): a biphasic response can develop up to several hours after apparent resolution of the initial anaphylactic episode, which is why extended observation is recommended. Option A drastically understates the true window of risk. Option B is incorrect because biphasic reactions can still occur even after appropriate epinephrine treatment. Option D overstates the risk, since not every anaphylaxis case develops a biphasic component.
  13. The correct answer is D (Hyponatremia related to her diuretic medication use): certain diuretics can cause significant sodium loss, and hyponatremia commonly presents with confusion, weakness, and nausea, especially in older adults on long-term therapy. Hyperkalemia (A) typically presents with cardiac and neuromuscular changes rather than this symptom pattern and is not linked to increased potassium intake here. Hypernatremia (B) would be an unusual effect of diuretic use and does not fit this scenario. Hypercalcemia (C) is possible in malignancy but there is no supporting history provided in this presentation.
  14. The correct answer is B (He describes a specific, immediate plan and has access to means): having a specific plan combined with access to the means to carry it out is one of the strongest indicators of acute, imminent suicide risk. A supportive family checking in daily (A) is a protective factor that tends to lower risk. No prior history of attempts or planning (C) is generally reassuring rather than concerning. Willingness to go to hospital (D) is also a positive sign suggesting some insight and cooperation, not an increased risk factor.
  15. The correct answer is A (Cardiac tamponade causing obstructive shock from the pericardial injury): jugular venous distension, muffled heart sounds, and hypotension together form Beck's triad, a classic presentation of cardiac tamponade where blood accumulating in the pericardial sac restricts the heart's ability to fill and pump. Tension pneumothorax (B) would typically present with absent or decreased breath sounds on the affected side, which contradicts the clear equal breath sounds described here. A simple pneumothorax (C) would not cause this degree of haemodynamic compromise. Hypovolemic shock from external bleeding alone (D) does not explain the jugular venous distension or muffled heart sounds.
  16. The correct answer is C (All three subtypes result primarily from widespread inappropriate vasodilation): septic, anaphylactic, and neurogenic shock are all classified as distributive because each causes widespread vasodilation and maldistribution of blood flow rather than an actual loss of volume or direct pump failure. Option A describes obstructive shock mechanisms such as tension pneumothorax or tamponade. Option B describes hypovolemic shock from actual volume loss. Option D describes cardiogenic shock from impaired cardiac pump function, a distinct category from distributive shock.
Case C0891

A primary care paramedic crew is called to a home for a 28-year-old primigravida patient at 39 weeks gestation in active labour, with the family reporting contractions two minutes apart and the patient stating she feels a strong urge to push.

  1. The correct answer is B (Crowning with the fetal head visible and bulging perineum): crowning means the fetal head is visible at the vaginal opening and is the definitive sign that delivery is seconds to minutes away. Contractions every five minutes reflect early labour, not imminent birth. Clear fluid from ruptured membranes an hour earlier does not indicate imminent delivery on its own. A patient-reported cervical dilation is unreliable and cannot be confirmed in the field, so it should never guide the decision to delay transport.
  2. The correct answer is A (Gently slip the loose loop of cord over the infant's head): a loose nuchal cord with enough slack should be gently reduced over the infant's head, allowing delivery to proceed normally without cutting. Clamping and cutting before the shoulders deliver is reserved for a cord that is wrapped too tightly to slip over the head. Pushing the head back is never appropriate once crowning has occurred and risks fetal injury. Pulling firmly on the cord can tear it or compress fetal circulation and is not indicated when the loop is loose.
  3. The correct answer is D (Flex the mother's hips sharply toward her abdomen (McRoberts position)): this maneuver, hyperflexing the mother's hips toward her abdomen, is the first-line manoeuvre for suspected shoulder dystocia because it rotates the pelvis and often frees the impacted anterior shoulder. Fundal pressure is contraindicated because it can worsen impaction of the shoulder against the pubic symphysis. Delivering the posterior arm is a secondary manoeuvre attempted only if repositioning fails. Episiotomy alone does not relieve a bony obstruction and is not the first step.
  4. The correct answer is C (Perform firm, circular fundal massage to encourage uterine contraction): a boggy, poorly contracted uterus with bleeding is classic uterine atony, and firm fundal massage stimulates the uterine muscle to contract and compress the bleeding vessels at the placental site. Leg elevation and oxygen support perfusion but do nothing to address the underlying cause of the bleeding. Vaginal packing is not part of paramedic scope and can mask ongoing blood loss. Delaying intervention risks rapid progression to hypovolemic shock.
  5. The correct answer is A (Treat for hypovolemic shock with oxygen, warmth, and rapid transport): ongoing bleeding with pallor, diaphoresis, and falling blood pressure signals decompensating hypovolemic shock from postpartum hemorrhage, so the priority shifts to aggressive shock management, including oxygen, keeping the patient warm, continuing fundal massage en route, and rapid transport to definitive care. Continuing massage alone without addressing perfusion and transport underestimates the severity of ongoing blood loss. Breastfeeding can help via natural oxytocin release but is not a substitute for shock treatment and transport. Delaying transport while a patient is actively decompensating is inappropriate.
  6. The correct answer is C (1, 2 and 3): the first stage runs from onset of regular contractions to full dilation, the second stage covers full dilation through delivery of the infant, and the third stage is delivery of the placenta, which are the three standard obstetric stages. Statement 4 is false because crowning occurs during the second stage, not the first. Statement 5 is false because a strong urge to push during the first stage before full dilation can indicate precipitous labour or a complication and should not be treated as normal.
  7. The correct answer is D (Placenta previa): placenta previa classically presents as painless, bright red vaginal bleeding in the third trimester with a soft, non-tender abdomen because the placenta implants over or near the cervix and bleeding occurs without uterine irritation. Placental abruption typically causes painful bleeding with a firm, tender, sometimes rigid uterus, the opposite of this presentation. Ectopic pregnancy occurs earlier in pregnancy, before viability, and is not consistent with a 32-week gestation. Braxton Hicks contractions are painless tightening episodes and do not cause bright red bleeding.
  8. The correct answer is B (Pre-eclampsia): pre-eclampsia is defined by new-onset hypertension after 20 weeks gestation accompanied by symptoms such as severe headache, visual changes, and edema of the hands and face, all of which this patient demonstrates. Placental abruption presents with vaginal bleeding and abdominal pain, not headache and visual disturbance as the leading complaint. Supine hypotensive syndrome causes low blood pressure from vena cava compression, the opposite of this patient's severely elevated reading. Gestational diabetes does not cause hypertension, headache, or facial edema.
  9. The correct answer is C (1, 3 and 4): eclamptic seizure management focuses on protecting the airway and preventing injury during the seizure, suctioning and providing oxygen once convulsions end, and rapid transport since eclampsia is a true obstetric emergency requiring definitive hospital care. Statement 2 is incorrect because a supine position can worsen aortocaval compression in a pregnant patient and left lateral positioning is preferred instead. Statement 5 is incorrect because physically restraining a seizing patient's limbs can cause fractures or dislocations and does nothing to stop the seizure itself.
  10. The correct answer is A (Position the mother knee-chest and manually elevate the presenting part off the cord): with a prolapsed cord, the presenting fetal part can compress the cord against the pelvis and cut off fetal circulation, so the priority is placing the mother in a knee-chest or exaggerated Sims position and using a gloved hand to manually lift the presenting part off the cord while awaiting rapid transport. Pushing the cord back inside is not recommended and can worsen compression or cause additional trauma. Encouraging pushing would drive the presenting part further onto the cord. Pulling on the cord risks vasospasm and further compromises fetal circulation.
  11. The correct answer is C (Support the delivering body and avoid pulling, allowing spontaneous delivery): in a breech delivery the paramedic's role is to support the infant's body as it delivers spontaneously, avoiding any traction that could injure the spine or cause the head to become entrapped. Pulling on the legs risks spinal injury and head entrapment, a leading cause of fetal death in breech births. Pushing the buttocks back inside is never appropriate once delivery has begun. Breech delivery is a high-risk obstetric emergency, so rapid transport should still be arranged rather than delayed on scene once feasible.
  12. The correct answer is D (Ectopic pregnancy with possible rupture): sudden severe unilateral abdominal pain, vaginal spotting, dizziness, and a missed period strongly suggest an ectopic pregnancy, which can rupture and cause life-threatening internal hemorrhage requiring urgent transport and shock precautions. An ovarian cyst rupture can cause similar pain but does not explain the missed period as directly as a pregnancy-related cause and is a less urgent working diagnosis to lead with. Normal early pregnancy cramping does not cause dizziness or severe unilateral pain. A urinary tract infection typically causes dysuria and suprapubic discomfort, not this constellation of findings.
  13. The correct answer is B (1, 2 and 3): uterine atony, retained placental tissue, and lacerations of the vagina or perineum are all recognized direct causes of excessive bleeding after delivery. Supine hypotensive syndrome is caused by the gravid uterus compressing the vena cava before delivery and is unrelated to postpartum bleeding. Braxton Hicks contractions are painless prelabour tightening episodes and have no association with postpartum hemorrhage. Only statements naming true anatomic or physiologic sources of postpartum bleeding belong in the correct combination.
  14. The correct answer is D (Provide positive pressure ventilation with a bag-valve-mask): a newborn with a heart rate below 100 and inadequate, gasping respirations needs positive pressure ventilation, which is the single most important intervention in neonatal resuscitation. Chest compressions are reserved for a heart rate below 60 despite thirty seconds of effective ventilation, which has not yet been established here. Blow-by oxygen alone does not address the inadequate respiratory effort and heart rate. A heart rate of 90 with poor respiratory effort still requires active ventilatory support, not observation alone.
  15. The correct answer is C (9): this infant scores 2 for heart rate over 100, 2 for a vigorous cry, 2 for active movement, 2 for a strong grimace reflex, and 1 for colour because acrocyanosis of the hands and feet with a pink body is common and normal, giving a total of 9. A score of 10 would require completely pink colour throughout, including the extremities. A score of 6 or 7 would require deficits in more than just the colour category, which is not supported by the described findings.
  16. The correct answer is A (Begin chest compressions coordinated with ventilation): a heart rate below 60 beats per minute after thirty seconds of effective positive pressure ventilation is the specific threshold for starting chest compressions coordinated with ventilation in neonatal resuscitation. Continuing ventilation alone without compressions is inadequate once the heart rate has fallen below 60 despite adequate ventilation. Stopping resuscitation is never appropriate at this stage of a viable, actively deteriorating newborn. Blow-by oxygen provides no positive pressure and cannot address a bradycardic, poorly perfused newborn.
  17. The correct answer is D (1, 3 and 4): drying and stimulating are initial steps that also help trigger spontaneous respirations, keeping the newborn warm reduces cold stress that worsens outcomes, and effective ventilation is the cornerstone intervention for a newborn in respiratory difficulty. Statement 2 is false because routine deep suctioning of every newborn is not recommended and is reserved for airway obstruction or when secretions are visibly compromising the airway. Statement 5 is false because compressions are never started before an initial assessment and a trial of effective ventilation.
  18. The correct answer is C (Provide immediate positive pressure ventilation, prioritizing an open airway): a non-vigorous newborn born through meconium-stained fluid needs prompt attention to establishing an open airway and initiating positive pressure ventilation without delay, since respiratory support takes priority over other steps in a depressed newborn. Vigorous stimulation before addressing the airway can waste critical time in a newborn who is already limp and poorly responsive. Clamping and cutting the cord should not delay resuscitative efforts in a depressed newborn. Chest compressions are not indicated until an inadequate response to effective ventilation has been established.
  19. The correct answer is B (The larger tongue relative to the oral cavity): the infant's proportionally larger tongue can easily fall back and occlude the airway, especially if the head is hyperextended or flexed improperly, making correct positioning in a neutral sniffing position critical. The anterior, superior larynx position affects visualization during intubation but is not the primary cause of positional airway obstruction. A floppy epiglottis increases risk during laryngoscopy and can cause obstruction on its own but is a distinct anatomic feature from tongue-related obstruction. A shorter, narrower trachea increases the risk of complete obstruction from swelling but does not relate directly to head positioning.
Case C0910

A primary care paramedic crew responds to an 18-month-old child with a barking cough and low-grade fever that began earlier in the evening and has progressed to include stridor audible at rest, prompting the caregiver to call 911.

  1. The correct answer is D (Increased work of breathing with stridor audible at rest): audible stridor at rest combined with visibly increased work of breathing reflects the work of breathing leg of the pediatric assessment triangle and signals significant airway compromise requiring urgent intervention. Playful interaction with a toy actually reflects a reassuring appearance, the opposite of a concerning finding. Normal skin colour and warm extremities reflect adequate circulation to skin, which is reassuring rather than concerning. Age-appropriate babbling and eye contact reflect a normal appearance leg and would not be the most concerning finding here.
  2. The correct answer is A (Croup (laryngotracheobronchitis)): a barking cough, low-grade fever, and gradually worsening stridor over hours are the classic presentation of croup, a viral infection causing subglottic swelling. Epiglottitis typically presents with a sudden high fever, drooling, and a toxic appearance without a barking cough, a very different picture. Foreign body aspiration usually has a sudden onset with a clear choking event, not a gradual viral prodrome. Bronchiolitis causes wheezing and crackles from lower airway involvement rather than the barking cough and stridor described here.
  3. The correct answer is B (In a position of comfort, typically upright on a caregiver's lap): a child with croup should be kept in a position of comfort, usually sitting upright on a caregiver's lap, since forcing an anxious child into an unfamiliar position can increase crying and worsen airway swelling and distress. Supine positioning can worsen airway obstruction in a child with upper airway swelling. Prone positioning is not appropriate for a distressed, conscious pediatric patient with airway compromise. Trendelenburg position has no role in managing upper airway obstruction and would increase the child's distress.
  4. The correct answer is A (Symptoms that fluctuate and partially improve with cool air exposure): croup symptoms classically wax and wane and often improve temporarily with exposure to cool or humid air, which fits this child's history of recurring episodes that respond to cool night air. Sudden onset with drooling and a muffled voice is more typical of epiglottitis, a distinct and more dangerous airway emergency. A toxic, listless appearance in a tripod position is a hallmark of epiglottitis, not typical croup. High fever with no cough at all would be atypical for croup, which classically includes the barking cough as a defining feature.
  5. The correct answer is D (It provides a rapid, hands-off general impression of illness severity): the pediatric assessment triangle is a rapid, hands-off visual assessment performed within seconds of encountering a child, used to form a general impression of illness or injury severity and guide the urgency of further assessment. It does not replace a full physical exam but instead directs how quickly that exam and treatment should proceed. It applies across the entire pediatric age range, not just children older than five. It is specifically designed to be completed before hands-on assessment or vital signs are obtained.
  6. The correct answer is C (The child is limp, will not make eye contact, and is difficult to console): a limp child who avoids eye contact and cannot be consoled demonstrates poor tone, interactiveness, and consolability, all components used to judge an abnormal appearance on the pediatric assessment triangle. Alert tracking and reaching for a toy reflects a normal, reassuring appearance. Brief crying at a stranger that settles quickly is a normal, age-appropriate response, not a red flag. A strong, equal cry with normal tone is reassuring rather than concerning.
  7. The correct answer is B (1, 2 and 3): nasal flaring, grunting, and chest wall retractions are all classic signs of increased work of breathing in infants, reflecting the body's compensatory effort to move air. A strong, vigorous cry with good eye contact reflects normal appearance, not respiratory distress. Warm, pink extremities with brisk capillary refill reflect normal circulation to skin, which is reassuring and unrelated to breathing effort. Only true respiratory effort signs belong in the correct combination, not signs from the appearance or circulation legs of the assessment.
  8. The correct answer is D (Protect the airway, monitor closely, and transport for evaluation): simple febrile seizures are typically brief and self-limiting, so paramedic management focuses on protecting the airway, closely monitoring the child, and transporting for a physician evaluation to rule out other causes of fever and seizure. Antipyretics may be given for comfort and fever control but do not stop an active seizure. Restraining a seizing child's limbs risks injury and does not shorten the seizure. Even simple febrile seizures warrant transport since an underlying source of infection and the seizure itself need medical assessment.
  9. The correct answer is B (The infant shows signs of significant dehydration): dry mucous membranes, absent tears, and a sunken fontanelle are classic signs of significant dehydration in an infant, especially in the setting of ongoing vomiting and diarrhea causing fluid losses. These findings are abnormal and clinically significant, not a normal variant for a healthy infant. Describing the infant as well hydrated directly contradicts the physical findings presented. While meningitis can also cause a bulging, not sunken, fontanelle along with other distinct signs, these particular findings point specifically to volume depletion rather than meningeal irritation.
  10. The correct answer is A (24 breaths per minute): a respiratory rate around 24 breaths per minute falls within the expected normal range for a toddler at rest, since normal pediatric respiratory rates are higher than adult rates and decrease with increasing age. A rate of 8 breaths per minute would be dangerously low and suggest respiratory depression in a toddler. A rate of 60 breaths per minute would be markedly tachypneic and concerning for respiratory distress in this age group. A rate of 80 breaths per minute would represent severe tachypnea and a significant abnormal finding requiring urgent evaluation.
  11. The correct answer is C (1, 2 and 3): tachycardia, delayed capillary refill, and cool, mottled extremities together reflect the body's compensatory vasoconstriction and increased heart rate in early shock, before blood pressure begins to fall. A child in compensated shock can still be alert initially, but combined with strong bounding pulses this would not fit the overall pattern of poor perfusion described by the other findings. Strong, bounding pulses reflect good perfusion and are inconsistent with shock, contradicting the delayed capillary refill and cool extremities in the correct combination. Only findings reflecting poor peripheral perfusion belong together as evidence of compensated shock.
  12. The correct answer is D (Document objective findings carefully and report the concern per protocol): a mechanism of injury that does not match the type of fracture is a recognized red flag for non-accidental trauma, and paramedics have a duty to objectively document exactly what was observed and reported, then follow local protocol for reporting suspected child abuse to appropriate authorities. Dismissing an inconsistency between mechanism and injury ignores a genuine warning sign that protects vulnerable children. Confronting or accusing a caregiver on scene is inappropriate, can escalate a volatile situation, and is not the paramedic's role. Failing to document the inconsistency removes crucial information from the medical and legal record.
  13. The correct answer is A (Report the suspicion directly to a children's aid society): under Ontario law, any person, including paramedics, who has reasonable grounds to suspect a child is or may be in need of protection has a legal duty to report that suspicion directly to a children's aid society without delay. The duty to report does not depend on the caregiver admitting anything, since suspicion alone based on objective findings is sufficient. Deferring the report to hospital staff does not satisfy the paramedic's own independent legal obligation to report. All regulated health professionals, including paramedics, are bound by this mandatory reporting duty.
  14. The correct answer is C (Bruising in these locations in a non-mobile infant is rarely accidental): bruising to the torso, ears, and neck, sometimes remembered by the acronym TEN, is highly specific for abusive injury in infants who are not yet mobile, since these areas are rarely bruised accidentally before a child can crawl or pull to stand. These are precisely the locations considered unusual, not common, for accidental injury in a pre-mobile infant. While bleeding disorders can occasionally cause unusual bruising, the specific pattern and location described here is a recognized abuse indicator rather than a diagnosis of a bleeding disorder. This bruising pattern is not an expected part of normal infant development.
  15. The correct answer is B (1, 2 and 4): an unexplained spiral humerus fracture, retinal hemorrhages, and posterior rib fractures of varying ages are all classic, well-documented red flags for non-accidental trauma, particularly abusive head trauma and inflicted skeletal injury. A single forehead bruise consistent with a developmentally appropriate fall while learning to sit is a typical, low-concern accidental injury. A skinned knee consistent with learning to crawl is also a normal developmental injury with a plausible mechanism. Combining these developmentally consistent, minor injuries with genuine abuse red flags would dilute rather than reflect the pattern most concerning for abuse.
  16. The correct answer is D (Abusive head trauma, historically referred to as shaken baby syndrome): a history of shaking combined with unresponsiveness and a bulging fontanelle, suggesting raised intracranial pressure, is strongly consistent with abusive head trauma from violent shaking, a life-threatening form of non-accidental trauma. A febrile seizure would not typically be preceded by a reported shaking event or produce a bulging fontanelle. Reflux-related breathing pauses do not cause a bulging fontanelle or prolonged unresponsiveness. Breath-holding spells are typically brief, self-resolving, and not associated with this history or these findings.
  17. The correct answer is A (Objectively document exact statements, physical findings, and the scene): when non-accidental trauma is suspected, documentation should objectively capture exact caregiver statements in quotation marks, precise physical findings, and relevant scene observations, since this record may later support a child protection or legal investigation. Recording personal opinions instead of objective findings can undermine the credibility and usefulness of the report. Omitting details to spare family feelings compromises the child's safety and the integrity of the record. Vague summaries reduce the value of the documentation exactly when precision matters most.
  18. The correct answer is C (Elder abuse, which can include physical, financial, and neglect components): elder abuse is a broad category that includes physical abuse, financial exploitation, emotional abuse, and neglect, and this patient shows evidence of both financial exploitation and neglect occurring together, which paramedics must recognize and report per local protocol. These findings are not simply normal age-related decline, since poor hygiene and untreated pressure sores reflect a failure of adequate care rather than expected aging. A medication side effect would not explain financial exploitation or neglect indicators together. These findings are not an unavoidable consequence of illness but a recognizable pattern of abuse or neglect requiring intervention.
  19. The correct answer is D (1, 2 and 4): untreated pressure ulcers with severe weight loss, an unclean living environment, and dehydration with confusion together form a recognizable pattern of neglect, where basic needs for hygiene, nutrition, and hydration are not being met. A recent, well-documented physician-directed medication change is an expected part of routine medical care, not a sign of neglect. A single small shin bruise with a plausible, minor explanation is a common and low-concern finding in elderly patients who bruise easily. Mixing routine, well-explained findings with the true red flags would understate the genuine concern present in this case.
  20. The correct answer is B (Polypharmacy leading to drug interactions or adverse effects): polypharmacy, the use of multiple medications simultaneously, significantly increases the risk of drug interactions, cumulative side effects, and adverse reactions, which commonly present in older adults as new confusion or unsteady gait. New confusion is not simply normal aging and always warrants a specific medical explanation and evaluation. A single missed dose is one possible contributor but does not account for the broader risk created by taking twelve concurrent medications. While dehydration can also cause confusion, it should be considered alongside polypharmacy rather than assumed as the primary explanation alone.
  21. The correct answer is C (Elderly and diabetic patients often present atypically without classic chest pain): elderly patients, particularly those with diabetes, frequently experience atypical presentations of acute coronary syndrome due to autonomic neuropathy that blunts typical pain signals, so vague symptoms like fatigue and nausea may be the only clues to underlying ischemia. Chest pain is not always present in elderly cardiac patients, which is precisely why atypical presentations are a documented clinical concern. Diabetic patients absolutely can and do experience cardiac ischemia, often with fewer typical warning signs. Atypical symptoms in elderly patients should never be dismissed as insignificant, since they can represent serious underlying pathology.
  22. The correct answer is D (Withhold resuscitation efforts in accordance with the valid form): a properly completed and valid provincial Do Not Resuscitate Confirmation Form is legally recognized and instructs paramedics to withhold resuscitation efforts such as CPR and advanced airway interventions, while still providing comfort measures. Beginning full resuscitation despite a valid, presented form contradicts the patient's documented wishes and the legal standard paramedics must follow. Paramedics can act directly on a valid form in the field and do not need to call medical control simply to confirm permission to honour it. The form is specifically designed to be actionable by paramedics on scene, not solely by physicians.
  23. The correct answer is B (1, 2 and 4): with a valid Do Not Resuscitate order, appropriate care focuses on comfort measures such as supplemental oxygen, positioning and pain management within scope, and emotional support for the family, all consistent with honouring the patient's wishes. Beginning chest compressions directly contradicts a valid order to withhold resuscitation and should not be performed. Inserting an advanced airway is an invasive resuscitative measure that also contradicts the intent of a valid DNR order. Any resuscitative intervention, including compressions or advanced airway placement, is inconsistent with honouring a confirmed DNR form.
  24. The correct answer is A (Investigate potential underlying medical causes for the fall, not just injuries): falls in elderly patients often have an underlying medical cause, such as arrhythmia, orthostatic hypotension, hypoglycemia, or stroke, so paramedics should investigate for these causes rather than assuming the fall was purely mechanical. Assuming a purely mechanical cause without further evaluation risks missing a serious, potentially recurring medical condition. Focusing only on fractures ignores the reported lightheadedness, an important clue pointing toward a medical cause. Attributing falls entirely to normal aging dismisses a symptom that frequently signals a treatable underlying problem.
  25. The correct answer is C (An acute onset with fluctuating levels of consciousness): delirium is characterized by an acute or sudden onset with fluctuating attention and level of consciousness, often triggered by an underlying medical cause such as infection or medication effect, making it a medical emergency requiring evaluation. A gradual decline in memory over years is more characteristic of dementia, a chronic progressive condition. A stable, unchanging level of alertness is inconsistent with delirium, which by definition fluctuates. A slow, progressive loss of function over time describes the typical course of dementia, not the acute presentation of delirium.
  26. The correct answer is A (Elevate the head, shoulders, and torso in a ramped position): ramping a bariatric patient, elevating the head, shoulders, and upper torso so the external ear canal aligns with the sternal notch, improves airway alignment, reduces the weight of chest and abdominal tissue on the diaphragm, and optimizes both spontaneous and assisted ventilation. Keeping the patient supine and flat allows excess tissue to compress the airway and diaphragm, worsening respiratory effort. A left lateral position alone is not the standard airway management approach for a bariatric patient in distress. Avoiding elevation entirely ignores a well-established technique specifically designed to improve airway patency in this population.
  27. The correct answer is B (1, 2 and 4): requesting additional crew or specialized lift equipment in advance, checking weight capacities of equipment, and using bariatric-rated devices when available are all essential steps to safely move a severely bariatric patient without injury to the patient or crew. Assuming standard equipment will accommodate any weight ignores documented weight limits and risks equipment failure or patient harm. Delaying a call for additional resources until after an attempted move increases risk rather than preventing it, since planning should happen before the move begins. Proper bariatric transport planning is proactive, not reactive.
  28. The correct answer is A (Abdominal and chest wall tissue is compressing the diaphragm in the supine position): in severely bariatric patients, lying flat allows excess abdominal and chest wall tissue to press upward against the diaphragm, reducing functional lung capacity and worsening oxygenation, a phenomenon sometimes called supine hypoxia in this population. Claiming the supine position has no effect ignores well-documented respiratory mechanics unique to bariatric patients. Nothing in the scenario suggests an allergic trigger, and the timing directly correlates with the position change instead. Supine positioning typically worsens, rather than improves, respiratory mechanics in bariatric patients, which is why elevated or ramped positioning is preferred.
  29. The correct answer is C (Rapid development of pressure injuries from prolonged immobilization): bariatric patients are at increased risk for rapid pressure injury development due to increased tissue weight concentrating pressure over bony prominences and reduced perfusion to compressed skin, so extended immobilization time should be minimized and skin monitored when feasible. Claiming there is no skin breakdown risk in this population directly contradicts well-established clinical evidence. Bariatric patients actually face a higher, not reduced, risk of positional skin injury compared to average-weight patients. Ongoing reassessment of skin integrity remains important throughout any prolonged transport, particularly in this higher-risk population.
  30. The correct answer is A (1, 2 and 4): pre-eclampsia is correctly defined by new hypertension with organ involvement after 20 weeks, eclampsia is specifically the occurrence of seizures in a pre-eclamptic patient, and severe headache with visual changes are recognized warning signs of worsening disease. Statement 3 is false because HELLP syndrome, involving hemolysis, elevated liver enzymes, and low platelets, is a severe variant directly related to pre-eclampsia, not an unrelated condition. Statement 5 is false because blood pressure does not automatically or immediately normalize on hospital arrival and often requires active medical management to control.
  31. The correct answer is B (Continued supportive care, shock management, and rapid transport for definitive treatment): regardless of the specific underlying cause, paramedic management of postpartum hemorrhage centres on supportive care, treating for shock, continuing appropriate measures such as fundal massage, and rapid transport, since definitive treatment of retained tissue requires hospital-based intervention. Manually removing tissue from the vaginal canal is outside paramedic scope of practice and could cause further harm. Withholding transport to first confirm an exact diagnosis delays access to definitive care the patient urgently needs. Discontinuing bleeding control measures simply because massage alone has not resolved the bleeding abandons an intervention that may still provide some benefit while other measures are arranged.
Case C0941

You are dispatched to a retirement residence for an 81-year-old female found on the bathroom floor by staff. She is alert, complains of left hip pain, and cannot bear weight. Her medication list includes warfarin, metoprolol, and a diuretic. Staff report she has been increasingly confused over the past two days and missed breakfast this morning.

  1. The correct answer is B (New confusion that is worse than her baseline mental status): occult internal bleeding, especially in a patient on warfarin, often presents subtly through altered mentation before overt haemodynamic instability appears. A normal blood pressure and normal oxygen saturation can mask early, compensated blood loss in an anticoagulated older adult. Bruising alone does not confirm the extent of internal injury following a fall. Any change in cognition from baseline should prompt a high index of suspicion for a serious underlying cause.
  2. The correct answer is D (Heart rate rising while blood pressure slowly trends downward over time): a rising heart rate paired with a falling blood pressure over serial checks is the classic trend of progressive, decompensating hemorrhage. Beta blockers such as metoprolol can blunt the tachycardic response, so even a modest rise deserves attention in this patient. A single stable reading does not rule out an evolving bleed. Trending vital signs together is far more informative than any isolated number.
  3. The correct answer is A (Splint and pad the injured limb in the position found): fractures should generally be immobilized in the position in which they are found unless distal circulation is compromised, since forced repositioning can worsen soft tissue and vascular injury. Applying traction or forcing the limb straight is not an appropriate prehospital intervention for a suspected hip fracture. Asking her to stand risks converting a stable injury into a more severe one. Gentle splinting and padding minimize further pain and tissue damage before transport.
  4. The correct answer is C (Check a blood glucose level and reassess her mental status trend): new or worsening confusion in an elderly patient always warrants an objective search for a reversible cause, and hypoglycemia from missed food intake and diuretic-related dehydration are both readily checkable on scene. Assuming confusion is simply due to age risks missing a treatable medical emergency. Documenting it as baseline without verification is inappropriate when staff themselves report a change. Prompt reassessment allows trends to be tracked and communicated to hospital staff.
  5. The correct answer is D (1, 3 and 4): the correct neonatal resuscitation sequence begins with drying, stimulating, and positioning the airway, followed by positive pressure ventilation if the newborn remains apneic or bradycardic, then reassessment of heart rate after a brief period of effective ventilation. Chest compressions are reserved for a heart rate that remains below 60 despite adequate ventilation, not started before ventilation is attempted. An oral airway is not a routine first step and is not indicated here. Ventilation, not compressions, is the priority intervention for most compromised newborns.
  6. The correct answer is A (Prepare for imminent delivery on scene rather than attempting transport): a multiparous woman with a strong urge to push and a visible or palpable presenting part is in imminent delivery, and attempting to transport in this situation risks an uncontrolled delivery in a moving ambulance. Crossing the legs cannot safely delay an imminent birth and can cause harm. High concentration oxygen has no role in slowing labour. Preparing the equipment and environment on scene for a controlled delivery is the safest course of action.
  7. The correct answer is C (The newborn's muscle tone, reflex irritability, and colour observed): the APGAR score evaluates five components, appearance, pulse, grimace, activity, and respiration, which include colour, heart rate, reflex irritability, muscle tone, and respiratory effort. Gestational age, maternal blood pressure, and cord length are not part of the scoring system at all. The score is used at one and five minutes to track how well the newborn is adapting after birth. It guides, but does not replace, ongoing clinical assessment of the newborn.
  8.
