The correct answer is B (1, 2 and 3): the pediatric assessment triangle consists of appearance, work of breathing, and circulation to skin, all assessed visually before any hands-on contact with the child. Blood pressure and capillary refill are hands-on measurements and are not part of this initial visual triangle. Appearance includes tone and interactiveness, work of breathing includes visible respiratory effort, and circulation to skin includes colour findings such as pallor or mottling. This rapid visual tool helps prioritize how quickly a child needs intervention.
  9. The correct answer is D (A ruptured ectopic pregnancy causing internal hemorrhage and shock): sudden severe unilateral pelvic pain with vaginal spotting and orthostatic dizziness in early pregnancy is a classic presentation of a ruptured ectopic pregnancy causing internal hemorrhage. Implantation bleeding and round ligament pain are typically mild and would not cause dizziness or significant pain. A urinary tract infection does not explain the sudden severe unilateral pain or the haemodynamic symptoms described. This presentation requires rapid transport and preparation for possible shock management.
  10. The correct answer is B (Support the body while allowing the head to deliver spontaneously): in a breech delivery, paramedics should support the body as it delivers and allow the head to deliver on its own without applying traction, since pulling can cause spinal or neurological injury to the infant. Pushing on the mother's abdomen does not safely assist a breech delivery and can cause harm. Manually rotating the fetus is beyond prehospital scope and can worsen the situation. Rapid transport should also be initiated given the higher risk associated with breech presentations.
  11. The correct answer is A (1, 2 and 4): beta blockers can mask a compensatory tachycardic response, elderly patients frequently have silent or painless myocardial infarctions, and blunted pain perception can hide the true severity of illness in older adults. Polypharmacy increases, rather than eliminates, the risk of drug interactions, so that statement is false. A normal blood pressure does not rule out significant compromise, particularly in someone taking antihypertensive medications. Recognizing these atypical patterns is essential to avoid underestimating illness severity in elderly patients.
  12. The correct answer is C (Attempting to visualize the airway with a tongue depressor directly): this presentation is classic for epiglottitis, and any oropharyngeal instrumentation, including a tongue depressor, can provoke laryngospasm and complete airway obstruction in a child with a swollen epiglottis. Allowing the child to remain in a position of comfort avoids unnecessary agitation that could precipitate airway compromise. Blow-by oxygen without touching the child is an appropriate non-invasive supportive measure. Prompt, calm transport while avoiding airway manipulation is the safest approach for this presentation.
  13. The correct answer is D (The child shows nasal flaring and visible intercostal retractions): nasal flaring and retractions are direct visual indicators of increased respiratory effort and fall under the work of breathing component of the pediatric assessment triangle. Pallor and mottled skin relate to circulation to skin, and being limp and uninteractive relates to appearance. Capillary refill is a hands-on finding, not part of the initial visual triangle. Recognizing which component a finding belongs to helps organize the rapid initial assessment of a sick child.
  14. The correct answer is A (Increased likelihood of adverse drug interactions and medication errors): taking numerous medications from multiple uncoordinated prescribers significantly raises the risk of drug interactions, duplicate therapies, and dosing errors in elderly patients. Polypharmacy does not decrease the likelihood of side effects and, in fact, increases it. It also does not meaningfully reduce the need for a thorough medication history, which remains essential prehospital information. There is no guarantee that taking many medications improves chronic disease management, particularly without coordinated oversight.
Case C0955

You respond to a private residence for a 3-year-old male who had a witnessed generalized seizure lasting about ninety seconds. On your arrival he is postictal, breathing adequately, with a rectal temperature of 39.4 degrees Celsius. His mother reports he had a runny nose and mild cough for the past day and has never had a seizure before.

  1. The correct answer is B (A simple febrile seizure related to the rapid rise in temperature): a brief generalized seizure in a young child with a rapidly rising fever and a viral upper respiratory illness, with no prior seizure history, is characteristic of a simple febrile seizure. Idiopathic epilepsy is not diagnosed from a single febrile event. Bacterial meningitis is possible but is not the most likely diagnosis without additional red flag findings such as nuchal rigidity. Breath-holding spells are triggered by crying or pain, not by fever, and this child's history does not support that diagnosis.
  2. The correct answer is C (A stiff neck with a bulging fontanelle on physical examination): nuchal rigidity and a bulging fontanelle are red flags suggesting meningitis or increased intracranial pressure rather than a simple febrile seizure. A single brief seizure, a short postictal period, and a mild preceding cold are all typical and expected features of a simple febrile seizure. Any sign of meningeal irritation should prompt heightened concern and prompt transport with clear communication to the receiving facility. This distinction guides the urgency of further hospital workup.
  3. The correct answer is D (Position him on his side and continuously monitor his airway closely): once a seizure has stopped, positioning the postictal child on his side protects the airway from secretions or vomitus and allows ongoing monitoring of breathing. Restraining the limbs and inserting objects into the mouth are both outdated and dangerous practices that can cause injury. Administering oral medication is inappropriate in a postictal child whose airway control has not been confirmed. Continuous reassessment of airway, breathing, and mental status is the priority after a seizure resolves.
  4. The correct answer is B (A single simple febrile seizure does not itself confirm epilepsy): most children who experience a simple febrile seizure do not go on to develop epilepsy, and a single episode alone is not diagnostic of a seizure disorder. Stating that febrile seizures often progress to epilepsy is inaccurate and could unnecessarily alarm the family. Febrile seizures are not mainly caused by an underlying seizure disorder, they are triggered by the rapid temperature change itself. Accurate, reassuring communication helps the family understand what to expect at the hospital.
  5. The correct answer is A (Report the suspected abuse to the appropriate child welfare authority): under Ontario law, any professional who has reasonable grounds to suspect a child has been or may be abused has a mandatory legal duty to report that suspicion to a children's aid society. Confronting the caregiver directly can escalate the situation and is not the paramedic's role. Remaining silent unless asked does not fulfill the legal reporting obligation. Waiting for police to independently identify the pattern does not relieve the paramedic of their own personal duty to report.
  6. The correct answer is C (1, 3 and 5): a sunken fontanelle with dry mucous membranes, decreased urine output, and tachycardia with lethargy are all recognized signs of moderate to severe dehydration in an infant. Normal capillary refill and normal skin turgor are reassuring findings that would be more consistent with mild or no dehydration, not moderate to severe dehydration. Combining several concerning findings together gives a more reliable picture of dehydration severity than any single sign alone. Recognizing this pattern helps guide the urgency of prehospital fluid management.
  7. The correct answer is D (Excess soft tissue can make airway visualization and ventilation more difficult): increased soft tissue in the neck, jaw, and pharynx in bariatric patients commonly complicates airway visualization, mask seal, and ventilation, making airway management more challenging. Bariatric patients typically have a relatively larger, not smaller, tongue contributing to this difficulty. They generally have reduced, not increased, functional respiratory reserve due to decreased chest wall compliance. Anticipating these challenges allows paramedics to prepare additional resources and positioning strategies in advance.
  8. The correct answer is C (Consider intravenous fluid therapy per local protocol while monitoring closely): a toddler with moderate dehydration who cannot tolerate oral fluids may require intravenous fluid therapy per local protocol to prevent progression to hypovolemic shock. Withholding all fluids until hospital arrival could allow dehydration to worsen during transport. Encouraging large volumes of oral fluid is inappropriate when the child has already been unable to tolerate oral intake. A warming blanket does not address the underlying fluid deficit and is not the primary treatment for dehydration.
  9. The correct answer is A (1, 2, 3 and 5): severe headache and visual changes in late pregnancy with significantly elevated blood pressure are consistent with worsening pre-eclampsia, eclampsia is specifically defined by seizure activity in this context, magnesium sulfate is a recognized first-line agent to prevent seizures, and reducing environmental stimulation can help lower seizure risk. A blood pressure below 140/90 mmHg would not support a diagnosis of pre-eclampsia, so that statement is false. Recognizing these features early allows paramedics to anticipate and prepare for a possible eclamptic seizure during transport.
  10. The correct answer is B (Wrap and transport the placenta with the mother for hospital examination): the delivered placenta should be wrapped and transported with the mother so hospital staff can examine it for completeness, since retained fragments can cause ongoing postpartum hemorrhage. Discarding the placenta removes important diagnostic information from the receiving team. Attempting to manually separate fragments is beyond prehospital scope once the placenta has already delivered. Applying traction is inappropriate once delivery has already occurred spontaneously and completely.
  11. The correct answer is D (Dry the newborn thoroughly and wrap in a warm blanket): newborns are highly susceptible to rapid heat loss through evaporation and their large surface area relative to body mass, so drying and wrapping in a warm blanket is a priority action immediately after delivery. Bathing the newborn promptly actually increases heat loss and is not recommended in the immediate postpartum period. Placing the newborn on a cold surface or leaving them uncovered would worsen heat loss rather than prevent it. Maintaining warmth supports the newborn's overall physiologic stability during transport.
  12. The correct answer is A (1, 2 and 4): croup typically develops gradually after upper respiratory symptoms and results from subglottic swelling, usually of viral origin, while epiglottitis classically presents with drooling and a tripod-like upright sitting posture. Examining the oropharynx with a tongue depressor is not safe in suspected epiglottitis because it can trigger complete airway obstruction. Epiglottitis has actually become far less common, not more common, since routine Haemophilus influenzae type B vaccination began. Correctly distinguishing these two conditions changes how paramedics manage the airway on scene.
  13. The correct answer is C (Honour the valid DNR order and withhold resuscitative interventions accordingly): when a DNR order is presented and the identifying information matches the patient, paramedics should honour the documented wishes and withhold resuscitation. Beginning full resuscitation despite a valid, presented form contradicts the patient's documented wishes and the legal standard paramedics must follow. Delaying to contact medical control is unnecessary once the order is confirmed valid and matches the patient. Performing resuscitation only until hospital arrival still violates the patient's documented wishes and is not appropriate.
  14. The correct answer is B (Keep the child calm in a position of comfort with the caregiver): a toddler with mild croup symptoms and stridor only with crying, who is currently calm and comfortable, benefits most from staying in a position of comfort with a parent to minimize agitation that could worsen airway swelling. Separating the child from the caregiver or laying him supine can increase crying and distress. Advanced airway management is not indicated for a child with mild symptoms and reassuring vital signs. Minimizing distress helps prevent escalation of the airway obstruction.
  15. The correct answer is D (1, 2, 4 and 5): posterior rib fractures in a non-mobile infant, along with bruises in multiple stages of healing, are recognized red flags for non-accidental trauma, and Ontario law makes reporting suspected child abuse a mandatory duty requiring objective, factual documentation. Paramedics do not have legal discretion to decide against reporting when abuse is reasonably suspected, making that statement false. Accurate, non-speculative documentation supports any subsequent investigation. Recognizing these injury patterns is an essential skill for identifying children who may be at risk.
Case C0970

You are called to a residence for a 32-year-old female at 34 weeks gestation who reports sudden onset of painless, bright red vaginal bleeding while resting in bed. She denies contractions or abdominal trauma. Her skin is pale and slightly diaphoretic, and her heart rate is 112 beats per minute.

  1. The correct answer is C (Placenta previa presenting as painless third trimester vaginal bleeding): sudden, painless, bright red vaginal bleeding in the third trimester without contractions or trauma is the classic presentation of placenta previa. Placental abruption typically presents with painful, tender, and often rigid abdominal findings, which are absent here. Normal bloody show is usually scant and mucoid, not the significant bright red bleeding described. A ruptured ectopic pregnancy occurs early in pregnancy, not at 34 weeks gestation, making it inconsistent with this presentation.
  2. The correct answer is A (Left lateral position to relieve pressure on the vena cava): positioning a pregnant patient in the left lateral position relieves compression of the inferior vena cava by the gravid uterus, improving venous return and cardiac output, which is especially important in a patient showing early signs of shock. An upright sitting position does not address vena cava compression. A supine flat position can worsen hypotension through vena cava compression, and elevating the legs does not correct the underlying issue. Prone positioning is not appropriate or feasible for a pregnant patient.
  3. The correct answer is B (Early compensated hemorrhagic shock from ongoing significant vaginal blood loss): a rising heart rate in the setting of significant vaginal bleeding suggests the body is compensating for volume loss, an early warning sign of hemorrhagic shock. Pre-eclampsia is associated with elevated blood pressure and other findings, not vaginal bleeding, and is not supported by this presentation. This tachycardia is not a normal, unrelated finding given the reported bleeding. Painless bleeding in placenta previa does not necessarily mean delivery is imminent, so anticipating shock is the priority.
  4. The correct answer is D (Performing any digital vaginal examination to assess her cervical dilation): a digital vaginal examination should never be performed in a patient with suspected placenta previa, because it can disrupt the low-lying placenta and provoke catastrophic hemorrhage. Early notification to the receiving obstetrical unit allows the hospital team to prepare appropriately. Establishing intravenous access and continuous reassessment support ongoing monitoring for shock. Supplemental oxygen with saturation monitoring is an appropriate supportive measure for this patient.
  5. The correct answer is C (A prolonged labour with an overdistended uterus from multiple gestation): an overdistended uterus, such as from a multiple gestation pregnancy, along with a prolonged labour, impairs the uterus's ability to contract effectively after delivery, increasing the risk of postpartum hemorrhage from uterine atony. A short, uncomplicated labour and an uncomplicated singleton term pregnancy are not associated with increased hemorrhage risk. Delivery of the placenta within five minutes is a normal, reassuring finding rather than a risk factor. Recognizing risk factors helps paramedics anticipate and prepare for postpartum complications.
  6. The correct answer is A (1, 2, 3 and 5): requesting additional resources early, anticipating that standard equipment weight limits may be exceeded, recognizing reduced functional respiratory reserve, and using a semi-Fowler's position to improve comfort are all accurate considerations when managing a bariatric patient. Bariatric patients often do require supplemental oxygen due to reduced respiratory reserve, making the statement that they never need it false. Planning ahead for equipment, staffing, and positioning improves both patient safety and crew safety during a bariatric transport. These considerations should be addressed as early as possible in the call.
  7. The correct answer is B (Infants have a proportionally larger tongue relative to a smaller airway): an infant's tongue is proportionally larger relative to the small size of the oral cavity and airway, making airway obstruction more likely, particularly when the infant is supine or the neck is flexed. Infants do not have a smaller tongue relative to their oral cavity, the opposite is true. Their trachea is soft and more easily compressible, not rigid, compared to an adult's. Infants typically have a higher, not lower, resting respiratory rate than adults.
  8. The correct answer is D (Request additional resources and appropriate specialized equipment before moving him): moving a bariatric patient through a narrow stairwell safely requires planning ahead, including requesting additional personnel and specialized bariatric equipment suited to the space and weight involved. Attempting the move immediately without planning risks injury to both the patient and the crew. Asking an anxious, heavy patient to walk down stairs unassisted introduces unnecessary risk of falls. Improvising without a plan increases the likelihood of a dangerous outcome during a technically difficult move.
  9. The correct answer is A (1, 2 and 4): unexplained injuries inconsistent with the given history warrant further inquiry, speaking with the patient privately when feasible can reveal important information, and suspected elder abuse should be documented objectively and reported through appropriate channels. A valid DNR order reflects end-of-life wishes and does not by itself indicate a history of abuse or neglect. A reluctant caregiver alone is a concerning observation but is not, on its own, sufficient proof that abuse has occurred. Combining multiple findings together supports a more reliable suspicion of elder abuse.
  10. The correct answer is C (Elderly patients often present with silent or atypical ischemic symptoms): elderly patients, and diabetic patients in particular, frequently present with atypical or silent cardiac ischemia, such as vague fatigue or nausea, rather than classic chest pain. Elderly patients absolutely can and do experience myocardial infarction, making that statement false. Fatigue and nausea can indeed be associated with cardiac ischemia, especially in older or diabetic patients. This presentation does not rule out a cardiac cause and should prompt cardiac monitoring and further evaluation.
  11. The correct answer is B (Provide routine care, including drying, warming, and clearing the airway): a vigorous newborn with a strong cry, good tone, and a heart rate above 100 beats per minute, even with meconium staining, should receive routine newborn care rather than invasive suctioning. Immediate intubation and deep suctioning are reserved for a non-vigorous newborn in significant distress, not this presentation. Withholding stimulation is inappropriate for a newborn who is already breathing and crying well. Delaying warming could increase the risk of neonatal heat loss unnecessarily.
  12. The correct answer is D (1, 2, 3 and 5): heart rate assessment by auscultating the precordium, initiating ventilation for a heart rate below 100, beginning chest compressions when the heart rate stays below 60 despite adequate ventilation, and correcting ventilation before starting compressions are all accurate principles of neonatal resuscitation. Umbilical cord pulsation is not considered the most reliable method to confirm heart rate compared to auscultation, making that statement false. Ensuring effective ventilation is always addressed before escalating to chest compressions is a core resuscitation principle. Accurate, repeated heart rate assessment guides every step of the resuscitation sequence.
  13. The correct answer is C (Young age with a viral prodrome and a first wheezing episode): bronchiolitis typically occurs in infants under two years old during a first wheezing episode preceded by several days of viral upper respiratory symptoms, matching this presentation closely. A strong family history of asthma would raise suspicion for asthma rather than bronchiolitis. Immediate resolution of wheeze with a bronchodilator is more typical of asthma, whereas bronchiolitis often responds poorly to bronchodilators. Recurrent prior wheezing episodes over time would suggest asthma rather than a first bronchiolitis presentation.
  14. The correct answer is B (Record objective findings and the caregiver's exact stated explanation verbatim): documentation of a suspected non-accidental injury should be limited to objective clinical findings and the caregiver's exact stated explanation, without adding personal opinions or speculation about guilt. Recording personal suspicions is inappropriate and can undermine the credibility and legal usefulness of the report. Omitting the injury entirely fails the paramedic's professional and legal responsibilities. A vague general summary without specific detail provides little useful information to child protective services or investigators.
  15. The correct answer is A (1, 2 and 4): bronchiolitis is most commonly caused by respiratory syncytial virus, infants under one year are at higher risk of significant respiratory distress from it, and nasal suctioning can meaningfully improve an infant's work of breathing by clearing obstructing secretions. Bronchiolitis primarily affects the small airways and bronchioles, not the large upper airway, making that statement false. Wheezing in an infant does not always indicate asthma, since bronchiolitis is a far more common cause of first-time wheezing at this age. Recognizing the typical age group and cause supports appropriate prehospital management.
  16. The correct answer is D (Placental abruption presenting with painful bleeding and uterine rigidity): sudden, severe, constant abdominal pain with a firm, rigid uterus and dark vaginal bleeding is the classic presentation of placental abruption, where the placenta separates prematurely from the uterine wall. Placenta previa typically presents with painless, bright red bleeding rather than pain and rigidity. Normal Braxton Hicks contractions are intermittent and not associated with constant severe pain or bleeding. Round ligament pain is a mild, self-limited discomfort unrelated to bleeding or uterine rigidity.
  17. The correct answer is C (Untreated pressure ulcers along with signs of poor personal hygiene): untreated pressure ulcers combined with poor personal hygiene in a patient with an available caregiver are significant indicators of possible neglect and should raise suspicion for elder abuse. A single bruise consistent with a plausible explanation is not, by itself, a strong indicator of abuse. Clean clothing and an adequately stocked home are reassuring findings rather than concerning ones. A caregiver who answers questions calmly does not rule out neglect, but the absence of concerning physical findings would be reassuring, which is not the case here.
  18. The correct answer is A (1, 2 and 3): uterine atony is the most common cause of postpartum hemorrhage, fundal massage helps stimulate uterine contraction to control bleeding, and breastfeeding can promote the release of oxytocin that helps the uterus contract. A boggy uterus after delivery reflects poor uterine tone rather than an unseparated placenta, since the scenario describes delivery already having occurred. Postpartum hemorrhage absolutely can cause significant hypovolemic shock, even in a previously healthy patient, making that statement false. Recognizing and addressing uterine atony promptly is essential to controlling postpartum bleeding.
  19. The correct answer is B (Decreased tears, dry lips, and a sunken anterior fontanelle noted): decreased tears, dry mucous membranes, and a sunken fontanelle are classic clinical signs of dehydration in an infant. Moist mucous membranes with normal urine output are reassuring findings inconsistent with significant dehydration. Brisk capillary refill and normal skin turgor also suggest adequate hydration status rather than dehydration. An alert, playful infant with a strong cry is a reassuring sign, whereas dehydrated infants often appear lethargic or irritable instead.
  20. The correct answer is D (After brief delay per local protocol once the newborn is stable): current practice generally supports a brief delay before clamping and cutting the umbilical cord in a vigorous, stable newborn, following local protocol, to allow additional placental blood transfusion to the infant. Clamping immediately at the moment of head delivery is not standard practice and is premature. Waiting until after full placental delivery unnecessarily delays cord management for a stable newborn. Paramedics are trained and equipped to clamp and cut the cord when appropriate, so suggesting they rarely should is inaccurate.
  21. The correct answer is A (1, 2 and 4): a prolapsed umbilical cord is a true obstetrical emergency because the presenting fetal part can compress the cord and cut off fetal circulation, so rapid transport, a knee-chest or Trendelenburg position, and gently inserting gloved fingers to hold the presenting part off the cord are all correct actions. Manually pushing the cord back inside is not recommended and can worsen cord compression or introduce infection. Applying traction to the cord is dangerous and can cause fetal injury or cord avulsion. Relieving pressure on the cord while transporting rapidly gives the fetus the best chance of adequate perfusion.
  22. The correct answer is C (Begin resuscitation since the DNR cannot be confirmed as valid): when the identifying information on a DNR form does not match the patient in front of them, paramedics cannot confirm the order applies to this specific patient and should proceed with resuscitation. Honouring an order that cannot be verified as belonging to this patient risks withholding care inappropriately. Simply having a DNR form physically present is not sufficient without confirmed identity matching. Verbal family confirmation does not substitute for a properly matched, valid written order in this situation.
  23. The correct answer is B (Pallor, mottling, or cyanosis observed on the child's skin): circulation to skin is assessed visually by looking for pallor, mottling, or cyanosis, which reflect how well the child's circulatory system is perfusing peripheral tissues. Stridor or grunting relates to the work of breathing component, not circulation. Eye contact and interactiveness relate to the appearance component of the triangle. Accessory muscle use is also part of the work of breathing component rather than circulation to skin.
  24. The correct answer is D (The fetus remains perfused, making rapid transport a priority now): a palpable pulse in a prolapsed cord confirms the fetus is still receiving blood flow, making immediate relief of cord compression and rapid transport to hospital for emergency delivery the priority. It does not mean the fetus has died, which would be suggested by an absent pulse instead. Clamping and cutting the cord in this situation would eliminate the fetus's only source of oxygenated blood and must never be done. Delivery should not be rushed on scene, since ongoing management of cord compression during transport is the priority.
  25. The correct answer is C (An appropriately sized cuff is needed for an accurate blood pressure reading): using a blood pressure cuff sized appropriately for a bariatric patient's larger arm circumference is essential, since an undersized cuff can produce falsely elevated readings. Bariatric patients actually have a higher, not lower, baseline risk of cardiovascular disease due to associated comorbidities. Cuff size absolutely affects blood pressure accuracy, contrary to the claim that it does not matter. Increased chest wall tissue can also affect ECG electrode contact and signal quality, so that consideration cannot be ignored either.
  26. The correct answer is A (They typically occur between six months and five years of age): simple febrile seizures most commonly occur in children between six months and five years of age during a rapid rise in temperature. They are not frequently caused by a central nervous system infection, most occur with common viral illnesses and no underlying neurological pathology. A single simple febrile seizure does not automatically require long-term daily anticonvulsant therapy. Febrile seizures are actually uncommon in children over ten years old, making that statement inaccurate.
  27. The correct answer is B (A syncopal or cardiac event may have caused the fall itself): in elderly patients, a fall without an obvious trip hazard should raise suspicion that a medical event, such as a cardiac arrhythmia or syncopal episode, may have precipitated the fall rather than a purely mechanical cause. Elderly patients can and do experience medical events that lead to falls, so assuming this rarely happens would be inaccurate. Not every fall in an elderly patient is purely mechanical, particularly when confusion or an unclear sequence of events is present. Investigating the underlying cause does not need to delay appropriate trauma assessment and can occur alongside it.
  28. The correct answer is D (Use a properly sized mask that creates an adequate face seal): an appropriately sized neonatal mask that creates a good seal over the mouth and nose is essential for delivering effective positive pressure ventilation to a newborn. Using an adult-sized mask would prevent an adequate seal and risk ineffective ventilation or injury. Ventilations should be delivered gently, not forcefully, to avoid barotrauma to fragile neonatal lungs. Heart rate must be reassessed periodically during resuscitation to determine whether the current intervention is effective or needs to be escalated.
  29. The correct answer is C (Epiglottitis incidence has significantly decreased in vaccinated populations overall): widespread Haemophilus influenzae type B vaccination has dramatically reduced the incidence of epiglottitis, since this bacterium was previously the leading cause of the condition in children. It is not accurate to say incidence has risen because of vaccination, the relationship is the opposite. Stating vaccination had no measurable effect contradicts well documented epidemiological trends. Epiglottitis certainly still occurs in unvaccinated or under-vaccinated children and is not exclusive to fully vaccinated ones.
  30. The correct answer is A (Signs of hypovolemic shock with severe unilateral abdominal pain present): the combination of severe unilateral pain with pallor, diaphoresis, tachycardia, and dizziness on standing indicates hypovolemic shock from internal bleeding, which is the key distinguishing feature of a ruptured ectopic pregnancy rather than normal pregnancy discomfort. Mild bilateral cramping without other symptoms is far more consistent with normal early pregnancy changes. Breast tenderness and fatigue are common, expected findings of normal early pregnancy rather than a warning sign. Mild morning nausea is also a typical, benign finding in early pregnancy that does not suggest an emergency.
  31. The correct answer is B (Face the patient directly, speak clearly, and allow extra time to respond): facing a hearing-impaired patient directly allows them to see facial expressions and lip movements, and speaking clearly while allowing extra time improves comprehension and reduces anxiety. Speaking loudly and quickly can distort speech and increase the patient's frustration rather than helping. Directing all questions to family members bypasses the patient's own ability to participate in their care whenever possible. Avoiding questions the patient may struggle to hear denies them the chance to communicate important information about their condition.
